The Thing With Feathers

by thetalee

Summary

After disappearing for a month, or four years and change from his perspective, Buck has returned to everyone he left behind. Except things don't miraculously fix themselves over night...

Or: In which Buck made a choice, that wasn't really much of a choice, and is now saddled with the consequences.

Notes

This story continues on from Chapter 73 of Hold Steady, Hold Steady.

See the end of the work for more notes

The Fractured Man

Once upon a time, on a day that was supposed to be about togetherness and love and family, Eddie gathered everyone around - everyone who was still around - and broke Buck’s heart with a simple sentence:

“I’m leaving the One Eighteen.”

Throughout Buck’s life, being left behind was the norm. It was the expected outcome of all his relationships, to the point that he clung tightly to everyone around him that stuck around for longer than a week. Perhaps too tightly, too unwisely. He knew, he had been taught, that everyone would end up leaving him eventually, even if he had managed to delude himself that certain people were exempt from that rule. And so after hearing that announcement - only three months after he was left behind by his sister, again; only three months after his friend, after someone who had been a brother to him, had left as well, taking Buck’s niece with him and cutting him out of their life - he did something he would never quite manage to forgive himself for.

Buck panicked, and Buck ran away.

His ill-thought out flight ended with him getting horribly, terribly lost. He found himself at a crossroads, meeting a man who called himself Ichabod. The man, who was much more than a simple stranger, asked him what he wanted, and Buck could not find it in him to resist answering. So he said,

“I want to fix whatever broke my family but I don’t even know what that was. I don’t even know when it was.”

The entity that wasn’t a demon gave him what he wanted, or at least he tried to. Buck woke up on the morning of his first day as an actual firefighter, four years and some months earlier. He had been convinced it was a dream, until he walked into the station to faces he knew so very well. From then on, it was a matter of retreading a path he had already walked, and avoiding the pitfalls he knew were to come. He relived those four years of his life, changing what he could and mitigating what he couldn’t. He saved Shannon and he protected Maddie; he avoided repeating his own mistakes; he did his best to keep Athena and Eddie safe, ending up with a cracked skull for one, and letting himself get shot for the other.

He got together with Eddie, after struggling with the fact that he was not the same Eddie he had known before. He loved Eddie, and Eddie loved him. They were happy.

And then, it turned out that Buck hadn’t been fixing things at all, but was simply keeping them from breaking to begin with. He was not retracing his steps, but making a new path, while everyone he had known and loved had been left behind. Ichabod - a being who had as much of a choice in anything as Buck did, which was to say, very little - had a solution that was as unacceptable as it was existentially terrifying.

“I send you back to Edmundo and all the others you left behind, let you try again at actually fixing things.”

“And Eddie? Chris?” Buck had asked. “The ones I’d be abandoning if I did that?”

“I erase them, obviously. Trim the branch like all the others. You can’t abandon them if they no longer exist.”

Ichabod was bound by his nature to give him what he wanted. Buck had said he wanted to fix things, but he wasn’t truthfully fixing anything, and so Ichabod truthfully had very little choice in the matter. Yet with some desperation, and some twisting of the rules to his own ends, Buck managed to find a way that they both got what they wanted, what they needed.

A solution that resulted in his situation now.

It had been over a month since Buck’s return from the crossroads, since he again told Ichabod what he wanted and this time got it exactly. And now he was existing in a state of limbo, finding it difficult to adjust again to a new- an old life. He couldn’t decide if he was surrounded by ghosts, or if he himself was the ghost. This timeline was so different from the other, yet similar enough to be agony.

Nights at casa de Diaz were spent on the couch, when before he would have spent them in Eddie’s bed. He accompanied Edmundo to and from Frank’s office for the therapy he had willingly started going to, after the encounter with the Eddie of the other timeline. Edmundo was still working as the department’s liaison at the dispatch center, not quite ready to return to the station. 

The others - the people he had once considered family - were all concerned about him. They had spent a month wondering what had happened to him, and when he miraculously returned to them, it was as a changed man. He had made a choice that wasn’t really a choice, and now he was stuck wallowing in the consequences of it as another version of himself reaped the benefits, but Buck couldn’t be too bitter about it. Or at least, he tried not to be. Not when the alternative involved him getting stuck here anyway, while the other timeline was erased entirely.

The night that Edmundo had offered a spot in his bed, Buck had so desperately wanted to just fall into his arms. It would have been easy, so very easy, to just pretend that he was the Eddie from the other timeline. But as he said, it wouldn’t have been fair to him, and it wouldn’t have been fair to himself.

It was the last night he spent at the Diaz home. In the time since, he still took Edmundo to his appointment with Frank, and he still went over to hang out with both him and Chris, but afterwards he started insisting on going back to the loft after the kid was in bed. It was easier, though it wasn’t less painful. He knew it was hurting Edmundo, but that was inevitable with every path forward that he could see, and at least this one seemed to promise the least amount.

The problem with the loft, however, was that it was even less of a home now than it had been before his unexpected journey. He spent most nights in that bed tossing and turning, unable to fall asleep, and when he did, he was usually woken up by nightmares. The old nightmares, the ones he would have called Eddie about. And during the day, he had absolutely nothing to fill his time. He just existed, and continued to exist, and found himself wishing that he could stop existing.

It was a dangerous wish.

Trying to reconnect with the people who had once been his family felt impossible at times, and like he was trying to squeeze himself back into his childhood clothes. It was different and somehow worse than how it had been when he first found himself back in time. Those versions of everyone hadn't any preconceived notions. He was an utter stranger to them, and while it had been painful to have them look through him, it hadn’t been anywhere near like this.

He had been so close to these people once, and now it felt like there was an impassable gulf between them. It was there with Bobby, and Maddie, and Hen, and Chimney, and Athena, and all the others. It was even there with Edmundo, though it wasn’t as bad. He had changed, it was impossible for him not to, and he could easily imagine the conversations they were having behind his back, trying to figure out what exactly had happened.

They all voiced concerns about how tired he was looking, or at least the ones who bothered to visit him did. Aside from Edmundo, it was mostly just Athena and Hen, and occasionally Maddie, although his sister was still struggling with her own mental health. Bobby seemed to mostly be content with getting updates about his well-being from his wife, much as he was with getting updates about Edmundo from May.

Buck tried not to let the man’s hands-off approach bother him, tried not to let it get to him that the only person from the station that was regularly checking on him was Hen, except he really couldn’t help it. It was just a confirmation of all his fears from when he met Red. The fact that no one else but him was really talking to Edmundo, either, really just highlighted it.

“Before you came home, the last time I saw Bobby was New Years Eve, just before I officially transferred to the dispatch center,” Edmundo had said, a few nights before he’d invited him to his bed. “I talked to Chimney right after he and Maddie came back, and then again when we were supposed to be cleaning your loft.”

“What about Hen?” Buck had asked, because by this point her visits to see him at the loft had become a regular thing.

“A couple of times, when I took Chris over to see Denny.”

“Life gets busy, especially when you don’t work at the same firehouse anymore,” Buck quoted, the words seared into his brain.

Eventually, however, Buck decided to finish his leave and go back to work. Not because he was desperately looking forward to it, not because he was emotionally prepared to try and rebuild what he had, but because vying with a station full of ghosts was a better alternative to haunting the loft.

It was like an out of body experience, working at this particular 118 again. Buck spent his first shift back, and the ones after, feeling disconnected from everything. Much like when he returned to the past, he kept turning his head and expecting Eddie to be there. It was just awkward, everything about it was awkward, especially Chimney.

The paramedic was obviously still feeling guilty for his actions before he ran off to chase after Maddie, and he clearly didn’t believe him about it being all water under the bridge. Perhaps it was the coldly delivered death threat Buck had given him, to warn him away from behaving like that with anyone else in the future. Whatever the cause, the man couldn’t seem to decide whether to avoid him completely or to go out of his way to make amends.

Then there was Bobby, who cared. Buck knew that he cared. Except he didn’t care enough to actually see him in person after the impromptu reunion at the hospital. And some of that was Buck’s fault, yes. Athena had given him multiple invites to dinner at the Grant-Nash house, but he just hadn’t been up to it yet. It had been a two-way street, though, and Bobby had simply settled for checking on him via his wife.

On his first day back, the captain had made noises about his obviously worn appearance. Buck wasn’t certain if he’d been gearing up to “suggest” he take more time off, but he’d shut it down quickly and efficiently anyway. It rankled him, that the man couldn’t be arsed enough to actually see him himself, but still thought he could claim to be worried about him. And perhaps, he’d been a bit rude about it.

“This is literally the first time you’ve seen me since Athena dragged me to the hospital, Cap,” he had said. “You don’t get to play the concerned father card now.”

In response, Bobby had made him the man behind for the entire rest of the day. The whole thing reminded Buck that he hadn’t slapped this version of the captain upside the head with a lesson on objectivity. While he had kept the other Bobby from making mistakes, this one hadn’t ever learned from his.

Things with Hen and Ravi weren’t nearly as strained, although that wasn’t to say things were easy. Hen had dropped by the loft at least a couple times a week to see him, and so her concern about his well-being wasn’t nearly as galling as Bobby’s. And with how little time they actually spent together, compared to everyone else, Ravi had next to no pre-existing ideas about how Buck should be.

Oh, he surely noticed that Buck was more withdrawn than he had been before Christmas, but he was perfectly willing to let it be. As a consequence, Buck spent most of his time on shifts with Ravi, whenever he wasn’t avoiding everyone entirely. Or whenever he was desperate to distract himself from the glaring absence of a certain someone.

Buck could have easily slapped on the fake smile and joked with everyone and acted like everything was fine, but that required more energy and effort than he honestly had. It was exhausting to come into work every day and be reminded that these were not the same people he had spent the last four years with. It was exhausting to spend empty hours at the loft while Edmundo worked a regular nine to five. It was exhausting to find himself missing “his” family, when these versions had been the ones he knew first.

It was exhausting, to wake up with the incessant and urgent need to scrub at his hands until his skin was red and raw, again. To wake up clutching at his leg as he remembered in vivid details the myriad of ways it was injured that were somehow worse than the truck. To wake up remembering the look on Eddie’s face as he desperately tried to keep him from drowning, or the way he had screamed when he watched Buck fall off the cliff. To wake up from all the scenarios his brain cooked up for whatever followed his litany of deaths in the timelines Ichabod claimed to have torn from the tree of time, of the way he knew a version of Eddie had walked into the kitchen after the tsunami to find him gone, too.

It was exhausting to wake up from dreams about Eddie, any dream about Eddie, only to be constantly reminded that he was alone in his bed.

One night, at the end of his first week back at the station, he finally cracked. Just a little. But he gave in and called Edmundo, after tossing and turning in his bed for what felt like forever. Despite the late hour, the other man had answered right away, a reminder that he was still having trouble sleeping, too.

“It was real, right?” Buck asked, because he needed to know, he needed that reassurance. The question had started haunting him days ago, and it hadn’t let up. “I’m not crazy? I didn’t just disappear for a month and dream it all up?”

“I don’t know about everything,” Edmundo said. “But I know you didn’t hallucinate the crossroads, or the man with the black eyes, or the other me. Or if you did, then I did too.”

“Okay. Okay,” he breathed out. “It’s just… Some days I sit here and I can’t help but wonder…”

“You don’t ever talk about them,” the other man noted. “About what happened. Do you want to?”

Yes, he wanted to say.

“No,” he said instead. “Thank you, but… But no.”

They didn’t talk for long, and Buck ended up laying awake for hours after.

Being here was a mistake, and Buck knew it was a mistake.

They’d responded to a call involving a family being held hostage with a bomb in their truck, set to explode if they dropped below a certain speed. Apparently, it was ripped straight out of some movie, but he had never heard of it before. Chimney had seemed torn about whether or not to rib him for it.

Buck had ended up leaping from the 118’s ladder truck into the bed of the family’s pick-up, while someone from another station did the same. From there, they had worked together to evacuate the family. It had been odd to hear Edmundo’s voice coming from dispatch. It had been stressful to learn about the pressure switch, and the danger of moving the driver.

Buck had been intending to put his own foot on the pedal and shove the dad out the back window. But then Lucy Donato - from the 147 - had ended up solving it herself while he was getting the man’s wife to Ravi, in a move about as reckless as what he was planning to do, if in a different way.

But all that brought him here, to the mistake he was in the process of making. Because after shift, one of them - not him - had the brilliant idea of celebrating at a pub. So now here he was, stuck at a table surrounded by people who used to know him so well, and joined by someone that was an actual stranger. Surrounded by people that didn’t know how he went into the same spiral as Bobby had, so many years before the captain met any of them.

Buck hadn’t really wanted to go out, and had originally intended to go straight to the Diaz residence after shift. He probably should have anyway, but more and more it seemed to just be a reminder of what he didn’t have any more. The other alternative was to go to the empty loft, and that was its own version of hell. So he allowed himself to be cajoled into going, and he sat down with them as Lucy gushed about being in the news, and Bobby bragged about Minnesota, and Chimney and Hen ragged on him for it.

And then Lucy ordered him a drink.

He should have just left it on the table and ignored it completely. It would have been odd to the others, it would have been another strike against him in the tally he was certain they’d started since his return, but he knew it would have been the smart thing to do. He willfully did the stupid thing instead, and threw it back like it was water. 

And then he ordered another.

One night, for just one night, he wanted to be numb. For one night, he wanted to forget. He wanted to forget about Eddie, about Ichabod, about Doug. He wanted to forget that home was no longer home, or rather, that he was no longer able to go home. He wanted to forget about the time he drowned while his leg was crushed by a sunken car, with Eddie desperately trying to free him. He wanted to forget about the sight of Chris with his chest so very still, and the time he slit his arm open and let himself bleed out because he was fully convinced it would fix everything. He wanted to forget the sight of his blood on Eddie’s shocked face, and the feel of Edmundo’s blood on his own.

(He wanted to forget that moment earlier in the night, when he watched the family’s truck explode and found himself wishing he had been in it.)

So one drink became two became four became too many. He played pool with Lucy, who had never met him before and so didn’t look at him with that mixture of concern and disappointment that was almost a constant in everyone’s else’s gaze. She kept ordering him drinks, too, because she didn’t know better and he hadn’t told anyone here. What would he have said, anyway? That in the month he claimed not to remember, he suddenly turned into a teetotaler?

It would be fine. He’d have this one, single night of letting loose, of being drunk and happy, and then tomorrow he’d go back to being sober and not-happy. He was fine, and this was fine, and everything was fine.

Lucy kissing him was fine, too. It was after a round of margaritas, in the middle of a game of pool. She had been flirting with him, and he might have been flirting back, because it was fun and meaningless and the attention felt good. And she kissed him, and he kissed her back, because it was nice. Kissing was nice, kissing was fun. And it had been so long, too long, since he had kissed Eddie.

Except he wasn’t kissing Eddie, and the realization was a kick in the ass.

“I- I’m sorry, this was a mistake,” he sputtered out, pushing himself away from her. “I- I shouldn’t have…”

What the fuck had he done?

What the fuck had he done?

What the fuck had he done?

That question bounced around his head as he stumbled out of the pub and into the cool air outside, nearly tripping and falling on his face in the parking lot. He didn’t want to be here, it had been a stupid idea and he was stupid for coming. He wanted to be home, to be with Eddie and Chris and Shannon and all the others. He just… He just wanted to go home.

So he ordered an Uber and did exactly that.

Eddie was not having the best of nights. He had thought things were getting better, since that night Buck miraculously showed up at his house. And truthfully, they were. The sessions with Frank were kicking his ass, but he at least had his best friend - and hopefully more than that, one day - to decompress with afterwards. It was just that, well, things didn’t get better overnight. Or even over multiple nights.

He had tried to sleep, he truthfully had. But he spent most of the time tossing and turning, and ended up moving to the couch to sit in the dark. It didn’t really help, and only really served to highlight the problems in his life right now. Because not too long ago, Buck would have been sleeping on this couch.

And then he had to go and fuck it up by pushing too hard, by asking for too much.

“What are you doing?” Chris asked, shuffling into the living room, and his son’s voice had Eddie jerking up in surprise.

“Hey buddy,” he said. “Sorry. Did I wake you?”

“I don’t know, why are you awake?”

“I guess I’m just not used to the nine-to-five life yet,” he lied, not really wanting to burden his pre-teen son with his problems. “Body’s still on the twenty-four hour shifts now and then.”

“That doesn’t make any sense,” Chris said, shaking his head.

“No? Alright, Doctor Diaz,” Eddie said, trying not to laugh even as he struggled against a yawn. “What’s your diagnosis, then?”

“You miss being a firefighter.” It was a statement of fact, and not one he could argue against.

“You’re right,” he said. It wasn’t what was keeping him up this time, but that didn’t make it untrue. “I do.”

“So why’d you stop?”

Eddie didn’t answer right away, taking his time to get his thoughts in order. He had promised, so long ago, that he wouldn’t lie to his son when it mattered. So he wasn’t going to lie to him now.

“Because I was scared.” It was something he’d only confessed to Buck and Frank so far. He hadn’t even admitted it to that fun-house mirror version of himself, denying it each time it was thrown in his face. “And sometimes people do silly things when they’re scared.”

He had been so angry, when that other Eddie had confronted him with it. A hissing, feral cat in the face of a truth he was fully intent on denying. The entire truth that he had been denying, falling back on old habits because it was better to be angry than to admit weakness. And the truth had been that he made the same mistake as he had years before, with the exact same results, and had the audacity to be surprised and upset about it.

A part of him wondered even now what would have happened if he had taken the time to talk to Buck first, before that fateful Christmas announcement. Would he have seen the lie for what it was and called him out on it? Would Buck have stayed, instead of breaking and running like he had? Would Eddie still have ended up with this changed and fractured version of the man he loved?

(Would Eddie have ever gotten to the point where his feelings for Buck were so utterly undeniable?)

“But you’re really brave,” Chris said, interrupting his musings. He looked and sounded so very confused.

“I haven’t felt really brave recently.”

“Then I can be brave for you,” the boy said, reaching forward to cup his face.

“You really are the best kid,” Eddie told him, reaching up to cover his hand with his own.

He let Chris stay up for a little bit longer, before insisting he head back to bed. He himself was about to try for sleep again, when the sound of someone stumbling through the front door drew him back into the living room, the ever trusty bat at the ready. It was with a breath of relief that he found it was only Buck.

“Eds! Eddie!” the other man cried, coming at him with open arms.

“Keep it down,” Eddie hissed, setting the bat aside. “Chris is in bed.”

“Oh, woops.” Buck giggled to himself.

“Are you drunk?”

“Yes,” he slurred. “But only a little.”

“More like a lot,” Eddie said, grabbing his arms and trying to guide him to the couch. It was really more of a drag, because the man was very uncoordinated at the moment. “Let’s get you laying down.”

“That’s a wonderful plan,” the other man said, and let his head fall forward so his face was in the crook of Eddie’s neck. He nearly leaped out of his skin when he felt Buck start trailing kisses up to his jaw. “Let’s go to bed.”

“What?” Eddie pushed his head away from his neck. “You’re drunk.”

“So?” Buck’s hands tried to slip under his shirt, and he grabbed them by the wrist to stop it. “I’ve missed you so much, love.”

Eddie closed his eyes and grit his teeth, and did his best to ignore the way the words broke his heart.

“No, Buck,” he said, practically shoved him backwards, sending the drunken man stumbling. The wounded look that crossed the other man’s face at the rejection almost made him want to take it back, but this was not what he wanted. This wasn’t what Buck wanted, either. “I’m the wrong Eddie.”

Buck blinked at him uncomprehendingly, the words taking a moment to pierce their way through the haze of alcohol. When they finally did, his face twisted with despair.

“I just wanted to come home.”

“You are home,” Eddie tried, but it was clearly the wrong thing to say.

“No, I’m not!”

“Buck, man, keep it-”

“This isn’t home! This isn’t- I don’t want to be here!”

“What?” Eddie snapped, forgetting the need for hushed tones. Buck had told him that he had wanted to be here, that he had chosen to be here. “What does that mean?”

“It means that I don’t want to be here!” Buck repeated, throwing his arm out to his sides. “I want to be back home, with Eddie and Chris and Mads and all the others! With my family!”

“You said that you chose-”

“I didn’t have a choice!” 

Buck’s words reminded him of that near argument they’d had, that morning after he showed back up. Eddie had been angry at the implication, but he let it go and then pushed it out of his mind. Now here it was again, flung into his face and impossible to ignore.

“So you were going to stay with your other family,” he bit out, doing his best to not disturb Chris, even as his temper rose.

“He was going to erase everything!” Buck cried, with actual tears even. “Everyone would have been gone, and I’d have been stuck here anyway! I was going to lose everyone anyway! If I had been given the choice, I wouldn't have come back!”

“What?"

The small voice cut through their shouting like a knife. Both men turned their heads to the hall, and to where Chris was blinking up at them.

“Christopher,” Eddie said, immediately starting on damage control. “You’re supposed to be back in bed.”

“I heard shouting,” the boy said, gaze focused on Buck. “What did you mean?”

“I- I-” Buck stuttered out, shaking his head and backing away. “I- Oh, God, Chris, I’m-”

“Dad, what did he mean?”

“Oh, God.”

Eddie didn’t really have a chance to react before Buck was spinning on his heel and running for the door. He flung it wide and then let it slam closed behind him. By the time Eddie got to it and yanked it open again, he was no longer in the yard.

“Buck!” he shouted after him. Running to the end of the sidewalk, he could see the other man was already half-way down the street. “Buck!”

But Buck didn’t even slow down.

A Swan Dive into the Rocks Below

Chapter Notes

See the end of the chapter for notes

“You’re not buying us a house,” Eddie said as the two of them walked into the station, work bags slung over their shoulders. Buck wasn’t sure how long they’d been arguing about this before arriving for work, but he knew it had been for a while now.

“Why not?” he asked. “I have the money. Like, I still have a lot of money.”

“Do you? Because I remember how many presents Chris and Jee got at Christmas.”

“Eds, love, I don’t think you understand just how much money is in my savings account.”

The bickering continued to the locker room, and from there to the loft, where Bobby was in the middle of cooking breakfast. Chimney, Hen, and Ravi were all seated around the island, patiently waiting. They all looked up as one when the pair reached them.

“What is he trying to talk you into now?” Hen asked.

“Letting him buy us a house,” his boyfriend answered.

“It takes a while to get approved for a mortgage, and-” Bobby started, but Eddie interrupted him.

“No, Cap. I mean he’s trying to talk me into letting him just straight up buy a house. Today. There’s one for sale that he was looking at this morning.”

The others all turned questioning gazes on Buck, who blushed and shrunk beneath their scrutiny.

“It’s closer to here, and to Chris’ school, and both Abuela and Shannon,” he said, defending himself.

“Can you afford that?” Ravi asked. “To just buy a house right this moment?”

“Uh, yeah,” Buck said, scratching at his cheek and not looking at any of them. Eddie, traitorous bastard that he was, was clearly trying not to laugh at him.

“How!?” the probie demanded, followed by a loud gasp from Chimney.

“You!” he cried out, pointing a finger at Buck. “You son of a bitch, you knew where it was the whole time!”

“Knew where what was?” Ravi asked.

“The treasure! This time traveling bastard cheated!”

“Hey, there’s children present.” Shannon’s voice was laced with amusement, and Buck twisted around to find that she was coming up the stairs right behind Chris, Maddie following along with Jee-Yun. “And also we brought the kids.”

“If they’re the kids, who’s the children?” Eddie asked.

“You and your boyfriend, obviously,” his ex-wife said, causing Chris to laugh at the face he made in response.

“Shannon!” Buck wasn’t sure why, but he felt like he hadn’t seen the woman in ages. He stepped close enough to wrap her in a hug, squeezing her tight. “How’s my favorite baby mama?”

“When you put it like that, it sounds like you have more than one,” she pointed out, wiggling out of his grip. “And I saw you five seconds ago, in the bay.”

“What?”

“She means me,” his own voice answered, and he saw himself coming up the stairs. Everyone all cheered, and rushed to greet his double.

“There’s the Buckaroo we were waiting for,” Hen said, brushing right past Buck.

“What’s going on? Who is this?”

“My boyfriend,” Eddie said, stepping away from him to wrap an arm around the other Buck’s waist.

“That’s… I don’t know who this is, but I’m your boyfriend.”

“No you’re not,” Eddie said, leaning in to kiss the imposter’s cheek. “Don’t you remember?”

“Remember what?”

“You left,” the other Buck said. “And I stayed.”

“B- But I wanted to stay,” he said. The words sounded weak and childish even to his own ears. “I didn’t want to leave.”

“I got what I wanted,” his double said, shrugging carelessly. The others had all fallen silent, staring at- No, staring through him, like he wasn’t even there.

“But what about what I wanted?”

“This is what you wanted,” the other Buck said, blue eyes turning black.

“No, this-” He cut himself off, shaking his head. “I wanted to stay here.”

“You wanted it both ways,” the other said, stepping away from Eddie, moving closer until he was in Buck’s space. “One of you here, one of you there. And that’s exactly what you got.”

“This isn’t fair,” he protested.

“You know that fairness had nothing to do with it,” his double told him, a hint of pity in his voice. There was none to be found in his black-eyed gaze, however. “Nothing about this has ever been fair.”

“I miss them,” Buck whispered.

His double, his replacement, leaned in until their noses was almost touching, and whispered back,

“They don’t miss you.”

Buck opened his eyes and then immediately squeezed them shut again, feeling like someone was driving ice picks through his corneas and into the pain centers of his brain. The sun was not his friend, nor was the source of the footsteps that echoed like gongs in his skull. Everything in his head was agony, and his stomach was threatening rebellion. 

Someone’s fingers started carding through his sweaty fringe, and he felt himself leaning into the touch.

“You alive, Buckaroo?”

He blinked his eyes back open, confused at the bleary image of Athena kneeling down beside his head.

“‘Thena? What are you doing here?”

“Making sure you didn’t die in the middle of the night.”

He sat up slowly, the motion almost too much for his nausea. He was on the couch in the Grant-Nash residence, with a pillow and a blanket. Someone had taken his shoes off, but he was still in what he wore to the pub the night before.

“Did I call you to pick me up?” Buck asked, trying to remember.

“No, Eddie did,” she said, and it was an answer that brought far too much clarity to him.

“Oh God,” he groaned, moving to get up. Athena - blessed Goddess that she was - immediately shoved a trash can into his chest. It would definitely have to be cleaned out, or perhaps thrown out entirely, but it served its purpose.

He remembered taking an Uber to the Diaz residence and mistaking Edmundo for Eddie. He remembered trying to sleep with him and then getting shot down, understandably so. He remembered the argument that followed, the things he said, the things Chris overheard. He remembered running out the door and down the street, and he remembered curling up on a park bench, where Athena eventually found him.

“I’m so sorry, ‘Thena,” he whispered when he was done emptying his stomach, gingerly setting the trash can on the floor. “Seriously, you… You shouldn’t have had to get out of bed because of me.”

“I wasn’t in bed yet,” she told him.

“Still-”

“You’re family, Buck. It’s not a problem.”

“How, uh.” He nervously licked his lips, which were very dry at the moment and tasted disgusting. “How upset is Bobby?”

“I’m not upset,” the captain said, stepping into the room. He had a glass of water, which he handed to Buck. “Worried, definitely, but not upset.”

If he let himself, he could pretend that he was back in the other timeline, that he was talking to the other Bobby, the other Athena. It was so much like the days and weeks after his encounter with Hudson, including the agonizing pain in his head. When he would spend days at the Grant-Nash residence while Eddie was working and Athena was on leave, getting mothered and doted on.

But he wasn’t there, and forgetting that was what led to last night’s mess.

“Nothing to be worried about, Cap,” he lied, sipping at his water. He put on his best plastic smile, but it felt wobbly.

“What exactly happened?” Bobby asked, reaching for one of the end tables. Buck’s smile became a little more real when he saw the bottle of tylenol, and he took the offered pills eagerly.

“I just… Drank too much and did something stupid. And then I ran away from it, because that’s what Buckleys do best,” he answered, tossing them back and washing them down. “Then I went to Eddie’s and did something stupid there before running away from that, too.”

He’d have to go back and apologize, he knew. To both of them. Assuming they could even stomach to look at him. Assuming Chris didn’t rightfully hate him. Assuming Edmundo didn’t just bar him from the house. 

“At least you didn’t drink and drive,” Athena said.

“Yeah, I wasn’t that flavor of stupid,” he said. Someone rang the doorbell and a frowning Bobby went to go answer it. “I need to do a walk of shame for my jeep.”

“I’ll take you after we’ve gotten some food into you,” she said, reaching out again to run her fingers through his hair. “I’ll drop you off at your jeep before I head into work.”

“Oh, I was planning on taking him.”

Edmundo’s voice had Buck sitting up straight on the couch and twisting around to look at him. He’d followed Bobby down the stairs and was now awkwardly standing at the bottom. Unlike Buck, he’d changed from last night’s clothes, and was dressed for work. Also unlike Buck, he didn’t look like he’d slept at all.

“Eddie-” he started to say, but then cut himself off when he realized he didn’t know what to say at that moment. And the other man hadn’t even looked at him once, he noted, keeping his eyes fixed on Athena instead.

The sergeant glanced between them, and then shared a look with her husband.

“Athena and I have already had breakfast,” Bobby said. “We were about to make sure Buck ate, though. You’re welcome to join.”

Edmundo glanced at the captain, and Buck was struck with the thought that this was probably the first time either man had laid eyes on the other since his return. It was heartbreaking to realize that the only thing really linking everyone together was the job, and that without it they would all happily drift apart. Or maybe not happily, but it didn’t seem like they’d fight it.

“Sure,” Edmundo finally said, nodding. “I was late getting out the door with Chris today, didn’t really have a chance to feed myself.”

“H- How is…?”

“He’s fine,” was the clipped answer, and Buck winced at the tone. The brief exchange earned them curious glances from Bobby and Athena, but neither of them pressed. Edmundo headed for the kitchen with the captain, barely glancing at Buck on his way by.

Oh, he had fucked up so much.

Buck eased himself to his feet, bending down to pick up the trash can. He had every intent to go ahead and wash it out, but Athena shook her head and took it out of his hands.

“Go get some food in you,” she said.

“Are you sure?”

She raised an eyebrow.

“Are you questioning me, Buckaroo?”

“N- No, ma’am.”

“That’s what I thought. Now get.”

Still unsure, he did as he was told anyway, creeping into the kitchen to find Bobby at the stove with a pan of bacon, and Edmundo sitting at the dining table. He wasn’t really sure what to do at this point. Once upon a time, he would have happily stepped up to the stove to help cook, but he had never felt so distant from his captain since he was an actual probie. And with how things currently were with his best friend, he wasn’t certain if he was actually welcome at the same table as the man.

“Go ahead and sit down, Buck,” Bobby said, making the decision for him. “It may have been a while, but I remember how bad the mornings after could be.”

“Er, yeah,” he said, taking a seat as far from Edmundo as he could.

What followed wasn’t the most awkward of meals ever, but it was up there. Bobby did his best to keep up a conversation, but it was stilted on Buck’s end, and Edmundo didn’t seem very interested in talking, either. Midway through, Athena came in - having changed into her police uniform - and kissed her husband goodbye.

“Take care, Buck,” she said. “You too, Eddie.”

“Yes, ma’am,” they chorused.

Buck watched her go before turning back to his plate. He’d been mostly picking at the bacon and eggs Bobby had made for them. He knew he needed to eat, but while the tylenol had muffled the headache of the hangover, it hadn’t touched the nausea causing his stomach to roil like a stormy sea. Still, he forced himself to take bites, because he could already feel Bobby’s worried gaze weighing on him.

“So, Eddie,” Bobby said, trying for conversation again. “How has it been at dispatch?”

“I figured May was keeping you up to date,” Edmundo answered, the comment sounding calm but pointed, and Buck looked up in time to see Bobby wince. “It’s been fine, though. Boring. May’s been giving me words of the day. Yesterday was ‘defenestrate’, the day before that was ‘absquatulate,’ which means-”

“To run away,” Buck said, dropping his eyes back down to the table.

“She said it meant to leave somewhere abruptly, but sure, we’ll go with yours.”

Buck nibbled at another piece of bacon before throwing the remnants of it down on his plate, which he then pushed away.

“Speaking of uh, leaving abruptly,” he said, standing up. “I- We should probably-”

He wasn’t actually looking forward to being stuck in the truck with Edmundo, but he also didn’t really want to stay here for any longer. Plus, he’d just realized that the other man was probably going to be late getting to the dispatch center as it was. Because of him.

“Right,” Bobby said, sounding uncertain. “Your shoes are by the couch, along with your things. Your jacket’s in the closet.”

“Right, thanks,” he mumbled, hurrying off to get them.

Buck’s shoes were indeed right by the couch, and with them his keys and phone. He was in the middle of tugging them on, hopping on one leg and trying not to fall on his face, when he heard Edmundo’s suddenly raised voice,

“You think!?”

Oh, he was curious about that conversation. It was either about him or about Edmundo, and either option had him itching to eavesdrop. But also, if they were talking about him, he wasn’t certain he wanted to hear what they were saying. Probably variations upon the theme of, ‘something’s wrong with Buck.’ Which, well. What Edmundo said.

So instead, he went to the closet and got his jacket out, before making a point to close it as loudly as he could. He regretted it as soon as he did it, because even while muffled by over the counter painkillers, his headache was still a thing.

“You ready to go?” Edmundo asked, stepping out of the kitchen. Bobby was right behind him, frowning heavily.

“Yeah,” Buck said, suddenly feeling guilty. “Man, are you sure? You’ve gotta be running late already, and my jeep is-”

“Shut up and move,” the other man said, brushing past him to the stairs.

“See you at work tomorrow,” Bobby said, although it sounded more like a question. Just the slightest bit confused, Buck nodded at him before hurrying up the stairs.

Edmundo was silent as they made the way to his truck, and remained so after Buck was buckled in and they started driving off. Even when he told him where to go to pick up the truck, which was in the opposite direction of the dispatch center, the man didn’t say anything. It was worrisome and stressful, which did not help his nausea any.

“So you’re really pissed at me, huh?” he blurted out.

“Livid,” Edmundo finally said.

“I- I’m sorry, okay? I was really drunk, and-”

“So does that mean everything you said was a lie?”

Buck didn’t answer, which was an answer in and of itself.

“You upset Chris last night,” Edmundo said, and the words were a knife to the heart. “He heard you say that shit, and then you ran the fuck away, leaving me to deal with it. Again.”

“I’m sorry,” he whispered, curling in on himself against the window.

“Good,” the other man snapped, glaring at the road. “But that doesn’t fix this, does it?”

“What… What can I do, then? To fix this?”

“Fuck if I know, Buck.” There was such venom in the words that he flinched. “I don’t even know if this is fixable, considering my kid now knows you didn’t want to come back to him.”

“I- I did-”

“Bullshit,” Edmundo snarled, directing the glare at him now that they were at a stoplight. And oh, the anger now was so much worse than that day at the grocery store. Buck felt so small in the face of it. “Or at least, you didn’t want it enough to not choose someone else over him. Over us.”

Unable to meet the other man’s eyes any longer, he ducked his head. He felt the other man’s gaze burning holes into the side of his head for several moments, until someone behind them started impatiently blasting their horn. Edmundo looked back at the road, and at the green light, and started driving again.

“Why did you call Athena last night?” Buck asked.

“Because I wasn’t going to leave Chris alone when he was upset,” was the pointed answer. “But I also didn’t want to risk you disappearing for another month.”

“I wasn’t lost,” he said, because that was something he could focus on. “I- I wasn’t going to disappear again, at least not like before. You have to be lost to end up… There.”

It was why Eddie - the other Eddie - had given him that St. Christopher medallion for Christmas, to help keep him from being lost again. Thinking about it hurt, because he’d only had it for a day, but he still found himself reaching for where it would be under his shirt. Not that it had helped any, because he still ended up back with Ichabod. He’d never truly left the crossroads, the body he was in now having been left behind like an empty glass, its water poured out into another.

“Why did you come over and insist on being the one to take me to my jeep, if you’re so pissed at me?” Buck asked, needing to know, especially since he knew Edmundo was already running late.

“Because I love you and I wanted to make sure you were okay.”

His eyes were suddenly burning, and he closed them tight against the words. It was the exact thing he would have expected Eddie to say, which made sense. When it got down to the nitty gritty details beneath trauma and experience, they were the same person. It was what made everything so difficult, at times.

“Nice catch last night,” Buck said. “With the pressure switch.”

“Thank you,” Edmundo said, no longer sounding quite so angry. It was still there, tension obvious in the way he clenched his jaw, but some of it had seeped out with the change in topic. “Josh bitched at me for it. Told me not to hijack a call again.”

“Fuck him,” he said, and it surprised a laugh out of the other man. “What did he want you to do, try to explain it to him so he could explain it to someone else? And pray something didn’t get lost along the way?”

“I think he was just upset that I didn't check in with him first.”

“I stand firmly by my ‘fuck him’ statement,” Buck said. He hadn’t really heard anything from the dispatcher since his return, and couldn’t remember how much he’d talked with him before Christmas. His social circle had gotten so small, not that it had ever been very big, and he barely had the energy anymore to care.

They fell into an uneasy silence after that. It took a while to get to the pub from the night before, its parking lot mostly empty at this time of day. Edmundo pulled in beside the jeep, and Buck hesitated for a moment before getting out.

“I- Are you and I-”

“Are we what?” the other man asked, somehow managing to sound patient and aggravated at the same time.

“Are we okay?”

“No, Buck,” Edmundo said, and he cringed at the answer. “I don’t think we are. But I still expect you to come over tonight. Christopher deserves more than you breaking his heart and then running away from it.”

“Yeah, he does,” Buck admitted quietly, before slipping out of the truck. Edmundo stuck around, waiting for him to get into the jeep and start the engine, before pulling out and leaving him. Something cold settled in his heart at the sight.

Oh, how was he supposed to fix this?

Athena slipped into the house with a sigh. It had been a productive day, in her opinion. Sure, she’d had to deal with a hoarder - never the best of experiences - but she managed to find the man who decided to reenact Speed with his neighbors. They caught the bad guy and put a family’s fears to rest. It was a good day.

She was worried about Buck, though. It was almost her default state of being anymore, ever since Christmas when the young man had driven away and disappeared for a month. Having him suddenly show back up had filled her with such joy and relief, enough that she was willing to believe his story that he didn’t remember the time in between at all. It shouldn’t have mattered, things should have been better.

They weren’t, though.

The Buck they had gotten back was so different, so changed. If she didn’t know better, she’d think the man had been gone for years instead of mere weeks. He seemed so sad whenever they spoke, much as he tried to hide it. Hen had noticed it as well, and so had Maddie. Athena had hoped that it would get better when he started back at the station, instead of being holed up in his empty loft all day, but it seemed to only be getting worse.

When Eddie had called to tell her that Buck had run off into the night, drunk and upset, she had been utterly terrified. She wasn’t sure what she would have done if she hadn’t found him, if he had gone missing again. But she had found him, thank God. Curled up on a park bench a couple blocks from Eddie’s, looking utterly miserable.

She’d been too relieved to be properly angry.

“How was your day?” Bobby asked her when he stepped out of the kitchen. He had a towel thrown over his shoulder, and she could smell something wonderful cooking.

“Productive,” she said. “We caught the guy behind the bomb.”

“Good job.”

She let out a tired hum of acknowledgement.

“Bobby, last night, did Buck seem… Has he ever gotten that drunk before?”

“Not that I know of,” her husband admitted. “Maybe in the days before Abby, before he started taking the job seriously. Why?”

“No reason,” she said, because one night of poor choices did not make a pattern, regardless of her fears. “I notice there’s a lack of paperwork tonight.”

“Yeah, I finally decided to fill the empty spot.”

“Oh? Is Eddie finally going back?”

“Uh, no,” Bobby said, shaking his head. “Someone from the One Forty-Seven, the station that helped us yesterday, is going to transfer over. Lucy Donato. She worked well with Buck during the call and got along with everyone last night.”

Athena frowned at this. She didn’t want to jump to conclusions, but instincts honed over years on the job screamed at her. Buck did not typically go out and drink to the extent that he had the night before, or at least he hadn’t in all the time she’d known him. She wasn’t certain if Donato’s added presence actually had anything to do with it, but very rarely were coincidences merely coincidences.

“You look like you have an opinion,” he noted.

“No, just… I think I speak for everyone when I say I just can’t wait for Eddie to come back home,” she said, pushing away her concerns for the moment. Athena hadn’t even met this Lucy Donato woman, she couldn’t be passing any judgments yet.

“You never know, perhaps Buck and Lucy will get along just as well,” Bobby offered, an obvious attempt to assuage her worries.

It didn’t help.

Buck sat outside the Diaz residence for several minutes, psyching himself up. The night before he had made an utter ass of himself, and now he was dreading to walk in there and face up to it. He had to, though. Edmundo had been right that morning, Chris deserved better.

“This is ridiculous,” he chided himself, yanking the keys out of the ignition and getting out of the jeep. After everything he had willingly faced and done, he was perfectly capable of facing Chris. He marched to the front door, and then hesitated when he started to simply let himself in.

He slipped his keys into his pocket, and knocked.

“Since when didn’t you just let yourself in?” Edmundo asked when he opened the door, frowning at him.

“Since now, I guess,” Buck said, shrugging as he stepped inside. “I wasn’t sure if…”

The other man let out a sigh but didn’t say anything, not a reassurance or a joking insult. Chris was sitting at a table just inside the living room, dutifully doing his homework. Or, he had been, but had looked up with a frown of his own when he heard Buck’s voice.

“Dinner’s almost ready,” Edmundo said, returning to the kitchen.

“That’s awesome,” he said, feeling awkward. He had never felt awkward in this place before. Chris was still staring at him, and so he made his way over to his side. Kneeling down so he was eye level, he took a breath and let it out. “Hey, Superman. I am so sorry for- For what you heard last night, for how I… I am just so sorry.”

The boy didn’t say anything, continuing to frown at him instead.

“Chris?”

“I’m angry with you,” he finally said.

“That’s perfectly fine-”

“And I don’t want you here.”

“W- What?”

“You left, and you didn’t want to come back,” Chris said, and now the frown had turned into a scowl. He looked so much like his father, and in any other context Buck would be cooing over it. All it did now was hurt. “You said you wouldn’t have come back, if you had the choice.”

“I- I did say that,” Buck stuttered out, unable to formulate an argument against his own words. “But Chris-”

“I don’t want you here,” the boy said again. “I don’t want to see you. I want you to go.”

His leg being crushed by the ladder truck had hurt less. Getting his skull bashed in had hurt less. Being shot had hurt less. He had died a half dozen deaths, and none of them had hurt as much as this moment.

“Chris- That’s-” He didn’t know what to say, except, “Okay.”

Buck stood up and made his way to the kitchen, popping his head through the doorway. He’d fixed a smile on his face in the few steps it took, and it felt sturdier than he did at the moment.

“Hey, Eds? Change of plans. I’m, uh. I’m gonna head out.”

“What?” Edmundo frowned at him, pausing with an oven mitt half way on his hand. “Why?”

“Chris wants me to go,” he said, and was proud of the way his voice didn’t break or waver.

“What?” the other man repeated, confusion taking over his face. “Chris-?”

“Doesn’t want me to be here. So I’m going to go. I’ll uh, be seeing you, I guess.” He didn’t wait for a response and turned to leave. Pausing at the door, he looked back at Chris. “Bye, Superman.”

Chris just frowned at him, and so he nodded and let himself out.

Buck drove in a haze, not really registering the distance between points A and B. He remembered, vaguely, making the spontaneous decision to stop by the store on his way, but that was about it. He was entering his loft, paper bag in hand, in what felt like no time. Pulling out his phone, he sent Edmundo a quick text telling him he made it okay, before shutting it off completely and tossing it without a care onto his kitchen island. The bag was set next to it.

It felt so childish, to say he was upset because his friend’s kid didn’t want to see him anymore. Except Chris had stopped being just his friend’s kid a long, long time ago. Now, it was more like his own kid wanted nothing to do with him.

And it was entirely his own fault. Worse, he was entirely deserving of it.

Buck went to the bathroom to wash his face, which had become so tear stained that even the apathetic sales clerk had asked if he was okay. His reflection over the sink caught his eye, and the sight of his own face filled him with rage. He wasn’t sure who he hated more in that moment - himself, who had thoroughly fucked everything up; the other him, who was reaping the benefits of the other timeline; or Ichabod, who was undoubtedly watching with glee - but he knew who was here that he could direct it at.

Cracks spiderwebbed across the glass with the first punch. The second one had shards falling into the basin. The third one, and the ones after, left half the mirror on the floor and some of it in his hand. His knuckles were bloodied and bruised and swelling, and there was definitely bits of the mirror sticking out. But there were more important things for him to focus on.

Like the bottle of whiskey sitting in his kitchen.

Chapter End Notes

*whistles innocently*

Turn My Head Off, Forever

The problem with having gone to so much therapy, in this timeline and the other, was that Buck couldn’t plead ignorance when it came to his actions. He knew, with every step he took, exactly what kind of self-destructive path he was following. It was just that… Well, he didn’t really care at the moment. The night he punched out the mirror, he drank more than half the bottle of whiskey he had bought and slipped into some of the best sleep he’d had in weeks. Of course, he had forgotten to turn his phone back on, so he was almost late to work the next morning. But only almost.

After a hurried shower, and a handful of ibuprofen washed down with his morning coffee, he made it to the station with just enough time to change before his shift started. Under the flourescent lights of the locker room, his hand looked awful. The knuckles were slightly swollen, and there were cuts of various sizes all over the back of his hand and fingers. It hurt whenever he flexed his fingers, and the movement cracked the still forming scabs so that they bled a little.

Buck probably should have gone to the hospital, if only to make sure nothing was broken or in need of stitches. Instead, he had cleaned everything up himself and ignored it afterwards. It would be fine, though. Edmundo had managed to get through his fight club phase with no one at the station getting suspicious, except Buck himself. He could manage a few days while this healed, he could-

“Morning, Buckaroo,” Hen said, greeting him as he came out of the locker room. “You’re looking- What the hell happened to your hand?”

“Oh, uh.” Fuck you, Universe. “Put it through some glass.”

There, not technically a lie. He could get away with that far better than he could outright falsehoods.

“How?” she demanded, grabbing his wrist so she could peer at his hand from behind her glasses. He bit down on a hiss of pain when she prodded at one of his knuckles. “Actually, nevermind that for now. Did you get this looked at?”

“No?” He shrugged at her reproachful glare. “It’s not that bad.”

“It looks like you punched a window, Buck,” she said, shaking her head.

“That is not at all what happened.” Suddenly, Buck was very thankful that her visits to the loft had ended when he started back at the station. The last thing he needed was for her to see the remnants of his bathroom mirror and start reaching accurate conclusions. “It’s not as bad as it looks, I swear.”

“Yeah, uh-huh, sure.” Hen clearly didn’t believe him and literally dragged him to the ambulance, ignoring his protests the entire way.

“We need to get up there for Cap’s-”

“It can wait,” she said, practically pushing him into a seat. “I don’t like the look of some of these cuts.”

“I might have had to pull out some glass…”

“Buck.” The name came out as a sigh, the exasperation in her voice reminding him uncomfortably of the way his mother used to say his name whenever he let himself get hurt as a kid. He flinched away from it, and only her grip on his wrist kept him from yanking his hand away.

“I’m fine,” he told her, doing his best to keep from snapping at her. She clearly didn’t believe him, pressing not-so-lightly at spots and watching his reactions closely. Only his horribly vast experience with pain let him keep from wincing whenever she did so. This was, after all, far from the worst hurt he’d experienced in his life. Hell, it wasn’t even the most painful thing he’d experienced this week.

“Nothing feels broken, at least.” Small mercies did exist, apparently. “But we really should get you in for an x-ray, and then probably some stitches.”

“No.” This time he couldn’t help the snappish tone, and immediately felt guilty for it. Hen was just trying to help, after all. “I- I know you’re trying to help, but I really don’t need to get it checked out. I don’t want to.”

“Then I’m treating it here,” she announced, pulling out the supplies she needed. He just nodded and gave in, because it was easier that way. And honestly, after last night, it felt nice to have someone care. Even if he didn’t deserve it.

The paramedic wiped away some of the blood that started oozing from the reopened wounds, and then sealed everything with dollops of surgical glue. Afterwards, she wrapped gauze around his hand, from wrist to fingers. There was absolutely no way that Bobby wasn’t going to notice, and that conversation was not one he wanted to deal with at the moment. When Hen was done, Buck tested how well he could move his hand and fingers, and then both of them nodded with contentment at the results.

“Nicely done, Dr. Wilson,” he joked, though his heart wasn’t really in it. Hen frowned at him and reached up to cup his face.

“I wish you would tell us what was going on,” she said.

“I-” he started to say, unsure how he was going to answer. But then the moment was broken.

“Hey guys, Cap’s looking for you,” Chimney said, popping around the corner. Buck startled a bit at his appearance, hiding his injured hand out of sight. “He’s about to do the morning brief.”

“We’ll be right there,” Hen told him, seeming reluctant to tear her eyes away from Buck’s face.

“In fact, we were just on our way up,” Buck said. He did his best to scramble out of the ambulance without letting Chimney see his hand, and heard Hen let out a sigh as she followed him.

Everyone else was already crowded in the loft area of the station when they got there. It was easy to forget just how many people were on their shift. It seemed that they all tended to disappear at times, leaving their core group isolated from all the others. He wasn’t entirely certain what was up with that.

Slipping through the crowd, Buck froze when he caught sight of the woman standing next to Bobby.

“Oh, what the hell?” he whispered. Hen and Chimney glanced at him curiously.

“Now that everyone is here,” the captain said, nodding at Hen and Buck. “As you all know, we’ve been down a person these last few months. We’ve been managing, but I’ve decided it was time to fill the spot. This is Lucy Donato. She’s transferred over from the One Forty-Seven.”

Buck couldn’t believe what he was hearing. Bobby was replacing Edmundo? With her? The woman he got drunk and made out with before coming back to his senses? This really was the absolute worst fucking timeline.

He barely listened to the rest of the captain’s announcements, which mostly consisted of the day’s chore list and a couple other matters. When the crowd dispersed, he followed him to his office, anger bubbling under skin.

“Bobby, what the hell?” he demanded the moment the door was closed behind him. The captain looked up, eyebrows going high in surprise. “You’re replacing Eddie?”

“I am filling the empty spot, yes,” Bobby said.

“We have had an empty spot here since October,” Buck just about spat. “How long was Chimney gone before Christmas, and you never even thought about getting a floater?”

“Because we knew he was coming back.”

“Eddie is coming back.”

“Buck.” Bobby’s voice sounded so sympathetic that it took everything Buck had not to gnash his teeth at him. “We don’t know that.”

“Yes we do!” he snapped. “I know that! Because I actually care enough to talk to him, instead of asking others about him!”

“What happened?” The captain asked, ignoring the outburst to focus on his injured hand. Buck had forgotten to hide it from him in his anger, and now quickly tucked away behind his back and out of sight.

“Nothing you,” he snapped, “need to be worried about.”

This wasn’t his Bobby. His Bobby wouldn’t have just cut Eddie off like this one had, wouldn’t have just replaced him like this. Buck knew that with the same certainty he knew the sky would be blue if he went outside and looked. 

“Buck,” Bobby tried, but Buck shook his head.

“We were fine for months while Chimney was gone. You were fine for all those weeks that I wasn’t here after Eddie transferred out. Why now, huh? Hell, why her?”

“I… I figured she’d be a good partner for you.”

“Eddie is my partner!” Buck shouted.

“He isn’t here now,” the captain said, sternness in his voice. “And no, we don’t know when or if he’s coming back. Buck, honestly, I am concerned about you.”

“I don’t want your concern!” Maybe he would have welcomed it before the years spent rebuilding his family from the ground up and then keeping it from splitting at the seams. But not now. “It’s too late for that. You should have been concerned about things before Christmas, instead of just plugging your head into the dirt and ignoring everything like a goddamn ostrich.”

“That is enough!” Bobby snapped. “Buck, I have been patient and understanding, but I really think you should talk to someone.”

“Or what? You’ll replace me, too? Again?” Buck scoffed. “You know who you should have made that suggestion to? Eddie, when he made an impulsive overnight decision and you just accepted it, instead of asking questions. And then you cut him off like you didn’t give a fuck!”

“I said that’s enough!” The captain slammed his fist on his desk, startling Buck into silence. “Go take a walk. Now. And you're the man behind for the day.”

“Whatever,” he scoffed, shaking his head as he stormed out of the room. The moment he flung the door open he saw at least three other firefighters quickly scramble away, doing their best to pretend like they hadn’t been eavesdropping to the best of their ability. He felt eyes on him, and looked up to see Hen peering down at him from the loft above. Shaking his head at her, he looked away again and headed for the lockers. If he was the man behind, then he had plenty of time to hit the gym and try to work some of this anger out. 

It wouldn’t work, just like it hadn’t last time, but it was better than any other alternatives.

A pinky promise was a pinky promise, and Buck was not going to break it, no matter how much he had fucked everything else up. Actually, he was especially not going to break it because of how much he’d fucked everything else up. So, on Friday afternoon, he pulled up at the Diaz house to take Edmundo to his appointment with Frank. It was the first time he’d been back since he had, in essence, been run out by an understandably upset Christopher.

Originally, Edmundo’s appointments had been on the same day of the week, every week, which made working things out with Chris very easy. But ever since Buck had started back at the station, and with Edmundo’s insistence that he go with him, the appointments had been scheduled around his shifts. This meant that he was going to be stuck in a waiting room with a child whom he loved so much and who was so very upset with him.

(He caught himself absently wishing for something to drink, and did his best to smash the errant thought like a bug.)

Just like on Wednesday night, he knocked instead of simply letting himself in. This time, it was Chris who answered the door. The boy’s smile immediately twisted into an unhappy frown, and the simple action was another twist to the knife that seemed to be permanently stuck in his heart.

“Hi, Chris,” Buck said, trying for a smile. It felt more like a grimace. “Is uh, is your dad ready to go?”

Chris didn’t say anything. He just nodded and walked off, leaving Buck to awkwardly follow him in. Edmundo was indeed just inside, getting his shoes on. The man looked up and let out a relieved sigh at the sight of him.

“I wasn’t sure if…” He trailed off.

“Pinky promise,” Buck reminded him.

“You ready to go, Chris?” Edmundo asked.

“Yeah,” the boy said, heading for the door. He’d grabbed his school bag, presumably to have something to do while they waited. In that moment, Buck became absolutely certain he was in for an hour of awkward silence.

He was dreading it.

They left the house and Edmundo locked the door behind them, before they piled into the jeep. Buck pulled out his keys and started the engine, and then heard a sharp gasp from Edmundo.

“What?” he asked, concerned. “What’s wrong?”

“Your hand,” the other man said, staring at it.

“My- Oh. Yeah.” He’d forgotten about it, honestly. And up until now he’d had his hands in his pockets, so Edmundo hadn't had a chance to see it. “It’s nothing.”

“It doesn’t look like nothing,” he said, reaching out and tugging at Buck’s wrist so he could see it for himself, as if there wasn’t a layer of bandages hiding everything from view. All anyone could see were the small cuts that littered the backs and sides of his fingers. “What happened?”

“I, uh… Hurt it on a call,” he lied, gently pulling his hand back. He didn’t look at Edmundo as he said it, focusing instead on starting the engine and pulling away from the curb. “It’s fine. Hen took care of me.”

“Does it hurt?” a small voice asked, and Buck glanced at Chris through the rearview mirror.

“Oh, uh, no,” he assured the boy. “Not at all, buddy.”

Christopher nodded and didn’t say anything else, turning his head to look out the window. The jeep fell into silence after that, with Edmundo casting worried glances at his hand and Chris clearly still too upset with him to keep up his usual constant stream of chatter. Buck wasn’t certain when the last time it was that the three of them sat in his jeep in silence. Actually, he was fairly certain it had never happened before, in either timeline.

“So, um,” Buck started midway through the drive, flailing for a topic of conversation. “Uh, wh- what was today’s word?”

“What?”

“The word of the day?” he offered, keeping his eyes on the road.

“Oh. Pernicious,” Edmundo said, leaning against the window and watching him. “It’s an adjective, apparently. Causing insidious harm or ruin.”

That described far too much about Buck’s life.

“I wonder how many points that would be worth in Scrabble.”

“Less than yesterday’s, probably,” the other man said. “Zeugma. A figure of speech where a word has double meaning in the sentence.”

“If nothing else, working with May at Dispatch is going to really help you out with Scrabble.”

“We haven’t had a proper game night in a while,” Edmundo said, sounding contemplative. “What do you say, bud? Want to beat Buck’s butt at board games again tonight?”

“No.” That was all Chris said, a simple syllable with so much weight to it.

“What about a movie, then?” he tried.

“No,” was once again the answer.

“Well, is there anything you want to do after I’m done with Frank?”

“Not with Buck.”

Three little words, no more than a syllable each, and they managed to leave him feeling utterly shattered. Even Edmundo seemed taken aback by that answer, as if he had never before contemplated the notion that his son was capable of holding a grudge. He glanced between Buck in the driver’s seat and Chris in the back, obviously trying to figure out something to say, before just letting out a sigh and slumping against the car door.

“It’s fine,” Buck lied. When the other man shot him a disbelieving look, he doubled down. “No, really, it is. I’ll uh, see if Maddie is doing anything tonight. I haven’t really been doing a good job earning the title of Best Uncle since I came back.”

He had absolutely no plan on following through with the idea, but Edmundo didn’t need to know that.

“How is Maddie doing?” Edmundo asked as they pulled into the parking lot for the building Frank’s office was in. “She’s not back at Dispatch yet, so…”

“She’s… I don’t know,” Buck confessed with a sigh. From the times he had seen her since his return, he got the impression that whatever progress she might have made in Boston had been nearly undone by his sudden disappearance. He knew that she had dropped everything to rush back to Los Angeles the moment Chimney told her he was missing. So maybe she was doing better than she had been in September, but she was definitely nowhere near as well off as the other Maddie.

Buck and Chris escorted Edmundo to Frank’s office, and then waited with him until he was called in. Christopher pulled out his homework and started working on it at one of the tables, frowning his way through what looked like a science worksheet. The boy hadn’t looked Buck’s way once since they got there, and seemed perfectly content to ignore him completely.

The last time they’d been here, waiting for Edmundo to finish flaying himself in the name of healing, he and Chris had played tic-tac-toe and hangman in between watching silly TikTok videos together until the hour was up. That was not happening today. So instead, Buck did his best to get comfortable in the far too uncomfortable chairs and started playing on his phone.

The fifty minutes seemed to drag by, far slower than all the times it had just been Buck waiting here for Edmundo. He kept shooting glances over at Chris, who had finished the science worksheet and moved on to a math one. Every once in a while, he caught himself opening his mouth to try and say something, before realizing that he had no idea what to say.

Besides, Chris would probably shoot him down if he tried.

Finally, after what seemed like forever, Edmundo came back out. He looked worn and wrung out, as if the comparison to being flayed wasn’t entirely off the mark, but he smiled when he laid eyes on his kid. Buck got to his feet, and they waited for Chris to gather his things and tug his bag on before leaving.

“Okay, so, stop somewhere for something greasy?” Buck suggested, glancing at Edmundo. “Before I drop you two off?”

“Drop us off?” Edmundo frowned at him. “You’re not planning on staying at all?”

“Uh, no? I mean…” He glanced at Chris, who was making good progress on leaving them behind, and shrugged. “We’ve had the conversation about safe places before, Eds. Or, I think we have.”

“We have.”

“Well, I’m not going to invade Chris’, not when he doesn’t want me there. Hell, do you even want me there? You were just as angry with me as he was.”

“Buck, I always want you with me,” Edmundo said. “No matter how angry I am with you.”

And Buck didn’t know what to say to that, so he didn’t say anything at all.

Buck had just finished the whiskey from Wednesday night and was about to open the new bottle he had just bought when someone started knocking at the door. With the frantic energy of a teenager trying to hide a dirty deed from their parents, he quickly stuffed both bottles into a cabinet and his glass into the dishwasher, thankful that whoever it was, it wasn’t Maddie or Edmundo. They both had their own keys, and would have just let themselves in.

Well, Edmundo would have. He wasn’t sure about Maddie these days.

He wasn’t entirely certain why he felt the need to hide everything, either. It wasn’t like he was doing anything wrong, drinking alone in his loft. Okay, no, that was an utter lie. He knew exactly why he felt the need to hide it, he was just doing his best to ignore it. But afterwards, he went to answer the door, and was suddenly very thankful that he had.

“Oh, uh, hi ‘Thena,” he awkwardly greeted the sergeant, stepping aside to let her in. She wasn’t in uniform, so he was assuming nothing bad had occurred. “It’s kinda late for a visit, isn’t it?”

“This visit is definitely late, yes,” Athena agreed with a nod as she walked in. It wasn’t the first time she had been to the loft since his return, but it had been a while since the last time. She and Hen had stopped their regular visits once he went back to the station, presumably content with their ability to check in on him there. “Honestly, I’m surprised to find you here.”

“Well, I live here…”

“I mean that I figured you’d be at Eddie’s. I went there first.”

“Oh.” Buck probably had a concerned text to look forward to, whenever he got around to checking his phone. “Yeah, no, I- It doesn’t matter. Wh- What were you looking for me for?”

“To check on you, mostly,” she said.

“Oh,” he said again, feeling dumb for it. “There’s no need for that, really. I- I’m fine.”

“Right.” 

She stepped closer and he stepped back, knowing there was no way she wouldn’t be able to smell the alcohol on his breath. Needing something to explain that action away, he turned and headed for the kitchen.

“Do you want anything to drink?” he offered, pausing at the cabinet with the glasses.

“No, thank you, Buckaroo,” Athena said, watching as he filled a glass with water. “I really did just come here to check on you, make sure you were okay after Wednesday.”

“Oh, well, I am,” he assured her, trying to sound believable. “Suffered for a bit, but I’m perfectly fine.” He caught her eyes falling to his injured hand and gave her a shaky smile. “Mostly fine.”

“Hen said you put your hand through some glass,” she said, glancing about the loft.

“Obviously I cleaned it up, I didn’t just leave a bunch of broken glass around,” he said, not able to keep from rolling his eyes. “Nice to know the gossip flow is the same.”

Maybe he should carry through with his his old idea of having Harry inform him whenever he heard Athena and the others talking about him. Sure, the kid would do his best to fleece him, but it would be worth it. Probably.

“I like being kept up to date about my kids, Buckaroo,” Athena said, tone soft but the words making him feel like he was being scolded. “Bobby told me you two got into it yesterday morning.”

“There might have been words,” he confessed, not meeting her eyes. He didn’t feel bad about his outburst, but he also wasn’t proud of it. “He replaced Eddie.”

“I’ve gotten the impression, based on what he said that you said, that there’s more to it than that,” Athena said. “At least on your end.”

So, so much more. A mountain of disappointment and a ravine filled with regrets.

“Well, the last time he replaced someone who wanted to come back, the department got sued, so…”

“Does Eddie want to come back?” the sergeant asked, gazing at him patiently.

“Yes! And Bobby would know that if-” He cut himself off, not wanting to get into it with her.

“If?” She prodded.

“Did you know that, before I went back to work, I hadn’t seen him at all after I saw everyone again at the hospital?” Buck asked, eyes fixed on his glass of water. He hadn’t actually drunk any of it, using it mainly as a prop to keep his hands filled. “And I know he’s barely seen Eddie since he transferred to the Dispatch center. We’re pretty sure he’s relying on May to keep tabs on him.”

“He is,” Athena confirmed with a sigh.

“Then he barely knows anything about what’s going on with Eddie, because May barely knows anything,” he said. “I- I mean, I know if I called him, he’d come running over. But what’s stopping him from running over now if he’s so worried? What’s stopping him from asking Eddie himself about his day at Dispatch instead of getting gossip from May?”

“Nothing, I would assume,” the sergeant confessed. “But Buck, I’m not here about Eddie. I’m here about you. Because getting into a shouting match with Bobby at the station? Getting so drunk I have to bring you home? That isn’t like you.”

“It… It’s just been a bad week.”

“Why? What happened?”

“Oh, I- I-” Think of something, think of something… “I made an utter ass of myself at Eddie’s when I was drunk, and now Chris is upset with me.”

Athena stared at him for a moment, clearly not believing that was the full extent of everything. But then, finally, she let out a sigh and nodded.

“Whatever it is you did, he’ll forgive you,” she said, patting his arm. “That boy adores you. He’s not going to stay upset forever.”

Buck could only hope.

On the Edge

Chapter Notes

See the end of the chapter for notes

The days and weeks fell into a pattern. On his nights off, Buck would drink just enough to get comfortably numb and fall asleep easier. Of course, he gradually had to drink more and more to get there, but that wasn’t a problem. Once a week, he would stay sober long enough to drive Edmundo to Frank’s office and sit in the waiting room with a Christopher who was still giving him the cold shoulder. Edmundo was gradually looking more and more worn again, and Buck wondered how much of that was his fault.

He hadn’t spent so much as an hour at the Diaz residence since his bout of drunken stupidity, but if Chris didn’t want him there, then he wasn’t going to impose. Chris came first, after all.

At work, everyone was walking on eggshells. Things were tense still between him and Bobby, to the point that they barely exchanged any words outside of a call. There was something going on with Chimney, who was now acting about as awkward with him now as he had during the Daniel fiasco. There was Lucy, who reminded him far too much of how he’d been in his first year of firefighting, and whom he absolutely refused to get to know better. And everyone at the station had heard about his blow-up with the captain, and were now treating him like a landmine waiting to explode.

The only ones he truly felt comfortable enough to spend downtime with, or rather, the only ones who felt comfortable spending downtime with him, were Hen and Ravi. Although honestly, he did his best to avoid everyone at the station when he could. Not that he was very successful, because Hen would track him down and force him to socialize with her.

He just didn’t have it in him any more to give a shit.

(Occasionally, Buck caught himself remembering that he had wanted to fix things, that that was what started this whole mess. He would literally drown those thoughts whenever they popped up.)

Most days he sat on his couch watching entertainingly bad reality television while he drank, though occasionally he sat out on his balcony. He didn’t do it often, usually when he caught himself imagining someone’s - Eddie’s - nagging voice telling him he needed to get out and get some fresh air. And he was technically getting out of the loft when he was on the balcony.

Buck was sitting on one of his patio chairs, making significant progress through today’s bottle, when a flash of blue caught his eye.

“What the hell are you doing here?” he demanded, setting down his glass. The butterfly, a bright blue that stuck out against the gray of the balcony wall, didn’t answer. Not that he expected it to, it was a bug.
