But it was the same butterfly he’d seen every time he prevented the truck bombing, the same butterfly that had joined Ichabod at the crossroads, the same butterfly that had befucked his head and split him in two, so he knew it wasn’t a normal bug.

He just didn’t know why it was here now.

“Icky-bob said you liked drama and chaos, but there’s none of that here,” Buck told it. “Just me drinking alone. Just another day ending in Y.”

The butterfly didn’t say a damn thing, it just sat there and stared at him. Or he thought it was staring at him. It felt very judgmental, whatever it was doing.

“No, seriously, fuck off!” Buck shouted at it, swiping his hand at the insect. It took to the air to avoid being hit, but instead of fucking off like he requested, it fluttered over to perch on the edge of his glass. He frantically grabbed at it, sending the insect flying away again to land on the table proper. “Oh, no, this is mine! Go find your own drink to drown in!”

The butterfly wiggled its wings a bit before it seemed to settle itself down on the table top, like it was getting itself comfortable for something. It was ridiculous and it was aggravating, and Buck was really tempted to go find something heavy to smash it with. He eyed the half-full bottle of bourbon and debated if it could do the job.

“Are you here because I’m not being entertaining enough for the not-demon?” he asked, hand creeping over to grab the bottle by its neck. “Is he upset that I’m not doing what he wanted? Well that’s too bad, because I don’t think things can be fixed. I pushed Humpty Dumpty off the wall, and nothing is gonna put him back together again. I don’t think I even want to fix everything anymore. So you can just fuck. Off!”

He slammed the bottle down on top of where the butterfly was, but it managed to move at the last moment, fluttering around his head tauntingly. Letting out a wordless cry of rage and frustration he started swatting at the air, but he kept missing the damn thing.

“God damn it, go the fuck away and leave me alone!” he shouted. And then, because the not-really-a-butterfly wasn’t enough of a bother, someone started knocking at his door. He set glass back down as he let out a groan. “Ugh, no. Seriously? Who the fuck is it now?”

The answer to that turned out to be Maddie, and Jee-Yun, both of whom were frowning when he answered the door.

“Who were you shouting at?” his sister asked as she walked in pushing Jee-Yun’s stroller. She was glancing about, clearly trying to figure out if someone else was there.

“No one,” he lied, shooting a glare at the butterfly behind her back. It had followed him inside and was now sitting peacefully above them on the banister. “Phone call about my car warranty.”

“Oh God, I hate those.” Maddie was looking… He wasn’t certain. Not as bad as when he had first returned, but still not… Healthy? She looked tired, and lines were starting to form from constant frowning. She also felt thinner under his arms when he hugged her, like she hadn’t been eating enough. “Your breath, Buck. Have you been drinking?”

“Maybe.” He quickly stepped away from her, as if it would hide what she already noticed.

“It’s the middle of the day.” The way she was looking at him now was remarkably similar to how it was before they both left Hershey, when he did something stupid and she had to help patch him up.

“Time is an illusion,” he said, kneeling down to tickle Jee’s stomach. She was finally getting used to his presence, no longer seeing him as a scary stranger, although she didn’t get as excited at seeing him as the other Jee-Yun had. It was a difference that sent pangs into his heart and made him want to grab the bourbon again. “So what’s got you and the princess dropping in on me?”

“Do I need an excuse to see my brother?” Maddie asked. And she didn’t, not really. But this was honestly the first time he’d see her in person since… The beginning of the month, maybe? Things had been strained between him and this version of his sister since his return, probably starting with how he hadn’t been as excited to see her as he probably should have been.

Buck had been happy to see her, don’t get him wrong. He’d been happy and surprised and relieved. But he hadn’t been as excited as he probably should have been, because he had literally seen her just the day before from his perspective. Add to it that his disappearing act had clearly set her mental health back, and things… Hadn’t been as okay as they probably would have been otherwise.

“Of course not,” he quickly assured her, bopping Jee on the nose. “But I figured you’d be spending the day with Chimney, since he’s off.”

“Oh. He didn’t tell you.”

“Tell me what?” Buck asked, glancing up at her.

“We broke up,” she said.

Well, shit. That would explain Chimney’s recent awkwardness at the station.

“When!? Please don’t tell me you broke up with him on his birthday.”

“Why are you automatically assuming that I broke up with him?”

“Did you?”

“It was mutual,” Maddie sighed. “Things between us aren’t okay. They haven’t been okay. And Jee deserves better than two parents who are too busy ignoring the rot in the foundation.”

Buck couldn’t help but wonder about the Maddie and Chimney in the other timeline. Were they broken up now, too? No, probably not. God knew he could understand how months apart could kill a relationship, and the other Maddie hadn’t run off to Boston. Although, if that was the cause of death, it was odd that they put it off for weeks, because they’d both come back before he did.

“I’m sorry,” he lamely said, standing up. “Uh… Can I offer you something to drink during this trying time?”

“Sure. What do you have?”

“Uh.” He went into the kitchen area and to his fridge, already knowing what he wasn’t going to find there. “I have bourbon and beer. And water.”

“Jesus, Buck.”

“What?” He ducked his head, drawing his shoulders up to his ears. “I- I haven’t been to the grocery store this week yet.”

“Oh, but you’ve made time for the liquor store.” She was shaking her head at him. “Chimney told me you came into work with a hangover the other day. Again.”

“I did not,” he lied. He totally had. These days, ibuprofen was as much a regular part of his breakfast before work as bacon and eggs were. “Do any of you do anything else besides talk about me behind my back? Athena and Bobby, you and Chimney. I bet you all have a whole group chat for it.”

“Buck, we’re just-”

“Concerned?” he snapped, and then felt bad for it when Jee-Yun whined in her stroller. “Shove your concern, Mads. It’s a bit too late, and a lot unhelpful.”

“Was it my fault?” she asked, looking so guilty it threw him out of his building anger.

“Was what your fault?”

“Christmas.”

“No!”

(But wasn’t it? a small part of him whispered. At least partially? If she had stayed instead of breaking and running, then would he have broken and run? If she had stayed instead of leaving, all those years before, would he have been so terrified of being left behind then?

Buck did his best to ignore that quiet whisper, because his choices were no one’s fault but his own.)

“I just can’t help but think that you needed me,” Maddie said, gnawing at her lip. “You needed me and I wasn’t there. Again.”

“Hey, no.” He rounded the island to gently take her by the arms. “You were sick.”

“And you weren’t?” she asked, and he jerked in surprise at the question.

“What?”

“Healthy people don’t go into fugue states, Buck,” she said. “And since you’ve been back, you’ve been… Not okay.”

“What? No, I- I’m fine.”

That had his sister scoffing at him.

“And that’s why you smell like a brewery at three in the afternoon,” Maddie said, rolling her eyes. Indignation was a much better look on her than guilt, in his opinion. “That’s why you’re getting into fights with Bobby and holing up in here. Because you’re fine.”

“I am,” he bit out, trying to keep his temper in check as he stepped away from her, letting his hands drop to his sides. He was too sober for this. “Please don’t tell me this is why you came over. To nag at me.”

“I came over because you’re my brother and I’m worried about you.” She took a step forward to grab his hand, but he stepped back out of her reach. “Buck. We are all worried about you.”

“Well, don’t be,” he said, wrapping his arms around himself. “I’m fine.”

“Yeah, sure. And what does Taylor have to say about everything? What does she have to say about you drinking in the middle of the afternoon like this?”

“Taylor?”

“Taylor Kelly,” Maddie said, looking at him like he was being particularly thick. “Your girlfriend.”

“She hasn’t been my girlfriend for weeks now, Mads. We broke up.”

“When!?”

“Three days after I came back,” Buck answered. Did he seriously forget to tell them all that? Woops.

“You come back after going missing for a month and she breaks up with you?” Despite everything, it was nice to have someone getting indignant on his behalf. But…

“I broke up with her.”

“What?” Maddie was looking at him like he’d grown a second head. “Why?”

“Because I didn’t love her. Because I wasn’t happy with her. Because I deserved better.” He shrugged. “Take your pick.”

“What is going on with you, Buck? This isn’t like you.”

“Seriously?” He couldn’t help rolling his eyes. “I’m the one ending a relationship for once and suddenly you think I’m a pod person?”

“I didn’t say that.”

“You’ve changed, Buck,” he mocked. “We’re worried about you, Buck.”

“Because you have and we are!”

“Don’t be, I’m fine.”

“No you’re not. Buck, please,” she begged. “Whatever happened during that month, you can tell us about it.”

“Like I’ve told you and everyone else, I don’t remember what happened.”

“You’re still not a good liar.”

“Fine, you want honesty?” He pointed at the door. “I want you to go.”

Maddie clearly hadn’t been expecting that and spent several seconds glancing between him and the door in confusion.

“What?” she finally said.

“Go!” he snapped. “I want you to leave. It’s what you’re good at, isn’t it? Leaving.”

Buck regretted the words the moment they were out of his mouth. If he could, if he was able, he’d grab them out of the air and take them back. He could literally see them hit her, could see the way she flinched and teared up. Maddie wobbled in place, taking a step back to keep her balance.

“Mads-” he tried, but she already had Jee-Yun’s stroller by the handle and was wrenching open the door. By the time his rushed “I’m sorry” made it out his mouth, the door was already falling shut in her wake.

And then he was left alone. Or, almost alone.

“Was that dramatic enough for you?” he spat at the blue butterfly that was still sitting placidly on the railing above it all.

It didn’t answer, but then again, Buck wasn’t expecting it to.

“We are at the recycling plant…”

Eddie sat in his shitty office for his shitty job, watching the news cover a call he knew he should have been on. A call he knew he would have been on, had he not run away from it like he had, because he could see the 118’s truck at the bottom of the screen. Bobby would probably be sending Buck up there to grab the jumper as she talked. Or maybe not. He couldn’t really say he knew what any of them would do on a call, anymore.

Linda, one of the dispatchers, knocked on the open door to get his attention.

“Hey, Eddie.” She handed him a paper with a list on it. “I need you to tweet about these road closures. We’ve got a jumper.”

Of course. Because tweeting about calls was his job now.

“Do they know who she is?” he asked as he reluctantly started doing it.

“Remember that lady from the Gas-N-Slip who almost set herself on fire?” Linda asked. “Apparently, she’s lost the ability to be afraid. Is that even a real thing?”

Never before, Eddie thought as he turned on his radio to listen, had he felt so jealous of a jumper before. Lost the ability to be afraid? He could only wish that was him. He had spent so long being so afraid that it had become ingrained in him. Even now, as miserable as he was, the thought of going back out there with the others terrified him.

“I can’t function like this.” The jumper’s voice over the radio sounded raspy. “I feel like I am coming out of my skin, literally.”

He finished sending out the information, slamming the button perhaps a bit too hard. Not really sure what he was doing, he clipped the radio to his belt and walked to the main part of dispatch. It was Josh on the call, so he walked over to hover behind him.

“Josh, Hen wants to talk to her,” May said from her station.

“Why?” Eddie blurted out.

“She thinks she knows what’s wrong with her.”

“This lady already knows exactly what’s wrong with her.” Whether or not it was the actual cause, the jumper had already come up with both the problem and the solution. “She isn’t looking to be diagnosed while she’s literally on the edge. Hell, if someone started doing that to me, I’d probably go ahead and take the leap.”

With how Buck had been recently, having a victim literally jumping to her death in front of him would push him right over the edge as well. And that just was not a chance Eddie was willing to take.

“So what do you suggest instead, Firefighter Diaz?” Josh asked, getting snippy. 

“Let me talk to her,” Eddie said, already grabbing a spare earpiece and tuning in.

“I really did try to make the best of it,” the jumper was saying.

“Fine,” the dispatcher huffed. Behind them, May was informing someone - her mom or Hen herself - that Eddie was hijacking another call.  “Pauline, I have someone who wants to talk to you. They think they can help.”

“No one can help me,” Pauline said.

“I have someone who wants to try.”

“Hi, Pauline,” Eddie said, taking that as his cue. “I’ve been told that you can’t feel fear?”

“I haven’t felt afraid in months,” she said.

“Is it in poor taste to say that I’m extremely jealous?”

“Why?” she spat. “You don’t know how it feels, how empty I am.”

“I know it feels like I’m always afraid,” he told her, ignoring the looks both May and Josh shot him at those words. “And I spent so long pretending like I wasn’t, like being afraid was some sort of weakness. A friend of mine - my best friend, one of the most important people in my life - told me that there’s no shame in fear. But I don’t think those words are helpful here, are they?”

“No,” Pauline said. “I’m up here, praying and hoping that my stomach will drop or that the hairs on the back of my neck will stand up. But there’s nothing. I just feel so empty. If I operate out of fear and it goes away, how am I supposed to operate?”

“I wish I could answer that, but I literally can’t imagine a day where I’m not afraid,” he told her. “A few months ago, I would have been there on the ground looking up at you.” Lies. He would have been going up with Buck to grab her, but she didn’t need to know that. “But I was afraid and refused to admit it, and then the moment I had an excuse, I ran away. And now, even though I hate my new job, I’m still too terrified to go back.”

“So, what, you’re saying I’m lucky?”

“I’m saying that I’m jealous, just like I told you before. How well do you sleep?”

“I don’t.”

“Well damn, I guess you can’t have it all.” Eddie focused on the woman on the television screen at the end of the room, ignoring the others around him. There, right on the edge of the screen, he could see someone’s helmeted head peeking around a corner. “I don’t get much sleep either. I have to make sure the doors and windows are locked every night. And then I make sure again, because, what if? You know? And then I lay there and every. Single. Noise. Outside has me leaping from my skin.”

“Someone tried to rob me at a gas station today,” she said. “They had a gun, threatened to shoot me.”

“I’ve been shot before. It isn’t fun.”

“I didn’t care. I ended up dousing us both in gasoline and then threatened to light us both up.” Pauline’s sigh had the line crackling. “I shouldn’t have done that.”

“My friend, the one I told you about? He went missing on Christmas,” Eddie said. “I broke his heart and he ran off. I spent weeks imagining all of the things that could have happened to him. Every night I came up with a new scenario that was worse than the last. And yet, until our son practically begged me to, I was too terrified to go find him. The second most important person in my life, and I was almost too scared to make sure he was okay.”

“Did you find him?”

“I did.”

“And was he okay?”

“I don’t think either of us are okay,” he said, and then he asked, “Why did you call?”

“What?” The surprise at the non-sequitur was obvious in her voice. The question had the others frowning at him, too.

“Why did you call?” Eddie repeated. On the television he could see Buck - even at the distance from the camera, he could recognize Buck anywhere - creeping up behind Pauline with someone that might have been Ravi. “You said no one can help you. You say you can’t feel fear. So why did you call?”

“I… I don’t know.”

“I think you do know,” he told her. “I think that, even though you said no one could help you, you still wanted someone to help you. There’s no shame in that, you know? Wanting help. Needing help. Because there are plenty of people who will.”

And then, because they were in sync even when they were an entire city apart, Buck lunged for Pauline just as Eddie finished speaking. He could hear her grunt in his ear as she was grabbed, and watched on the television as she was dragged back from the edge.

“We’ve got her, Eds,” Buck told him, having caught her phone before it could fall in the commotion.

“Good,” he breathed out, before yanking out the earpiece and flinging it onto Josh’s station. It felt like everyone was staring at him, and May definitely was. After waiting a moment to watch as Buck and the other firefighter - he was almost certain it was Ravi - guided Pauline to safety, he fled from the room. His office was shitty, and more like a cave than something resembling comfortable.

But it was safe.

And he still had to do the homework Frank had given him, although he had plenty of excuses to put it off for now.

Another night ending in Y, and Buck was spending it getting drunk. Again. Sometimes he wished he could spend all day, every day drunk as a skunk, but he wasn’t so far gone as to forget what that would mean for his job. Being a firefighter was one of the only things keeping him afloat right now, and he did not want to risk losing it.

He had sent Maddie a flurry of texts apologizing for his words after she didn’t answer five calls in a row. The only response he had gotten from her had been a succinct “it’s fine” and then silence. But it wasn’t fine, nothing about it was fine.

Maybe he should just stop talking to people. Maybe then he’d stop saying things that hurt them.

The call earlier that day had been… Something. He wasn’t sure what it was, but it was something. He hadn’t heard whatever Edmundo had been telling the woman, had only known he was talking to her because May had told them all over the radio what he was doing. He could have switched over to the channel they were using, but then he wouldn’t have been able to communicate with Bobby on the ground. Buck had managed to catch the jumper’s - Pauline’s - side of the conversation, though.

“Did you find him?” she had asked, and then a moment later, “And was he okay?”

Buck didn’t know why Edmundo was talking to her about him, but he had been. That much was obvious based on the looks everyone was giving him when he and Ravi made it to the ground with her. He’d sent Edmundo a quick text of congratulations, something that read so incredibly generic, and gotten a one word reply of thanks.

He wanted to go to the Diaz residence after shift, he wanted to go home. But that wasn’t his home, and Christopher was still giving him the cold shoulder. So he was drinking alone in his loft again. Or not alone, because that fucking butterfly had once again shown up. This time it had taken up residence in his hair, despite all his efforts to make it go away.

His phone started ringing while he was opening the fifth bottle of a six pack, which he’d started on after he finished the bourbon. Buck had almost ignored the ringing, and almost let it go to voicemail, but then his drunken mind recognized the ringtone.

“Chris!” he slurred, so very happy. His kid was calling him! His kid actually wanted to talk to him again! This was-

“Dad!” The boy was shouting on the other end of the line, and even through the haze he understood something was wrong. The fact that he could hear Edmundo shouting and something breaking in the background helped get the point across. “Dad! Dad!”

“Chris?”

“Buck! Something’s wrong with Dad!”

“O- Okay. I’ll be right there, okay?” Buck said, scrabbling to get his shoes on, nearly falling on his face. “I- I’m on my way. I promise.”

Fuck, where were his keys? Where had he thrown his- There! Scooping them off the floor where they’d fallen, he stumbled out his door, barely remembering to lock it behind him. He rushed down the stairs, taking the steps two and three at a time, keeping a stream of reassurances for Chris going the entire way.

“Okay, Chris, I’m getting into the jeep, so I’m hanging up now,” he said. “I- I’ll be there soon, okay?”

“Okay,” Chris said, sounding so very young and so very scared.

“Everything will be fine,” Buck told him, before ending the call and tossing his phone into the passenger seat. He’d have preferred staying on the line with him, but he left his bluetooth in the loft and Athena would kill him if she caught him driving with his phone to his ear. Catching a glimpse of himself in the rearview mirror, he saw the butterfly was still on his head, like the weirdest hairpin. It had stayed there during his entire sprint to his jeep, as it had been since it showed up again while he was drinking.

While he was drinking.

Shit.

“Oh God, what am I doing,” he whispered, leaning forward to rest his forehead on the steering wheel. He should not be driving right now. He knew better than to be driving right now. But… “They need me. An Uber would take too long. Oh God, oh God…”

Chris and Edmundo needed him. Now.

He shouldn’t be driving.

But they needed him.

“Just this once,” Buck promised himself. “It’ll be fine. Just this once.”

He sat back in the seat. The butterfly moved from his head to sit on the dashboard in front of him. It felt like it was staring at him.

“Please,” he begged, unsure of who he was begging to. “They need me. I- I’ll do anything.”

The butterfly stayed silent, not like he had expected anything else.

Buck started the engine.

Chapter End Notes

PSA:
DO NOT DRINK AND DRIVE.
DO NOT.
IS VERY BAD.
DO NOT DO IT.

Impact

Buck wasn’t quite certain how he made the trip from his loft without crashing or being pulled over. He knew he was doing the stupidest thing ever, worse than any other reckless stunt he’d ever pulled. But Chris needed him, Edmundo needed him, and- Well, There was an entire history of him making poor decisions when it came to that.

Perhaps the adrenaline sobered him up enough, perhaps he was just lucky, perhaps the universe or something like it actually had his back for once. Either way, he pulled up in front of the Diaz residence less than half an hour after Chris had called him, and rushed inside, barely paying attention to the butterfly that lingered in the jeep. This time, unlike the last times he was over, he didn’t hesitate to use his key to let himself in.

“Chris?” Buck called out as he entered, looking around. He spotted the boy down the hall, standing in front of the bathroom, looking stressed. Rushing to him, he practically slid to his knees. “Hey, buddy. Are you okay?”

“He won’t come out,” the boy told him. “I keep calling him, but he won’t answer.”

Dread, which had been making itself at home in his stomach since the phone call, creeped through his veins and coiled around his heart. For Edmundo to not answer when Chris called for him…

“Y- You just wait here, okay?” Buck said, cupping the back of Chris’ neck. “I’m gonna go get him, okay?”

“Okay.”

Even with all the ways he had failed, Chris still had utter faith in him here.

Getting to his feet, he ran to the end of the hall, to Edmundo’s door. Gripping the doorknob, he hesitated for the briefest of moments.

“H- Hey, Eds? It’s me. Can I come in?” Utter silence. He tried the knob, but it was locked, a fact which only made him more concerned. This door was almost never locked. The only times it was, was- Well, definitely not the case here. “A- Alright, I’m gonna come in, okay? Stay away from the door.”

He glanced behind him, at where Chris was watching, eyes wide. Buck took a deep breath, and then threw his weight into the door. The latch splintered easily, a part of it flying through the air as it swung open. Inside, the bedroom was wrecked. There were holes in the wall, the curtains were yanked off the window, pieces of furniture were toppled over and picture frames were smashed.

He didn’t see the other man, but he could hear what sounded far too much like someone crying.

“Eddie?” Looking around, he spotted movement on the other side of the bed, where the stupid treadmill had been tipped over. “Oh God, Eds?”

He rushed around, trying his best to keep from tripping over the broken pieces of Eddie’s life, to get to his side.

“Hey, hey, what’s going on?” Buck asked, crouching down, eyes flickering over him. He didn’t look hurt, there wasn’t any blood aside from on his knuckles, where it looked like he’d made some of those holes with his own fists. He was also clutching his bat, hunched over it as his entire body shook.

“They’re all dead.”

“Who? Who’s dead?”

“Everybody that I saved,” he said, looking as broken as the room around him. “They’re all dead.”

Not knowing what else to do, Buck followed his instincts. He wrapped his arms around the other man, pulling him in and squeezing him tight. Edmundo stiffened for a moment, before letting himself sink against Buck’s chest. 

“I’m, uh. Sorry for your loss?” he tried. It managed to get a small snort of laughter out of the man. Edmundo sniffed, and then sniffed again, pulling away enough to look at him weird.

“Buck-?”

“Dad?” Chris’ voice interrupted whatever his father was about to ask.

“Stay out there, buddy!” Buck told him. “There’s glass.”

“Oh, God,” Edmundo said, the words coming out as a gasping sob. “Chris…”

“I’ve got him,” he assured the other man. “Don’t worry about it, okay? I’ve got him.”

With another squeeze of his arms around him, he let go and went to check on Chris. The boy was hovering just outside of the bedroom, peering anxiously at the wreckage. Buck wrapped an arm around him, gently guiding him away. He didn’t think Edmundo particularly wanted his son to see him in this state, and he didn’t think it would do Chris any good, either.

“Let’s get you back to your room, buddy,” he said.

“But dad…”

“He’s… He isn’t hurt, bud.” He couldn’t say that Edmundo was okay, because he very clearly was not. When they got to Chris’ bedroom, he hesitated a moment before following him in, pulling the door to behind him. An extra barrier to shield the kid from the absolute shitstorm that had made landfall in the house.

“What happened?” Chris demanded. “What’s wrong with dad?”

“He, uh.” Had a minor breakdown. Just a small one. Nothing to worry about. “Got some news and didn’t react well to it. But he’ll be fine, okay? I promise.”

“What kind of news?”

“Uh, bad news. I’m not sure.”

Chris stared at him intently from where he was sitting on the bed, like he was trying to catch him in a lie. Finally, after a long, long moment, he nodded.

“He’s going to be okay?”

“Absolutely. I promise,” Buck said, and then, “Thank you for calling me, Chris.”

Thank you for trusting me with this even after I fucked everything up, he didn’t say.

He spent a few minutes more, making sure Chris was okay and getting him calmed down. The kid’s terror had settled down into a thrumming anxiety, and he was understandably worried about his father. But Buck managed to assure him that everything would be alright, even though things very much weren’t in the moment.

Easing himself out the bedroom, still rattling off assurances, he glanced around for where Edmundo had ended up. He had heard him walking past the door, and so knew he was no longer in the wreckage of the bedroom. It didn’t take long to spot him in the living room, gazing out the front window. He turned around when Buck closed Chris’ door, frowning heavily.

“Is he okay?”

“Better off than you, right now,” Buck told him. “Kinda scared, mostly worried.”

The words seemed to break Edmundo just that bit more, bringing fresh tears. There was no hesitating this time as Buck rushed across the room, wrapping his arms around the other man again. Edmundo practically collapsed against him, sagging into his chest.

“I- I didn’t mean to-” he tried, stuttering out the words. “I just… Lost it.”

“Why?” Buck asked him. This wasn’t like the other man, either version of him. The closest to it was maybe the fight club, but even that had been an exercise of tightly coiled control. “What happened, Eddie?”

“Frank wanted me to reach out to some of the people I served with,” he explained, mostly into Buck’s shoulder. “Therapy homework.”

“You kept in touch with them?”

“No. I haven’t talked to any of them since I moved out here,” Edmundo said. “I pulled four people out of that chopper alive. Chief died in country a couple years later.”

“And the others?” Buck figured he already knew the answer, but was compelled to ask all the same.

“Norwhal died in a car crash, Binder OD’d, and Mills… Mills shot herself last August.”

God, no wonder Edmundo took a bat to everything. Buck wanted to take a bat to everything. This… This wasn’t fair. Nothing about this was fair, or right.

“I’m sorry,” he said, and knew it meant nothing in the face of this.

“I got that stupid, fucking medal for saving them. But I didn’t really save any of them.”

And Buck honestly didn’t know what to say to that. He didn’t know what magical words were needed to make it all okay. Perhaps a better, smarter man would know them. But there was only Buck. So instead of saying anything, he guided/manhandled Edmundo over to the couch, ending up with the other man half-laying on top of him.

“Is there, uh. Is there anything you want to watch?” Buck asked, entirely unsure of what to do here. He spotted the remote on the coffee table and debated if he could reach it without sending Edmundo to the floor.

“Not really, no.”

“Okay,” he said, leaving the remote where it was. “Is… Is there anything I can…?”

“Just… Can you just stay here? Please?”

“Yeah, I can do that.”

They fell into silence then, and Buck took the opportunity to toe off his shoes. He really should have done it the moment he got in, and he knew Edmundo would have bitched at him for it if he’d been in a better state.

“Buck?”

“Yeah?”

“Did you-” Edmundo cut himself off, shaking his head. “Do you think that demon-”

“Ichabod isn’t a demon,” was Buck’s automatic correction. “And whatever you’re thinking, don’t.”

The other man looked like he wanted to argue, but seemed to decide it was better to let it drop. They fell into silence, Buck holding Edmundo as his shaking, shuddering gasps finally turned into the even breaths of a restless slumber. It took a bit of doing, because the other man had grabbed hold of his shirt with a grip as tight as anything, but he managed to wiggle out from under him and get him laying on the couch properly.

He looked so very small, and so very broken.

Unbidden, unwanted, Buck wondered how Eddie was doing. Did he just find out about the deaths of those he had saved? Had he reacted the same? Or had the other Buck been there to catch him, before he fell? Probably. That other Buck doubtlessly hadn’t made a mess of things like he had.

Hesitating a moment to run a soothing hand through the sleeping man’s hair, he went to Chris’ room to check on him again, waiting for permission after knocking before he poked his head in. The boy was at his computer, half-heartedly playing something. Buck had the thought that maybe he should be telling the kid to get ready for bed, but it was a Saturday, and these were extenuating circumstances.

“Hey, bud. Have you two eaten dinner already?”

“Yeah.”

“Okay. Good. Uh, your dad’s asleep on the couch,” Buck told him, glancing back down the hall to make sure it was still true. “If you need me, I’ll be in his bedroom, picking up. Okay?”

“Okay.”

“Don’t stay up too late,” he said. The boy frowned at him but nodded, and he eased the door shut again. In the other timeline, with the other Chris, he wouldn’t have hesitated in these parenting decisions. But did he really have the right anymore to tell this Chris what to do?

Instead of heading for the bedroom right away, he cut through the dining room and into the kitchen. He didn’t find what he wanted in the fridge, though. It only had milk and juice. Letting out a groan, he closed it back, letting his head rest against it.

“Probably for the best,” he said aloud. God, what the fuck had he been thinking? No, he knew what he’d been thinking. That they had needed him, that he needed to get here as soon as possible. Stupid, stupid, so fucking stupid. Anything, anything, could have happened to him on the way over. It was pure luck that he hadn’t been pulled over or hadn’t crashed. It was pure luck that he hadn’t caused a pile up, like that drunk driver last year.

His own drunken, desperate words to that butterfly - “Please. They need me. I- I’ll do anything.” - came back to him then, and Buck let out another groan. He sincerely hoped it had been purely luck that kept his stupid decisions from exploding in his face again. The last thing he needed was to be… What? Indebted, somehow? He didn't know, didn’t want to know. Ichabod had warned him so long ago about saying those sorts of words in the presence of things less kind than the not-demon. Which was an impressive concept in hindsight, because Ichabod was a complete and utter dick.

Of course, thinking about that warning reminded him of what led up to that warning, and… Well, he hurried out of the kitchen and to Edmundo’s room. It was still an utter mess, not having been magically fixed up in his absence. After a glance at the debris on the floor, he backtracked to grab the broom. He’d have to get some plaster from the store later for the holes in the wall, but the rest of it he should be able to get done tonight.

Buck was supposed to have a shift tomorrow, but screw that. He’ll call Bobby and tell him something came up, and then focus on the things that couldn’t just be patched over.

On Sunday, the day after Edmundo’s minor breakdown, Buck did indeed end up calling into work. He had absolutely no regrets about it, too. He cooked breakfast for his boys, and for the first time in weeks, Christopher managed to tolerate his presence without looking at him like something he’d scraped off the bottom of his shoe. Not to say that things were peachy-keen, because they weren’t. There was an awkwardness between the three of them that clung to everything they did.

None of them really talked about what happened the night before, side stepping it with an ease that came from practice. Chris didn’t question it and was simply content with visible proof that his father hadn’t literally broken apart at the seams. They curled up on the couch for most of the day, watching questionable television and streaming movies, with the boy clinging to his father far more than he typically did anymore. For dinner, instead of cooking anything, they ordered pizza.

Edmundo once again fell asleep on the couch come evening, sometime after Christopher had gone to bed himself. It was only through sheer force of will that Buck didn’t follow him, because he hadn’t actually slept the night before. He got him covered in a blanket, and then tried to figure out where he was sleeping. Or rather, he debated the pros and cons of camping on the floor versus sleeping in the bed. It wasn’t really much of a debate, to be honest.

The bed smelled the exact same as he remembered, and even though it made him ache, it was the best sleep he’d gotten since he came back. 

In the morning, Buck struggled with the decision of waking Edmundo up to take Chris to school, or to let him keep sleeping. It was an easy choice, because the bags under his eyes had started making a return in the past few weeks and he barely stirred at all during the controlled chaos of the morning routine, but Buck was loath to risk him waking up alone.

What was it he had put in that letter for Maddie, nearly a year ago now? That they had an entire family willing to help, if only they asked?

“Buck?” Bobby sounded wide awake when he called, which made him feel much less guilty about this.

“Hey, Cap. Could you, uh, come to Eddie’s?”

“Of course,” Bobby said, because he would always come running if they called. That had never been in doubt. “What’s going on?”

“I’ll explain when you get here,” he said, and then ended the call.

The captain pulled up just as he was getting Christopher out the door, and he patiently waited while Buck made sure the boy was settled in the jeep. Chris was happy to see him, of course, having not actually laid eyes on the man since… Probably since the day of Buck’s return, when they were all gathered at the hospital.

“So what’s going on?” Bobby asked him, the moment the backdoor of the jeep was shut.

“Eddie found out the other night that some of his friends from his army days passed,” Buck said, not going into details because it was none of the captain’s business. “And he, uh. Didn’t take it well.”

“That’s why you called in yesterday.” It wasn’t a question, but Buck nodded anyway. “Where is he now?”

“Inside, I didn’t want to wake him up, and I don’t want him waking up alone.”

“I didn’t realize things were that bad,” Bobby said, and the words had Buck’s temper flaring.

“Maybe you would if you checked in yourself, instead of-” He bit off the words, shaking his head to clear his head. Priorities, Buck. Priorities. “I need to get Chris to school, and then I have to make a couple stops.”

He definitely needed to stop by the loft for a change of clothes - he had borrowed some of Edmundo’s the day before - and then the store for the things he’d need to fix the bedroom wall.

“He’ll be fine until you get back,” Bobby said, still looking a bit taken aback with being snapped at. Buck didn’t say anything, simply nodded and hopped into the jeep. 

Taking Chris to school was something he had done plenty of times before, in one form or another, and it didn’t take too long. Afterwards, he went to the loft for a change of clothes, taking a quick shower and packing a bag, because he had the feeling he would be staying another night at the Diaz residence, at least. He hit the store on the way back to Edmundo, getting everything he would need to patch up the bedroom wall. He didn’t get paint, because he had no clue what particular shade of off-beige was needed.

Edmundo was sitting up on the couch and talking to Bobby when he came in, bags dangling from his hands. Both men fell into silence when he entered, pausing their conversation to focus on him. It was unsettling, and made him thankful he left the beer he had impulse bought back in the jeep.

“Hey Cap,” he said, setting the shopping bags down. “Eds, you’ve eaten yet?”

“I made him breakfast,” Bobby told him.

“Thanks, for getting Chris to school,” Edmundo said, and Buck scoffed.

“You really don’t need to thank me for that,” he said, shaking his head.

“I’m going to head off, then,” Bobby said, patting Edmundo on the shoulder. He headed for the door, but paused when he got to Buck’s side. “I’m going to assume that you aren’t making it to your shift this afternoon, either?”

Buck didn’t even have to think about it.

“No.”

The captain merely nodded and patted Buck on the arm as well, before going out the door.

“What’s all that, then?” Edmundo asked, getting up to peek into the bags.

“Stuff for your wall,” he said. “I actually get to put my drywalling skills to work after however many years.”

“Thank you,” the other man said, practically a whisper.

“Hey, you weren’t wrong when you said I’m the guy that likes to fix things,” Buck said, shrugging as he toed off his shoes and left them by the door.

“I guess I wasn’t.”

Buck picked the bags back up and carried them to the bedroom, Edmundo following him the entire way. The other man was silent as he worked, seemingly content to sit on the bed and watch Buck mend the wounds he’d inflicted on his own home. None of them were very big, and so it didn’t take a lot of work. He mostly slapped mesh patches over them and covered them up in spackling paste.

“We’ll have to get paint later,” Buck told him as he covered up the last hole.

“It’s a date,” Edmundo joked. Or, it sounded like it was supposed to be a joke, but the energy wasn’t in it.

“I’ve had worse ones.”

“With Abby? Taylor? Or him?”

Buck sighed and set down the taping knife he’d been using. He was done with it, anyway.

“Pretty sure nothing is worse than having to get an emergency tracheotomy because you choked on the bread,” he said, turning around to look up at Edmundo from his spot crouched on the floor. “And Taylor and I never really went out on a date.”

“And him?”

“Do you really want to talk about him?” Buck asked, sitting down and getting comfortable against the part of the wall that hadn’t needed fixing. “Because he hated being compared to you.”

“You’re right, I don’t want to talk about him,” Edmundo said, sliding off the bed to sit on the floor as well, stretching out his legs as far as he could. “Let’s talk about you.”

“Talk about me?” Haha, no. “We should talk about you. You’re the one-”

“My crisis is slow burning. We can focus on you for the moment.”

Buck glared at him and then pointed at the wall above, where the spackle was still drying.

“That was not slow burning. Your son calling me, terrified out of his mind, is not slow burning.”

“Fine,” Edmundo snapped. “Then let me put it another way. The people I saved died anyway, and I can’t fix that. So I’m going to focus on what I can do something about.”

“Yeah? Like what?”

“How many times have you gone into work drunk?”

Buck jerked back so harshly at that, that he slammed his head into the wall behind him. He winced and had to check that there wasn’t another hole in need of patching up.

“How many- What? Why the hell are you asking me that?” he asked, turning back to the other man.

“Answer the fucking question, Buck.”

“I don’t- Never, okay? I have never gone into work drunk. What the hell, Eddie?”

“Then how many times have you gone in hungover?” he asked, glaring so fiercely Buck was certain he would actually catch fire. “Besides almost every single shift for the past couple of weeks.”

“Who the hell have you been gossiping with?”

“Hen mentioned something, the last time I took Chris over there,” Edmundo said. “And I thought, surely she was just exaggerating. Surely-”

“Y’know, one day everyone might figure out that talking behind someone’s back isn’t-”

“Buck!” the other man snapped, shutting him up. “You drove over here drunk, the other night.”

“No, I didn’t,” he lied, pulling his legs up to his chest and hunching up his shoulders. Edmundo scoffed and rolled his eyes.

“I fucking smelled it on your breath, and I saw your jeep sitting out front.” Buck hadn’t seen the other man this pissed off since The Grocery Store, probably. He was seething with anger. “What the fuck, Buck?”

“It still rhymes.”

“Buck! What the fuck were you thinking?”

“I was thinking that you needed me!” Buck finally shouted back. “I was thinking that something was wrong and that Chris was fucking terrified and that I needed to be here!”

“And if something had happened? If you got pulled over? If you crashed? We’ve seen what happens when idiots drive while drunk!”

“It was just the once,” he said, ducking away from Edmundo’s glare. Because he had thought of that, and decided the risks were worth it. “It won’t happen again.”

“It fucking better not,” Edmundo snarled. “What the hell is going on with you? Because ever since the night you came here drunk, it seems like it’s been a downward slide with you.”

“I- I’ve been fine.”

“Bull-fucking-shit. You’re getting into fights with Bobby, you’re being cruel to Maddie, you’re going into work hungover, you’re lying about hurting your hand- Yeah, I asked Hen about that, too, when I saw her. What’s going on?”

“Nothing’s going on.”

“I don’t believe you.”

“That sounds like a you problem,” Buck sniped back, getting hit by an odd sense of deja vu.

“Buck…”

“No, seriously. Nothing’s going on, everything’s fine.”

“Athena found you drinking alone in your loft.” Fuck, so she had noticed. “Maddie found you drinking alone in your loft. I bet you were drinking alone when Chris called you. Buck, please.”

Edmundo got up on his knees, shuffling over so he was right in Buck’s space. He reached forward and gripped his shoulder, thumb pressing into his collarbone.

“Talk to me. Please.”

Buck looked down at his hand, and then back at him. And then, he said,

“I just want to forget, sometimes…”

Mending

“I just want to forget, sometimes,” Buck told him, leaning his head back against the wall. “Or, all the time, really. Everything here is just so… So… I don’t know. Different, but not? The same, but not? I miss them, I miss him, and I just… Want to forget.”

“So you’re drinking to forget the other versions of everyone?” Edmundo said, doubt evident in both tone and expression.

“Yes. No. Not… Not just…” Buck let out a sigh and wrapped his arms around his legs, resting his chin on his knees. Edmundo still had a hand on his shoulder, his thumb rubbing circles into his collar bone. There was guilt there, that the other man was comforting him when it had been Edmundo who broke down just the other night. Maybe they were just taking turns, now. “Four years and change, Eds. And I missed you and Chris so fucking much, every fucking day. They weren’t you. They were, but they weren’t. And you aren’t him, except that you are, in a lot of ways. Does that make sense?”

“No,” was Edmundo’s honest answer.

“He accused me of loving a ghost, once,” he said. “And the worst part is that he was right. Because from the day I met him, I saw it as like… Like meeting you, again. And I never took the time to think about how changing things would change him.”

“He told me that you’d been looking back at me the entire time he knew you.”

“Because I was. And it hurt him so much, Eds.” He let out another sigh. “What all did you talk about during your little chat?”

It was still such a wild idea to Buck. That both versions of the man he loved had managed to stumble upon each other at the crossroads, that Ichabod had thrown them together and probably sat back with popcorn to watch the show. It explained a lot about Eddie’s behavior after Buck woke up in that hospital bed. But he still didn’t know what all they talked about, except that Eddie was apparently not very nice to Edmundo.

Which wasn’t surprising, honestly. Eddie might not have hated Edmundo, but he certainly didn’t like him. He had made his opinions of Edmundo’s choices very, very clear over the years.

“You, mostly,” Edmundo answered, letting go of his shoulder and settling back on his haunches.

“Me?”

“And why I left the One Eighteen.”

“You told me, before you started seeing Frank again, that it was because you were scared. That you were running away, too.”

“The other me, the other Eddie, called me out on my bullshit. He was a dick about it, but… I should have talked to you before Christmas.”

“I wouldn’t have run away, if you had,” Buck said, and the other man grimaced but acknowledged the point with a nod. “I wouldn’t have reacted well, but I wouldn’t have panicked. If we had just sat down and talked, a lot of things wouldn’t have happened.”

“Like you letting yourself get shot?” Edmundo asked, and the question had Buck reaching to rub at a spot where a scar never was. “You and I talked about that afterwards, at least. I thought I was perfectly clear about how you weren’t expendable, about how it wouldn’t have been better if you were the one who got shot.”

“I know what your blood tastes like, Eds.”

“And he knew how yours tasted,” he said. “It would not have been better, Buck. With how upset he was, the fact that he ended up at that place, I can tell you that it wasn’t better. I shouldn’t… I should have gone about it a better way, I think, than heaving custody of our kid at you and expecting you to understand what I wasn’t saying. I should have made it clearer that I didn’t want you getting hurt because I didn’t want you getting hurt, and not because of anything else.”

“Chris is important, though,” Buck said. “What happens to him is important.”

“And so are you. You’re important, too. What happens to you is important, too.” Edmundo’s face turned into a scowl. “I am still pissed that you’d risk your fucking life by driving while drunk.”

“I… I’m sorry,” Buck said, dropping his eyes to the floor. “I shouldn’t have done that. I knew better, and… And I shouldn’t have done that.”

“We’re going to the loft after this,” the other man declared.

“Why?”

“So I can clear out whatever alcohol you have stashed away, because you have a problem and fuck you if you think I’m not going to do something about it. And then… And then you’re going to stay here, so I can make sure you don’t go out and get more.”

Eddie and Edmundo really were alike, in all the ways that counted.

“Eds, I-”

“Please, Buck.” Edmundo’s voice broke a little. “I… I would sleep better, if you were here.”

Buck lifted his eyes up to look at the other man’s face. He’d taken note of the return of bags beneath Edmundo’s eyes, before. He knew they’d started going away after his return, and that they’d made a comeback after he stopped spending so much time at the Diaz residence. But he’d just thought that was because… He didn’t know what he’d thought, anymore.

“Eddie?”

“I got shot in broad daylight in the middle of the street, Buck,” Eddie said. Whispered, really. “My wife died crossing the street. You and Chris went out for a day at the pier and were nearly killed by the ocean. That call before Christmas, that man was nearly killed backing up into his own driveway. A family almost lost their lives because someone decided to stick a bomb under their truck for who knows what reason. You… You left one day, and didn’t come back for a month, and no one could find you. You just disappeared, because some sort of not-demon scooped you up and dumped you elsewhere.”

“You’re afraid,” Buck breathed out. The other man had mentioned it before, when he was talking about going back to Frank. He had told him that he was scared of something happening to cause him to leave Chris behind. But Buck hadn’t realized how much more that fear was, how all encompassing it was.

“All the time,” Edmundo admitted, tears gathering at the corners of his eyes. “Of everything, it feels like. The entire time you were gone, I couldn’t… I kept thinking about all the things that could have happened to you. Anything, anything, could have happened to you. Every time I closed my eyes, all I could see was your jeep on the side of the road somewhere, and you… You…”

He broke off to swallow, and Buck reached up to wipe away one of the tears that had started trailing down the other man’s face.

“I was so afraid of what could have happened to you, but I was too afraid to go looking for you until Chris was practically begging me.” He sniffed and rubbed at his nose. “I slept better, having you here. I slept better knowing that you were here.”

“Because you knew I was safe.”

“Because I felt safer,” Edmundo said, and the confession nearly shattered Buck’s heart.

“You kept locking the doors, when I was over,” Buck said, thinking aloud. “You made a huge point of it, and of making sure all the windows were locked. I thought it was some sort of, I dunno. Some sort of joke, maybe. Like you were telling me you weren’t going to let me leave. But it wasn’t that, was it?”

“It wasn’t just that.”

“I’m sorry, Eddie. I’m so sorry. I should have… I should have realized, I should have known…”

“How?” Edmundo asked. “I didn’t tell you. I never talked to you, not about this, not about anything that was actually wrong with me after the shooting. Even when I thought I was having a heart attack, I still didn’t tell you anything, not until that cardiologist let it slip and you were hounding me about it. And even then…”

“Eddie…” Buck grabbed his hand. “How can I help? What can I do to help? Because I’ll do it. I’ll do whatever you need me to do so you feel safer, I swear.”

“Even if it’s checking under my bed for monsters?”

“Yes,” was Buck’s immediate answer. “Is that what you need me to do? Because I’ll do it right now.”

“I was joking.”

“I wasn’t. Whatever you need, I’ll do it.”

“I need you to stop drinking.”

Fuck.

“Okay. Okay, I can do that.” He hesitated a moment, before digging his keys out of his pants pocket and handing them to a confused looking Edmundo. “Backseat of my jeep.”

“Backseat of- Buck.” The exasperation in his voice was quite familiar. “Did you buy some while you were at the store? And then hide it in your jeep?”

“Maybe,” he mumbled, not really wanting to admit it.

“You have a problem.”

“We covered that already.”

“I should have noticed. I’m sorry that I didn’t notice.”

“I didn’t want you to notice. I didn’t want anyone to notice. That’s why I was drinking alone, Eds.”

Edmundo scowled at him for a long moment, before letting out a sigh.

“Well, I’m not going to let you be alone any more. I want you to stay here.”

“Christopher doesn’t want me here.”

“Yes he does.”

“No he doesn’t,” Buck said. “He hates me.”

“He called you when he was terrified and worried about me and needed help,” Edmundo said. “He has everyone else’s numbers saved to his phone. He could have called anyone else, but he called you. Not Nine One One, not Tia Pepa. You.”

“Well, yeah, I’m like, at the top of his In Case of Emergencies list.”

“Buck, he called you because he knew you would come. Just like you knew Bobby would come if you called,” he said. “He doesn’t hate you, he’s just upset with you.”

“I left him, Eddie. And then he heard my stupid, drunken ass say I wouldn’t have come back if I had the choice,” Buck said, and the way Edmundo flinched at his words sent a pang through his heart. “How the hell do I fix that?”

“The same way we’re going to try fixing everything else,” Edmundo said. “By talking. Like we both said we should have done at the beginning.”

“And if he doesn’t want to talk to me?”

“And what if he does?” he countered. “You’ve been running away from him. You messed up, yeah, but it’s not going to get better if you keep running away from it.”

“That sounds like the therapy talking,” Buck accused, narrowing his eyes at him.

“It might be. A little bit.” Edmundo stood up with a groan. “So, backseat you said.”

“Yeah, on the floorboard. Maybe… Maybe more like, under the backseat?”

“Dios, Buck.” The other man shook his head. “Who were you afraid was going to see it? Me or Bobby?”

“Either, both,” he answered, giving an awkward shrug. “Go dispose of my temptations while I pick up in here. And then I’ll peek under your bed for monsters.”

Edmundo snorted softly, the smallest puffs of air.

“Thanks.”

“You have my back, I have yours.”

There weren’t any monsters under the bed when Buck looked, nor were there any in the closet.

True to his word, Eddie forced Buck to take them both to the loft after he got rid of the six pack he found stashed under the backseat of the jeep, along with the single bottle he’d found tucked away in Buck’s duffle. In the loft itself, he didn’t find much of anything except empty glass bottles filling up his trash. There were a concerning number of them, actually. Honestly, Eddie wasn’t entirely certain that there’d been much to Buck’s diet in recent weeks that wasn’t alcohol, outside of meals at the station or the fast food they got after his therapy sessions.

But what was really, truly worrying was what Eddie found in the bathroom. Or, rather, didn’t find.

“You punched your mirror,” he said, coming out to glare at the other man. “That’s how you hurt your hand.”

Buck looked up from where he was tying closed a trash bag.

“Mirror? Fuck, I can’t believe I forgot about the mirror.”

“Pendejo.”

“I wasn’t really happy with myself, at the time.”

“So you took it out on your reflection? I’m sure that worked out well.”

“About as well as you destroying your room,” Buck pointed out with a shrug. It was a good point. “I need to apologize to Maddie.”

“For being a dick to her?” Eddie had heard all about that through the grapevine, mostly from May. He didn’t know the full details - everything was third or fourth hand, after all, and so things got lost - but he knew Maddie had walked in on a drunken Buck, who then blew up at her after she expressed concern for him.

“Yeah. She didn’t deserve that.”

“No, she didn’t,” he agreed. “Did they really not know you broke up with Taylor?”

“I guess not,” Buck admitted. “I… I haven’t really been talking to them. Not really.”

“Why?”

“Ghosts. It’s like I’m talking to ghosts, or… Or like they’re talking to a ghost. It was four years and change for me, Eddie. I’m not the person they knew. I can’t be the person they knew. You were right, when you said they’d notice.”

“Is it better or worse than when you first went back?”

“Different,” he said. “Not better, not worse, just different. They didn’t know me either, but they had never known me. I hid in the bathroom for a bit and cried that first day.”

“Seriously?” Eddie moved to perch on a stool at the island, Buck setting down the bag as he leaned against the counter.

“I didn’t know what was going on,” he said. “I’d woken up that morning thinking all of this had been some super realistic dream, only to be slapped with the fact that it wasn’t. I realized Maddie was still with Doug, and… And you and Chris weren’t even in the city yet. It was ironic, really. I panicked and ran away that day because I was terrified I was being left behind again. Only to end up losing everyone, anyway.”

“I’m sorry.”

“My choice, Eds,” Buck said. “Everything was my choice, up until the end.”

Up until his choice apparently no longer mattered. Up until that not-demon apparently decided to throw him back here, regardless of what the other man wanted. And Christ, Eddie still had trouble wrapping his head around it, sometimes. That the black eyed creature, which had sent him tumbling through different crossroads to meet first himself and then Buck, was capable of erasing everything.

It absolutely did not help his seemingly ever present fear.

“Tell me about him,” Edmundo said on a whim. “The other me.”

“Why?” Buck asked, looking honestly confused.

“Because you love him,” he said. Love, not loved, because he knew that just because the other Eddie was out of Buck’s reach didn’t mean Buck stopped loving him. “And not talking about him, about the others, is hurting you.”

He watched as the other man chewed on his lip, clearly debating with himself.

“Okay,” Buck said. “Uh… I’m pretty sure I had him convinced there was a robot uprising in the future.”

“You dick.”

“Your technophobia is funny. Um, yours and his, I mean.”

“You’re still a dick.”

Buck shrugged, utterly shameless about the fact.

“He also, like, moved me in without telling me.”

“Que?”

“It was after…” Buck gestured at his shoulder, and Eddie nodded in understanding. “I kept finding my things at ho- at the house, things I knew I had left at the apartment-”

“The apartment?” Eddie interrupted. “Not here?”

“This place wasn’t available when I started apartment hunting. And I… I didn’t want it, anyways.”

That was a confusing statement, because as far as Eddie was aware, Buck loved this place. Of course, up until just a few months ago, he had thought Buck felt something for Taylor, and that he was straight.

“The apartment actually isn’t that far from ho- From your place,” Buck continued. “I’ll show you where it is, but uh, anyways. I found clothes I knew had been left in my closet, shoes I knew I hadn’t worn over. He’d been going over whenever I took Chris to the zoo, stuffing a bunch of my things into a bag and then sneaking them to the house.”

“Huh.” Would that work twice? It was something to consider, for later. For now though, he couldn’t help but ask, “Why is he so different from me?”

“You’re the same in a lot of ways,” Buck said, and then under his breath, “Today definitely proved that.”

“You said he hated being compared to me, so he obviously thought we were different,” Eddie said. “And he was different. Even taking into account that he wasn’t the one that got shot, he was less… I dunno. He wasn’t me.”

“Ichabod told me that… That some things were defined by the, uh… The cracks in their being.”

“So what cracked him?” he asked, and then reconsidered at the look that crossed Buck’s face. “Wait, no, you… I remember at the crossroads, when I first… I heard you say that you had wanted to fix whatever broke everything.”

The other man gave a hesitant nod, not meeting his eyes.

“Buck,” he said. “What cracked me? What did you change that kept him from cracking?”

“Shannon.” He breathed out the name, like he was afraid to say it. “I saved Shannon.”

“Oh.”

“You loved her, and she was Chris’ mother,” Buck said, not looking at him. Like he was afraid that Eddie would be upset or something. “So I, uh. Got the day off and then went to that street corner and just waited until she came out of the store. Kept her on the sidewalk until…”

That made sense. That made so much sense. Shannon had told him she was pregnant, then said she wasn’t and asked for a divorce, and then died in front of him. All in the span of a week or so. He had been left with a tangle of resentment and love and guilt and bitterness and sorrow, and he’d never really gotten closure. Instead of dealing with it, he’d just done exactly what his dad had taught him. He had pushed it down, sucked it up, and moved on.

It made so much sense now, why that other version of himself was so different, if he didn’t have all of that hanging around his neck like an albatross.

“I love you,” Eddie blurted out.

“Eds…”

“I know you’re still mourning him. I know that we’re waiting. I know that neither of us are really in a state to start anything and make it last,” he said, trying to reassure the other man, because Buck was looking vulnerable and overwhelmed. “But I love you. It’s as true now as it was the day you came back, and the day you left. It'll still be true tomorrow and next month. I just…” He trailed off with a shrug.

“I love you, too,” Buck whispered.

Eddie nodded.

“Tell me about Shannon,” he said. Begged, really. Pleaded, even. “Tell me about how she… How she would have been if…”

“I don’t think she liked me very much, at first,” Buck said, a far-off look in his eyes. “I’m pretty sure she became one of Maddie’s best friends, though.”

“That sounds like a terrifying combination.”

“It was, but Shan really helped her,” he said. “I miss her.”

“So do I,” Eddie admitted. Even years later, that was still true. “C’mon, keep going. Please.”

“I used to hang out with her and Chris, whenever he was working and I wasn’t…”

Buck wasn’t really looking forward to talking to Chris, a fact which left a bitter taste in his mouth, because he used to always look forward to spending time with the kid. Knowing that the current awkward state of affairs was entirely his fault just made everything worse. But Edmundo wanted - perhaps needed - him close, so he had to man up and start fixing his mistakes, instead of running away from them.

He’d gone with Edmundo to pick him up, mostly because the other man refused to let him out of his sight. It was like he was afraid Buck would either run off and get drunk, or that something would happen to him the moment Edmundo took his eyes off him. Actually, judging by their conversation earlier in the day, that latter fear was probably exactly what was going on.

Chris was already chattering away about his day when he climbed into the truck. When he saw Buck in the front passenger seat, he paused for a moment, before continuing on like nothing had happened. He hadn’t told Buck to get lost, or glared at him. He just focused on his father, and ignored Buck completely.

It was progress, though. Maybe.

Afterwards, when they got home, Buck hid away in the kitchen like a coward while Edmundo spoke to Chris. He kept the door open, so he could shamelessly eavesdrop.

“Buck is going to stay here for a while, okay?”

“To help you?”

“Yeah, to help me.”

“Okay,” he heard Chris say. “Can I go to my room?”

“Sure.”

Buck heard the sound of crutches clacking down the hall, and a few moments later Edmundo slipped into the kitchen with him.

“He didn’t explode,” he said. “He doesn’t have a problem with you staying here.”

“That doesn’t mean anything, he’s your son.”

“What does that mean?”

“I mean you were perfectly willing to stay with Ana just because Chris liked her,” Buck said. “He’s probably just willing to put up with me being here because of you.”

Edmundo frowned at him, before practically shoving him out of the kitchen.

“Stop making excuses and go talk to him,” he demanded.

“Fine, fine,” Buck said, straightening his shirt as he made his way to Chris’ door. He could do this, certainly. He’d done a lot of things that were totally scarier and a lot more dangerous. He could talk to Chris.

He hesitated a moment at the bedroom door, glancing behind him at where Edmundo was staring him down, before lightly knocking.

“Hey uh, can I come in?” Silence. “Please?”

More silence. Enough silence that it was disheartening. Silence for long enough that he nearly turned around and gave up. And then,

“Fine.”

Biting his lip, and feeling so incredibly nervous, he gently turned the knob and opened the door. Chris was sitting at his desk, turned slightly so he could look at him.

“Hey, Chris. I think, uh. I think we need to talk.”

“Is this about dad?”

“No, this is… This is about me. About what I said the other night.”

“Oh.”  Chris was frowning now. Not quite the full Diaz Scowl, not yet.

“Yeah. So, can we? Talk about it?”

Chris didn’t answer except to nod. Buck looked around for a moment, trying to decide where to stand or lean or sit, before opting for a spot on the floor beside the open door. It was far enough away that he wouldn’t be crowding the boy.

“What did you hear that night?” he asked, stretching out his legs in front of him.

“You said you didn’t have a choice,” Chris answered. It sounded more like an accusation, actually. “You said you wouldn’t have come back if you had a choice.”

“Did… Did you hear anything before that?”

“Dad said something about another family,” he said. The frown had fully evolved into the scowl, now. “Is that why you didn’t want to come back? Because you found another family?”

Buck didn’t know how to salvage anything out of this mess. Did he lie, or did he tell the truth? How much of the truth did he tell? He glanced out the door and into the hallway, where Edmundo was hiding just out of sight.

“I didn’t want to leave you again,” he said, turning back to Chris.

“That doesn’t make sense,” Chris said. “You already left me.”

God, nothing had ever hurt him worse than the words of an upset Christopher.

“I- I know, I knew that, that’s why…” Buck glanced at Edmundo again. The truth, he decided. The whole truth, or as much of it as he ever told anyone. “I lied, when I said I didn’t remember where I was, where I went.”

“What do you mean?” Chris asked.

“I mean, I… After I ran away on Christmas, I… I got really, really lost and met someone named Ichabod…”

Stay 'til the Morning Come

Christopher sits and he listens and he believes, or at least he seems to. Eddie can’t really see his son’s face from where he was sitting just outside of the room, but he can hear both his and Buck’s voices. When Chris interrupts, with questions and comments, none of it sounds as accusatory as his words had at the beginning. None of it was laced with the skepticism that Eddie himself would have had, if he hadn’t gotten lost and tripped through a fairy tale himself.

Chris also doesn’t call Buck out on the moments where he hesitates and stumbles over his words. He doesn’t call the man out on the obvious gaps in the story, the parts that he hedges around and tries to pretend he wasn’t avoiding. And there are so many of those moments, even in this abridged retelling. Eddie can guess the subject matter for some of them, can guess that some of it was Buck trying not to hurt or frighten Chris.

Sometimes, though, Buck would say something and then seemingly realize he had said more than he meant to, because he’d hurry forward with the apparent hope that neither of them would catch it.

(It’s those moments that clue Eddie in on how, perhaps, things hadn’t been so perfect on the other side. It’s those moments that hold the answers as to why his friend - his best friend, his partner, his future something-more - came back just that bit more broken.)

“Ichabod doesn’t sound very nice,” Chris said, when Buck finally got to the end.

“I think he’s just lonely,” the man said, because of course he’d empathize with the evil not-demon thing. “And he didn’t really have much of a choice, either.”

“That’s sad,” his son said, because of course he’d sympathize with it, too. “How old are you?”

“Kid, you know I can’t do math.” Buck sounded like he was just on the edge of laughing, and it loosened something in Eddie’s chest. “Uh, I’m thirty-four, I think. Maybe closer to thirty-five. Actually, no, I’m pretty sure I am thirty-five, now.”

Eddie frowned at the answer, because he knew when Buck’s birthday was, and he knew when he disappeared, and he knew what day Buck said he went back to. Eddie wasn’t much better at math, not that he’d ever admit to it, but he knew that three or four extra months did not put him anywhere near to being thirty-five. And that could have been Buck failing at basic arithmetic again, but some of Buck’s phrasing had been… Suspect.

(“I made sure everyone went to therapy after I let my leg get hurt again. That version of your dad was so grumpy about it, too.”)

“So, does this mean ghosts are real?” Chris’ voice pulled Eddie out of his thoughts on just what exactly Buck would be so hesitant to talk about, when he was perfectly fine telling a child that demons were not only real, but were perfectly willing to fuck with people for their own entertainment.

“I don’t actually know, but I’ve decided to go with yes, just to be safe,” Buck answered, and Eddie almost scoffed. Almost. But what if they were? Ichabod was real, so what did that mean about everything else? Shit, Buck had been so insistent that a ghost had called 9-1-1, that one Halloween. They had all made fun of him for it, Bobby had even brought in pumpkins with ghosts carved into them.

But what if a ghost really did call 9-1-1? What if ghosts were real? A locked door didn’t mean anything to something that could just walk through a wall. Buck hadn’t been joking earlier, about checking under the bed for monsters. Did Buck know something he didn’t? Were bed monsters real?

Oh God, there might actually be monsters under his bed, and ghosts that he couldn’t see, and oh God, oh God.

Oh God, his home wasn’t safe.

Eddie’s chest was growing tight, and the edges of his vision were turning black, and- Oh. It had been a short while since his last panic attack. Closing his eyes, he tried to focus less on the unknowable dangers lurking all around him and more on breathing. In through his nose, out through his mouth.

In through his nose, out through his mouth

In through his nose…

Out through his mouth…

Eddie opened his eyes and saw Buck staring at him through Chris’ open door, concern etched into every line on his face. 

‘I’m fine,’ he mouthed, and the other man’s doubts were clear as day. He was fine, though, or he would be. Sure, yes, okay, he now had to deal with there probably being things in the world that his baseball bat was useless against, but Buck didn’t seem too concerned about that. He had said that he was assuming ghosts were real. Assuming. So… So maybe there was a chance that they weren’t.

Okay, panic under control.

(Eddie was definitely going to get a crucifix or six from Pepa later and hang them around the house. Just to be safe.)

“Buck?” Chris’ voice drew both of their attention away from Eddie.

“Yeah, bud?”

“You said… You told Ichabod that you wanted to fix things.” The boy hesitated with the words. “What sort of things?”

“Things that went wrong,” Buck said. “Like your dad getting hurt, and- And Maddie feeling like she had to leave.”

“Like my mom?” Chris asked, and Eddie sucked in a breath. Buck glanced at him again, clearly unsure of what to say.

“Yeah,” Buck finally answered. “Like your mom.”

“So, is… Is my mom alive?” Chris asked, and Eddie felt his heart crack and bleed at the question, at the way his son’s voice trembled with the words. “There? Is she alive there?”

“Chris…” Buck let out a sigh. “Yeah. Yeah, she’s alive there. She was my friend.”

He was answered by several seconds of silence, before,

“Can I be alone, please?”

“Yeah, sure bud,” Buck said, standing up. He glanced down at the still sitting Eddie. “Do you want me to send your dad in?”

“No, thank you,” Chris answered, and the other man nodded before stepping out, closing the bedroom door as he did. Buck stood there, staring at it with an expression on his face that was a mixture of sorrow and frustration, along with something that Eddie couldn’t quite name.

“He didn’t cry, when I told him you got shot,” Buck said, voice low. “I sobbed my eyes out and he ended up comforting me.”

“He’s one of the strongest people I know,” Eddie said, and the other man turned that gaze on him with that unnamable something still there, lurking in the blue of his eyes. Once upon a time, Eddie could have taught college courses on how to read Buck. Once upon a time, Eddie would have been able to see every expression and known exactly what Buck was thinking.

Now, however, Eddie had no idea what was going through the other man’s head.

“Yeah,” Buck said, looking back at the door, his hand and fingers twitching like he wanted to open it. “Just like his dad.”

And Eddie couldn’t quite figure out if he meant that as a compliment or not.

Buck spent the rest of the afternoon and into the evening feeling raw and torn, second guessing his decision to tell Chris anything, much less as much as he had. He was also doubting his decision to concede to the boy’s request to be alone, everything in him screaming to open that door back up and be there for him. But this was the Chris he went to the zoo with and pretended everything was fine. It wasn’t the one he went to the zoo with and talked through all of the things that weren’t okay.

He was tempted to order pizza for dinner again, but couldn’t quite justify doing it two days in a row. Instead, Buck and Edmundo cooked dinner together, which was new. Pleasant, but new. He’d cooked together with Eddie on occasion, after he bullied the man into learning and his arm was healed, but those times had been rare.

He’d never cooked with Edmundo before.

Chris crept out of his room to eat, and he didn’t seem upset. He didn’t mention Shannon, but he did ask Buck a couple more questions about Ichabod and Over There. The entire time they ate and talked, Buck wanted to grab him by the shoulders and tell him the same things he told the other Chris in his letter. He wanted to say,

“You don’t have to be brave or strong all the time. It’s okay to cry, it’s okay to be afraid, it’s okay to not be okay.”

But he wasn’t sure those words would land the way he’d want them to. He was certain that he had lost the right to offer that kind of sage advice the night he walked in here drunk, if not the day he turned and ran.

“Do you work tomorrow?” Edmundo asked as he went about setting up the couch to sleep in for the night, the motions familiar even though he hadn’t slept on this couch for weeks, now.

“Wednesday,” Buck answered.

“Good, good.” The other man seemed about ready to say something else, but just nodded at him and went to bed.

Saturday night he didn’t sleep at all, and Sunday night he had been too exhausted to dream. This night, though, seemed set to make up for it. His mind broke out some classics to torment him with: all the myriad ways his leg had been injured instead; an unbreathing Christopher beneath a white sheet; a crying Edmundo asking why he left.

What he did to Doug.

Buck woke up to a dark living room, itching with the incessant need to scrub his hands clean. Sitting up, he started to reach for his phone to check the time when movement at the end of the couch had him freezing. There was a shape there, a person sitting on the floor with their back pressed against the couch.

“Eds?”

“Sorry,” the other man said, and Buck relaxed at the sound of his voice. “Did I wake you?”

“No, it was- No.”

“You were having a nightmare.” A statement, not a question.

“Yeah,” Buck admitted. “It’s nothing new.”

“That’s why you’ve been drinking so much.” Another statement, another non-question. “To help you sleep.”

“Never claimed to have the best coping methods,” he said. “Is that why you’re up? Nightmares?”

“I couldn’t get to sleep,” Edmundo said. “Couldn’t stop… Thinking, I guess. And there was a noise.”

“Need me to check for monsters?” Buck tried to joke, but the words fell flat between them.

“Maybe.”

With a sigh, Buck swung his legs off the couch and slid to the floor so he could sit next to the other man. Jostling their shoulders together, he waited for Edmundo to look at him. There was enough light from the street lamps outside for him to make out his face, his eyes glittering a bit.

“What’s got you all tangled up tonight?” he asked.

Edmundo didn’t answer straight away, instead staring at him silently. Buck was fine with that, though. Silence with Edmundo wasn’t anywhere near as awful as silence by himself.

“They died anyway,” Edmundo finally said. “Chief, Norwhal, Binder, Mills. I saved them, but they all died anyway. And you… You went through and fixed everything the best you could, except it didn’t matter, because you’re right back where you were.”

“Because I wasn’t actually fixing anything.”

“That’s my point. That’s… Is there even a point?” he asked. “Everything we do, all the things we put ourselves through. Does it even matter?”

“Of course it matters, Eds. It has to matter.”

“Does it?”

“Every step, every word, every life. It all matters,” Buck said. “Even if it doesn’t seem like it at the time.”

“Well, it definitely doesn’t seem like it now,” Edmundo scoffed.

“So, what? The next person we find in danger, we should just leave them? Because they might die anyway a couple years later?”

“W- Well- No, of course not.”

“I didn’t help Maddie,” Buck said, and the apparent non sequitur seemed to confuse the other man. “The other Maddie, I mean.”

“So, she left too?”

“No, she stayed,” he said. “Because of Shannon. A butterfly flaps its wings and creates a storm half-way across the world. I saved Shannon, and then she saved Maddie.”

“The Butterfly Effect, Buck? Really?”

“It literally haunts my life,” Buck said, thinking about the bothersome blue butterfly he hadn’t laid eyes on since the night of Edmundo’s breakdown. He stood and stretched a bit. “Where was the noise?”

“What?”

“The noise you heard. I’ll go check it out so you can get back to bed. I know you’re using some of your PTO for this week, but you still need to sleep.”

“It- It wasn’t anything. I know it was nothing,” Edmundo said. “I already looked.”
