“But you didn’t go back to bed.”

“You were in here.”

It was said so casually, with a bit of a careless shrug. But Buck heard it and remembered the words from just that morning, the confession that Edmundo had slept better when Buck was over because he felt safer. So, with that in mind, he scooped his phone off the coffee table and held out his hand for Edmundo to take, smiling widely at him when he did so.

“C’mon,” he said, dragging his friend/partner/something down the hall, pausing briefly to check on Chris. “Back to bed.”

“I don’t remember you being such a mother hen,” the other man grumbled, allowing himself to be bullied under the covers. “I’m not sure I- What are you doing?”

Buck paused in the process of slipping in beside him to stare at him.

“Going to bed.” He left the ‘duh’ unspoken.

“But, here? I thought… Are you sure?”

“Eddie.” Buck put his phone on the bedside table and lay down, settling on his side so he could face him. “We both need to sleep, and… And I think we’ll both sleep better like this.”

Edmundo blinked at him, stiff and uncertain.

“What are your nightmares about?” he asked.

“Maybe I’ll tell you one day,” Buck said. “Don’t worry about it.”

He rolled over, and got himself a bit more comfortable under the comforter.

“G’night, Eds,” he mumbled, mostly into his pillow.

He heard the other man sigh and shift, and say,

“Good night, Evan.”

It was the best night’s sleep either of them had gotten since Christmas.

Obsessing Over What Could Be, Instead

Buck entered the station on Wednesday morning with far more pep in his step than he had since returning. Two nights in a row of some of the best sleep he’d gotten in weeks had made an immediately noticeable difference, as did the fact that he wasn’t suffering from a hangover for once. To make matters even better, Chris was warming up to him again. Not to how it had been before his screw up, but he was no longer being looked at like the scum he was.

That morning, as well as the Tuesday morning before, he had woken up with Edmundo wrapped around him. It would have been a nice way to wake up - it was a nice way to wake up - except each time he woke up, he saw Eddie’s sleeping face and nearly kissed him awake. And then he’d remember that it was Edmundo, not Eddie, and he’d feel so god damn awful. 

Awful for conflating them, again.

Awful for replacing one with the other, even if unthinkingly, even if only momentarily.

Awful for being disappointed, just for the briefest of moments.

And that guilt would follow him for the rest of the morning, but it was still the best he’d felt in far too long.

The others clocked onto Buck’s improved mood almost the moment he stepped into the loft area. He had perfected the art of faking being fine ages ago, but hadn’t been bothering with it since his fight with Bobby. He just hadn’t the energy or the desire to make the effort. 

The fact that he wasn’t squintily glaring at everything on his way to the coffee probably also gave something away.

After pouring himself a cup, he turned to find that everyone was staring at him. Well, okay, not everyone. Over at the den area, Lucy was frowning at something on her phone and Ravi was just ignoring all of them in favor of the television, because he was very much the wisest and most well-adjusted person on their shift. But Bobby had paused in the process of fixing breakfast to look at him curiously, and both Hen and Chimney had stopped their chattering.

“What?” Buck demanded, feeling his heckles raise at the blatant gawking.

“Nothing,” the captain assured him, almost purposefully going back to his task. There was a muffled buzz from his pocket at the same time that Hen’s phone chimed, and Buck turned his head to glower at Chimney, who was not-so-stealthily trying to stuff his own phone away.

“Right,” Buck drawled, turning back. “Listen, Bobby, thanks for… Y’know.”

“You’re welcome,” the other man said. “Anytime, Buck. I’m just a call away. You know that.”

“Yeah, I do,” he admitted, before taking his coffee and escaping from the awkwardness. He sat down on the couch with Ravi, on the other end from where Lucy was sitting in one of the chairs. The younger man glanced at him and smiled, but didn’t say anything, which was perhaps why he was quickly becoming Buck’s favorite.

Pulling out his own phone, he sent off a text to Edmundo to check on him. He’d gone back to work for the day, which was probably a good thing, because otherwise he’d have been home alone. And neither of them needed to be home alone at the moment. The other man replied back almost instantly, complaining about Twitter.

“How’s Eddie?” Ravi asked, drawing his attention away from smiling at his phone.

“He’s fine,” he answered, and then, “How did you know I was texting him?”

“I made a guess,” the younger man said, sounding like he was laughing at something. Buck was about to ask what, precisely, the joke was when a familiar voice came from the television.

“Ugh,” Buck and Lucy chorused as Taylor Kelly started reporting. She was talking - still, or again - about the catch Lucy had made while Buck was busy helping put Edmundo back together. It was an impressive save, don’t get him wrong, but it wasn’t any more impressive than any of the other seemingly impossible saves people in the station had managed. But the way Hen and Chimney had been talking about it - before they interrupted themselves to stare at him - one would think that Lucy had swung across buildings on webbing to grab the woman an inch away from death.

Would they have been just as impressed by it if Ravi had done it? Buck wondered.

“She’s one of the ones who just will not stop calling me,” Lucy said, scowling at Taylor Kelly on the television. “I’m about ready to change my number.”

“She’ll stop as soon as she gets something else to chew on,” Buck told her. “Or someone makes her stop.”

“She’s your girlfriend, isn’t she? Tell her to stop.”

He wondered idly who had told her about that, considering he and Taylor had long since stopped being a thing before he even knew Lucy existed. Actually, if everyone had only just found out that he and Taylor were no longer a thing, why had no one confronted him about that drunken kiss with Lucy? Yes, he had been very inebriated and she had kissed him, but one would think that someone would have taken him aside and asked him what, pray tell, the fuck he was doing.

“Ex-girlfriend,” Buck said. At the expression on her face, he said, “You had literally nothing to do with it.”

“When did that happen?” Ravi asked curiously.

“A couple months ago. I thought everyone knew by now. You didn’t hear?”

“I’m not part of the group chat.”

“What? Yeah you-” Buck paused at the momentary flash of guilty panic on Ravi’s face. He looked over to where Hen and Chimney were huddled together at the kitchen island, and then back to the younger firefighter. “There’s a group chat to gossip about me?”

“I think it was named ‘Buckaroo Intervention’ or something,” Ravi admitted, looking sheepish. “I saw it looking over Hen’s shoulder the other day.”

“I can’t- No, wait, yes I can. I can believe this.” Buck was immediately texting this outrageous revelation to Edmundo.

they have gc abt me

bobby hen chim

mayb thena + mads

I know.

WHAT

et 2 ed

I’m not in it.

May is.

“May!?”

“It’s gonna be?” Ravi joked, and Buck blinked at him for a moment before shaking his head.

“I knew they were talking about me behind my back,” he grumbled, scowling over at the others. “It’s not paranoia if it’s true.”

“Pretty sure it’s still paranoia,” Lucy commented. Her phone started ringing in her hand and she glared as she declined whoever it was.

“No, no, it’s only paranoia if it isn’t true.” Buck was tempted to call up Edmundo and whine about the injustice of it all, or ask him to interrogate May for him, but then Bobby was calling everyone for the morning brief.

The rest of the day, Buck made a point of scowling whenever Hen, Chimney, and Bobby’s phones chimed in sync.

The start to Eddie’s first day back to work after his totally minor breakdown was awkward, mostly for and because of him. He just couldn’t escape the feeling that they all knew, even though there was absolutely no way any of them could have known. May intercepted him just as he got off the elevator with the word of the day - imprimatur, an official approval - and his first thought was that Bobby had told her about what happened.

It was a thought that was immediately mollified when he remembered that Bobby didn’t know anything more than what Buck told him, and Buck had apparently been exceedingly vague.

May’s phone went off, distracting him from his thoughts, and the dispatcher pulled it out to check it. Eddie caught sight of the screen and was only half-surprised to find that it was a group chat that he wasn’t a part of. After all, Buck and he had commiserated and complained about the distance which had grown between him and the others ever since he transferred. 

The half-surprise turned into confusion when he saw the name of the group chat was ‘Buckaroo Intervention.’

Things made somewhat more sense when, a few minutes later, Buck was texting him with utter indignation. Eddie wasn’t certain which part he himself was more upset about: that they were talking about his friend behind his back, or that they hadn’t even thought to include him in it. 

Obviously, not including him was the smart move, because he would have told them all to shove it and then immediately tell Buck what was going on. Still, though.

The rest of the day, he fielded Buck’s attempts to convince him to interrogate May on his behalf, which he was not going to do. Not only did they all have better things to do, but May was far too much like her mother for any sort of interrogation to work. No, the weak link would obviously be Chimney, because that man had not gotten better at keeping secrets during his time away.

(Buck had, though. Or maybe he had always been good at keeping secrets, and it was only obvious now that Eddie was aware that there were secrets to be kept.)

“C’mon, Eddie, please? For me?” Buck whined, having called him shortly before Eddie was set to leave for the day. Eddie couldn’t lie and say that it wasn’t nice to hear his voice, even if he was still trying to get him to bother May.

“No. Go ask Chimney, if you’re that desperate.”

“But then they’ll know that I know.” He heard Buck sigh. “Fine. Hey, you’re good for tonight, right? You don’t need me to step out and, uh. Cook dinner? Or anything? Because I will.”

“I can cook,” he said, knowing damn well that wasn’t actually what the other man was offering. “Chris and I will be fine for the night.”

“Okay, if you’re sure.” On the other end of the line, the station’s alarm went off. “Gotta go. Call me if you need anything, okay? Anything!”

“Will do,” Eddie promised, and the words sounded like ‘yes, dear’ to his own ears. May stepped in just as he was hanging up, knocking on the door with a slip of paper in her hand. “Another road closure?”

“Yeah, there’s a pretty bad wreck,” she said. Gesturing at his phone, she asked, “Was that Buck?”

“It was,” he confirmed, and after a moment’s thought, said, “He knows.”

Perhaps he’d watched too many of those telenovelas and they’d made him a sucker for drama. Or maybe he’d always been a sucker for drama, and that was why he enjoyed them so much. Either way, he smiled at her puzzled frown as he took the paper and typed up the road closure announcement.

“Knows what?” May asked, legitimately confused, because the statement had been both vague and out of left field.

“Buckaroo Intervention,” he said, hitting send. Her eyes went wide. “Is there a chat to talk about me, or is Bobby the only one interested in that gossip?”

“I- That- No,” she finally managed to get out. “There’s not- There’s only the one. For Buck.”

“And you didn’t invite me? I thought we were friends, May.”

“You would have told him,” she said, and Eddie could only shrug and nod. “How did he find out?”

“Ravi told him.”

“Ravi knows!?”

“May, no one at the One Eighteen is subtle. Of course he knows.”

“Buck didn’t know about it,” she huffed.

“Buck didn’t know that you had a dedicated group chat about it,” Eddie corrected her. “He was very aware that everyone’s been talking about him behind his back, though.”

“Everyone’s just… Worried. About him.”

“That’s understandable,” he said, glancing at the clock and gathering his things. It was finally time to go. “It doesn’t really seem like a good way to go about expressing it. To me, anyway.”

“Do you have another way?” she asked, following him out of his office and to the elevators. “Because we’re all willing to listen, I think.”

“A good first step would probably be to stop asking other people about him, and actually check in on him yourselves.”

“The last person who did that got run out of his apartment, Eddie,” May said.

“Then don’t let yourself be run out,” he replied with a shrug. “And maybe try to visit more than once a fortnight before going for that intervention.”

Not that an intervention was particularly necessary anymore, he thought as the elevator doors closed between them. Not at the moment. Nor would it be needed in the near future, if he could help it.

Christopher was already home, thanks to Pepa, and they both greeted him happily when he walked through the door. His aunt hung around long enough to check in on him before kissing him on the cheek and heading home. He waited for her to make it to her car and drive off, before he closed the door and locked it firmly.

He wasn’t sure why, but he also sent Buck a text that he made it home, and got a series of emojis in return that he assumed were positive in meaning.

“No Buck?” Chris asked from where he was doing homework at the dining table. The question was a pleasant surprise, because it was the first time he’d asked after the man since that night.

“No Buck,” Eddie confirmed. “He’s working tonight.

Christopher nodded, and Eddie went down the hall to change out of his uniform. His pointless, useless uniform, because it wasn’t like he wore it anywhere else anymore. More and more, he was understanding exactly how much Buck must have been chafing as a Fire Marshall, and he hadn’t even asked for it like Eddie had.

If only he wasn’t so terrified by the idea of going back to the job that had once been so fulfilling.

Changed into a tee and sweats, he made his way to the kitchen, pausing to check on Chris as he went. He was about to get started on dinner - he was inordinately proud of finally being able to properly take care of his son in this way, on his own - when the sound of soft, shuffling footsteps had him turning.

“What’s up?”

“Do you think…” Chris trailed off, looking unsure of himself.

“Do I think, what?” Eddie asked, taking a seat at the kitchen table, his kid following his example.

“Do you think Buck would go back? If he could?”

“No, I don’t think he would,” he said. “You remember what he said, right? There’s already another Buck there.”

“Yeah, but Buck, our Buck, he seems so… Sad. What if… What if he was happier? Over there?”

“He still wouldn’t go. Because it would mean choosing to leave us again.”

He remembered that night with Buck’s ghost so very well. He remembered his tears, he remembered the way he had begged, and the way his hand had gone straight through Buck as he tried to physically drag him home. He remembered the way that black-eyed thing had reappeared after Buck had suddenly disappeared, to send him back on his way with a snarky comment about getting what he wanted.

He remembered, most of all, Buck’s words.

(“Because when I left before, I didn’t mean to abandon everyone. But if I leave now, if I choose to abandon them now…”)

“Would you?”

“Would I-? No!”

“Even though mom’s there?”

The question broke something in Eddie, and he was quickly up and on the other side of the table, wrapping Chris in his arms so he could bring him in tight.

“Never, okay?” he said, mostly into his son’s curls. “You’re here. I don’t care who’s over there, because you’re here. And I’m not going to leave you, okay?”

He felt more than saw Chris nod, and squeezed him tightly.

“Do you want to help me figure out what to make for dinner?”

Another nod.

“Okay. I dragged Buck grocery shopping with me yesterday, let’s see what we have in the fridge…”

Buck paced around the loft of the station, repeatedly checking his phone for updates. Chimney had rushed off shortly after the lady impaled by a flying stop sign, running out of the station with a thrown comment about Jee-Yun being sick at the ER. And the worst part was that Buck hadn’t even known, hadn’t even been told that anything was wrong.

His niece was in the ER, and he only knew about it because Chimney had remembered to tell everyone why he was leaving.

The worst part was that it was his own fault. Not Jee-Yun being sick, he didn’t have that big of an ego, but the fact that Maddie hadn’t told him. Ever since he had stabbed his sister straight in the insecurities during a fit of drunken pique, things between them had been as silent and chilly as a winter’s night. Just like with Chris before her, he had broken things so much that Maddie hadn’t even felt comfortable turning to him when she was stressed out in the ER.

“Any word from Chimney or your sister?” Lucy’s question had him stopping mid-pace. She was sitting on one of the couches, a tablet clutched in her hands. A video was playing, and he recognized it as the one that had been playing on the news, the one of her catching that woman.

“Still waiting on updates,” he said, uncertain if he would even get an update if there was one to be given. “Why do you keep watching that?”

“She should have died,” Lucy answered with a scoff. “Nine times out of ten, I don’t catch her. Or I don’t see her in time, I don’t move fast enough. Or I grab her and she slips through, and she takes me with her.”

“You’re obsessing over things that didn’t happen?” Hadn’t she been doing this just as long as he had, if not longer? That was the sort of thing he would have done during his probationary year. Actually, it was exactly something he had done that year.

“I’m trying to figure out how I could have saved her in a way that doesn’t involve luck,” she said. “So I’m ready for next time.”

“That is some of the stupidest shit I have ever heard,” Buck said. “And I’m counting things I’ve heard myself say.”

“Excuse me?” Lucy looked as if she couldn’t decide between being insulted or flabbergasted, her expression settling on some combination of the two.

“I could tell you about the first guy I lost and how I obsessed over all the ways I could have possibly prevented it, or a dozen other stories, but they all boil down to the same thing.”

“And that is?”

“Don’t worry about the things you can’t do anything about,” Buck told her. “Devon died because he didn’t take my hand. That woman’s alive because you managed to catch her. And yeah, a lot of that is because of luck or other things out of our control, but that’s the same for a lot of calls. Sometimes it’s luck that the floor manages to hold out just long enough, sometimes something has us rerouting on the way to the scene and those extra seconds cost us everything.”

“So, what? Accept it was a lucky break and move on?”

“Basically,” he said with a shrug. “No one died, no one really got hurt. Obsessing over all the ways someone could have died or gotten hurt isn’t going to do you any good.”

In his hand, his phone started to ring, and he smiled at the name that came up on the screen.

“Your sister?” Lucy asked.

“Nope,” he said, moving away for privacy as he answered. “Hey, Eds.”

“Hey.” He sounded tired but awake.

“Need me to come over?” Because Buck had absolutely no qualms about running out of the station if he was needed, and then beg for forgiveness from Bobby later.

“No, I’m managing,” Edmundo said, and then, a bit quieter, “There was a noise.”

“Maybe we need to start setting traps for raccoons,” Buck offered. “Do you think you would sleep better or worse if we got you something to block out noise?”

“I don’t know.”

“Then I’ll find you something, ear plugs or a white-”  The alarm interrupted him, and he let out several colorful curses.

“Go,” Edmundo ordered. “I’ll be fine.”

“I’ll text you when I get back,” Buck told him as he hurried down the loft stairs. “Call me if you’re awake.”

“I will,” the other man promised, and then hung up on him.

Buck got into his gear, and then spent the entire ride to the scene looking up ways to help someone sleep when they didn’t feel safe in their own home.

Solutions (of the Non-Alcoholic Variety)

“Are you still having trouble sleeping?” Frank asked.

Eddie was in the man's office, once again there to open up and talk about his feelings and be vulnerable and do all those other things his father had taught him to despise. As always, ever since the promise at the playground, Buck was sitting patiently in the waiting room with Chris. The two had even been talking when he was called in, with Chris once again offering more than monosyllabic answers or cold shoulders.

He hadn’t yet told Buck, however, about Chris’ questions from the night before. That way led to self-flagellation, and they’d had enough of that for the moment. In fact, Frank had just gotten done kindly scolding him for doing just that while talking about the events that led to his Silver Medal.

“A bit,” Eddie answered honestly.

“I ask because you’re looking better rested than you did the last time I saw you,” Frank said. “What’s changed?”

“Buck’s been staying over this week, except for when he’s had to work.”

“And him being there has helped?”

“Yes,” he admitted, and then with much reluctance, “I feel… Safer. When he’s there.”

Confessing this to Buck was one thing, an easy thing. It was Buck, after all. Telling Frank, though, was another beast altogether. It was awful and humiliating and, somehow, a relief. 

“And you don’t feel safe when you’re home alone?”

“No. No I don’t.”

“Why not? What’s making you feel unsafe?”

“The noises at night, mostly.”

“Are they new noises? Different noises?”

“No, they’re the same noises that have always been there,” Eddie said with an aggravated sigh. “My neighborhood is quiet, but that’s only relative to the rest of the city. It’s still LA. We have stray cats, we have raccoons, we have possums. I think there’s a damn owl somewhere on the street, too. And that’s not counting the people throwing things out, or that are coming and going because their hours are just as weird as mine used to be.”

“So nothing about the ambient sounds have changed,” Frank said, and Eddie nodded. “Except now they’re a source of anxiety for you.”

“Back in the fall, back in November,” Eddie started, swallowing. “When I was still at the station, we responded to a fire at a prison, set during a riot. Buck and I were taken hostage by a pair of prisoners who pretended to be guards.”

“That sounds stressful.”

“It was. And at one point, they took our phones and our IDs. They… They read off my address to me.”

“They knew where you lived.”

“They knew where I lived,” Eddie confirmed. “And now every. Single. Noise, I find myself thinking… I know it’s not them, one of them killed themselves that night and the other is locked away again. But…”

“But.” Frank sighed. “What are some ways you’ve tried to manage your anxiety with this?”

“Made sure the doors are locked, along with the windows. And there’s a baseball bat that I keep close by.”

“Anything else?”

“What else is there to do?” Eddie asked, getting frustrated. “There’s no one ever there, there’ll never be anyone there. It’s just me being stupid. Buck mentioned last night about getting me ear plugs or something else to block out noises…”

The rest of the session consisted of Frank explaining to him that, no, his anxieties and fears were not just him being stupid. And after that, they talked about ways to handle that anxiety so he could sleep better. It all added up to Eddie feeling exhausted and worn when the fifty minutes were up, just as he always did after talking to Frank. He may have chosen to put himself through this, willingly and (mostly) unprompted, but that didn’t mean it was pleasant for him. It didn’t mean that it was easy or enjoyable.

Stepping out to find Buck and Chris at one of the tables, heads bent over paper as they focused on whatever they were working on with colored pencils, helped relieve a lot of what he was feeling.

“Hey,” he said, getting their attention as he stepped closer. They were both coloring in hand drawn hearts, one anatomically correct - complete with labels - and the other much more cartoony looking. “What are we working on?”

“Homework for biology,” Chris said, packing away everything. “I had to draw and label the human heart.”

“I might have misunderstood the assignment,” Buck confessed in a faux-whisper, helping slide the drawings into Chris’ bag so they didn’t get crumpled. “Ready to go?”

“Yup,” Eddie said, waiting for his son to get situated with bag and crutches before they started off. “Did you really misunderstand, or did you just want to draw a giant smiley heart?”

“I plead the Fifth.”

They got greasy food on the way home, their post-therapy tradition. Unlike after Eddie’s last few sessions with Frank, Buck didn’t make any noise about dropping him and Chris off and then heading to his loft. He hopped out with them after turning off the engine, although he did swing around the back to get something out.

“I, uh, made a stop after work,” Buck nervously explained. “And got you a couple of things.”

He took out a large body pillow and handed it to Eddie, followed by a comforter that was still in its packaging and much heavier than it appeared.

“Is this all you got?” Eddie asked as he adjusted the items in his arms. He trusted Buck, he did, but he needed to ask. “You didn’t get anything else?”

“Is this-? Oh, no. No, I… I didn’t get anything else,” Buck promised him, and he nodded.

“So what is all this then?” Eddie asked, taking the other man at his word.

“Well, that’s a body pillow, obviously,” he explained as he made his way to the front door, where Chris was (im)patiently waiting. “Because you turn into a koala at night. Or an octopus.”

“And it bothers you?” Eddie guessed, feeling something unpleasant churn in his stomach at the idea. “You got this so I’ll be a koala with it, instead of you?”

“I got it so you can be a koala with it when I’m at work,” Buck corrected, not looking at him as he let them all in. Chris seemed both unbothered and unsurprised that Buck and Eddie were sleeping in the same bed, and Eddie briefly wondered just what, exactly, his son thought their current relationship status was.

“Oh, that’s…” Better. It was better. “And this is a weighted blanket?”

A weighted blanket was actually one of the things Frank had suggested. Trust Buck to have already looked things up to help.

“Yeah. They’re supposed to help with things like anxiety,” Buck explained as Chris unloaded his bag at the dining table, so he could finish his homework. “It’s supposed to be like a hug, or something. Release the happy chemicals, like serotonin, and reduce the stress ones.”

“Definitely need less of the stress.”

“I almost got a white noise machine,” the other man continued. “But you said you didn’t know if having all the noises blocked out would help or not. I figured, my next overnight, we can see if the pillow and blanket work. And if they don’t, we’ll try ear plugs or the white noise or something.”

“You didn’t have to,” Eddie tried, only for Buck to immediately start shaking his head.

“I want to.”

“Well. Thank you,” he said, and then scurried off to his bedroom with his new pillow and blanket before he did something embarrassing, like cry in front of his son.

Maddie was hesitant to knock on her brother’s door, and that was not a welcome realization. The last time she was here, Buck had been drunk and then he’d gotten mean, and she’d run out in tears. She had avoided him in the days after, she could admit that. She hadn’t answered his calls, and her replies to his texts had been terse and succinct.

Ironically, her behavior had probably proven him right.

Now, weeks later, she was standing in front of his door. After the stressful twenty-four hours with Jee-Yun in the ER for intussusception - for part of her intestine obstructing itself - Buck had texted her, asking to meet with her at his loft after he got off shift. He'd been pleading for her to show, almost.

Maddie had agreed, and now she was standing in front of his door. Not knocking.

It had to have meant something that Buck had reached out to her, for the first time since… Since after she nearly drowned herself in the ocean and fled for Boston. Since she started ignoring his calls and texts, calling him only the once to accuse him of breaking his word.

She still remembered that moment, when she opened up that email from Howie and read the words, “Something’s happened to Buck.” She had called him up immediately and demanded to know more, demanded to know what happened and when and how. She’d checked herself out of the clinic, and then she and Howie had rushed back to LA, barely taking the time for a reunion. 

Finding out that her brother had simply left on Christmas and disappeared without a trace was horrifying. Finding out that Hen had told Howie about it right after New Years, after a week had gone by without any sign, but that he hadn’t told her until so much later had been infuriating.

Perhaps that had been what had truthfully killed their relationship, Maddie thought. The depression caused by her postpartum thyroiditis had been a cancer, and the months after she had run were a hemorrhaging wound, but Howie keeping the news of Buck’s disappearance from her had been a stake to its heart. Even if it had taken them so very long to acknowledge its death.

Buck had reappeared a full month after he had disappeared, a week after she arrived back, and things were supposed to have been okay after that. Except, they weren’t. The brother that returned to them was as much of a stranger to her now as when she first came to Los Angeles. More, maybe. Something had happened in that month he claimed not to remember, something that had changed him.

It was his eyes, mostly. His eyes were older, somehow.

And his story did not add up. Buck had told Athena that he remembered nothing between leaving on Christmas and ending up at Eddie’s. But then Maddie had heard him tell Chris that Eddie had found him. So either he had lied to Athena, or he had lied to Chris, or he had lied to both.

His behavior when he got back had been questionable, too. Buck had been happy enough to see her, when she rushed to the hospital with Howie after Athena informed everyone of his return. But he’d reacted more like her presence was a pleasant surprise, and less like he hadn’t seen her in months. And she had been disappointed, yes, and a little bit hurt, but she’d mostly been overwhelmingly relieved that he was okay.

Except that he wasn’t okay, was he? He had been subdued when interacting with everyone, with the exception of Christopher. Maybe Jee-Yun would have been an exception as well, but she’d reacted to him much as Maddie had been afraid she’d react to her. But in the days and weeks that followed, Buck had been subdued, and withdrawn, and reluctant to return to a job that he had once fought so very hard for. Somewhere in all that, he’d also broken up with his girlfriend that everyone had told her he’d claimed to love.

Maddie had read up on fugue states, after that became the official diagnosis. She knew that depression afterwards was a possibility. She didn’t know, though, if a post-fugue depression was supposed to last this long. She didn’t know, though, if that was what was actually going on.

More and more, it seemed like he was grieving something.

And then Buck had started drinking and hadn’t stopped drinking, except for long enough to go to work sober. Hungover at the start of every shift, but sober. A new pattern of behavior that had them all scrambling to figure out what was wrong, especially after Athena had visited him shortly after he injured his hand to find him drinking alone. Worse, he had tried to hide it.

The group chat had perhaps been - as Athena had said, when she absolutely refused to be a part of it - a stupid idea. But they’d just been trying to pool their resources while they figured out how to approach him, especially when he didn’t seem to actually be talking to anyone about what was going on. They’d been desperate enough to rope May into it, in case Eddie knew and mentioned something.

Eddie, who found out about the group chat and told May to stop talking about him and start talking to him, instead. Except, Maddie had tried, hadn’t she? She had come over, she had tried talking to him, tried telling him that she was concerned. And in response, Buck had gotten angry and mean. And in response to that, she had distanced herself from him.

Now here Maddie was, staring at Buck’s door after her brother had reached out to her for the first time in months, and she still hadn’t knocked.

When she finally did, after several more moments of hemming and hawing, he answered almost immediately. In contrast to the last time she laid eyes on him, he seemed genuinely happy to see her. Happy and once again surprised, as if he hadn’t thought she would actually come.

He was also sober. Not drunk, not even tipsy, but utterly sober.

“Hey!” Buck greeted her, stepping aside so she could come in. “No, uh. No Jee today?”

“I left her with Howie,” Maddie answered, and turned to look at him just in time to see a flash of pain and guilt go over his face. “It’s his time with her.”

“Oh, okay.” He seemed relieved to hear it and she realized that, however briefly, he had thought she hadn’t brought Jee-Yun to keep her away from him. “Uh, want a glass of water?”

“Not going to offer bourbon and beer, this time?” she sniped, and then immediately felt awful for it. Especially with the way Buck winced and hung his head.

“No. Uh, none of that here today,” he said, grabbing her a glass and filling it from the tap. Maddie hadn’t actually said she wanted any, but it was probably more something for him to do than anything. He set it down in front of her as she slipped into one of the stools at the island, and then he started anxiously fiddling with the sleeves of his shirt.

“What am I doing here, Buck?” Maddie asked. “Because the last time I was here, you were very adamant that you didn’t want me to be.”

(“I want you to leave. It’s what you’re good at, isn’t it? Leaving.”)

“I shouldn’t have said that,” he said, hunching his shoulders. “It was cruel, it was awful, it- I shouldn’t have said that. I should never have said that.” He looked up at her. “I am so, so sorry I said that, Mads. I’m so sorry.”

“It’s…” It wasn’t okay. He had struck her right where it hurt most, and he had known what he was doing. “I forgive you.”

Perhaps she was forgiving him too easily, but she knew her brother and she had read the texts he had sent, listened to the voicemail he left. He’d been beating himself up for it ever since the words left his mouth.

Buck nodded, not looking the least bit relieved or any less guilty.

“That wasn’t the reason I asked you to come over, though,” he said, running his hand through his unstyled curls. “I- I mean, it was part of the reason. But it wasn’t the main reason.”

“What was the main reason, then?” Maddie asked, taking a sip of the water.

“Well, you never said where you were staying, now that you and Chimney aren’t… You and Chimney.”

“Couchsurfing, mainly,” she said, sighing. “I still don’t have a job and I used up most of my savings when I left, so apartment hunting hasn’t really been happening.”

The face her brother made was perhaps the textbook example of ‘guilt stricken.’

“What if it didn’t have to happen? What if you stayed here?”

“Are you offering me your couch?” Maddie wasn’t entirely certain how she felt about living with her brother again, but it would mean she could keep an eye on him and make sure he was actually as okay as he pretended to be. It had to mean something, too, that he wasn’t only inviting her to visit but inviting her to live with him.

“Uh, not exactly,” he said, dashing all her hopes there. “I’m kinda offering you the couch, and the bed, and… everything else, too.”

“What.” The hell was this? “Are you moving?”

“No, no. Kinda? But no. I’ve been staying at Eddie’s the past few days,” Buck said, and Maddie remembered Howie’s all-caps declaration in ‘Buckaroo Intervention’ that her brother had come to work without a hangover for the first time in weeks. She wondered, with a hint of jealousy, if Eddie would share the secret of however he managed to get through to Buck. Assuming the secret wasn’t simply, ‘Be Eddie.’ “And I’m going to be staying there for… For a while, probably.”

“So you’re giving me the loft.”

“Kinda. I’m staying on the lease, so obviously I’ll be paying for everything still. It’s like room and board, only without the board. Because you’re on your own for groceries,” Buck said. “The stairs need to be baby proofed, because there’s no way it’s safe for Jee to be going up and down them. And the balcony there is probably not toddler friendly.”

“I do remember you trying to convince us to let you put locks on our windows,” Maddie said. She set down her glass. “What’s going on, Buck? What’s changed? Why are you staying with Eddie?”

For a moment, she thought she had pressed too far. But then her brother sighed and said,

“He needs me. And… And I need him.” So the secret really was simply ‘Be Eddie.’ She didn’t think she would ever understand their relationship. “Speaking of Eddie, I need to get back over to his before he gets worried, so I’m gonna take off. You still have your key, right?”

“I do,” she confirmed with a nod.

“Right, awesome.” Buck hesitated for a moment on the way past, before dashing forward and hugging her tightly. “I’ll, uh. I’ll see you later?”

Maddie nodded again, and then watched him run out the door. Skipping almost, actually. After a moment’s debate, she pulled out her phone and sent to the group chat,

Buck and Eddie are living together now

Hearth-felt Conversations

“So, first overnight since I got you the blanket and pillow,” Buck said as he and Edmundo worked on dinner together, something that was quickly becoming routine. “You didn’t call me in the middle of the night. Does that mean you slept better?”

“I did, actually,” the other man said. They were following a casserole recipe that Linda from Dispatch had suggested to him, which was a weird thing to think about. Linda was one of the names Buck had always associated with Maddie’s social circle, and he hadn’t been prepared for that circle to become Edmundo’s.

But at least he was fitting in well over there, Josh’s occasional bitchiness aside. Even if that thought filled Buck with a sense of dread that he tried to quash down.

“I think it was mostly the blanket,” Edmundo continued to say. “I didn’t… How did you put it? I didn’t turn into a koala with the body pillow.”

“So you’re saying I’m special?” Buck joked, only to be taken completely aback when the other man simply nodded and said,

“Yes.”

It felt, in a lot of ways, like the two of them were dancing on the edge of a cliff, simply waiting for the both of them to be ready before they took that plunge together. Sometimes, like now, it felt like maybe they had already taken that leap and just hadn’t realized it yet. Buck wanted it, he did, had in fact been wanting it far before he was even aware that he wanted it. But he wasn’t sure how, wasn't sure if he could, not when he was terrified of treating Edmundo like a replacement for Eddie.

The only people he could talk to that could, perhaps, understand his situation were Bobby and Edmundo, and perhaps even Athena. Each of them had partners that they loved dearly and then lost, and then had to learn how to love someone else. But this Bobby and Athena didn’t know the circumstances, and without the proof he was able to show their other selves, they would definitely think Buck crazy. And he couldn’t talk to Edmundo about this, because of the obvious.

“W- Well, that’s very flattering,” Buck stuttered out, and then flailed for something else to talk about. “Uh, I told Maddie I was staying here.”

“I assumed you would,” Edmundo said, letting the change in topic go without comment. “Now that she’s staying there, she’d eventually notice that you weren’t.”

“That’s fair enough. Wanna make a bet on how long she lasted before she told everyone?”

“Not on your life. We’ll leave the gambling to the Wilsons.”

“Again, fair enough,” Buck said, nodding. “She probably didn’t even wait for the door to close behind me.”

The shift immediately after Edmundo had told May that they both knew about the group chat hadn’t been as awkward as Buck would have initially thought. Actually, no, it was, just not for him. He’d had a bit of fun with it, taking to either scowling or looking like a kicked puppy whenever their phones went off at the same time. The three of them, Bobby and Chimney and Hen, looked more and more guilty each time it happened.

Hen had been the only one to sit herself down next to him, a couple hours into the shift, and apologize for it.

“It was dumb, and Athena told us it was dumb, and we did it anyway,” she had said. “We were worried, and you weren’t letting us in, but that’s no excuse for talking about you behind your back. All of that aside, though? I’m really glad you seem to be doing better, now.”

But however much regret they felt for it, he knew Maddie had undoubtedly shared the latest gossip in their little chat, and they had most definitely eaten it up.

“Y’know, I honestly wasn’t all that surprised to find out about the group chat,” Buck told Edmundo. “I’m pretty sure I accused them of it when Maddie came over, before…”

“Have any of them actually reached out, though?” The tone was idle, but something about the set of his shoulders made Buck think of claws.

“Athena invited me to dinner again,” he answered. She’d been regularly sending him dinner invites for weeks now, he just kept refusing in favor of drinking alone. Honestly, he was surprised she hadn’t been banging on his door more often, but perhaps she’d opted on the same course of action he had with the other Maddie. That is, make sure he knew the offer was there and patiently wait for him to take her up on it.

“Are you going to go?” Edmundo asked, leaning against the counter as he wiped his hands with a dish towel. They were just waiting for the casserole to finish baking in the oven, now.

“I probably should. Can’t really complain about them never reaching out if I ignore them when they try,” Buck said with a shrug. “You’ll go with me, right? Things with Bobby and me are still awkward after our fight, and I’m not sure I trust myself to not get… Bitey, depending on where the dinner talk heads.”

“Of course I’ll go,” Edmundo said with an easy shrug. “Just give me a day, so I can ask Pepa to watch Chris.”

It used to be that Abuela had been the go-to for a babysitter, when he or Carla weren’t available, but she was living in El Paso now. The move happened during that month he was gone, and so he hadn’t even the chance to say goodbye.

“Thanks.” It was too small of a word to contain the full gratitude that Buck was feeling, but it would have to do.

“Hey, we said we’d have each other’s backs, and I guess that includes potentially awkward dinners.”

In lieu of answering - because, again, too much gratitude and not enough words - Buck took out his phone to text Athena, before he lost his nerve. They didn’t text often, because his texting habits tended to try her patience, but he didn’t feel up to a full-blown conversation with the police sergeant at the moment.

The response to his first message had been nearly instantaneous, and Buck had the impossible image in his head of her leaping across furniture to reply to him. Athena was probably just happy that he was finally taking her up on the offer. But now there was a delay in her response, and he could easily see the conversation playing out between her and Bobby right that moment.

Eddie is always welcome.

“Athena says tomorrow,” he told Edmundo. “She’s extended the offer to Chris, too.”

“I guess I won’t need to bother Pepa after all. Kinda soon, though.”

“She’s probably afraid I’ll change my mind and go back to being a recluse.” Not that the idea wasn’t tempting. “Speaking of dinner, how’s the food?”

Edmundo opened the oven door to peak in, and then opened it fully before tugging on a pair of oven mitts.

“The food is done,” he said, pulling it out and setting it on the counter.

“Awesome, then I’ll-”

His phone interrupted him with a chime, notifying him of another text. He opened it, thinking it was Athena again, only to see that it was his sister.

What happened to your bathroom mirror?
