“Fought with Blood Mary?” Edmundo was peaking at the screen over his shoulder, and sounded amused by what he was reading.

“Better than the truth,” Buck said as he tucked his phone away. “If she kept asking, I was gonna come up with something sex related. That usually makes her stop.”

“Think she’ll mention it to the others?”

“She’ll probably mention it to Chimney, at least.” He knew the two of them were still talking, even if they weren’t a couple. “It’s a problem for later, though.”

Buck went to peel Chris away from whichever game he was currently hooked on, as was his intention before Maddie’s interruption. The kid was definitely a preteen now, but he was still the sweet and agreeable kid he had always been, so it was a task accomplished with only a minor attempt at bargaining for more time. He got him to wash up, once again with only minor complaint, and went to help Edmundo set the dining table.

Dinner with the Diaz family, either version of it, was always a pleasant affair. It was different, so very different, from the family dinners Buck had grown up with. There was actual laughter, for example, and just a general warmth to the affair. The dinners he grew up with had been filled with awkward silences and an ever-present tenseness to the atmosphere that made him finish his food as quickly as possible so he could scurry back to his room. He and Chris gently roasted Edmundo’s cooking, even as they took second and third helpings

Afterwards, Chris helped clear the table, and then Buck helped wash the dishes while Edmundo dried. It was so domestic that Buck was left to wonder for the second time that night if they had already fallen off the cliff’s edge they were dancing on, and if it even mattered. Once again, that was a problem for the future, though.

Or so he thought, until a couple hours later.

“Hey, Buck?” Chris’ voice caught him on his way to the bathroom, and he came to a halt, leaning halfway into the kid’s open door.

“Yeah, bud?”

Chris was sitting on his bed, already dressed in his pajamas and ready for bed. He was frowning, too, the frown he wore when he was trying to figure out a problem with his homework.

“You and dad sleep in the bed together.” A statement, not a question.

“We do,” Buck confirmed, nodding. “Does that bother you?”

It hadn’t bothered the other Chris, but then again, he hadn’t pissed that Chris off quite so thoroughly as this one.

“He sleeps better.” The statement made Buck wince, because he hadn’t realized that the kid noticed how tired Edmundo had been looking. Which was stupid of him, because Chris had proven time and again to be very observant. “Are you and dad together?”

“It’s… Complicated.”

“Because you love the other him, still.”

“That’s right.”

Chris nodded, but didn’t say anything, so Buck thought they were done. He started for the bathroom, but barely left the doorway when the boy spoke up.

“Hey, Buck?”

“Yeah?” Buck said, turning back to him.

“Don’t leave again.”

“I won’t,” he promised.

Chris narrowed his eyes at him, but didn’t call him out on how he’d broken his word on that before, however unintentionally. Instead, he just nodded and fell quiet again. Buck waited a few moments, but when the boy didn’t say anything else, he continued on his way to the bathroom.

A few moments later, he heard Chris close his door.

Buck was filled with nervous energy in the hours leading up to the dinner with Athena and Bobby. Whenever he was sitting, at the table for breakfast and lunch or on the couch to waste away the hours with television, his leg was constantly bouncing. On the drive over - in the truck, because Eddie did not want to see that frenetic energy translated into his driving, thank you very much - he spent the entire time switching between stations on the radio, apparently incapable of choosing one he liked.

“Why are you so nervous about this?” Eddie finally asked when they got to the house, the two of them waiting for Chris to clamber out of the backseat.

“I don’t know,” Buck said, handing Chris his crutches. “I technically haven’t had dinner here in like, almost five years. And in the past month, any time Bobby and I have started talked about things unrelated to work, I’ve gotten… Bitey.”

Eddie noted the change in time, from “four years and change” to “almost five,” but decided not to call him out on it. Not now, at least. Instead, he said,

“I’ll do my best to keep you from getting bitey.”

They ambled up to the front door, Buck just about dragging his feet as he went, and rang the doorbell. They thankfully didn’t have to wait long, because Athena was opening the door for them only a minute or two later. She smiled warmly when she saw them, stepping aside to let them in.

“Come in, come in, I’m glad you all could make it.”

“Thanks for the invite,” Buck said, rubbing the back of his neck.

“Thank for you coming,” she said, and Eddie watched the other man duck his head.

“Thanks for letting Chris and I come,” he said, following his son down the stairs.

“The more the merrier,” she said. “You actually had good timing, the food’s just about done. I’ll go grab Harry from his room, you three go ahead and sit.”

She peeled away from them, leaving the trio to head into the kitchen/dining area on their own. Eddie’s mouth watered a bit at the smell of cooking food, struck by the memory of family dinners at the station and missing them dearly. The table was already set for six people, and Bobby was just pulling something out of the oven. Like his wife, he smiled when he saw them.

“Just in time,” he said. “Eddie, it’s nice to see you’re doing better.”

“Thank you,” Eddie said, sitting down at the table. Buck immediately took a seat next to him, his leg bouncing again.

Bobby set down what he’d pulled from the oven - a roasted chicken - at the same time as Athena and Harry entered the room. Harry immediately hurried over to take the empty seat next to Chris, the two of them falling into an excited, if hushed, conversation. Eddie wasn’t sure what it was about, some game they both played, but for once he was thankful for technology.

It had been a while, after all, since Chris and Harry had gotten to hang out together in person.

Athena and Bobby said grace, and then Bobby cut up the chicken to pass out, along with the sides that had made their way to the dining table. It was delicious, as Bobby’s cooking always was, and Eddie hadn’t realized how much he had missed it. The last time he’d eaten Bobby’s food, it was that breakfast on the morning he picked up Buck.

“So, Eddie, how has Dispatch been?” Athena asked, part way through the meal. “I know what May’s told us, but I haven’t had a chance to actually ask you.”

Something about the wording felt pointed, but Eddie opted not to delve too deeply into it.

“Awful,” he answered honestly. “I spend most of my time sitting in a tiny office, fielding phone calls, tweeting out info about road closures, and filing paperwork.”

“And sharing recipes with Linda,” Buck added, knocking a knee against his. “When he’s not, y’know, upsetting Josh by hijacking calls and saving the day.”

“He got over it,” Eddie said. “Most of the Dispatchers are friendly whenever I talk to them. Though there’s Claudette, who’s…” He trailed off, not really wanting to get into it, but Bobby and Athena were both nodding.

“Oh, we know all about Claudette,” the sergeant said.

“I don’t know anything about Claudette,” Buck said, glancing between the three of them. “Eds, have you been holding out on me?”

“Claudette is… She has a personality that doesn’t quite mesh with everyone,” Eddie said, trying for diplomacy.

“May says she’s a bitch,” Harry clarified, grinning cheekily even as Athena glared at him.

“We do not use that language, young man,” she said.

“Her first day there, she took May’s drink and called her an intern,” Bobby supplied. “And it hasn’t really gotten better, no matter how much May’s tried.”

“What a bitch,” Buck said, and then shared a grin with Harry. Athena let out a sigh and looked very unimpressed with him.

“What about you, Buck?” the sergeant said, and Eddie watched him blink rapidly in surprise.

“What about me?”

“What’s new with you?” she clarified. “It’s been far too long since we last caught up. I heard you’re letting Maddie stay at the loft.”

“Yeah, she needs a place to stay, and I’ve been staying at Eddie’s,” Buck said, and then with a pointed glance at Bobby, said, “But you already knew that part, I’m sure.”

“It’s been mentioned,” the captain conceded, and even his wife side-eyed that answer.

“Right,” Buck scoffed.

“Anything particularly stupid happen recently, Athena?” Eddie asked, stepping in before someone’s head was bitten off. It was literally the reason Buck had asked him to come, after all. “I heard about the kids stealing the stop signs…”

Aside from that, and a few other hiccups, dinner with the Grant-Nashes was a pleasant affair. After the chicken, they had some pie that Bobby had made, and then sat around the dining table digesting. Chris and Harry ran off to the latter’s room as soon as they were allowed, probably to play a game with too much violence. Eventually, however, Eddie had to excuse himself to the bathroom.

When he came back, he heard Buck and Bobby talking before he actually re-entered the dining area, and peered around the entry way. Athena was no where to be seen, the dining table was cleared, and the two men were by the sink. The two of them weren’t shouting or biting each other’s heads off, not yet, so Eddie opted to wait.

“-did try to talk him out of it,” Bobby was saying.

“I didn’t know that,” Buck said, head ducked down and shoulders hunched up. “Eddie didn’t tell me.”

“I didn’t just accept it or ignore everything like an ostrich,” the captain said, and Eddie wondered if that was something Buck had accused him off in their fight.

“You still cut him off, though.” Buck raised his head, and while Eddie couldn’t see his face, he could guess that he was glaring heatedly at the other man.

“Buck-”

“No, Bobby. How many times, during that month I was away, did you drop by to see him?” Eddie knew the answer to that, and so did Bobby, based on the very telling silence. “You saw him on New Years Eve, and then fucked off out of his life until I came back.”

“That isn’t fair.”

“I don’t give a shit about fair, Bobby. Eddie leaves the station, and everyone cuts him off except Hen. And that’s only because their kids are friends.” Eddie winced at the words, because they were sadly true. Hen was definitely the only one at the 118 that he spoke to or saw anymore, not counting Buck, and it was mostly because of Chris and Denny. He hadn’t seen Chimney in person since Buck’s return, and only a couple times before then. “I mean, what the hell? You said - you said! - that it would never happen to us, because we’re a family.”

“I checked in on him.”

“Asking May for workplace gossip is not checking in on him,” Buck snapped, practically snarling, and Eddie decide it was time for him to intervene. Except, before he could take a step forward, a hand on his shoulder stopped him. He turned and saw Athena, who shook her head at him.

“Sometimes, Bobby needs yelling at,” she whispered, guiding him away from the kitchen and into the den.

“He wasn’t okay!” Buck shouted, apparently in response to something Bobby said that Eddie had missed.

“So we’re just going to let that happen?” Eddie asked, glancing back at the other room, even though he couldn’t see them anymore.

“Yes,” the sergeant replied. “They’ll shout, and then they’ll hug it out. And then there’ll probably be more shouting, followed by more hugging.”

“I came when you called!” Bobby was finally raising his voice.

“That doesn’t mean jack-shit if that’s the only time you’ll show!” Buck shouted back. 

“I hope the kids can’t hear this,” Eddie said, glancing down the hall.

“I checked, they’re thoroughly distracted with killing pixels. And the door is closed,” Athena said. “Evidence suggests that Harry’s door is utterly sound proof. At least from outside noises, because he can never hear me when I yell for him.”

“Is this why you kept inviting Buck over?” he asked, wincing at yet more shouting. “For them to fight?”

“No, I kept inviting him over because I wanted to check in on him myself,” she corrected, sitting down on the couch. After a moment’s hesitation, he did the same. “He didn’t answer the door the last two times I went over to the loft, and I couldn’t break it down without a warrant.”

“And that stopped you?”

“Only because he was still going to work. I almost broke it down anyway when he called in last weekend, but then he called Bobby to come over the day after.” She looked at him, eyes going softer. “I’m sorry to hear about your friends.”

“Thank you,” he said, for lack of anything else. She nodded, as if it was an acceptable response.

“He’s looking better,” Athena said. The words coming from the kitchen were no longer quite so loud, but still rather sharp. “And he’s also right. We should have made more of a point to check in on you. On everyone, before Christmas. We really dropped the ball, there. I dropped the ball.”

“It wasn’t your fault,” he said.

“It’s just as much my fault as it is Bobby’s,” she countered. “I wonder, sometimes, what would have happened if we just did a better job of touching base with each other. What would have changed. If it would have kept Buck and Maddie from running.”

Knowing what he did about the events of the other timeline, he knew doing exactly that could’ve kept Maddie from running, at least. It was, after all, what that Shannon had apparently done.

“Maybe.”

“Has he told you anything?” Athena asked. “Buck, I mean. About that month he was gone.”

“He doesn’t remember it,” he lied, and prayed she didn’t call him out on it. She looked, for a moment, like she was going to. But then she shook her head.

“They’ve gotten quiet,” she said instead. “And I left my wine in there. Do you think it’s safe to go get it?”

“Only one way to find out, and I’m not that brave,” Eddie joked.

Athena rolled her eyes and got up, almost tiptoeing to the peek into the dining area. She looked back at him, mouthed ‘hugging’ and slipped inside.

“Don’t mind me,” she said, announcing herself to the two men. “I’m just getting something to drink…”

Chapter End Notes

This chapter gave me fits.

Absent Regrets

“Sue, we’ve gotten six other calls about this girl so far,” May said, a couple stations down from where Eddie was standing, getting info from a different dispatcher to tweet out. “But no address or last name, just Selene.”

“We’re getting calls from Maine, Arizona, Colorado,” Linda chimed in. “All of them just as clueless.”

“So everyone’s seen this video, but no one can tell us where she is or… Or who she is?” Sue asked.

“I looked her up on Instagram,” May said.  “She was in the middle of touring her new Brentwood mansion when she fell. She’s a big luxury influencer.”

“A what?” Sue echoed Eddie’s own thoughts. What the hell was an influencer? What the hell was a luxury influencer? Were these even real things, or some elaborate prank?

“A luxury influencer. It’s her job to be rich,” May explained.

“That’s it?”

“Pretty much.”

“Eddie.” Sue’s use of his name had him ambling over, tablet in hand. “Let’s tweet out a picture of that girl from the sauna, see if anyone can ID her.”

“What’s her name?” he asked, twiddling with the stylus.

“Selene,” May answered as Sue left them to it. “I just sent you her profile.”

“Got it.” He clicked the notification as it popped up. “Adding to tweet, and sent. Need a hand looking through her socials?”

What had his life become, that he was volunteering to scroll through someone’s social media? The alternatives weren’t much better. Answering phones and talking to people who were constantly proving that there were, actually, very stupid questions. Or wrestling the photocopier into submission for the third time that week.

It was only Monday.

He hadn’t even had his lunch break, yet.

“It can’t hurt to have another pair of eyes. So far, I can tell it’s a house in Brentwood.” May brought up a picture from Selene’s profile, covering up the map of Brentwood and then circling a building in the background. “You can see the Getty from the pool, which means it’s on the opposite side of the street. So it’s on the southern side of whatever street it’s on.”

“Try looking through a topographical map,” Eddie suggested. “Looks like the house is on a ridge.”

“All these houses are on ridges,” she said, pulling the map up anyways while he looked through Selene’s feed on the tablet. “It’s impossible to tell them apart.”

“Got it,” he said, enlarging one of the pictures, one of Selene on the sidewalk. “Curb number, right across the street. Eighty-four ninety-seven.”

“Okay,” May said, typing it in and pulling up a road map. “Now we need a street.”

Eddie checked the LAFD Metro’s twitter again and for a moment, felt stupidly proud when he saw all the notifications.

“Boom. Looks like I’m going viral,” he said, angling the tablet so May could see.

“She does have over a million followers.”

He frowned at one of the replies, because,

“This one is claiming to be her mother,” he said, angling it so May could see the reply from one Hannah Neyer.

“I’ll pass that along,” she said. He nodded, already messaging the woman with instructions to meet at an LAPD precinct.

It wasn’t even half an hour later that they were receiving information from the parents, enough to get a proper search going. Eddie hovered around May as she directed them, having snagged an extra earpiece so he could eavesdrop. She had smirked at him when he put it on, looking far too much like her mother for his comfort.

“You’re looking for a red Mini Cooper,” she told the searchers. “License plate Charles-Adam-Adam-Six-Nine-Three. Belongs to our victim, Rebecca Neyer.”

“Havenhurst clear,” Chimney said over the radio, not very long after.

Followed by a couple officers he didn’t recognize clearing their streets.

“Vantage is clear,” Bobby said.

“Engine One Eighteen, proceed to Mountain Avenue,” May instructed.

“Copy, Dispatch. On our way.” A couple minutes later, he said, “Dispatch, we found it.”

With a sigh, Eddie tugged off the earpiece and set it down on the desk. Initially, he went back to his little cave of an office and sat at his desk. There wasn’t really much for him to do, other than scroll through social media or go through incident reports. After a few minutes of debate - spent spinning in his chair - he opted to go ahead and take his lunch.

A lunch that Buck had packed for him, because that was apparently now a thing. He hadn’t even made a big deal of it, had simply shoved an innocent looking brown paper bag into Eddie’s hands when they split up after breakfast, Buck to the station and Eddie to take Chris to school. And Eddie hadn’t really known how to feel about it, still didn’t know how to feel about it.

The last person to pack him a lunch had been his Abuela, and then his mother before that. Shannon had definitely never done so, in that period after he returned from overseas but before she had left.

May was already in the breakroom when he went to get his lunch from the fridge, and so he grabbed an extra bottle of water for her.

“She’s on her way to the burn unit now,” she told him, updating him about the luxury influencer who had apparently been lying to everyone, from complete strangers on the internet to her parents.

“High price to pay for living a fake life,” he commented, sitting down at the counter with her, handing her the water. “You think her regular life was that bad?”

“Maybe she felt like she was missing out on a life she could have had, if she’d been born someone else or made different choices,” May said. “Don’t you wonder about stuff like that?”

Eddie thought about the very similar conversation he’d had with Chimney at Buck’s loft, about the things they would have done differently. He thought about Buck, who had run away and stumbled into that very scenario. He thought about what Athena had said just the other day, her regrets about not doing more. He thought about that other him, that Eddie who was so much more whole, that Eddie who hadn’t been nearly as shattered.

“Not really,” he lied, avoiding her eyes as he opened up his lunch. There were a pair of sandwiches in saran wrap, an apple, and one of Chris’ pudding cups, along with a plastic spoon. 

“Well, some people- Most people, other than you, worry that they’re not getting the full experience,” she said. “The unknown is scary. You know, FOMO.” He must have made a face, because she scoffed and said, “Fear of missing out.”

God, even after weeks spent on social media, he was still left feeling like one of those old people shaking their canes at the younguns.

“You make it sound like a sickness.”

“I guess it kind of is.”

Eddie started unwrapping one of the sandwiches - it looked like it was ham and cheese - when he spotted a bright pink post-it note attached to the bottom side. Reading it, he rolled his eyes, even as he couldn’t help but smile.

What do you call an alligator in a vest?

An in-vest-igator

“Jesus, Buck…” There was even a smiley face scribbled into the left-over space below.

“He’s packing you lunches now?”

“Yes,” Eddie answered, and then immediately scowled at the smile on May’s face. “Shut up.”

“I didn’t say anything,” she said, raising her hands to defend herself. “But, speaking of the fear of missing out…”

“I wish I was missing out on Buck’s puns,” Eddie retorted, but carefully folded the note and tucked it into his pocket all the same.

“I’d still like to know how, exactly, you broke the first one,” Maddie said, looking very unimpressed as she leaned against the bathroom doorway.

Buck was finally getting around to replacing the mirror he had shattered in his fit of self-loathing and misery. It was actually easy to find a new one that matched - he found the exact same one at Lowe’s - and he hauled it over the afternoon after he got off work. His sister had been happy to see him, though she seemed down about something.

“I told you,” he said, putting in the new screws with a drill he borrowed from Edmundo. “I got into a fight with Bloody Mary. I won, obviously. Though she fights dirty.”

“Yeah, see, I’m calling bullshit based on how Bloody Mary isn’t real.”

Buck paused in the process of hefting up the new mirror to contemplate on what he knew was real, and wondered if that meant Bloody Mary was, too. And then he decided he was never, ever going to test it to find out.

“Call bullshit all you like, Mads,” he said, stepping back to eyeball everything. Everything looked straight - straighter than himself, anyway - and he gave his reflection a pleased grin. “Doesn’t make it less true.”

“You also told me you broke it by being too pretty, and then by singing opera in the shower.”

“And all of that is equally true.”

“Because none of them are true.”

“Fine,” he sighed, turning to face her. “You want to know the truth? I broke it with Richard.”

“Richard?” Maddie cocked her head, grinning like a cat about to eat the canary.

“Yes, Richard. It’s a toy that was supposed to suction to the wall, but-”

“Okay never mind!” she shouted, covering her ears. “I apologize for asking, let’s please forget about it.”

“On that topic, I hope you haven’t gone digging into any of the boxes under the bed.”

“I am too sober for this conversation,” she bemoaned, turning on her heel and power walking for the kitchen area. Maddie didn’t get out any wine, though, settling for just filling up a glass with water. Buck didn’t know if that was because she simply didn’t have any there, or in deference to him. She hadn’t brought up his drinking since the day of their fight, but he knew everyone had noticed his renewed sobriety.

Noticed it, and then promptly started gossiping about it, no doubt.

Buck gathered up everything left from installing the mirror, gathering up the extra screws and wrapping the power cord around the drill. The box that the mirror came in was left beside the front door, to be thrown out later.

“Do you want to stay for dinner?” Maddie asked him when he was done and had sat down at the island with her.

“Tempting. But Eddie has yet another recipe from Linda he’s wanting to try, and far be it from me to deny him a taste tester.”

“Okay, no, that’s weird. It’s weird hearing about your friends being friends with my friends.”

Buck shrugged, because he’d already had to come to terms with that oddity.

“We were gone for a while, things changed,” he said, and caught the briefest flicker of… Something playing across his sister’s face. “Okay, what’s up? You were looking down about something when I got here, what’s going on?”

For a moment, it seemed Maddie wasn’t going to answer, or was going to avoid it much like he avoided talking about his problems. But then she sighed and sagged a little in her chair.

“I spent the day with Jee, and when I took her back to the apartment, Howie had her baby box out. He was putting stuff from Boston in there, and we got to talking, and apparently he did his best to try and capture everything that I missed with Jee-Yun,” she said. “It was so sweet, but I was just looking at the pictures thinking I should have been there.”

“You missed, what, three months? And a half?”

“You say that like those months were nothing, Buck. She’s only a year old, that was a quarter of her life.”

“Even if you were here living your normal life, you probably would have missed some of those moments anyway, right? You can’t be with her twenty-four, seven.”

“A few moments, sure. But every early milestone?” She shook her head. “Only a bad mother misses every one.”

“You’re not a bad mother,” Buck snapped, with such ferocity that his sister flinched away from him. “Things were fucked, you went and got help for yourself. That makes you, like, the exact opposite of a bad mother. And you haven’t missed every early milestone. You’ve been back with her for months now, right? Even if you’d been gone this entire time, she wouldn’t remember it.”

“You knew something was wrong with our family, even though you were a baby when Daniel died,” Maddie countered. “It haunted you your whole life, and I don’t want that for her.”

“No, I was haunted by our parents being awful people,” he said, and for once she didn’t speak up to defend them. That place in Boston must have really helped. “Stop comparing yourself to mom, because you’re not her. You’re not them. You’re so much better than them.”

“I’m just scared that I’ve scarred her for life.”

“You haven’t, I know you haven’t.” He reached her and gripped her hand in his, squeezing it tight. “You are one of the best mothers I have ever known. I mean, Jee’s not the first kid you raised, after all. And I’d like to think you didn’t do too bad of a job with me.”

Finally, finally, his words had her smiling. It was a small smile, but it was a smile nonetheless.

“I don’t know,” she drawled. “Howie’s told me a bit about how you were, before I got here. I don’t think I want to take credit for that.”

Buck groaned and rolled his eyes. He was happy that she was joking, but he still didn’t like how Maddie was putting herself down about this. The worst part was, there wasn’t much he could say to help, because it wasn’t as if he knew what it was like. The closest thing he could think of was that month - or four to five years, from certain perspectives - he was gone, but Chris hadn’t exactly hit any major milestones in that time.

And then, an idea struck him.

“Go get your shoes and grab your keys,” Buck instructed, letting go of her hand to pull out his phone so he could text Edmundo.

“Why?” she asked, not moving to do either of those things.

“Because you’re coming to dinner with me.”

Eddie could not recall, exactly, the last time Maddie had been in his house. He was fairly certain the one and only time had been at the impromptu gathering for Chimney’s birthday, right after Albert had first shown up, but he wasn’t entirely sure. So it was with some awkwardness that he welcomed her in, an awkwardness he saw mirrored in her as she trailed behind Buck.

He and Maddie weren’t unfriendly, but they weren’t the closest of friends, either. They were mostly friends by association, through Buck and Chimney. So he wasn’t exactly sure how to handle it, when Buck had called him on the way back to inform him that Maddie was coming over for dinner, and could he please talk to her.

At least dinner wasn’t uncomfortable. Buck and Christopher, separately, could keep a conversation going entirely on their own. Together, there was no chance in hell of any awkward silences popping up. They bounced off each other, drawing both Eddie and Maddie into whatever they were talking about, until the food was eaten and Christopher was asking to be excused.

“Y’know what, it’s been a while since you’ve shown me your Legos,” Buck said, getting up as well. “Why don’t we do that now.”

And it was a clumsy as hell excuse, but Chris wasn’t stupid. He looked at Buck, and then at Eddie and Maddie, and shrugged.

“Okay.”

It was a short walk from the dining table to Chris’ room, and just before he entered, Buck shot them both a thumbs up. And then he closed the door behind him.

“That wasn’t subtle. At all,” Maddie said, shaking her head.

“Your brother isn’t subtle. At all,” Eddie pointed out.

“I don’t really know what I’m doing here,” she said. “I mean, dinner was good. It gives me hope for Howie, because everyone’s told me that you couldn’t cook at all.”

“I’ve had time to learn,” he said, trying not to sound bitter about that. His choices, after all. “Do you think I’m a bad father?”

“Pretty sure if I answer with anything except a firm no, Buck will come bursting out of that room to yell at me,” she answered. “One of the first things he told me about you was how good of a dad you were. He hadn’t even known you for a month.”

Eddie ducked his head to hide his smile. Even at the beginning, when Eddie had been positive he was screwing everything up, Buck had been his cheerleader. With the exception of those brief couple of days that Buck hated him.

“You know I was in the army, right?”

“It has been mentioned.”

“I enlisted pretty much right after Shannon told me she was pregnant,” he told her. “Right after the shotgun wedding. I was on leave long enough to hold him when he was born, and then straight back to Afghanistan the week after. And then, when we got the diagnosis, I reenlisted.”

“Why are you telling me this?”

“I missed years of his life. I missed just about every milestone. When he first started to crawl, when he took his first step. I missed his first word and his first baby tooth.” Those were all moments he was never going to get back, and he regretted every one. “And you’ve missed, what? Three months?”

“I ran away from my child.”

“So did I.”

Maddie blinked at him, as if not fully comprehending what he said.

“What?”

“I ran away,” he told her. “All the way to another hemisphere. I said it was for the insurance, and to help pay the bills. And I got to be real noble about it, too. But I was running away from him. I missed years of his life, and so many milestones. So, tell me, am I a bad father?”

He wasn’t, he knew that he wasn’t. It had taken years and years to get to the point where he could confidently put those doubts to rest, but he was there. And if he ever slipped up and thought otherwise, he knew Buck would do exactly what Maddie had joked about him doing.

“Well, I- I mean, clearly you’ve done everything you could to make up for that,” Maddie said, struggling for an answer. “And- And it doesn’t seem to me as if it’s affected him any…”

“So then why are you a bad mother for three months?”

“Because…”

“Because why?” There was no answer forthcoming, and he let out a sigh. “You’re not, that’s the answer. You’re not a bad mother for missing three months.”

“I feel like I’m back in therapy,” Maddie joked, looking away from him. Eddie let out a disgusted noise, surprising a giggle out of her.

“I hate therapy,” he told her. “And I hate that it’s helping.”

“And it really does help,” she said. “I should… I should probably get going.”

“Are you sure?” He actually, truthfully, didn't want her to leave. “You’re more than welcome to stay longer. Buck and I are working through the Good Place on Netflix, right now.”

“I’m sure, but thank you,” Maddie said, getting up from the table. “And… And thank you.”

“You’re welcome.”

Eddie watched her go knock on Chris’ door, which opened up quickly enough that he wondered if Buck had been eavesdropping. The other man’s face dropped a little, when she said she was heading out, but they hugged and he walked her to the door.

Eddie was still sitting at the table, when Buck came back.

“Thank you,” he said. “For talking to her.”

“She’s your sister, I wasn’t going to say no.” Eddie stood up. ”You wash, I’ll dry?”

“‘Course.”

The two of them worked together to gather up the dishes and ferry them to the kitchen sink. Buck bumped him with his shoulder as he turned on the faucet and grabbed the dish soap.

“You’re not a bad father, y’know.” So he had been eavesdropping.

“I know.”

Buck nodded, clearly pleased with that answer.

“Good. What do you want for lunch tomorrow?”

“I can make my own lunch.”

“I know. What do you want?”

Eddie let out a sigh.

“Surprise me…”

The Burden of Knowledge

“I mean, I get it,” Hen said, venting to them about Karen’s ennui from the night before while they rode the truck to a call. “She spent her twenties going after a very specific goal. You know, she didn’t waste time trying to figure out what she wanted, like I did. Now she’s feeling like she missed out on a key part of life.”

“I thought Karen liked her job,” Bobby said, twisting around in the captain’s seat.

“She does, it’s not really that,” she said. “It’s like… She never got to be really free.”

“So she wants to be twenty again,” Chimney threw in. “Work all day, party all night.”

“I wouldn’t go back to my twenties if you paid me,” the captain said.

“Same,” Lucy chimed in.

“I don’t know,” Chimney started. “If you could do it all over again, you wouldn’t think about doing something different with your life?”

And Buck couldn’t help it, not after everything he’d gone through. He had quite literally lived through that exact scenario, and none of them had the slightest idea. They didn’t know, none of them knew, none of them would ever know. Buck had literally done it over again, had lived four and half years of his life over, doing things differently where he could, only to have it yanked away from him.

It was ridiculous. 

It was absurd. 

It was hilarious. 

So he couldn’t help it as the laughter bubbled up and erupted, leaving him clutching at his sides as his entire body shook with it, the others looking at him in worry and confusion.

“It wasn’t that funny,” Chimney said, eyebrows raised sky-high.

“You- You wouldn’t… You wouldn’t understand,” Buck managed to say, waving away the stares. He pulled out his phone to text Edmundo, because he’d surely understand why Buck was currently dying. “D- Don’t worry about it.”

“Uh, okay.” Lucy was leaning slightly away from him.

“Well.” Bobby drew the word out, clearly trying to get everyone back on track, despite Buck’s continued chuckling. “There was only one career option for boys in my family: firefighter.”

“You’re a legacy, Cap?” Hen asked, still frowning at Buck.

“Fourth generation.” Bobby sounded proud about it, too.

That was new. That was brand new information, why was Buck only just now finding out about this? After eight years of knowing the man - kinda, sorta - one would assume that information would have come up somewhere.

“Hen,” he tried, getting his laughter under control. “Do you think Karen might just be going through a midlife crisis?”

“When did I become middle-aged?” she asked, glaring at him now.

When you hit forty, is what Buck absolutely did not say.

“It’s not about the age, it’s about the feeling,” He said instead, remembering when he sat in front of Maddie and told her he was going to be something, even if he didn’t know what that was, yet. “The anxiety, the ennui-”

“Oh, big word,” Chimney quipped.

“Shut up.”

“Hen, you and Karen love each other,” Bobby cut in, interrupting Chimney and Buck. “You have a good life together. Is it possible you’re overthinking this?”

“I mean, she seemed worried that she missed out on something important,” she answered. “I’m not sure how to fix that.”

“You can’t,” the other paramedic said. “No matter how much we wish we could, there’s no such thing as making up for lost time.”

And that was yet another comment so ridiculously absurd in the context of his life that it was hilarious, but Buck couldn’t find it in him to laugh at it this time.

When they pulled up in the trucks, Buck initially thought it was some sort of amphitheater, with its semicircle of steps leading down that looked so much like stadium seating. But it wasn’t that. Instead…

“This whole area was a missile base right up through the Cold War,” Bobby explained, hurrying down the steps and towards a hole in the grass-covered floor. “My guess? It’s been years since they capped these silos and they’re not holding anymore.”

And that statement was unnecessarily ominous, considering where they were currently standing.

“Alright, Buck, you got this,” the captain said, before going to where a trio of girls were huddled. They were young, with the oldest being maybe Christopher’s age.

Buck knelt at the edge of the hole and peered down, but didn’t see anything but a bottomless void of darkness. Chimney soon joined him with a flashlight, but it didn’t reach anywhere near the bottom.

“It might be as far as eighty feet,” he guessed.

“If it was for a Minuteman,” Buck said, causing the others to look at him. There might have been a week spent watching documentaries about Cold War era things like ICBMs, when his leg was healing the second time. “Could be a hundred-fifty or more, if this was for an Atlas or a Titan.”

“There’s a gate over there,” Lucy said, and they looked to where she was pointing. “Maybe it’s a tunnel, we could get to her that way.”

“Rappelling down is gonna be the quickest way to get to her,” Hen said. “But it might be safer to bring her out that way.”

“Okay, you grab saws, I’ll grab harnesses,” Buck instructed Lucy, already heading to get the gear, mindful of the children that Bobby had moved to the trucks.

It didn’t take very long at all to get everything set up for Hen and Chimney to be lowered down, and it was easy to cut through the gate barring them from the access tunnel. Buck and Lucy hurried along the claustrophobic concrete maze with the blackboard, until they found stairs leading down. And there were a lot of stairs.

They double-timed it, especially when Hen announced over the radio that she’d touched down and was with the victim. They were almost to the bottom when Chimney radioed,

“Cap? Can you put the kids on? Mom needs to speak with them.”

“Can’t we do that up top?” Bobby asked.

“I think it would be best if she did it now.”

“Copy that.”

“I thought we were moving her,” Buck said when they finally reached them, Hen meeting them at the base of the stairs.

“She wants to talk to her kids first,” she said.

“She doesn’t think she’s making it out of here,” Lucy breathed out.

“She doesn’t want their last memory of her to be screaming.”

And Buck… Buck knew exactly what was going through the woman’s mind, right now. Or, he could guess. He could remember spending an entire night, trying to find just the right words to put onto the page. The need to cram as much feeling and love into every single one, because there was still so much to say but there was so little time left to say it.

Without really thinking about it, he handed Lucy the backboard and went to kneel beside the mother and took her hand, because this was hard. Saying goodbye was hard and saying goodbye to your kid was nearly impossible.

(“Chris cried, after reading your letter… You wrote him a goodbye letter, Buck. What were you expecting?”)

“Mom? Mom, are you there?” It sounded like the youngest, maybe.

“I’m here sweetie, are you girls okay?”

“We’re scared, but we’re okay.” A different voice, a different daughter.

“I know. I’m sorry that I scared you.”

“No, I’m sorry, Mom.” The oldest, now. “If we hadn’t been playing, you wouldn’t have fell.”

“No, not your fault. Just an accident. I know it’s hard when we’re apart, when it seems like I’m not there.” (And I’m sorry that things are about to be harder and scarier, and I am so sorry if I’m not there to help you through it.) “But the secret is, I’m always with you. Even when you can’t see me.” (Because I love you and I’ll keep loving you, no matter what happens, no matter what you do or don’t do.) “You girls are the best parts of me, so whenever you feel sad or alone, just reach for each other and that’s when you’ll feel me… With you…” (Because you’re my kid.) “And remember… That I love you…”

(It’s gonna be okay.)

“But…” The oldest again. “We’ll see you soon, right?”

She knew, Buck realized. She knew exactly what this was, she knew her mother had just said goodbye. She knew.

(“Chris cried, after reading your letter.”)

He felt like throwing up.

“Yeah, baby. Soon,” the mother lied, and then let out a breath and did not take another.

Chimney checked her pulse, and then told Bobby,

“Cap, she’s ready to move.”

“Copy that, Chim.”

“What are we gonna say?” Buck asked, still holding her hand. “When we get up there.”

“Nothing,” Chimney told him. “They don’t hear this news from strangers. We transport her and them to the hospital.” He rubbed at his nose and sniffed. “They’re gonna need their dad.”

“That’s nice,” he said, lifting his head to look directly at Hen, because out of the three of them, she was the one that would perhaps understand best. “But what do we say to the girl up there that already knows damn well that she just heard her mother’s last breath?”

The question struck them like the tsunami struck him, the three of them physically flinching at it. Chimney stared at him, eyes wide, because he might have been a father now, but he was the father of a toddler. A toddler who, as Buck had told Maddie, wouldn’t remember someone being gone for months or more. But that girl up there was Christopher’s age, and the burden of knowledge had been clear as day in her voice.

Because Christopher had understood what the words were the moment he read them, and she had understood what the words were the moment she heard them.

“I don’t know,” Hen finally answered. She took the backboard from Lucy. “You two go ahead. We’ll… We’ve got this.”

It felt like it took much longer, yet also much quicker, to make the journey back topside with Lucy. Bobby was already herding the girls into the truck, to get them out of the way and to keep them from seeing their mother when she was wheeled out. Buck climbed in behind them, and Lucy climbed in on the other side.

The youngest two were excited about being in the truck, all grins and laughter, but the oldest…

She knew, Buck thought again. She knew and it was obvious that she knew, so Buck focused on the two whose world had not yet been cracked asunder. But he held out a hand to the oldest, who stared at him for a moment and seemed to understand that he knew that she knew, before taking it in hers.

She squeezed it tight.

A short while later, after the ambulance had pulled away and the truck had followed behind it, Buck turned around to ask Bobby something. Except the question was lost the moment his eyes landed on the ambulance in front of them.

Its lights and sirens were not on.

And with dawning horror, he realized theirs weren’t either.

“Cap,” he hissed, reaching up with one hand to cover the mic of his headset and to grab the captain’s shoulder with the other. “Cap, the sirens. You need to tell them to-”

“Why aren’t the sirens on?” An innocent question, from a girl who did not know.

“Mom doesn’t need them anymore.” The truth, from a girl who did.

Buck closed his eyes and did not turn around, because he did not want to watch the burden of knowledge settle over the sisters. He could not imagine how awful it was, to be a child and be told that his mother was dead. He could not imagine how much worse it had to be, to be a child and to be the one to tell his siblings that truth.

(And it’s gonna be okay.)

Eddie knew something was wrong the moment Buck entered the house. Actually, no, he knew something was wrong when Bobby took it upon himself to call him, some hours before the end of Buck’s shift, while Eddie himself was still at work.

The fire captain had taken it upon himself to reach out more, ever since that argument he and Buck had at the Grant-Nash house. He had even, just the other day, sent Eddie a recipe, probably because it seemed like a safe path after the mention of Eddie doing the same with Linda. The bridge between them hadn’t been burnt, but it was definitely decayed and in need of maintenance.

But Bobby calling him was still unusual, after the months of radio silence.

“We had a call,” he explained. “We lost someone. Buck seems to be taking it… Harder than I expected.”

Bobby hadn’t given him a full rundown of the call, though he gave him the gist of it: mother fell too far, but lived long enough to say goodbye to her daughters, with the oldest fully understanding what was not being said. The captain had mostly been concerned with how Buck had acted afterwards. A bit sullen, a bit withdrawn, refusing to talk about it and snapping the one time someone tried.

Too similar to how he’d been in the days following his drunken visit to Eddie’s house.

Eddie knew when Buck’s shift ended, and he knew how long it was supposed to take to get home from the station. So he waited, the entire time wondering what he was going to do if Buck took longer than he was supposed to, if Buck made a quick - or long - pitstop somewhere. He wondered what he would do if Buck stumbled through the door drunk, again.

But thankfully, twenty minutes later - on the dot - he watched through the front window as the jeep pulled into its customary spot. A minute later, Buck was stumbling through the door - sober, but tired - dropping his work bag before freezing when he realized Eddie had been waiting for him.

“Bobby told you,” he said. It didn’t sound like an accusation.

“He told me you had a bad call.”

Buck nodded once, twice, and then Eddie was stumbling under the sudden weight of him. He was shaking, when Eddie wrapped his arms around him.

“Do you want to talk about it?”

“I want to have a drink,” Buck confessed. “I want- I want to be numb, again. Just for a little while. I want to forget, just for a night.”

“Well, we’re not doing that,” Eddie said. “But thank you, for coming here instead of going somewhere else to do that. I’m proud of you.”

After a moment’s thought, he half-dragged, half-guided him to the couch, the pair of them collapsing onto it. It was like a flipped image of that night, so few weeks ago. Instead of Eddie half-laying on Buck, Buck was half-laying on him. 

“What was it about that call, Buck?”

“She knew she was dying and she knew she was saying goodbye,” Buck answered, voice hushed and quiet, the words mostly spoken into Eddie’s shoulder. “And- And her daughter, she knew it, too.”

“Okay…” That matched what Bobby had said, but it still didn’t explain why it was hitting the other man so hard. “But that doesn’t explain why…”

Buck was silent for so long, that Eddie wasn’t certain he was going to answer. He was silent for so long that Eddie allowed his thoughts to wander as he waited, to wonder if he should treat them all with pizza instead of trying to cook anything. He was silent for long enough that Eddie legitimately began to think that he had fallen asleep.

But then, finally, Buck said,

“I said goodbye.” The words were quiet, still directed into Eddie’s shirt, but he could hear them all the same. “I- I wrote letters, before…”

Buck’s finger traced a spot on Eddie’s chest, just below his right shoulder.

(“And he said it was better that way! Tell me, damn it! How the fuck was that better!?”)

“I wrote letters for Maddie, and Chris, and Eddie,” Buck said. “I spent all night, the night before, working on them. They were goodbye letters. I wrote them goodbye letters and- And Eddie, the other Eddie, told me that Chris cried after reading it. Because he knew it was a goodbye letter, even if I never said the words.”

“Oh, Buck…”

“I had to say goodbye,” he continued, the words not stopping now that they were flowing. “Because- Because I didn’t know what would happen, after I changed things. I- I did my best to keep everything else the same, to just change that one small detail.” Bitch, what? Buck being shot instead was a ‘small detail’? “I didn’t know what would happen after. I didn’t know if I- If he… I was saying goodbye, and Chris knew I was saying goodbye, and I made him cry…”

There wasn’t really much that Eddie could think of to say that would make that better. In all honesty, he wasn’t certain there were words capable of making something like that better. But he was always far more comfortable with actions than words.

“Here’s what I’m going to do,” he declared, lifting Buck off him so he could get to his feet. “I’m going to grab Chris and send him in here, and then I’m going to order a pizza. And after that, we’re going to watch a Disney movie or six. Something with songs.”

“I’m pretty sure they all have songs, Eds.”

“Good, then. And Chris will insist on you choosing the movie, but we both know you’ll end up choosing one he likes, even if he’s almost to the age where he’ll insist he doesn’t like any Disney stuff.”

“I thought you were supposed to be cheering me up,” Buck said, but he was smiling as he did.

“I am, I will.” Eddie grabbed his phone off the table on the way to his son’s bedroom. He knocked and waited patiently for permission to enter, because it was his son’s room and he didn’t want to barge in without cause. “Hey, buddy. Can I pull you away for a bit?”

Chris looked at him from where he was sitting, glued to the computer. He frowned, but nodded.

“Okay. I’m gonna order us a pizza for dinner tonight,” Eddie said. “Buck’s had a rough day today, so he’s kinda sad-”

He didn’t get the chance to finish the explanation. Chris got to his feet and, without a word, marched past him and straight into the living room, climbing onto the couch beside Buck. Immediately, the man wrapped an arm around the boy, pulling him in close.

Eddie spent a long moment watching them, pleased that they were doing so much better. Then he sent Bobby a text to tell him that Buck would be fine, and pulled up the app for the nearest pizza place.

Metro Dispatch is on Fire

Chapter Notes

See the end of the chapter for notes

“I noticed that you’re taking some days off next week,” Bobby remarked, towards the beginning of shift, when everyone had gathered for breakfast,

“Yeah, I’m going to Texas with Eddie,” Buck told him, replying to a text sent by the aforementioned man. Apparently, Dispatch was in the process of going paperless, and things were an utter mess. “His dad’s retiring, and so his family’s gonna have a party or something.”

Silence fell across the table, heavy and judgmental, and he looked up to find that everyone was staring at him. Even Lucy, who had never met Edmundo before, had curious eyebrows raised.

“You’re going to Texas for Eddie’s dad’s retirement party?” Hen asked, sounding just as incredulous as she looked.

“Yeah?”

“Why?” Chimney asked.

“Because Eddie asked me to go.”

Edmundo had asked him about a week or so prior, shortly before the dinner with Athena and Bobby, and obviously Buck had said yes. He hadn’t gotten to see Abuela - this Abuela - before she moved away. And anyways, he knew enough about the Diaz parents to be wary about letting their son go back to them without him.

(Buck wondered, somewhat, how much of the invite had been because Edmundo genuinely wanted him to go, and how much was driven by fear and anxiety. How much of it was because Edmundo didn’t trust Buck to be left alone while he was states away? How much of it was because he was terrified to let Buck out of his sight? How much of it was because Buck was, in many ways, acting as a living, breathing security blanket?)

The others stared at him a bit more, Chimney opening his mouth to say or ask something else, but a not-very-subtle kick from Hen shut him up. That seemed to be everyone else’s cue to drop it and move on to other topics, although he could tell that Hen and Bobby were just as curious as Chimney. But Buck wasn’t exactly feeling up to an interrogation, and so he was thankful when he was able to eat his breakfast in peace.

Although, really, he should have known it was only a postponement.

It was only a short time later, when Buck was definitely not pouting because Edmundo had stopped texting him back - because, Duty calls. - that Hen sat herself on the couch next to him. He looked up to find her smiling, that kind of well-practiced, disarming smile she sometimes pulled out on patients.

It instantly made him suspicious.

“What can I do for ya, Henrietta?” he asked. The use of her full name did the trick of washing away that smile.

“Firstly, never call me that again,” she said. “And I just wanted to know what had you pouting over here.”

“I wasn’t pouting,” Buck lied. “Eddie’s stopped texting back, because he has work to do.”

The last bit might have been said with a bit of a whine, though he would never, ever admit to it.

“I’m sure that he does,” Hen said, nodding sagely. “Speaking of Eddie, however…”

That smile was back, though this time it was amused and self-satisfied, and Buck rolled his eyes. It was amazing, really, that this conversation had been put off for so long. Truly, it spoke to everyone’s self-control that he hadn’t been ambushed about it after they all found out he was staying with the other man.

(There would have been no stopping them, had they known the two of them were sleeping in the same bed.)

“If you have a question, just ask it, Hen,” he sighed. “Instead of doing whatever this is.”

“Fine.” She turned, so she could look at him better. “What exactly is going on between the two of you?”

“We’re friends and we’re living together.”

The look the paramedic shot at him was not amused.

“I know - we all know - that you weren’t exactly picky about your partners before Abby,” Hen said. “You never came out and said it, but you were never limited with who you flirted with on calls.”

Buck didn’t deny it, because it was true.

“And we all noticed there was… Something, between you and Eddie. After the Alpha Male posturing was over, at least,” she continued. “I mean, Chimney once caught him looking at your ass.”

“Wait, what? When!?”

“Right after the tsunami, before the… When you were still a fire marshal, when you came to drop off the drill results in person.”

Buck remembered that day. It wasn’t a fond memory.

“That long ago? Why the hell did none of you say anything?” Buck was… Annoyed, was perhaps too small of a word. He had spent literal years convinced that the man was straight, had carried that assumption with him to the other timeline, and had wasted so much time because of it.

And sure, okay, that period of their lives was probably the worst timing for a relationship upgrade if they wanted to make it last. 

And sure, okay, he hadn’t realized the full depths of his feelings for the man until after Ichabod and the crossroads.

But still!

“He wasn’t very subtle about it,” Hen said with a shrug. “The two of you have never been subtle about it. But nothing ever changed, and then you both had girlfriends, and we just… Figured there wasn’t anything to it, not really.”

“Well, perhaps if any of you had mentioned the ass-staring, there would have been something to it earlier.” Although, honestly, he wasn’t entirely certain about that. He had no idea whether Edmundo would have confessed his feelings, if not for everything that had happened since Christmas.

Then again, Eddie had seemed very self-assured about his sexuality. So there was every bit the chance that Buck could have gone to Edmundo years ago and not be rejected, not ruin everything.

“So you’re not denying it?” she asked. “That there’s something? I mean, you’re living with him, and he’s taking you to a get-together for his family. When he hijacked that call those weeks ago, he talked about Chris like he was your son, too.” Buck hadn’t known that. He’d been thinking of Chris as his kid for years now - had straight up called the other Chris his kid - and Edmundo had referred to him as ‘our kid’ the day Buck helped mend his bedroom wall, but it still felt like a revelation to hear it. “And, not to be… Rude? Impolite? But he told us he was leaving the station, and you proceeded to run away and disappear for a month. None of that is very platonic, Buck.”

It wasn’t. 

It very much wasn’t.

“We’ve talked about it,” he confessed. “And decided we weren’t there, yet.”

“Yet?” Hen echoed.

“I ran away and disappeared for a month. I… We talked about it a while ago, and I wasn’t in a good place then. Not a good enough place, anyways,” he told her with a shrug. “Not if we wanted anything to work. And he… We’re both aware of what could be, and now we’re just waiting until we’re both in a good enough place to make it.”

Recently, he had begun wondering if perhaps they should revisit that discussion. The lines had become blurred, after all. So very blurred.

“That is incredibly mature of you, Buckaroo,” Hen said. Buck tried not to feel insulted by how impressed she sounded, because she had no idea that he’d had an extra four to five years of emotional growth under his belt.

Buck opened his mouth to reply, but it was lost as Edmundo’s voice came from the speakers above. It was so unexpected, so unusual for it to happen without the accompanying alarm, that everyone stopped what they were doing to turn their faces to the ceiling.

“Mayday, mayday, mayday. This is Firefighter Eddie Diaz on a department-wide channel. We have a fire at Metro Dispatch downtown.” Buck was already moving after the second “mayday”, leaping to his feet and practically throwing himself down the stairs for his turnout gear, his heart lodging itself near his tonsils. It took several moments for everyone else to break out of their stupor and follow his lead. “Point of origin appears to be on the second floor records room. All non-essential personnel begin evacuation procedures.”

“C’mon, let’s go!” Buck shouted at everyone else, climbing into the truck even as he was still tugging on his coat. Why the hell were they all taking so long?

“Repeat. This is Firefighter Eddie Diaz. Metro Dispatch is on fire.”

Dispatch was a mess. Someone - Josh - had decided that they needed to go paperless, and now there was a constant parade of people moving up and down the building with boxes of paperwork to digitize. Already, Eddie could tell that the day was going to be annoying, and he had only just gotten his coffee. 

“I have to say, I never thought, not in a million years, that I would love a superfood creamer made from coconut milk and marine algae,” Linda commented as he walked with her to her station on the floor., happily drinking her own coffee

“The algae’s where they get the calcium,” he told her. He was half-distracted with his phone, texting Buck to complain about the mess.

“You truly have become one of us,” she laughed.

“Yeah.” And maybe that came out a bit too bitter, because she glanced at him curiously.

“But maybe you’re not planning on being one of us forever?”

“The whole desk thing is still a little new to me,” he told her. “I’m used to being out there, riding the rig, slinging the hoses, working up a sweat.”

“Eddie, you don’t like working here,” Linda said, cutting to the quick. “Or at least, you don’t like the job they have you doing here. We can all tell. So why aren’t you out there, riding the rig?”

“I want to,” he admitted. “I’m just not sure if I can, anymore.”

Not when fear was still such a constant presence in his life. Even now, after weeks and months of flaying himself alive in Frank’s office. Somedays, it felt like something had broken within him, and he was not certain it could be fixed.

“When I was in my twenties,” Linda started, “My mother told me - repeatedly - that, ‘You can tie yourself in knots trying to find a career that suits you, but at the end of the day, God will tell you where you need to be in this world.’”

(“It’s like the universe is screaming at you, and you refuse to listen.”)

“Well, here’s hoping he gives me a shout soon.”

“You took my ambulance!?” May suddenly yelled. The entire floor fell silent, everyone turning to look at the young woman. She pushed herself away from her desk, standing and glaring at Claudette.

“Excuse me?”

“You redirected my ambulance.”

“That unit was closer to a higher priority emergency,” Claudette said, fully on her high horse. “A bigger casualty risk.”

“But I’d already told my caller that-” May tried to say, but the other dispatcher interrupted her.

“Did you get a unit there?” 

“Yes.”

“And was your victim alive when you disconnected?”

“That’s not the point,” May scoffed.

“But it is, sweetie,” Claudette said, and Eddie briefly wondered if she was suicidal, or merely that full of herself. “That is exactly the point. We’re not children here, competing for the best LEGOs to build our pretend skyscrapers with. We are a team.”

“Is that what we are?” May asked, walking across the floor in slow, measured steps, presumably so the other woman was within grappling range. “We’re a team? Why didn’t I think of that?”

“I don’t know. Youth? Inexperience? Indifference?” Claudette was full on mocking May now, both of them apparently oblivious to Sue having shown up behind them, looking very disappointed. “You tell me.”

“No.” May shook her head. “The rest of us are a team, but you’re not.”

“Sweetie-”

“I’m not your sweetie!” The younger dispatcher screamed, clearly reaching the end of her tether. Thankfully, just as Eddie was debating how awkward it would be to have to call first responders to Dispatch, Sue finally stepped in.

“Enough! Come with me.”

“Get your girl, Sue,” Claudette said, as if she wasn’t at least half the problem.

“Both of you,” the supervisor clarified. “Let’s go.”

“That was entertaining,” Eddie commented to Linda after Sue had herded the two women away to time out.

“You are awful,” she told him, sitting down at her station. Just before she put on her earpiece, she asked, “Who do you think would win in a fight?”

“May,” was his immediate answer. He was not going to bet against Athena Grant’s daughter, not in this lifetime. “Enjoy your superfood creamer.”

“Oh, I am.”

Leaving her to it, Eddie headed for the liaison’s office. Sitting down at his desk, he turned on his monitor, the bottom of which was lined with bright pink post-it notes. Each of them had an awful pun written in Buck’s sloppy scrawl - Why was the science teacher angry? He was a Mad Scientist. -  along with a smiley face of some sort. There was undoubtedly a new one waiting for him in the brown paper bag he stuck in the breakroom fridge that morning.

And on the topic of Buck…

May and Claudette got into it.

was there blood?

No blood shed.

Just yelling.

Sue stopped it before May could go for the throat.

They’re both in time out now.

probs 4 the bst

2 mny ppl 2 get away w it

Eddie spent the morning that way, texting Buck in between tweeting out safety info and answering the occasional phone call. And then, something happened. A power surge, making the lights flicker and his decade old computer shut down. Nothing he tried seemed to convince it to come back to life, so he pulled out the backup laptop he’d been issued.

“Hey.” Josh knocked on the doorframe, stepping inside. “Did you get it, too?”

“Yeah, power surge,” Eddie said, glancing at him and then back to the laptop.

“No, this alert.” The dispatcher held up his tablet.

“Fire suppression system,” Eddie read off. “Is that the sprinklers or the new CAS? It’s that waterless system.”

“Yeah, sucks all the oxygen out of a room. I met the guy who was installing it.”

“If you met him, you should go talk to him.” Instead of bothering me, he didn’t say, turning back to his laptop.

“Oh, I- I can’t,” Josh stammered, and Eddie turned to him again. “I’ve- I’ve got the floor, and Sue is dealing with, um…”

“Oh, I heard it.”

“Everyone heard.”

“Alright.” Eddie let out a sigh. Going downstairs to talk to the guy was not what he wanted to do, but he wanted to do his actual job even less. “I’m on it.”

“Great.” Josh sounded too relieved to not have to deal with the installation guy, which meant he had probably embarrassed himself in front of him.

Grabbing his radio from where it was charging, Eddie made for the elevators, replying to Buck’s texts as he went.

eddieeeeeeeee

All humor died when he stepped out of the elevator and into the hallway for the server room, spotting someone laying face down on the floor on the other side of the glass walls. When he got closer, he recognized them as Terry, the center’s IT guy. Eddie rushed for the entrance and slammed his badge against the scanner, only for it to beep angrily at him.

“Terry!?” he shouted, rattling the door to no avail. “Terry!”

The man did not stir.

Looking around for something to break in with, he spotted the fire extinguisher. He grabbed it off the wall and slammed the bottom of it into the glass, sending cracks spider webbing across part of its surface. And then again. On the third time, he stepped back and hurled it through, sending shards raining onto the ground. Using his radio, he cleared the frame of the sharp and jagged edges, and carefully stepped through.

Rolling Terry onto his back, he checked for a pulse and was relieved when he found it. He took two knuckles and rubbed at his sternum, until the man started stirring.

“Hey, there we go. Can you hear me?”

“System shorted out,” the IT guy said. Gasped, really. “Couldn’t make it to the door.”

Eddie was nodding alone when something caught his eye. It was smoke, rolling along from farther down the hallway. He hauled Terry to his feet and then carefully slung him over his shoulder, carrying him out of the server room. There was a stack of boxes, and he sat him down on it.

“Can you hold yourself up?” he asked.

“Yeah,” Terry nodded, panting.

“Okay. Be right back,” Eddie promised.

He followed the smoke, going down the hall and then around the corner. It was coming from beneath a door farther down, one of the records room. He made his way to it and used the back of his hand to feel the door, and then the handle. Deeming it safe enough, he opened it.

Ludicrously, his first thought when he saw it was, “Josh wanted to go paperless.”

On the other side of the room, everything was burning. The boxes of records that they had been trying to get through were going up in flames, plumes of gray smoke coiling and curling along the ceiling.

Closing the door, he headed back for Terry to get him out, pulling the fire alarm as he went past it. A couple steps later, he realized that nothing happened. No blaring alarms, no flashing lights. It was like he hadn’t done anything.

He turned back, tried the alarm again, only for the same (lack of) result.

Well, shit.

With an irritated sigh, he used his radio instead.

“Mayday, mayday, mayday. This is Firefighter Eddie Diaz on a department-wide channel. We have a fire at Metro Dispatch downtown,” he announced. “Point of origin appears to be on the second floor records room. All non-essential personnel begin evacuation procedures.”

He wasn't afraid, he belatedly realized. He hadn’t been thinking about it, had been acting on instinct and training, but he wasn’t afraid. The fear that he would have expected, the fear that had felt like his constant companion, the fear that had crippled him from going back to the job he loved, was not there.

He was not afraid.

“Repeat. This is Firefighter Eddie Diaz. Metro Dispatch is on fire.”

A Sound of Thunder

Chapter Notes

See the end of the chapter for notes

It seemed like it took forever for the trucks to reach the dispatch center, although Buck was also certain that they had never moved faster. Bobby was in just as much of a hurry to get there as he himself was, and for very similar reasons. Buck was viciously reminded of the time the center had been held hostage, although he was just thankful that he could actually do something this time.

When they finally made it, he was leaping out of the truck even before they were fully stopped. Edmundo was walking out of the entrance, half-carrying someone Buck only vaguely recognized. He practically ran to the other man, the others only slightly behind him.

“Records room on the second floor is fully involved. Fire could spread to the third floor, the main floor.” Edmundo informed them, fully professional and ignoring the way Buck was hovering just at his side. He let Hen take the man he helped carry out. “The clean agent system knocked him out for a second. He took in a lot of gas, there could be a lingering narcotic effect.”

Buck ignored Bobby ordering Hen to set up a triage area, and he barely noticed the 133 captain - not Mehta - loaning her own paramedics to assist. He focused, instead, on making sure Edmundo wasn’t hurt. He focused, instead, on soothing the panic that had gripped his heart since he heard the man’s voice calling for help.

“Did you breathe in any smoke?” he asked, probably getting too much into the man’s space. “Or any of that clean agent stuff you were talking about?”

“I’m fine, Buck.”

“Are you actually fine or are you just saying you’re fine?”

“I’m actually fine. Promise.”

Edmundo did seem fine. Buck couldn’t see anything wrong, and he actually looked somehow better than he had before. More settled, maybe. It was something with his posture, like his back was a bit straighter and his head was a touch higher. Or perhaps it had just been awhile since Buck had seen the man under the influence of proper adrenaline and in full work mode.

But just because Edmundo looked, and was saying that he was fine…

“If you’re lying, I will tell Chris,” he threatened.

“Are you blackmailing me with our kid, Buckley?” Edmundo asked, trying to sound insulted, though the effect was entirely undone by the way he was grinning.

“I think it’s actually-”

“Buck, Lucy.” Bobby saying his name had him twisting around to focus on the captain. Right, there was a building that was currently burning down. “The two of you suit up and head inside, hit the fire at the source.”

Buck and Lucy nodded, though he still glanced back at Edmundo. There was a slight scowl on his face, and though Buck wanted nothing more than to stay there and make that go away, he couldn’t. Because he had to do his job, and that now meant leaving Edmundo behind.

Something bitter crept into his thoughts at that realization, and he did his best to quash it, before it got out of hand.

The two of them - he and Lucy, because she was his partner at the moment, not Edmundo - hurried to the trucks and the equipment stored within. They would need O2 tanks and masks, and hopefully someone had gotten around to hooking up the hoses while he was busy fretting over his friend/partner/something.

“So, that’s Diaz?” Lucy commented, apparently trying to make small talk.

“That’s Eddie,” he confirmed.

“I think I can make a guess why you and that reporter didn’t work out.”

Buck paused in the process of shrugging on the oxygen tank to fix her with a look.

“Taylor and I didn’t work because Taylor and I did not work,” he informed her. That had been the entire point of his little speech to the reporter, after all. It wasn’t him, it wasn’t her, it was very much them. Even if he’d had absolutely no feelings for anyone else, the relationship he’d found himself in with Taylor Kelly was simply too toxic, and he had realized that he deserved better.

Far too much of it involved simply ignoring all the things she did that he couldn’t live with, in the hopes that one day he would learn how to. Far too much of it involved him desperately clinging to someone, anyone, that seemed willing to stay. Far too much of it involved him lying to himself, and to her, and to everyone around them.

But yes, his absolutely undeniable love for someone else had certainly not helped.

Before Lucy could say anything else to that, the man in question was at his side, with Bobby a moment behind. Edmundo started pulling on the spare turnout gear they kept on the truck. His own gear was… Somewhere. Buck wasn’t sure, hadn’t actually seen it since their shift on Christmas Eve. It was either tucked away somewhere at the station, or buried deep at the Diaz residence.

(The third option, that the coat with DIAZ emblazoned upon it had been tossed out and thrown away, was simply too awful to contemplate.)

“Why is Cap going in?” Buck asked, as the captain was grabbing his own oxygen tank and slinging it over his back.

“May’s still up there,” Edmundo answered, shrugging into the spare coat.

Oh.

Oh, fuck.

“Feels like I’m missing something,” Lucy said, and Buck realized that she really was an outsider to their little group, no matter how long she’d been with them. Even more so than Ravi, whom Buck was certain put in actual effort to stay out of everything, like the wise young man he was.

Shit, did Edmundo even know who she was?

Oh, shit, he didn’t know about the drunken kiss.

“Family history,” was his cryptic answer to Lucy’s confusion, and Buck wondered if the sharpness of Edmundo’s tone was due to May being in danger or if it had anything to do with that scowl from earlier. “Let’s go.”

“Donato, you’re with me,” Bobby instructed as they headed into the burning building. “Eddie, you’re with Buck.”

Buck glanced at his partner and grinned.

Despite the circumstances, Eddie was grinning back.

Something was off, and Ichabod wasn’t quite sure what it was.

It seemed like forever - it had been forever, but also no time at all, because time did not properly exist in this space - since the mortal had finally woken up and driven off in that metal death trap of his. The other timeline was entirely out of his reach now, and so was the original. Buck’s want had finally been fulfilled, or was in the process of being fulfilled.

A lot of it had been up to Buck himself, and in that there had been something of a minor snag. It seemed things had been severely broken by the mortal’s month-long temporal displacement, far more than anticipated, and the fractured little man had allowed himself to almost give up. He had wallowed in his misery and failures, instead of actually putting forth any effort into fixing things.

Wallowing was boring. There was nothing entertaining in watching someone destroy their liver in the loneliness of their living space.

So, Ichabod had coerced his silent companion to go bug him, just a little. An attempt to nudge him into actually getting off his ass and doing something. Things had not yet been fixed, things had barely been on the way to being fixed, and it was giving the entity an awful headache.

Ichabod hadn’t even been aware he was capable of having a headache, before Evan Buckley stumbled into his domain.

Of course, a morose Buck was a stubborn Buck, and so he still refused to actually do something to make things better. But at least his irritation with the butterfly’s presence had been entertaining enough, and his spat with his sister soon after was even more so. Of course, that hadn’t lasted.

At least, not until that timeline’s Eddie Diaz had collapsed under the weight of his own trauma. Suddenly, Buck was very invested in the processes of mending and healing. After that, the couple fell into a pattern of domesticity that was… 

Well, it was only slightly less boring than the wallowing, in Ichabod’s opinion.

It was just as bad in the other timeline. Yes, there had been that period after Christmas, where the Buck that remained had been without years of memory. Ichabod wasn’t certain if it had been a simple consequence of the trauma involved in splitting a consciousness, or if the butterfly had done it on purpose. 

He hoped it was the latter.

But after that Buck had regained his memories, Ichabod had been stuck with a pair of timelines that were equally boring, in their own ways. At least, up until this fire.

Ichabod was watching them both, splitting his attention between the two, and he could not help but think that something was off. They were not identical events. In fact, in the one, the first responders had arrived much later than the other, and the fire was proving how much weight a mere handful of minutes could have.

(He knew someone had already died in that one, smothered to death by a safety measure, and he idly hoped more followed suit. Those that were left behind tended to be very entertaining, after all.)

But there was something… Not quite right. Like something was missing. Like hearing someone play a piece of music, one that was oh-so-familiar, and realizing that there was a note missing. Just a single note, easily missed and yet still so very jarring.

It did not quite remind him of the times Buck found the bomb beneath the ladder truck and delayed - not prevented - his leg injury. This was not a thing carved in stone, waiting in the wings of destiny for its inevitable moment. No, this was somewhat like Buck’s skull fracture, a seemingly inconsequential change that suddenly had dire consequences for someone else.

Ichabod could not think of a single thing Buck might have altered to cause this, not something that could have affected both timelines. The entity was a voyeur, he was not omniscient. He was all-seeing, if he put in the effort, but he was not all-knowing.

But he did know that it was going to be entertaining, when the consequences played themselves out.

For him, at least.

It was like the universe, once tilted and off-kilter, had righted itself. It was not Lucy or Ravi at Buck’s side as he ran into the burning building, but Eddie. It was Eddie that helped him haul a hose up two flights of stairs, and it was Eddie that helped him direct the rushing water into the heart of the flames.

For so long after his return, Buck had delayed going back to work, because he had known how awful it would be to try and do it without his partner. He had already lived it once, right after his encounter with Ichabod, and the experience was a special kind of agony. It had been a haunting absence, an open wound that followed him around the station.

Going through it the second time was not any better, and it was made substantially worse by Bobby trying to plug the hole with a near total stranger.

But now! Now, Eddie was at his side, where he was supposed to be. Now, everything was as it should have been this entire time.

There were conflicting emotions within Buck’s chest. Worry and fear for May’s safety, and utter exultation and joy that he was putting out a fire with Eddie. The latter, he was ashamed to admit, was winning.

They were in the process of putting out the fire in the second floor records room, when the voice of the 133’s captain came in over their radios.

“This is Captain Shore. I need two firefighters for search and rescue,” she said. “Worker named Carson Hayes, last seen on level Two, could still be in the building.”

Eddie stepped away from the hose and replied,

“This is Diaz. Buckley and I are on Two. We swept the whole floor.”

“This is dispatcher Russo,” Josh broke in. “I think I know where he is.”

“Go ahead, Josh.”
