“He told me he was going to the basement to connect power to the system,” the dispatcher said. “It’s probably the electrical vault. I can meet you on Two and take you down there.”

“Negative, we got this,” Eddie said, shooting him down. “Buck!”

It was almost instinct for Buck to answer his call, handing the hose off to a pair of firefighters from the 133 and moving to be at his side.

“But I’m right by the fire stairs,” Josh protested. “I could be down there right away.

“Josh, you’re a dispatcher, not a firefighter,” Eddie told him. “Today, you’re a guest in this house.”

“You’ve been waiting actual months to say that, haven’t you?” Buck asked, grinning as he followed him to the stairs.

“Maybe,” the other man admitted with a shrug.

“I love you,” he blurted out, and Eddie’s steps stuttered a little as he whipped his head to look at him.

“Buck! Time and place,” he scolded, but he was smiling, too.

Metro Dispatch was not a large building, and so it was not a long journey to reach the basement from where they were, even in the chaos of disaster. Before long, Eddie was leading them along the concrete halls, their flashlights cutting through the darkness and the smoke that had managed to follow its own path of least resistance downwards.

“This way, the electrical vault’s this way,” Eddie directed, and Buck followed because he knew his partner would not lead him astray. Soon, they reached a door that read “Electrical and Utility,” which Eddie opened up without hesitation.

Their light landed on a streak of blood on the floor. It led to the man they were looking for, propped up against a wall.

“Carson?” Eddie asked, making sure that this wasn’t simply an unaccounted person who just happened to be where they were looking.

“Yeah,” the man grunted out, obviously tired and in pain. “How’d you know?”

“Josh told us you were down here,” Buck answered, digging everything they’d need out of the medical bag they had grabbed on the way down.

“I’m glad he remembered.”

“Let me guess,” Eddie said, and Buck glanced up from slapping on a blood pressure cuff to see him shining his light at something on or near the ceiling. “Arc blast?”

“Yeah. The suppression blanket took the hit, but I got too close,” Carson told them. “Crawled over here for my phone.”

“Shitty signal down here, I bet,” Buck commented, frowning at what the cuff was telling him.

“My leg really hurts,” the man complained, grimacing from the pain.

“BP is One-Thirty over Eighty,” he told Eddie, who was already getting a syringe ready.

“Little high, we’re gonna give you something for the pain,” the former medic told Carson, handing Buck the syringe. “It should kick in soon. I’m also gonna need to cut through this pant leg.”

“Do whatever,” Carson said. “I tried to yell for help, nobody heard.”

“They were probably distracted by the building being on fire,” Buck said, sliding the needle in.

“Woops.”

“Don’t be taking the blame for this. This is a very old building, with I’m sure very old wiring.”

“Yeah, sure, wh-” Carson cut himself off with a shocked yelp as he glanced down at his leg, which Eddie had just uncovered, to reveal part of a leg bone sticking out. “Why did I tell you to do that!?”

“It looks bad, I know,” Eddie admitted. “But there isn’t a lot of blood. That’s a good thing.”

“Isn’t that always a good thing?”

“You got me there. How’s that morphine?”

“It’s making that sight slightly less horrific.”

“Okay, we’re gonna splint this,” Eddie told him, wrapping gauze around the leg as Buck got everything ready. “And then we’ll get you out of here.”

“Is this fixable, or should I start looking up peg-legs?” Carson asked.

“Hey, I had an entire fire truck land on my leg,” Buck told him, patting said leg for emphasis. “Look at it now.”

“That sounds horrible.”

“It was!” he cheerfully replied as Eddie put the splint on. “But the blood clots afterwards were worse. I had one reach my lung, and so I coughed up blood at my own Welcome Back party, and then they put me on these blood thinners that-”

“Buck,” Eddie cut in, shaking his head at him with a desperate Please Shut Up look to his eyes.

“You two should take the act on the road,” Carson quipped. “Be as popular as Abbott and Castello.”

“Who?” Buck asked.

“He’s on first,” was the reply he got. It sounded automatic, but it didn’t actually tell him anything, and so he stared blankly at the man. The man, in turn, turned wide eyes on Eddie. “Wait, was he not-? Does he not…?”

“He does not,” Eddie confirmed with a solemn shake of his head.

“That’s worse than my bone sticking out of my leg.”

“Why?” Buck asked.

“Left fielder,” was Carson’s completely unrelated answer, sounding tired and pained and also amused. And that would have been better than simply tired and pained, except it was amusement at Buck’s expense.

“What?’

“Second base.”

“Sir, do you know what day it is?” Buck asked, because this man had clearly suffered a head injury and they had very obviously missed it.

“He doesn’t have a concussion,” Eddie told him.

“How do you know?”

“Because.”

“Center fielder,” Carson chimed in, and Buck gestured frantically at him.

“He’s spouting off baseball positions, Eddie. But we weren’t talking about baseball!?”

“I think I need some more morphine,” their patient said. “Because this hurts more than anything else. Ever.”

“Try living with him,” Eddie told him, which, rude.

“You live with him?” Carson sounded very aghast, which, ruder. “And you haven’t shown him?”

“Tomorrow.”

“That’s the pitcher.”

“Eddie!”

“Let’s just get him out of here,” the medic said, having gotten the splint finished, because he was very good at what he did. Buck nodded, if still very worried, because this man clearly had some sort of traumatic brain injury and his partner was not nearly as concerned as he should be.

Was it the clean agent? Had Eddie inhaled some of it? Was it affecting him and they just hadn’t noticed it until now?

Those questions and more worried Buck as the two of them carried the man up the stairs and out of the building, taking him to triage. Hen met them as they were lowering him onto a gurney.

“Compound fracture,” Eddie told her.

“And maybe a concussion, though Eddie doesn’t think so,” Buck added.

“Why?” Hen asked.

“Left fielder,” Carson chimed in, right on cue, and the paramedic rolled her eyes.

“Oh,” she said, and only that.

“I’m gonna show him,” Eddie said, sounding like he was trying to reassure her of something, which only served to confuse Buck more. “Tomorrow.”

“Pitcher,” Carson said, except this time it was in a chorus with Hen.

“W- What?”

“He’s on second,” Eddie said. Buck hoped the look he gave him properly expressed all the confusion and betrayal he was feeling in that moment, because what the hell?

“Buck, sweetie,” Hen started, and it already sounded very patronizing. “There were things before you were born, and maybe you should start looking into them.”

“I know that! I have been!” Buck protested. “We watched all of the Star Wars movies with Chris, and that was on Chimney’s list.”

“Can I have more morphine, please?” Carson asked- No, begged the paramedic. “Because this talk hurts me.”

“There isn’t enough morphine in the world to numb you to Buckaroo,” Hen told him, and that was just rude. Very, very rude.

Buck let out a huff and stomped away from them and towards the trucks. He heard the paramedic chuckle, and then Eddie’s footsteps trailing behind him. Neither of them said anything until they made it to the 118’s engine, and Buck took a seat on its bumper.

“I feel ganged up on,” he complained.

“Now you know how I feel whenever you and Chris do it to me,” Eddie retorted.

“Y’know, just for that, I think your next lunch is going to be egg salad.” That didn’t get the amused snort he was looking for. Instead, it made the other man stiffen up and then slump against the engine. “Eds?”

“Your puns” He sounded so very sad, and Buck desperately wanted to fix whatever was causing it. “I had them all stuck to my monitor. Up there.”

He pointed up at the third floor, which had smoke billowing out of the windows, which were all alight with an orange glow.

“You kept them?” Buck asked, unusually touched by the notion. They were just supposed to be cheesy little jokes to make the other man feel better.

“Of course I did,” Eddie said, like it was simple and obvious. “I didn’t get to read today’s pun, though.”

“Oh! Uh…” He racked his brain, trying to remember it. “How do you get straight A’s?”

“How?”

“With a ruler.”

“Jesus, Buck.” Eddie managed to sound horrified, even as he couldn’t help but chuckle a bit. “That’s awful.”

“Made you laugh, though.”

“It did.”

They fell into silence for a few moments. It was a comfortable silence, interrupted occasionally by the crackle of their radios. Eventually, Eddie broke it.

“I wasn’t afraid,” he said, and then clarified, “When I opened the door to the records room, when I saw the fire. I wasn’t afraid.”

“Eds…” Buck wasn’t quite certain what to say, so he reached out and took his hand instead.

“I wasn’t afraid,” Eddie repeated, squeezing his hand and shining a smile down on him. “I’ve been so afraid. Of everything, it felt like. I was afraid I wouldn’t be able to come back because I’d be too afraid to be able to. I was afraid that I’d be afraid.”

“There’s no shame in that,” he told him, because he needed him to know that.

“There isn’t,” the other man agreed. “But Buck, I wasn’t afraid.”

It took a moment, and he would blame it on the lingering confusion and the exhaustion of the day for why he was so slow on the uptake. Eventually, with a shot of joy that felt like lightning, it clicked.

“You weren’t afraid,” he echoed, grinning widely. “You weren’t afraid. You could do the job.”

“I could do the-”

He was cut off as something happened in the burning building they had been in. There was a rumble, and a large plume of cloud and embers exploded from the roof. Almost immediately, frantic chatter came over the radio.

“This is Captain Shore. We have a major structural collapse. All teams evacuate the building and report in.”

“This is Morrison, One Thirty-three, we’re clear.”

“Quintana, One Eighteen, we’re in the parking garage. We’re clear.”

“Mitchell, One Eighteen. We’re clear of the exit on the Baker side.”

And then, Lucy’s voice, her worry a spear to their ears,

“Mayday, mayday, mayday. Captain Nash is down,” she said, and Buck and Eddie looked at each other. In unison, they leapt into movement, grabbing their helmets and rushing back in. “I need firefighters and equipment on the third floor now.”

When they got there - Eddie making a quick stop to grab a halligan - it was to find Lucy trying to move the rubble on her own. And there was a lot of it, huge blocks of the ceiling resting on top of each other. Terror filled Buck’s heart, but it was accompanied by anger, because beneath all that was Bobby and May and no. No. He was not going to lose two people that were as much family to him as Maddie. Not now, not when things had just started to mend between them, not when things seemed to be looking up.

Neither of them hesitated before jumping into the mess, working together to pull the large blocks out of the way. They were quickly joined by Hen and Chimney, and between the five of them, it became an assembly line. They cleared one section, and then the next, and then the next, until-

“Here!” May’s voice.

“Here! We’re down here!” Bobby’s voice.

They followed the sound, clearing more and more blocks of what used to be the building’s roof, until they uncovered the sight of Bobby huddled protectively over May, shielding her with his own body. Buck still did not breathe easy until they were both out and he could see them for himself, until Hen had checked them over and deemed them - relatively - fine.

Though, of course, his joy at finding them both alive and mostly well soured soon after they escorted/carried them to the triage set up in the parking garage. Because for some reason, he hadn’t thought about who’d be sent to cover the fire. 

He and Taylor Kelly made eye contact for one brief moment, before her gaze slid to his left, to Eddie. And then, she almost pointedly turned and walked away, her cameraman following.

“Awkward,” Eddie commented, and Buck merely grunted in agreement, because what else was there to say?

“God has spoken,” a nearby dispatcher said. She was sitting on one of the gurneys, and had an oxygen mask in hand. Buck had no clue who she was, nor what she was talking about, but Eddie seemed to. He smiled and nodded, and Buck would love to know what that meant, but something behind her caught his attention.

It was dancing under the lights beyond the triage area, just as it had that night - those nights - so very long ago. It was big, and it was blue, and the last time Buck had seen it, it was just irritating. Now, however…

Now it was making his blood thrum with fear and anxiety.

Now, while Eddie might not have been afraid, Buck was absolutely terrified.

“What is it, Buck?” Eddie asked, and followed his line of sight. “Oh. That butterfly is very blue.”

“Yeah.”

“You okay?”

“Yeah,” he said again, when what he really meant was, No. No, he was not.

His heartbeat sounded like thunder in his ears.

Chapter End Notes

Enjoy a classic comedy routine here.

Knowing What You Know

Something about the butterfly had spooked Buck, and Eddie could not figure out what it could have been. It was just a butterfly. A very pretty and very blue butterfly, but a butterfly all the same. Something about it, though, had deeply unsettled the other man.

As a group, they - he and Buck and the others - followed the ambulance carrying Bobby to the hospital, to get checked over. The others lingered around the waiting room, awaiting an update on their captain, but Eddie was focused on Buck. Buck, whose eyes kept bouncing around like he was checking for threats. Buck, who flinched at every new sound and every sudden movement.

Buck, who was reminding Eddie far too much of how he himself had been acting every time he heard a noise outside his house while trying to sleep.

“So you’re Diaz, right? Eddie?”

The voice belonged to the new firefighter at the 118. He was pretty sure Bobby had called her Lucy, but he wasn’t certain. Honestly, Eddie had been distracted by the sheer hatred he had felt for her, the moment the captain had paired them up. It was irrational and pointless, because of course Bobby would be partnering Buck up with someone else after Eddie left the station.

That didn’t mean he liked or accepted it, though.

“That’s me,” Eddie confirmed with a nod. “Lucy, right?”

“That’s me,” she said. “Any word about Cap?”

“Not yet,” he told her.

“Hey, uh, where’s Chimney?” Buck asked.

“Stayed behind,” she said. “I think he was talking to his brother?”

“Oh shit, Albert was there? I know he’s at the One Thirty-three, but I didn’t see him, though.”

“You were distracted by this one,” Lucy said, pointing at Eddie. “So is he the reason you freaked out after-?”

“Oh thank god, Hen,” Buck interrupted, looking even more panicked than before. Eddie didn’t know what Lucy had been about to say, and wasn’t sure he wanted to know, judging from the way she was smirking. Actually, that was a lie; he really, really wanted to know. “So, uh, he’s good, right? Bobby, I mean?”

“He had some pain in his leg on the way in,” the paramedic said when she joined their little huddle. “Might be a pinched nerve.”

“He had a roof fall on him,” Eddie said. “He’s lucky that’s all that it is.”

“I think a lot of people got lucky today,” Buck commented, although there was something off about his tone, something off about the expression on his face. Behind him, Eddie watched as more paramedics from the fire finally arrived, wheeling in Claudette, who was sitting up and talking.

They talked a bit longer, the four of them. Eddie thought, rather foolishly, that Buck was calming down from whatever had him spooked. He seemed to be, at least, no longer looking for invisible threats as he allowed himself to be drawn into the conversation.

And then, from somewhere deeper within the hospital, there was a loud slam. A door being thrown open and hitting the wall, Eddie thought. It was loud and sudden enough that it had him jumping, that it had Hen and Lucy look curiously in the direction it came from. He turned back to Buck, and froze midword.

The other man was using the nearby column to prop himself up, a hand clutching at his chest. His breaths were coming in short and shallow gasps, and his eyes were wide with panic. Eddie knew these symptoms, far too well and far too intimately. When he wrapped an arm around his back, the fingers of Buck’s other hand dug into his shirt.

Thankfully, Hen and Lucy were still distracted, which meant they didn’t notice as he guided Buck out the ER doors and into the warm evening. A short walk away was a bench, and he sat him down and knelt in front of him.

“What are five things you can see?” Eddie asked.

“W- What?” Buck gasped, still keeping a tight but shaky grip on him. “Eds, I don’t-”

“What are five things you can see?” he repeated. “C’mon, list them off.”

“Uh, uh… You. The ambulance over there. The uh, the moon. A streetlamp. The ER sign.”

“Four things you can feel?”

“The wind. This bench. Uh, a pebble under my foot. Your shirt.”

“Three things you can hear?”

“Cars. A bird somewhere. You, again.”

It was helping. Buck was no longer gasping for breath and he was no longer grasping at his own chest. He hadn’t let go of Eddie yet, though.

“Two things you can smell?”

“Smoke,” he answered. “And burnt rubber.”

“And something you can taste?”

“Blood.” The answer was quiet, though no less concerning for it. Eddie frowned, taking Buck’s chin in gentle hands to look at him. “I uh, I think I bit my cheek.”

“We’ll get you some water when we go back inside,” Eddie told him. “How are you feeling?”

“Better,” the other man answered. Buck did look better, though Eddie couldn’t decide if he was actually paler than he shold be or if it was the (lack of) lighting where they were. “I think I understand why you thought it was a heart attack, Jesus.”

“Yeah, panic attacks aren’t fun,” he said. “Buck, what’s going on? Something about that butterfly had you spooked, and I don’t understand. It was just a butterfly.”

“That thing,” Buck snarled, “is not just a fucking butterfly.”

“What is it, then?”

“I- I don’t know, really,” he confessed, leaning forward to rest his head on Eddie’s shoulder. “Something like Ichabod, maybe? Something worse? I don’t know.”

“What was it doing there?” Eddie asked, pushing aside the anxiety brought on by how Something had been lurking right beside them, and no one except Buck had known. That horror could wait until the current crisis was resolved.

“I don’t know,” Buck repeated. “I don’t know, I don’t know, I don’t know, I- I don’t want to die again.”

Eddie pushed Buck up and away, grabbing his shoulders and shaking him slightly.

“What the fuck, Buck?” he demanded. “What the fuck does that mean, ‘again?’”

“I don’t want to talk about it-”

“Too fucking bad!” Eddie snapped.

“Here! I don’t want to talk about it here,” Buck hurried to finish. And okay, that was fair. Eddie glanced around the completely open area they were in, glaring at someone in scrubs who was staring at them until they blanched and turned away.

“Fine,” he ground out, giving in. “But we are talking about it.”

“And we will,” Buck promised. “At home. Just… Just not here. Please.”

They lingered outside for a bit longer, until Hen came out looking for them. The doctors were taking Bobby for an MRI, just to make sure, and they needed to get the vehicles back to the station for the next shift. They left after Buck badgered Athena into keeping them updated, which she was obviously going to do anyway.

Eddie found himself awkwardly waiting around the station while Buck took a shower and changed. He would have done the same, but he didn’t have clothes to change into. So instead, he busied himself with stashing the spare gear he’d borrowed back where it needed to go on the truck and tried not to let his eyes stray to the turnout lockers that no longer had his name. It was weird being at the station again after so long away, because nothing had changed, but it felt so different.

Buck didn’t take long to get washed and changed, and before long he was coming out of the lockers rooms with his work bag slung over his shoulder. He looked much more at ease than he had at the hospital, and Eddie wasn’t certain if the shower was the reason or if he just felt so much safer at the station than he did anywhere else. 

“Do you want to get your truck?” he asked, spinning his keys on his finger.

“We can get it after we drop off Chris tomorrow,” Eddie answered. Buck nodded and they waved goodbye to the others, before climbing into the jeep.

The ride home was silent barring the radio and the traffic. They didn’t talk, with Eddie spending the twenty-minutes it took to get home glancing at Buck, and with Buck getting increasingly more jittery. 

Pepa greeted them when they arrived, staying without complaint to watch Chris for him.

“Chris saw it on the news,” she told him as Buck continued on to the kitchen, leaving his shoes by the door and his bag by the couch. “He was worried, until he saw that Buck was there with you.”

“I’ll go check on him, then,” Eddie said, leaning down so she could kiss his cheek before she left. He waited by the door until he saw her get into her car and drive away before he went down the hall. Chris was on his Switch when he poked his head in, and the kid smiled happily at the sight of him. “Hey, kiddo.”

“Hi dad. Your work was on fire.”

“It was,” he said with a nod. “I helped put it out.”

“Does that mean you want to be a firefighter again?” Chris asked. Eddie could have corrected him, could have said he never stopped being a firefighter, but he didn’t have it in him. He hadn’t felt like a firefighter in months, cooped up in that little office sending out tweets about road closures.

“Are you okay with that?” he asked instead.

“Yeah. I never wanted you to stop,” his son answered. “You’re not scared any more?”

“I’m less scared now, I think,” Eddie told him truthfully. “Pepa made you dinner already?”

“Yes, dad.”

After a quick reminder that Chris had to be getting to bed soon, Eddie left him to it. There was a conversation he needed to have with Buck, who hadn’t come out of the kitchen in the time he spent talking to Pepa and Chris. When he went in there looking for him, he found the other man standing by the sink, a mostly full glass of water clutched in his hand and his eyes fixed on the floor in front of the fridge.

“Chris okay?” he croaked.

“Pepa said he was worried, until he realized you were there with me,” Eddie answered, pulling out a chair and sitting at the table. Hearing something like that usually had Buck smiling, but this time it came out as more of a grimace. “So.”

“So,” Buck echoed.

“What the fuck did you mean, you don’t want to die?” Eddie asked. “Again?”

“The other week, you asked me what my nightmares were about.”

“They’re about you dying?”

“Some of them.” Buck sat the glass down on the counter and finally tore his eyes away from whatever had him so fascinated with the space he’d been focused on, but he still didn’t look at Eddie. “Ichabod said it was because I tried changing something that shouldn’t have been changed. Something that was carved in stone, instead of written in ink. I tried changing something, and it happened anyway, but worse.”

Eddie didn’t say anything, didn’t know what he could possibly say. This was far outside of his wheelhouse. Luckily, Buck was very good at filling in silence on his own.

“I found the bomb under the truck,” he said, and Eddie didn’t have to ask what bomb he was referring to. Because of course. If Buck could go back and redo everything, of course he’d try and change that. “I told Chimney, he called Athena and the bomb squad, and we all waited outside while they took care of it. And I saw that butterfly. That was the first time I’d seen it, just… It looked like it was dancing under the streetlight.

“And I thought everything was going to be okay after that, y’know? My leg was fine. I’d saved Shannon, and I stopped Do-” He caught himself and swallowed back the words, and Eddie desperately wanted to know what could have been worse than what they were talking about now. “I thought everything was going to be fine, but… But a couple weeks went by, and Bobby was still suspended. They were going to make him resign, because of St. Paul. And we - the other Eddie and I - went hiking, and I… We stopped to rest, on a part of the path that’s on a cliff. And I went to lean against a tree, and it gave.”

Buck paused to take a drink of his water, and Eddie both desperately did not want to hear the rest, and desperately needed to know what his partner had gone through.

“I remember him, the other Eddie, screaming my name. I remember falling so far, Eds. I hit the ground, and then the tree landed on me, on my leg,” Buck continued, hand seeming to go to his left leg of its own accord. “And then- There was a boulder, I think, or- Or something, that came loose and fell. I remember looking up to see it coming down right on top of me, and I closed my eyes and the next time I opened them I was back at the crossroads with Ichabod.

“And he made a pun, before sending me back to the morning of the bombing. Some shitty pun, because it was funny to him. Every single time it happened, he made a pun.”

“Every single time?” Eddie asked, feeling sick. “How many times, Buck?”

“Seven,” he answered, and Eddie was very glad he was already sitting down. “It took me seven times to learn my lesson. I’d go in, I’d find the bomb, we’d wait outside while bomb squad took care of it. Every single time, that butterfly was there. Every single time, Bobby was forced to resign instead of coming back. Every single time something would happen to my leg and I would… I drowned once. There was a truck that drove into a river, and we- Me and the other Eddie, we were trying to free the driver when it shifted and landed on my leg, and I watched him trying to move it but… And then, another time, there was a three-alarm fire. The floor gave way beneath me, and something landed on my leg again, and I think the smoke got me first, but I can’t remember.”

“Jesus Christ,” Eddie whispered.

“The longest I made it, I think, was the end of June? Start of July? There was… I dunno, something with wires and weights. I just remember something hitting my leg, and then it wasn’t there any more,” Buck said, voice having long gone devoid of emotion. “And then Ichabod told me that it might have been because I kept changing something that shouldn’t have been changed. So, after that time, I didn’t tell anyone about the bomb. I kept my mouth shut, and then the truck crushed my leg and Bobby got his job back.”

Suddenly, Eddie was entirely understanding of why Buck was drinking so much. Actually, he wanted to know how Buck spent those weeks prior to that disaster of a night not spending every moment absolutely shitfaced. Because that was all Eddie wanted to do now, and he was just hearing about it. But sadly, there wasn’t a drop of alcohol left in the house anymore, and they were left to handle this completely sober.

“How are you functioning?” he blurted out, and it at least got a laugh from the other man.

“Time and repression, I think,” Buck answered. “I don’t actually remember, y’know. Dying.”

“Just everything leading up to it. Jesus Christ.” Eddie rubbed his face, feeling more exhausted than he ever had before. This, at least, answered his lingering questions from Buck’s abridged story to Christopher. Some of them, at least. Maybe most of them. Although, there was maybe a new question or two.

He decided he didn’t want to know the answers to them, not right then.

“So, seeing the butterfly tonight freaked you out, because every time you saw it before, it meant you changed something and you were going to die?”

“Well, not… Not every time, I guess,” Buck hedged. Hope! There was hope! “It was there at the crossroads, that last night. It did something that let me come back here but stay over there, too. And it spent a couple days bugging me at the loft, before you…”

“Wait.” Eddie needed clarification here. “This butterfly, which is not a butterfly, was at your loft. Bothering you?”

“It wouldn’t go away,” the other man groused, crossing his arms and pouting. “I just wanted to be left alone and drink, and the stupid thing kept fluttering around me. I tried to smash it and it just ended up flying into my hair.”

Eddie could imagine it easily. Buck, drunk and probably pouting, with the giant blue butterfly nestled in his curls. It was such an absurd image that he started giggling at it. Buck glared at him for a moment or two before he, too, started giggling.

“Okay. Okay,” Eddie tried, getting himself under control. “So, so. So maybe this doesn’t mean what you’re thinking it means. It could have just been there.”

“It was doing the exact same as it did all the other times,” Buck counted.

“But you haven’t changed anything. I mean, this is your first time living through these last few months, right?” He asked, and the other nodded. “Then you couldn’t possibly have done anything to change anything.”

“You don’t know that.”

“Neither of us know anything. I certainly don’t know what to do about everything you’ve told me tonight.”

“Get drunk and forget?” Buck suggested, and Eddie rolled his eyes.

“No.” He stood and stretched, grimacing when he got a whiff of himself. “Okay, I am overdue for a shower, because I still smell like a burning building. And neither of us have had dinner yet.”

“I’ll fix us something while you wash off.”

Eddie nodded and started to go. Before he did, he rounded the table and dragged the other man in a hug.

“It’s going to be okay,” he promised, even though he couldn’t actually be sure of that.

“Go bathe,” Buck ordered, pushing him away and scrunching up his nose. “You smell like sweat and smoke, and you are not bringing that to bed with you.”

“It’s my bed,” Eddie protested, but allowed himself to be shoved out of the kitchen anyway.

It Perches in the Soul

Chapter Notes

See the end of the chapter for notes

“I don’t understand,” Eddie stated, leaning against the frame of the bathroom door. Buck glanced at his reflection in the mirror, raising an eyebrow in question since he was too busy brushing his teeth to actually ask what he meant. “How you can go into work and act like everything’s fine. I mean, you-” He stopped and glanced down the hall at Chris’ closed door, behind which the boy should be getting ready for school. “You said you died in a burning building. That you weren’t sure if the smoke got you first, or…”

Buck let out a sigh, dropping his gaze and spitting into the sink.

“I passed out from smoke inhalation, I know that much,” he told him as he rinsed his toothbrush. He didn’t tell him that he could remember the heat of the flames as they reached his face before he lost consciousness. “Look, I really don’t know what to tell you. I don’t really remember dying, and every time I’d wake up at the crossroads and then back at my apartment afterwards. I guess it was easy to just… Act like it was a dream, like it didn’t really happen.”

“But it did happen,” Eddie said as Buck switched the toothbrush for a comb and a tub of hair gel. “Or at least, you remember it happening. I guess I just don’t get how you were able to get up afterwards and go do a job that involves you running into a burning building.”

“I guess I just sucked it up and moved on,” Buck said with a shrug. In the mirror, he saw the other man’s face twist in guilt or shame or agony or all of that at the same time. He realized immediately exactly what moment Eddie just had a flashback to. “Don’t. That was just really bad word choice on my part, it had nothing to do with you. There was literally nothing else I could do, but to… To keep swimming. At no point could I go to someone and say, ‘hey I’ve died eight times now, can I go have a kiki?’ Not and have them believe me, at least. I mean, Bobby- The other Bobby and Athena, they thought I had lost it when I told them why I knew about the tsunami ahead of time.”

He focused on taming his curls into something that made him look less like a child playing dress-up. Eddie, meanwhile, had fallen silent. In the mirror, Buck could see him staring, and his expression now… The guilt over long-ago words was gone, and instead it was like the other man was trying to look through him. Like he was trying to peel Buck apart with his eyes and see what lay beneath everything.

“What?” he demanded, shifting under his gaze.

“Eight,” Eddie said.

“What?” Buck repeated, totally lost now.

“You said eight, just then. You said you died eight times,” he explained. And, okay, Buck did remember saying that. But he didn’t know why that had the other man looking at him like- Wait. “The other night you said seven. You told me it took you seven times to… To ‘learn your lesson’ about the truck bombing.”

“I’m not lying.” The other Eddie had believed him immediately, so he had just assumed this Eddie would, too. And when did he stop calling him ‘Edmundo’ in his head? Buck hadn’t even noticed the switch.

“I don’t think you’re lying.” Those words were, as always, a relief to hear. “But I do think you might have left something out.”

That was a vast understatement. And he could tell him all about it, or…

“What are you going to do today?” he asked instead, changing the subject. “Since you don’t have a work to go to at the moment.”

“Buck.”

“Eddie.”

Buck set down the comb and turned to face him, crossing his arms over his chest and leaning back against the sink. They could both be stubborn to a fault, and so he had no doubt that they could stand there staring at each other all day, waiting for the other to give in first. If they had the time, at least.

“After you take Chris to school,” Buck said, reminding the other man that there was a time limit to their contest. “What are you going to do today? Or do you want me to take him on my way to work?”

He watched Eddie work his jaw in frustration for a couple seconds, before letting out a sigh and giving in.

“I’ll take him,” Eddie said, allowing him to switch the topic and avoid talking about it.

“You’re always welcome at the station if you get bored,” he told him. “Or! Or! I bet Maddie would always welcome some adult conversation. I’m not sure who she’s talking to, outside of me and Chimney and Albert.”

“What is she doing these days?”

“I have no idea,” Buck admitted with a shrug. “I know she has Jee-Yun when Chimney’s working, but beyond that? I don’t know why she hasn’t gone back to Dispatch yet, or if she even wants to.”

“Well, maybe I’ll go to the store and get paint for the bedroom,” Eddie suggested, pushing himself off the doorframe. “I’m tired of staring at the patches.”

“Anything but that awful off-beige, please,” Buck practically begged. “Choose like, a blue. A dark blue, maybe. It’s supposed to make you produce more melatonin and less cortisol, help you sleep better.”

“That sounds remarkably like what that blanket is supposed to do.”

“I might have read about it at the same time,” he confessed.

“If I paint it blue, I’ll have to do the whole room,” Eddie pointed out. “Or, I could just get the same color and have less work to do.”

“But it’s such a boring color.”

“You’re welcome to sleep elsewhere if you don’t like it.”

Both of them knew that Buck wasn’t going to call that bluff.

Eddie turned to go make sure Chris was actually getting ready for school, but not before tossing out a final,

“And we are going to talk about whatever you’re avoiding talking about.”

Yeah, no. Over his actual dead body.

Buck managed to avoid the conversation for the rest of the morning, leaving the house with another insistent reminder that Eddie could swing by the station if he wanted. Actually, it was more like Buck told Eddie that he’d see him at lunch. He couldn’t decide if he simply wasn’t comfortable with the man being alone all day only a month after his breakdown, or if he just really, really wanted him at the station.

It was probably, almost definitely both.

It was Wednesday, only a couple days after the fire at Dispatch, but Bobby still came in with the others. Buck was unsurprised by this, since the man had managed to get out mostly unscathed and he didn’t do much heavy lifting most shifts anyway.

Buck stayed glued to his phone the entire morning, checking and rechecking it whenever he could. Even though he wasn’t working, and therefore didn’t have any complaints about Dispatch’s stubborn copier or observations about dispatcher drama to share, Eddie was still texting him just as much. Maybe a bit more, considering he obviously didn’t have much to do and no one to do it with.

The morning seemed to drag on even so. Not because it was a calm or uneventful shift, but just because time was an illusion and Buck desperately could not wait until lunch. He just could not wait until-

“Hey, uh, room for one more?” Eddie’s voice had him pausing in the middle of setting the table, twisting around to grin at him where he was standing at the top of the loft stairs. He was standing there awkwardly, like he wasn’t sure if he belonged, and Buck wanted to just make that uncertainty go away.

“Always,” Bobby told him, though he didn’t bother to ask anyone to get out another plate. Buck wasn’t subtle, and there was probably no doubt in the captain’s mind that he’d been expecting Eddie’s arrival.

The others - Hen and Chimney, mostly, along with Ravi - immediately started talking to him like he’d never left. It went a long way towards easing his discomfort. When the food was set out and it was time to eat, Buck practically shoved Eddie into the seat next to him.

With all of them sitting around the dining table, the station felt like home in a way that it hadn’t since he came back.

“So what’s dragged you in today, Eddie?” Hen asked him.

“Didn’t have anything to do,” he admitted, before nudging Buck with his elbow. “This one practically begged me to visit.”

“I didn’t beg,” Buck denied. “I told you to be here.”

“See what I have to live with?” Eddie asked them. “Hey, Chim, how’s Albert doing with his probationary year?”

“He quit,” the paramedic announced.

“What!?” Buck squawked, leaning halfway over the table to look at him. “When? Why?”

“Well, I’m not sure if he’s quit yet, but he’s planning to,” Chimney said. “He just… It just isn’t for him.”

“I’m sure he’ll find something that is,” Eddie offered, sounding confident about it. “Oh, hey, Cap? Could I talk to you about something after we’re done eating?”

“Of course,” Bobby answered.

Buck had no clue what that was about, which was unusual these days. Though, it wasn’t like he was privy to the entirety of Eddie’s business. Just, most of it. 99.9999% of it. This just happened to be part of the point-whatever that he wasn’t involved in. It was whatever. It didn’t bother him at all.

“So what is that about?” Hen asked him after everyone’s plates were empty and both Eddie and Bobby had sequestered themselves in the captain’s office.

“I have no idea,” he told her.

The two of them stayed in the office for a while, and the longer they did so, the more Buck wanted to pop his head in and see if everything was okay. He distinctly remembered the morning after his drunken fuck-up, when Eddie picked him up from the Grant-Nash home, the way the two of them had got into it about something in the kitchen. It hadn’t been anything like the way Buck and Bobby had gotten into it in the same room a month later. But something had been said to cause Eddie to raise his voice, and Buck still didn’t know what.

So maybe he lingered just outside the office door. Not to eavesdrop, but just in case he had to burst in and play mediator. There wasn’t any need, however, because they were laughing when they both finally stepped out.

“Everything good?” Buck asked, pushing off from where he’d been leaning against the wall.

“Everything’s good,” Eddie confirmed with a nod, Bobby rolling his eyes as he went past. “I need to get going. Y’know, if I want to stop and get paint before I need to pick up Chris.”

Buck let out a giant sigh, feeling himself deflate a little.

“Yeah, I guess,” he said, not bothering to hide his grumpiness at the thought of the man leaving. “You’re getting blue.”

“When did you get so bossy, Buckley?” the other man asked, rolling his eyes as he headed for the parking lot. “I’m getting beige, and you’re going to like it.”

“Blue!” Buck called after him. “It helps you sleep better!”

“That’s what I have you for!”

Behind him, there was a squeak and then the sound of something toppling over. Buck turned to find that a wide-eyed Ravi had knocked over a spare oxygen tank.

“Uh, um,” the younger firefighter stuttered. “I’m just-”

And then he was turning on his heel and running away, leaving Buck to wonder just when he’d gotten as weird as the rest of them.

When Buck got home, he found a pair of paint cans that were labeled Pacific Pleasure, and was told to shut up before he could say a single thing.

It was weird, Eddie thought, to knock on what he still thought of as Buck’s door and not expect him to open it. Hell, it was weird to be knocking on it at all, instead of just letting himself in with the key. He’d almost done just that, before thinking better of it.

“Eddie?” Maddie clearly had not been expecting him when she opened the door. “What are you doing here?”

“Visiting,” he answered, the “duh” implied but unsaid.

“You’re bored, aren’t you?” she teased, stepping aside so he could enter the loft. She hadn’t made many changes to the place since she started staying, the newest additions being mostly stuff for Jee-Yun, including a baby gate at the foot of the stairs. The toddler in question was sitting in her playpen, mouthing at a stuffed rabbit.

“So bored,” he told her, almost whining. “I’d go bother them at the station again, but it feels kinda pathetic to hang around when I’m not working there.”

“And so you came here.” 

“Buck mentioned that you were lacking in adult company,” Eddie told her, taking a seat at the kitchen island.

“Yeah, I think the most conversation I’ve gotten that isn’t babble is when I’m dropping off or picking up Jee,” Maddie admitted. “I’ve started just telling her my opinions about whatever I’m watching and acting as if she’s got any clue what I’m saying.”

“How are you doing with that?” he asked her. “Last time we spoke, I was convincing you that you weren’t a bad mother for missing a couple months of her life.”

“Three months,” she corrected, and then sighed. “It’s… Going. How did you and Shannon handle it? The whole, co-parenting while not being together, thing?”

“We didn’t,” he told her. “I was overseas, and then she left to take care of her mother. The closest we came to what you and Chimney are doing was those months we were trying again, and even then…” Eddie trailed off with a shrug. 

He was almost tempted to have her go to Buck for advice. In the other timeline, the one where Buck had saved Shannon, he knew they’d worked something out. From the way the man had described it, they were a masterclass in co-parenting. But that would require Maddie to know about the whatever-the-fuck Buck had gone through, and for her to believe it.

“I’m not sure Howie and I are going to be trying again.” It was a whisper of a confession. “I still love him, I do, but… Sometimes, I think we got together too quickly. Sometimes, I think I should have waited longer after Doug. To actually heal from that, instead of just leaping straight into another relationship. It feels like everything we had was built on a… A wonky foundation.”

“You built your house on sand,” he said, flashing back to Sunday mornings spent sitting in uncomfortable pews.

“Something like that,” she said, and then, “He didn’t tell me Buck was missing.”

“What?” That didn’t make sense, because when they arrived back in Los Angeles, she’d known what was going on.

“Hen told him about Buck right after New Years, when he’d been gone for a week with no sign,” Maddie explained, probably because Eddie’s confusion was visible on his face. “Howie didn’t tell me, didn’t email me about it until a week after that. And I… I don’t know why he didn’t let me know right away. Because he knew I was checking my email, right? After I called Buck and Buck told Howie where I was. But he didn’t. He knew for a week that something had happened to my brother, but he didn’t let me know.

“And then, when I called him after I read the email, it went to his voicemail. I guess he was busy with Jee, or taking a shower, or… Anyway, it went to his voicemail, and I heard… Unless it’s Buck, in which case, stop calling me.”

“That was cruel of him,” Eddie said, remembering the conversation he’d had with the man in this very kitchen, before he went off to find Buck. For a moment, he wondered if Maddie knew about the punch. If she did, she wasn’t mentioning it, and if she didn’t… Well, he wasn’t sure if that was a mess he should step into. If neither Buck nor Chimney had told her about it, then it probably wasn’t his place.

“It was,” she agreed, and then asked, “Has he told you anything?”

“Has who told me what?”

“Buck. Has he told you anything about that month he was gone?”

Far too much and not nearly enough.

“Not a thing,” Eddie lied. “Has he mentioned anything to you?”

“Not a thing,” Maddie said. “Y’know, I never thought to ask. Where did you find him?”

“I didn’t find him.” Another lie. “He just… Showed up at the house in the middle of the night. I woke up and nearly brained him with a bat. Why?”

“I just thought I remembered him telling Chris that you found him,” she said. Before he could gather a response to that, she moved on. “Do you know what’s going on with Dispatch?”

“They’re setting up some temporary location,” Eddie told her, thankful for the change in conversation. “I haven’t been by yet, so I have no clue what it looks like.”

“I’m thinking about going back,” Maddie said. “To Dispatch, I mean. Watching it burn down on the news, it just made me realize how much it meant to me. How much I missed working there.”

“I get that,” he said, and then, “I’m transferring back.”

“Oh?” Maddie sat up in her seat. “To the One Eighteen?”

“Yeah. I talked to Bobby when I was there the other day. I haven’t told Buck yet, though. I’m waiting for the paperwork to go through and to get an actual start date.”

“How is that going to work for you and Buck?” she asked.

“What do you mean?”

“I mean, with the two of you… How is it going to work with the two of you working together? Like, is it allowed? What’s the department policy on that?”

“Maddie.” Eddie folded his hands together on the surface of the kitchen island. “You know Buck and I aren’t-”

“Don’t you fucking bullshit me, Diaz,” she snapped, and he laughed. “You two are worse than how Chimney and I were at the beginning, when Buck was all, ‘at least I know when I’m dating someone.’” There was a mocking lilt to her voice as she waggled her head back and forth that had him laughing again.

“That is not at all how he sounds.”

“Whatever,” she scoffed.

“We’re just waiting,” he told her. “Until we’re certain we’re not building anything on sand.”

“That’s a more grown-up decision than I was expecting of him,” Maddie confessed, and Eddie bit his tongue to keep from saying anything. In the playpen behind him, Jee-Yun threw her stuffed rabbit over the side and started whining. “And that’s a sign that I’ve waited too long getting her fed. You want to stick around and be impressed by my daughter’s ability to hold a spoon?”

Eddie thought about it. It honestly wasn’t that hard of a decision.

“Sure.”

“Chris, hurry up!” Eddie called down the hall. “We still need to pick up Tia Pepa!” He stepped back into the room, shaking his head as he went to Chris’ wardrobe. “It should not take that long to pack a toothbrush.”

Buck snorted from where he was playing with one of Christopher’s dinosaur toys. He and Eddie were already packed, their bags waiting in the living room. There was a suitcase sitting open on Chris’ bed, and Eddie was going through the clothes trying to decide what to bring.

“You could be helping, you know,” he sniped. Buck snickered but made no move to set the toy down “What is the dress code at a retirement party? Ties is probably too much.” Eddie contemplated for a moment, and then he shrugged and scooped a bunch of folded shirts off the top shelf, throwing them into the suitcase. “It’s my parents we’re talking about, I’ll just grab it all.”

“Are you sure this is a good idea?” Buck asked.

“Good idea or not, we’re going,’ Eddie told him. “Mom made a huge deal about this party. Dad worked there for forty years, and Chris is excited to see everyone. He misses them.”

“Yeah, I get that.” He kept his eyes fixed on the dinosaur, absent-mindedly sticking his finger in its open maw. “But are you sure it’s a good idea for me to go?”

“Yes,” was the other man’s immediate answer. He turned to face him, frowning. “Why? Do you not want to go?”

“I- I do,” Buck stammered out. “It’s just that… I mean, this is a party for your dad, with your family, I-”

“Stopping you right there,” Eddie interrupted. “Before you finish that line of utter bullshit. You were nothing but excited about the idea a couple weeks ago. What is this really about?”

“We’re going to be on a plane,” he whispered. “We’re going on a metal tube that flies through a loophole in physics, Eddie. Literally anything could go wrong. And I- I just… Is it safe? For me to go with you guys?”

The other man stared at him for a solid moment, long enough to have him squirming, before sighing and dragging a hand down his face.

“Buck, I love you.” The words sent a thrill down his spine, just as they always did whenever he heard them. “But you're being really self-centered right now.”

“What?”

“Do you really think the universe, or that butterfly, or whatever- Do you really think it’s going to bring down an entire plane full of people just to get at you?”

“W- Well…” Put that way, it did sound rather silly. “No?”

“Exactly, it’ll be fine,” Eddie said, zipping up Chris’ suitcase. “The flight, I mean. The party, well, we’ll see. But if it gets messy, then there’s a hotel twenty minutes away. Three-and-a-half stars, it has a pool that Chris will love.”

“I didn’t pack any swimming trunks,” Buck pointed out. “They’re still at the loft.”

“If we end up going to the hotel, you can just buy a new pair,” the other man said, waving away the concern. “But we’re thinking positive today, remember?”

“We are?”

“We are. We’ll get there, we’ll see everyone, have some cake, and then get out. This will be a perfectly pleasant trip with no drama.”

Buck let out a sigh.

“You should probably go ahead and book that hotel room now.”

“Shut it,” Eddie told him. “It’ll be fine.”

“Do they even know you’re bringing a guest?” he asked. Eddie scowled and pointed a finger at him.

“It’ll be fine,” he repeated, which translated to: no, no they didn’t. Eddie yanked the suitcase off the bed and dragged it out of the room. “Chris! We’re going!”

Later, after they picked up Pepa and went through security and boarding, as they sat on the plane waiting for take off, Buck’s nerves started to get the better of him. His leg started bouncing in the small space they had, and it only got worse as they started to taxi down the runway. There were so very many ways things might go wrong, and he knew from experience that they could and probably would. 

“It’ll be fine,” Eddie told him, reaching over and taking his hand in his own.

He didn’t let go until they were landing in Texas.

Chapter End Notes

Plot twist: they all die in a fiery plane crash, story ends. /jkjkjk

Cat's in the Cradle

They arrived in El Paso just as the sun was starting to set. It had been a calm flight, with no mishaps or sudden disaster. There hadn’t even been any turbulence. The entire time, Eddie had held his hand, even when Buck was certain he’d been squeezing so hard that it had to hurt. It was a complete reversal of how they’d been just a few scant weeks before, where Buck would help Eddie through his night time fears.

He wasn’t certain if Pepa, sitting across the aisle from them, had noticed. If she did, she didn’t say anything.

After waiting for and grabbing their luggage, Eddie got a car from the rental place attached to the airport. Ever the gentleman, Buck allowed Pepa to take the front seat while he folded himself up in the back with Chris, who passed out almost the moment they pulled away. Allowing Pepa and Eddie’s chatter to wash over him from the front seat, he tried to quash the feeling of dread that hadn’t gone away when the plane touched down.

Eddie’s family was not actually expecting him, after all. And while these versions of Helena and Ramon didn’t have any strong feelings either way about their son’s coworker, he was fairly certain the other versions hadn’t liked him much after he defended both Eddie and Shannon from them at Eddie’s shield ceremony. Despite Eddie’s insistence that everything was going to be fine, Buck was not going to bet on anything except a dumpster fire of a visit second only to his own parents’.

There was a giant tent already set up in front of the house when they pulled up the long driveway. Abuela must have been keeping an eye out for their arrival, because she was already stepping out onto the porch before they were even parked.

“Oh, something’s wrong,” Pepa remarked.

“I haven’t even shut off the engine,” Eddie complained before doing just that. The two have them climbed out, but Buck wasn’t in much of a hurry to follow them. He stayed sitting in the backseat, listening to them through the open window.

“Mami,” Pepa greeted her mother. “¿Que paso?”

“Nada,” she answered, pulling them both in for a hug and a kiss on the cheek. “Where’s Christopher?”

Ah, a woman with her priorities in order.

“Out like a light ever since we left the car rental place,” Eddie told her.

“With Buck,” Pepa added.

“Ay, pobrecito mi angel-” She stopped in the middle of the word, and Buck could see her slowly blink at her daughter. “Buck?”

“Si, Buck. Our Eddie brought a guest with him,” Pepa said, and gestured at the car. Buck waved at them through the window. It was just hitting him that he couldn’t remember if he’d ever been given permission to call this version of her Abuela, as he had the other. With urging from Eddie, he cautiously opened the car door and stepped out to join them.

“It’s good to see you,” he told her, feeling awkward.

“It’s good to see you,” she replied, giving him a hug as well. “I was very worried, when you were gone.”

“Oh, I- I’m sorry, Isabe-” He was cut off by a smack to the arm, which solved his quandary. “Abuela. And uh, sorry I wasn’t there to see you off.”

“It’s fine. You’re here now.”

“So,” Pepa cut in. “What’s wrong?”

Abuela let out a sigh and faced Eddie.

“Your mother is in there doing all the cooking.”

“What?” her daughter demanded, sharing a look with Eddie. “I thought my fancy brother hired caterers for his big parranda.”

“The caterers are only allowed to warm and serve the food,” Abuela clarified. “Helena insisted on making everything herself.”

Pepa let out a sigh, and Buck didn’t have to look at Eddie to know that he was feeling the disappointment just like he was. He missed Abuela’s cooking, and the absence of it was a sign that his predictions for this trip were probably closer to the truth.

“Everything?” Eddie asked, sounding skeptical. “You didn’t help out at all?”

“I was allowed to make the tamales,” she confessed with a smile, and all three of them let out quiet cheers. Her tamales were the best, in Buck’s opinion. He’d never managed to get them quite right, no matter how often he tried, although he liked to think he’d gotten close.

Eddie woke Chris up and got him into the house, and then Buck helped him bring in the bags. There weren’t any immediate appearances of the Diaz parents, though he knew Helena was in the kitchen. There wasn’t a room set up for Buck, since Eddie hadn’t bothered to tell anyone he was coming, but that obviously wasn’t a problem. His bag just ended up on the same bed as Eddie’s, while Chris’ ended up in what he was fairly certain was Eddie’s childhood bedroom, based on the baseball trophies.

Buck hadn't had the chance to snoop the last time he was here, after the wildfires. Well, both times after the wildfires. He’d seen the living room, and then the kitchen, and that was all. And both times the visit had been cut short after Eddie and his parents had gotten into it over the latter’s insistence that Chris come stay with them during the pandemic.

“Where are the baby photos?” he asked, and got the grumpy glare he was aiming for.

“Hopefully in the trash,” Eddie told him. “C’mon, let’s go say hello to mom and dad.”

“It’s not too late to get a hotel room.”

“Positive. We’re thinking positive.”

Privately, Buck thought that Eddie was knee deep in denial.

They found Ramon in the living room, talking with his sister and mother, while Chris was rubbing his eyes as he sat on the couch. The man looked up when they entered and was in the middle of greeting his son when he noticed Buck’s presence.

“I didn’t know you were bringing a guest, Eddie,” he said, hesitantly offering his hand for Buck to shake. “It’s Buck, right? You came down with him for the wildfires.”

“Yes, sir,” he answered, feeling awkward as he shook his hand.

“It would have been nice to know you were coming,” Ramon commented. “We don’t even have a guest room set up for you.”

“Oh, it’s fine,” Eddie said, waving the problem away like smoke in the wind. “He’ll stay in mine.”

His father’s face twitched all at once, before scrunching up in confusion. The man seemed entirely uncertain on how to take or interpret that statement. Pepa, meanwhile, was smirking at his back.

“Right. Well, I should go make sure your mother knows you’re here,” Ramon finally said, giving a pat to his son’s shoulder and then leaving for the kitchen.

Buck, deciding to just sidestep the uncomfortable questions he knew was on Pepa’s tongue, took a seat next to Chris on the couch. The boy was still waking up, having been sleeping pretty heavily for how short of a nap it’d been.

“How are we doing kiddo?” he asked. “Excited to see everyone tomorrow?”

“Yeah,” Chris said, smiling and nodding. “Abuelo didn’t know you were coming?”

“Nope,” Buck confirmed, side-eyeing a sheepish looking Eddie. “Your dad forgot to pass on the message.”

At that, Chris joined him in side-eyeing his father, and together the pair shook their heads at him in disappointment. Eddie opened his mouth, probably to say something rude, but got interrupted by Ramon returning.

“Helena’s still cooking up a storm,” he told them. “I don’t know when she’ll be done.”

“She’d be done sooner if she let someone take part of the load,” Pepa sniped, and her brother scowled at her. “You spent all this money on help, Ramon, but she’s not letting them help.”

“She wants to cook for my party,” he said, throwing his hands up. “Who am I to tell her no?”

Pepa let out a scoff as she sat down, and soon everyone else was sitting as well. Ramon made an odd face when Eddie squeezed himself in beside Buck on the couch, like he wasn’t certain what he was seeing, like he was being confronted with a new puzzle. Buck didn’t think they were homophobic, but in the other timeline, they didn’t really have the chance to show their colors there either way. The one and only time he’d seen them in person after he and Eddie had gotten together, things had been overshadowed by their argument about Chris.

It didn’t matter that he and this Eddie weren’t actually together yet, because it was clearly an inevitability at this point and they were simply putting off the discussion.

A baseball game was playing on the television, and they made awkward small talk for a while. At some point, Ramon insisted on getting them drinks, and returned with bottles of beer for himself and his son. And for Buck, who didn’t quite manage to politely decline it and ended up taking it like a live grenade. 

He sat it on the end table and pushed it as far away from himself as he could, because it was far too great of a temptation in a stressful situation like this. Eddie, bless him, drank his beer and then stealthily switched his empty bottle for Buck’s still full one.

“Okay, that’s it,” he said, pushing himself off the couch with his (Buck’s) drink in hand. “I want to actually see mom at some point tonight. I’m surprised she hasn’t come out to say hi to Chris.”

“She’s cooking for tomorrow,” Ramon reminded him.

“She could take a break,” Eddie said, and started for the kitchen, taking the bottled temptation with him. A minute later, Ramon followed after him. Buck immediately got up as well, grabbing the empty bottle with the excuse of throwing it into the recycling.

He trailed after the older Diaz, knowing that no good would come from Eddie being alone with both of his parents. His relationship with them wasn’t as bad as Buck’s relationship with his own parents, but that was a very low bar with plenty of room for messy explosions.

“It’s been too long,” he heard Ramon say as he got to the kitchen, lingering at the doorway. “You should come visit more often.”

“Pretty sure planes fly in both directions,” Eddie snarked back, and yeah, there was plenty of room for messy explosions.

“Well, now that your father’s retired,” Helena said, standing at the stove. “We’ll have time to visit more.”

“Yeah, I can’t believe it’s actually happening,” Eddie replied. He looked as excited at the prospect of his parents’ visiting Los Angeles as Buck felt.

“Oh, did you show him the watch?” Helena asked her husband.

“It’s nothing,” Ramon tried to say.

“No, no, it was a nice gesture,” she insisted. “Show him.”

“Yeah, show me,” Eddie encouraged.

Giving in, Ramon turned and grabbed a black box off the counter behind him. He passed it to Eddie, who opened it with an appreciative look.

“Wow,” he said, taking out a note that was tucked inside and reading from it. “In recognition of your forty years of service helping us reach new horizons for our company, and for all of our stakeholders, the board thanks you. From our family to yours.”

“It’s not quite a Silver Star,” Ramon said, and when Eddie dropped his gaze, Buck had the violent urge to strangle the Diaz patriarch for the ignorant, insensitive comment. They didn’t know, he told himself. They just saw the shiny medal and declared, “My son’s a hero,” and didn’t put any thought into what he had gone through. “But it’s something, I guess.”

“It’s uh, it’s nice,” Eddie commented, setting the watch box down on the dining table. “Congratulations, dad.”

“Feels strange,” his father confessed. “Being unemployed, it’s… No meetings to rush off to, no phone calls to juggle. No… No reason to get out of bed at four AM. I don’t know what I’m gonna do with myself.”

“I bet,” his son replied, taking a swig of beer, and then Buck heard him mutter, “Why live if you’re not working?”

(In the other timeline, Buck had told Red that, “This job is my life.” The old firefighter had looked at him with such pity and disappointment, afterwards, and then told him how stupid he was for thinking that.)

“So,” Ramon started, dragging Buck out of his thoughts. “How’s life as an operator?”

He was so clearly trying to make an effort, but he was misstepping right out of the gate, and Buck couldn’t stand back as a silent observer anymore.

“He’s actually not an operator,” he said, entering the room fully. Helena startled a bit at his sudden presence, and her smile was welcoming, if a bit awkward. He waggled the empty bottle at Eddie, who took it from him and set it down on the counter. “He’s the, uh, what’s your official title?”

“Department Liaison,” Eddie answered.

“Still a firefighter, just… Administrative,” Buck said. He saw Ramon open his mouth and realized that he just left his partner open to more comments, and barreled through before the man could say anything. “And it was a good thing he was there last week. He saved at least one guy. Probably an entire building full of people.”

“Shut up, Buck,” Eddie demanded, trying to hide the pleased smile he was sporting by drinking more.

“It’s true!” he exclaimed. “Y’see, there was a uh, a power surge, right?” He glanced at Eddie, who nodded. “It knocked out the fire alarm and also started a fire. And he saved the IT guy from being smothered to death by the fire suppression system.” One day, when they were both on better footing, he’ll mock Eddie for leaving the station for a safer job, only for that safer job to literally burn down around him. One day, but not today. “Who knows what would have happened if… If Eddie wasn’t there…”

Buck trailed off with a frown, thoughts going to dark places and things that could be.

“Josh would have sent someone else down to talk to Terry, or gone himself,” Eddie said, like he was trying to deflect praise. But he was looking right at Buck, because he could no doubt guess what he was thinking.

“That’s our son, the hero,” Helena said, and Buck saw Eddie try to subtly roll his eyes. “So, Buck, right? None of us knew Eddie was bringing a friend with him…”

“Did I kill an entire building full of people?” Buck asked, later that night as they lay in bed, clutching at the pillow under his head. Eddie’s parents had given them Looks when they went to bed together, although Abuela and Pepa hadn’t seemed fazed in the least. 

“What are you talking about?” Eddie asked, already halfway asleep.

“The other Eddie, he didn’t leave the station,” he told him. “He… He wouldn’t have been there when Dispatch caught fire. Did I-?”

He was cut off by the other man blindly slapping a hand over his mouth.

“Buck,” he said, not even bothering to open his eyes, “I told you. Josh would have sent someone else to talk to Terry, or gone himself. Don’t do this to yourself.”

“But-”

“No. No buts. Just sleep,” Eddie ordered, grabbing Buck and pulling him closer. “You’re going to be meeting everyone tomorrow. Go to sleep.”

Buck sighed and closed his eyes and did his best to do just that.

There were a lot of people at Ramon’s retirement party. A lot of Diazes and non-Diazes that he did not recognize. Buck knew in a very vague sense that Eddie’s family was large, but he hadn’t actually understood what that meant until now. It made Helena’s insistence from the previous night on cooking all the food herself even more absurd, because there was no doubt in his mind that she had spent the entire day, from morning till night, doing so. She’d have had to, to make enough food for the crowd that had gathered.

For someone else, being thrown into a sea of strangers might have been overwhelming. After all, the only people Buck knew were Eddie’s parents, Abuela, Pepa, and his younger sisters. Both of whom were too young to have had the then twelve-year-old niece that Eddie had mentioned when they first met, so clearly the familial dynamics at play here were far more intricate and confusing than the 118 could ever hope to be. It was very likely that more than a few of the people present were family in the same sense that he and the others at the station were family, rather than by any blood or marital relation.

Luckily, Buck’s reputation as the station’s Golden Retriever was well-earned. He fluttered about the party, meeting and greeting and charming the pants off of everyone. More than a few of them vocally wondered who the hell he was and what he was doing there. Each time he explained that he came with Eddie, they all nodded in understanding and then asked how Ramon and Helena had been treating him, before moving on.

More than once, someone made a comment about him in Spanish, which resulted in embarrassed titters when he responded in kind.

(Once, someone made a comment about Shannon, and he told them to go fuck themselves before stomping off.)

“Where have you been?” Eddie demanded, when Buck rejoined him. He, unlike Buck, was not a social butterfly. He, unlike Buck, had been sticking close to Abuela and Pepa on the edge of the gathering.

“Being friendly,” Buck replied.

“You abandoned me.”

“I was being friendly!” he repeated. “Where’s Chris?”

“He also abandoned me,” Eddie answered, pointing over to where the kid was catching up with a group of (Buck was assuming) cousins his age.

“You’re a big boy, you can handle it,” Buck told him, utterly without sympathy, and gave him a mocking pat on the cheek. “And if not, you have Abuela here to protect you.”

“Fuck off.”

“I picked out a nice bolero.” Ramon’s voice was coming closer, and Buck turned to find him and Helena walking towards them. “And then we just get up and we say thank you and then we do our little dance and that’s it.”

“What’s going on?” Eddie asked them.

“Your father’s nervous about giving a speech,” his mother explained, and the Diazes all let out mocking groans.

“Oh, por favor!” Pepa exclaimed, “Ramon, you have never met a microphone you didn’t love.”

“Yes,” Helena agreed. “I’m surprised you’re not singing, too.”

“Please tell me he sings better than Eddie,” Buck begged. Pepa shook her head mournfully, which had him laughing and both Diaz men rolling their eyes.

“Okay, come on,” Eddie said, elbowing Buck in the side. “Tia Pepa’s right, I’m sure there’s a story you could tell.”

“Actually, I was gonna tell one about you,” Ramon said, and Buck perked up. He was always up for hearing an embarrassing story about Eddie’s childhood. There’d been a dreadful dearth of them so far. “You know, the day I came home and found that the entire side of my truck had been scraped off, including the side mirror and front bumper.”

“Oh my gosh, yes,” Helena said. “When Adriana was born.”

“You never told me about that,” Pepa said, chuckling.

“When Adriana was born and, uh, Ramon was off on business,” Helena explained, gesturing while she spoke. “I told Eddie that my water broke. And he jumped up and ran out of the room. I thought he was going to call someone to drive us to the hospital. But then I heard this loud crash, right? From the garage.”

“Oh no,” Buck snickered, bumping Eddie with his shoulder, his attention focused on Helena.

“Oh yes,” she said, nodding. “I walk in there and there’s Eddie behind the wheel of Ramon’s prized pickup. Barely seeing over the steering wheel, with the whole side of the truck torn to shreds. He was trying to drive me to the hospital.”

“Why am I not surprised, hm?” Abuela asked, reaching up to pat Eddi’s cheek. “Eddie to the rescue, my little hero.”

“Didn’t have much of a choice,” Eddie, and when Buck glanced at him he didn’t see him laughing at himself or ducking his head in embarrassment, like he was expecting. Instead, he was grimacing a smile.

The good humor began to curdle in Buck’s stomach.

“You had many choices,” Ramon countered. “But you were just a kid, I forgave you for that.”

Of course he did. Any decent parent would laugh it off, after scolding the kid for not thinking first. Buck’s parents wouldn’t, but any decent-

“No, actually, you didn’t,” Eddie denied. “You grounded me.”

“Edmundo, it’s- It’s just a story,” his mother tried, but her son wasn’t having it.

“Yeah, it’s a real sweet one, except he’s just… He’s missing the whole part where he tore me a new one for ruining his truck.” There was a mocking edge to his smile, though it would probably be better described as baring his teeth. “You know, kind of like the time he yelled at me for setting off the smoke alarms trying to make eggs for the girls when I was twelve.”

Jesus Christ, it was getting worse and worse the more he spoke. Was this how he, how both Eddies, felt whenever Buck went into a tirade about his own childhood?

“Edmundo,” Ramon tried, tone scolding, but his son was on a roll.

“I’m sure there’s a better story you can tell up there,” he continued. “Oh, why don’t you tell them about the time you pulled your ten-year-old son aside and told him it was time to step up? Be the man of the house?”

“You did what!?” Buck snapped, indignant fury surging to the fore on behalf of an Eddie even younger than Chris was now. “You don’t shove that onto your kid! What the hell? That’s supposed to be your job!”

“You wouldn’t understand,” Ramon snapped, clearly not pleased with the fact that a near utter stranger was disparaging his parenting. Neither was Helena, who was caught between fretting and glaring at him. “He had to be. I was busy providing for the family.”

“Providing?” Eddie repeated, letting out a dark and mocking chuckle. “Providing what, money?”

“Exactly,” his father said, getting agitated. “I had to do what I had to do.”

“A family needs more than money,” Eddie told him, on too much of a roll to notice how Ramon was starting to grip at his chest and arm. “I never had a childhood, a dad who took care of me. No, you were gone. The only-”

“Eds,” Buck interrupted, and his partner stopped mid sentence at the seriousness of his tone. A moment later, they were catching Ramon as he collapsed.

As it turned out, hiding trips to the cardiologist was a trait shared by the Diaz men. As it turned out, during one of Ramon’s many, many business trips, he went to Dallas to get arterial stents put in. As it turned out, Ramon was just as hesitant to show weakness as Eddie had been.

That last part hadn’t been much of a surprise. Buck already knew Eddie had learned such bad habits from his father, just like Chris had started to learn from the both of them.

As Eddie took his father inside so he could do a basic check up, Buck went to check in with Chris, who excitedly introduced him to his cousins. It was such a difference from his attitude of a month before, where the pre-teen had wanted absolutely nothing to do with him, that he stuck around to enjoy it. But five minutes began to edge towards ten, and there was still no sign of Eddie or his father.

Eddie was an adult, he knew, and could take care of himself. But Buck had promised to have his back, and that was especially relevant after the appalling revelations of the day. So he excused himself from the kids and headed into the house. His search led him to the kitchen, where he once again lingered just in the hall when he heard them talking.

“I spent my whole life trying not to be like you,” Eddie was saying. They were sitting at the table, both of them angled away from where he was standing.

“All these years, I tried to set a good example for you,” Ramon told him. “Maybe it was the wrong one, I don’t know. But I never understood why you were so angry with me.” And Buck could give a list of all the reasons Eddie was angry with his father, half of it just from what he had heard today. Or maybe those were simply all the reasons Buck was angry with Eddie’s father on his behalf. “Why it always felt like you were punishing me.”

“I think I was punishing myself more,” Eddie said. “But I’m tired of being that guy. I’m going to be better.”

“For Christopher,” his father assumed.

Eddie shook his head.

“For myself,” he corrected, and God, Buck was so proud of him. He had come so far from the man who put himself last each and every time. He had come so far from that man who stuck with a woman he did not love simply because his son liked her. He had come so far from that man who would rather hide behind the excuse of his son rather than admit he was afraid.

Eddie had come so very far, and Buck was so very proud of him.

“Maybe we can both be better,” Ramon cautiously offered. “I don’t want to miss out on any more of my son’s life.”

It took Eddie several moments, in which Buck could tell even from the hallway that he was choked up. But finally, he said,

“I’d like that.”

Clearly, there wasn’t any need to be concerned about whether or not Eddie would be okay alone with his father. Clearly, they were both getting along just fine despite the blow-up outside. Perhaps they were like him and Bobby, needing to shout at each other occasionally so they could hug it out.

Nodding to himself, he turned and was about to go back outside, except he heard Ramon say,

“So, tell me about Buck.”

His foremost fatal flaw had always been his curiosity, and so no one could possibly blame him for doing an about-face and sticking around to eavesdrop.

“I love him,” Eddie told his father. By the set of his jaw, Buck could tell he was almost daring the man to say something. “He’s beautiful, and he’s kind, and he’s selfless, and I love him.”

“Well.” Ramon clearly did not know quite what to say to such an overt confession. Buck felt his own stomach twist with nerves, and he could only imagine what Eddie was feeling in that moment. “As long as he has your best intentions at heart, I guess.”

“He saved my life,” Eddie said, looking more at ease now. “He has been saving my life since I met him. My world is better and brighter simply because he exists within it.”

Direct him to a fainting couch, because Buck was about to swoon.

He left then, to let them have their privacy and also because if he stuck around he would end up kissing Eddie silly right in front of his father. No parent wanted to watch their kid make out. Besides, Buck needed to do this properly.

He was terrified, almost, of hurting this Eddie in the same way he had hurt the other. He was terrified, almost, of getting lost in the similarities and not actually seeing Eddie for himself. He was terrified, almost, of unwittingly and unintentionally using Eddie as a replacement for the one he had lost. He was probably always going to be worried about repeating that mistake.

But he loved Eddie. This Eddie, and the other Eddie, and probably each and every Eddie he would ever meet. He loved him, and he wanted to be with him, and he pretty much was already and they simply hadn’t talked about it.

Well, they were going to talk about it.

“A picnic on the beach.”

The non sequitur stopped Eddie at the door to the guest room. All the guests for his father’s party had gone home, and they themselves had a flight to catch tomorrow. Buck was sitting on their bed, already dressed in his pajamas, and looking at him expectantly.

“Que?” Eddie asked.

“Our first date,” Buck said, like that clarified anything.

“We haven’t gone on a date.”

“I know. We’re going on it next week, we just need to decide which day and who’ll watch Chris for us.”

Closing the door behind him, Eddie made his way to his suitcase, trying to figure out what exactly was going on here. Because it sounded very much like Buck was telling him they were going on a date next week, and while it was a very welcome development, it left him feeling like they’d skipped forward a bit.

“I thought we were waiting,” he said, pulling out his own sleep clothes. “Waiting until we were in a better place, so we weren’t building anything on sand. Waiting until-”

“I am tired of waiting,” Buck told him, getting off the bed and crossing the room to be at his side. “I love you, and- And I’m pretty sure we’ve been together and simply haven’t talked about it for a while now. Literally nothing would change except we stop pretending that we hadn’t already fallen off that cliff together.”

“But you still love the other Eddie.” It wasn’t a question.

“I love every version of you I have ever met, and I’ll love every version of you I will ever meet,” Buck told him, like he was announcing football scores or the weather. “But you have to promise me one thing, okay?”

“Anything,” Eddie swore, clutching tight to the cut-off sweats he was holding. The other man reached up to frame his face with his hands, so very similar to that night at home some weeks ago.
