“You have to promise me that if it ever seems like I’m not- That I’m not seeing you for you, that you’ll tell me, okay?” he said. “If you ever, ever feel like I’m seeing through you, you have to tell me.”

“Buck-”

“No, Eddie. I hurt him so much without even knowing it. He accused me of loving a ghost, and I don’t ever want you to feel like that. I don’t want you to feel like you’re competing with another version of yourself, like I made him feel. So you have to promise me, okay? If I ever, ever make you feel like that, you will tell me.”

“Alright, I promise.” It was one of the easiest promises he had ever made. It was helped, probably, by the weeks of therapy he’d been through.

“Okay, good,” Buck breathed out. “So, can you kiss me now? Because I would really like to be kissing you now.”

In lieu of words, Eddie dropped the sweats he was holding and pulled him closer and let his actions speak for him.

Lemonaded and Dined

Chapter Notes

It's my birthday today. I'm officially Old.

If one took out the constant, nagging worry that he was liable to trip over something and die at any given moment, Buck would probably describe the day after Ramon’s retirement party as utterly blissful. On their second morning there, which was also their last, he woke Eddie up by peppering his face in kisses until the other man pushed him away with a laugh and a complaint about morning breath. They got dressed and had a late breakfast with the Diaz parents, during which Ramon made an actual effort to talk to Buck.

There was still an awkwardness there, like neither Helena nor Ramon quite knew how to act with him. Buck wasn’t certain if it was because he was a guy or simply because they didn’t know how to properly act towards their son’s significant other after spending so many years running roughshod over Shannon, and then not even getting to meet Ana before that mistake finally came to an end. But they were making an effort, and Eddie definitely seemed to appreciate it, which meant Buck appreciated it.

(It didn’t mean that Buck wasn’t still prepared to throw hands at Ramon on Eddie’s behalf.)

Their return flight was scheduled for the early afternoon, and there was a sedate rush to pack everything back up and get their rental car turned back in. They gathered in the front yard to say goodbye, and Abuela crossed each of them in turn. Including Buck, who ducked his head to hide a pleased smile and happily wrapped her in the tightest hug he could give without worrying he was going to break her.

“See, I told you,” Eddie said after they boarded the plane and found their seats. He’d immediately taken Buck’s hand in his after they sat down. “A perfectly pleasant trip.”

“You said it was going to be a perfectly pleasant trip with no drama,” he pointed out. Which was always going to be a pipedream. Diazes were some of the most dramatic people he knew. An impressive feat, considering all the people he knew.

“Okay, so there was a little drama-”

“More than a little.”

“But it was pleasant!”

“Eh, it was okay,” he hedged, making the other man scoff and roll his eyes.

“Pendejo,” Eddie said, sounding so very fond, and Buck was about to respond except the plane began to take off and the anxiety from before rushed back. He must have started squeezing the hand he was holding without realizing it, because Eddie brought their joined hands to his lips so he could kiss the back of Buck’s.

Pepa was smiling at them from across the aisle, and he couldn’t be bothered to hide the blush on his face or his own wide grin.

For the first time in a very long time, Buck was legitimately reluctant to go into work. Eddie was scheduled to return to Dispatch at its new temporary location at the same time as Buck was set to return to the station, which was a couple days after they returned from Texas. So there was a day in which neither of them had needed to leave the house except to take Chris to and from school, a serendipitous event that they took much advantage of.

Before the trip, they had planned to use the time to get started on painting the walls of Eddie’s bedroom. They didn’t quite manage to get around to it.

Still, as disappointed as he was to have to separate himself from Eddie, he practically skipped into work. He passed Hen on the way to the coffee machine and felt her staring at him as he filled up his usual mug, almost bouncing in place as he did so.

“So you had a nice trip, I’m guessing?”

“It wasn’t quite the dumpster fire I was expecting,” he admitted, still grinning. “I kinda wanted to punch his dad a couple times, but it was fine.”

“Well, whatever it was about Texas that has you looking like this, I approve,” Hen told him, looking at him in contemplation. “I haven’t seen you look this happy since… I’m not sure. Not this year, definitely.”

“Why wouldn’t I be happy?” Buck asked, grinning into his coffee. “The sun is shining, I’m at the job I love, Eddie kissed me, I got to eat Abuela’s delicious tamales again…”

“Hold up,” the paramedic demanded. “Repeat that.”

“I got to eat Abuela’s delicious tamales again?”

“No, before that.”

“I’m at the job I love?”

“Buckaroo. Eddie kissed you?”

“Yes,” he answered simply. “We’re going on a date sometime soon- Oof!”

“Finally!” she cried as she barreled into him, laughing and hugging him tight. “I’m so happy for you!”

“Why are we hugging?” Chimney asked, as he and Bobby joined them.

“The boys aren’t idiots!” she joyfully told him. “They’re going on a date! An actual date!”

Buck watched Bobby nod to himself and hold up a finger, as if asking for a moment, before turning around and heading back down the stairs. He was going to ask what that was about, but he was viciously distracted by Chimney rushing into his other side to wrap him in a hug as well.

“About time!” he said, squeezing him just as tightly as Hen was, if not tighter, and the pair worked together to try and sway him side-to-side.

“Okay, okay!” Buck said, laughing and holding his coffee above them. “Let me go! You’re gonna make me spill.”

“Damn right you’re gonna spill,” Hen said, both paramedics dragging him to one of the tables and forcing him into a seat. “Come on, tell us.”

“Tell you what?”

“Everything! Well, not everything. There’s some things I do not want to know about,” she clarified. “But last week you told me the two of you were waiting.”

“Eddie told Maddie the same thing,” Chimney added. “Y’know, I caught him staring at your ass once.”

“I am aware,” Buck said, feeling like his face was going to split open from how much he was smiling. “The one time your gossiping would have come in handy, and it took me actual years to hear about it.”

“He wasn’t subtle!”

“Okay, enough about that,” Hen interrupted. “Tell us what changed.”

“I got tired of waiting,” Buck answered with a shrug. “But nothing’s changed, really, except for the kissing and the… Other stuff. I just felt like, well, we were pretty much already together so why not admit it and make it official?”

“Yeah, I do not want to know about the other stuff,” she told him, scrunching up her nose. “But I get what you mean. What with how you’re living together.”

“And you pack him lunches to take into work with him,” Chimney added.

“And co-parenting a kid that he calls your son.”

“And you were already sleeping together, according to Ravi.”

The last one was said just as Buck was taking a drink of his coffee and thus, he ended up choking on it. He spent several moments sputtering and pounding at his chest, trying to clear the hot liquid out of his airways.

“According to- What!? Why would he-!?” He looked around, spotting the younger firefighter coming up the stairs at the other end of the loft. “Ravi!”

Ravi flinched and ducked at the sound of his name being shouted at him.

“I just got here!” he yelled back. “I didn’t do anything!”

“I thought he was better than that,” Buck huffed, shaking his head. “And Eddie and I weren’t- I mean, we have been, but it was just sleeping! There’s only the one bed, and it helps us both to sleep better, and- Shut up!”

“We literally haven’t spoken a word,” Hen said, whose face had been saying plenty of things on its own. “I’m just glad that you two… How did you put it? That you’re both in a good enough place to make it.”

“We are in a better place,” he clarified. “I mean, we both still have our issues that we’re ironing out. But we’re both willing and able to put the effort in, and we’ve both promised to talk about things when we inevitably trip up.”

“Do not pretend like everything’s okay when it’s not,” Chimney told him, tone and expression both solemn.

“I won’t,” Buck promised.

“Enough with the heavy talk,” Hen butted in. “Tell us about Texas, and about this date you two are going on.”

Laughing, he did just that, filling them in on meeting Eddie’s various cousins and in-laws, and how his mother had spent an entire day cooking on her own because she refused to let anyone else help. He told them about the minor blow-up between Eddie and his father, but didn’t go into the details of it, because he wasn’t about to start airing out his partner’s childhood traumas.

In the middle of it, Bobby came back up and dropped some paperwork on the table in front of him, along with a pen. He recognized the forms from the other timeline, though these were entirely clean of dust and hadn’t already been mostly filled out by a frustrated captain waiting for them to get their heads out of their asses.

“Ugh, man,” he groaned.

“Fill those out,” Bobby instructed. “You’re the man behind until it’s done.”

“Yes, Cap.”

After two full days, Eddie was not certain if he liked the new - temporary - location for Metro Dispatch. Actually, no he was absolutely positive that he hated it. The only good thing about the place, with its awful wall of corporate buzzwords, was the breakroom that was constantly supplied with doughnuts. A breakroom which they, unfortunately, had to share with an accounting firm next door.

He’d almost come to blows with one of them when he walked in and found them trying to steal the brown paper bag that Buck had packed him. It turned out that they, too, had their lunch stored the same way, but still. 

Before, Buck would include bright pink post-it notes with ridiculous puns written on them, and Eddie had kept them all on his monitor in his office. Unfortunately, he’d lost all of them in the fire. Now, after Texas, the notes continued but had gotten a touch more personal. The first one had read, “Missing you already - XO” and the second day’s was a simple, “I Love You.” The Department Liaison didn’t have their own office in this temporary location, and so he didn’t stick them to his monitor as he had before.

Eddie wasn’t sure what he was going to do with the notes, but he did know he was going to keep them. Maybe he’d tape them all to the inside of his locker after he returned to the station.

Anyways, by the end of the second day he was exceedingly grateful that he’d be moving locations even sooner than everyone else. There still wasn’t an official date for his transfer back, and so he still hadn’t shared the news with Buck, but ever since the fire - ever since he had proven to himself that he could do the job that had once fulfilled him - he had been champing at the bit to get back to it.

“Honey, I’m home!” Eddie shouted when he walked through the front door after shift, simply because he could.

“In here!” Buck shouted back, and Eddie followed his voice and the smell of cooking food into the kitchen, to find his partner turning down the burner and placing a lid back on a pot. “Chris is with Pepa, she said she didn’t mind dropping him off at school in the morning if we wanted.”

“And he’s at Pepa’s, why?”

“Date night. I know I said our first date was going to be a picnic on the beach, but I didn’t want to wait anymore.” Buck turned to look at him and let out a huff. “You’re still dressed for work.”

“Because I just got home.”

“Yeah, well.” His partner seemed to flounder at being confronted with logic. “Go get changed.”

“Yes, dear,” he said, and watched as Buck’s face did an interesting little twitch, a furrow forming between his brows before smoothing away. He wanted to ask what that was about, but decided to let that sleeping dog lie for the moment. Instead, he made a show of obnoxiously rolling his eyes before leaving to do as he was bid.

When he got back, he found Buck setting the dining table, putting down actual cloth napkins and setting out battery powered candles. He offered to help, but was unceremoniously shoved into a seat and told to, “sit there and look pretty, Diaz." Eddie had never been the one who was wined and dined, he had always expected and been expected to do the wining and dining.

It was nice, he found.

Buck carried out two plates piled high with food, and then a pair of glasses and a pitcher of lemonade. He set it down and poured their drinks, looking sheepish as he explained,

“I know it’s supposed to be wine for a fancy dinner date, but-”

“It’s fine, Buck,” Eddie assured him, picking up the drink and taking a sip. “Lemonade is fine. Sit down, let’s eat.”

They talked while they ate. They talked about the calls Buck had had the day before, since they’d barely seen each other long enough that morning to say hello. They talked about the temporary Dispatch center, and how annoying their neighbors at the accounting firm were, and how Claudette and May seemed to have come to some sort of accord in the midst of their near-death experience. 

And Eddie noticed something odd, while they talked. Buck would, occasionally, start to call him “love,” only to catch himself at the last minute. He would look guilty every time he did it, before hurrying to move on and put it behind him. Finally, the sixth or seventh time it happened, Buck dropped his fork and buried his face in his hands, and said,

“I’m trying, Eds. I swear, I promise that I’m trying.”

“I know,” he told him, moving seats so he was sitting right next to him. “It’s just a pet name, it’s fine.”

“I know, but I don’t want you to feel like-”

“I don’t,” Eddie interrupted. “I feel like this is bothering you more than it’s bothering me. Because, Evan, the only time you have ever made me feel like you weren’t seeing me for me, was that night you came home drunk.”

“I don’t want to treat you like a replacement or a stand-in, though,” he said. “You both deserve better than that.”

Eddie knew that. It was what Buck had said way back in February, when they first came to the decision that they needed more time. He knew it, and he understood it, and he appreciated that Buck was so concerned about it.

“So how about this, we find different things to call each other,” he offered. “You won’t call me love, and I won’t call you dear.” 

“How did you…?”

“Your face did a thing when I said it earlier.”

“He only ever did it when I was being bossy and he was being sarcastic,” Buck told him. “He never called me Evan, though. You have, you just did, but he never did.”

“Maybe I’ll start calling you by it more, so you’ll worry less about mistaking me for him,” Eddie said, before standing up and gesturing to his plate. “Are you done?”

Buck glanced at his plate, most of the food gone, and let out a sigh.

“Yeah, I- What are you doing?”

“Picking up,” he told him, grabbing both of their plates. “You cooked, I’ll clean.”

“Hey, no.” Buck grabbed their glasses and the pitcher, following him into the kitchen. “I was supposed to be wining and dining you. Well, lemonading and dining.”

“Consider me lemonaded and dined,” Eddie said, setting down their plates and relieving Buck of what he was carrying, before directing him to a chair. “Now sit down and look pretty, Evan.”

(Near-)Death Experience

Chapter Notes

See the end of the chapter for notes

It was already shaping up to be a weird call from the beginning. It was to some wellness retreat, which seemed to Buck to consist mostly of some tents near a cliff’s edge. When they arrived, the people there were acting… Odd.

Odd in a very familiar way.

“Can someone tell me what happened?” Bobby asked the nearest person, who happened to be a woman who seemed to be doing some sort of yoga pose-slash-dance routine? Buck wasn’t certain.

“We unleashed our primal selves,” she answered, parting the air with her hands, and then let out an ugly chortle.

“These people appear to be hallucinating,” he heard Chimney comment under his breath.

“How would you know?” Hen asked.

“You looked like this when you were dosed,” he told her.

“No, I didn’t,” she denied.

“Yes, you did,” Buck broke in, remembering with fondness the day she spent sniffing Athena, while Eddie fretted about shrunken women.

“And how would you know?” Hen demanded, sharing a look with Chimney. “You were just as high.”

“Right, yeah, you’re right. I was.” Woops.

Ravi and Lucy both looked like they wanted to ask questions, probably something to the effect of, “why the hell were you both high?” But luckily, one of the people currently floating like a kite was speaking.

“He dosed us, man,” he was saying, “and then he went over.”

“Over where?” Bobby asked.

“Uh…”

The yoga woman switched from stabbing the air with her fingers like a praying mantis, to balancing on one leg and gesturing with her hands towards the cliff’s edge. The man, who was crouched and had been pretty focused on a bunch of stones arranged in some intricate design before they started asking questions, gestured over his shoulder in the same direction.

“Ortiz, keep everyone away from the edge,” Bobby ordered one of the others, before he and everyone else went over themselves to look down. And ‘lo and behold, there was their victim: his back pressed against the cliffside halfway down, desperately holding on for dear life.

“Sir!” Hen shouted down. “Are you hurt?”

“I think I sprained my ankle on the way down,” he called back up.

“We’re gonna come down and get you,” Bobby assured him, earning a tired and relieved jubilation that Buck couldn’t quite make out. “Okay, everybody, ladder rescue. Hen, Chim, prep a basket. Ravi, you’re helping me extend the ladder over the edge. Buck, you’re going down with Donato.”

They all nodded in confirmation of the orders and set off to do what was needed. It was routine to get harnessed up, although it never stopped being weird to have someone other than Eddie checking his harness. He didn’t dislike Lucy, even if there was still that lingering awkwardness and bitterness, but he could not wait until things were as close to being back as normal again as they could get.

Whenever the hell that was going to be.

They anchored lines to the ladder truck and started abseiling down the cliffside, Buck a little bit ahead - or below - of Lucy.

“Eugene, right?” Buck called down. “Just hang on, we’re almost to you.”

“Oh, thank God,” the man replied, just a few feet below them.

And then, there was a shake in the cliff just as Buck pushed off again. His line slackened and jerked. Dirt and pebbles fell on and bounced off his helmeted head, in a way that he was terrifyingly, intimately familiar with.

“That didn’t feel right,” Lucy told him, and their radios started screaming.

“Buck, Lucy, unhook now!” Bobby was shouting at them. “The cliff’s going down, and the ladder’s going with it.”

Buck scrambled to get down to the shelf that Eugene was on, his heart in his throat the entire time, as the ladder let out a concerning groan of creaking metal. He and Lucy hurriedly unhooked themselves from their safety lines that were now threatening to anchor them to their doom, and then pressed themselves flat against the rocks. Just like Eugene had been doing the entire time.

This was it, Buck thought. The moment he’d known had been coming ever since he’d seen that fucking butterfly after the Dispatch fire. And of course it was going to be the ladder truck, why would it be anything else? Even better, it was going to be the ladder truck falling off a cliff to crush his head again, like a twisted ode to the very first time he dared try to prevent the inevitable.

Like Ichabod, or that butterfly, or the universe itself was conspiring to mock him by rubbing his nose in it, before punishing him for whatever it was he’d done this time.

There was a waterfall of dirt, and then the sound of screeching metal. Buck watched as the truck fell towards him, just like that boulder did so long ago, and closed his eyes again just as he had then.

There was a crash.

There was a shattering of glass.

There was the sound of an impact, and then the pitter-patter of rocks bouncing off steel.

Buck peeled his eyes open and looked down to see the ladder truck crumpled far below them, near the base of the cliff. It had come so close, leaving a gouge just mere feet from where he was cowering, and he didn’t understand. It had come so close, but it had missed, and-

He didn’t understand.

He did not understand.

Why was he still alive? That was supposed to have killed him, he was supposed to have been crushed by a ladder truck for a third and final time. He knew it. So why was he still breathing?

Except, he wasn’t.

There was a vice on his chest, his lungs seemingly unable to expand fully. He hadn’t noticed before, and now that he had, he couldn’t not notice.

He couldn’t breathe.

“That- That’s bad,” he heard Eugene say, sounding a bit awed. “Right?”

He couldn’t breathe.

“Yeah, that’s bad,” he heard Lucy agree.

He wished Eddie was here. Eddie would- Right, yes, Eddie. Eddie had helped him at the hospital with his last panic attack. How had he…?

Right. That checklist.

Five things he could see:

Lucy, looking at him with a concerned frown; Eugene, still clinging for dear life; scrubby trees stubbornly holding on by their roots and defying gravity; the long gouge in the cliff that hadn’t been there when they first started down; the demolished ladder truck just below, and that one was not helping, Buck, look at something else.

He couldn’t stop looking at the ladder truck, though.

“Are you both okay down there?” Bobby asked over the radio.

“Yeah, I- I think so,” Lucy replied, sounding uncertain. “Buckley? Buck?”

Four things he could feel:

The harness straps digging into him, uncomfortably tight in a way they only ever were whenever someone other than Eddie checked them; a rock sticking out of the cliff and pressing into his back, or maybe it was his back pressing into the rock; Lucy grabbing his shoulder to grab his attention; his heart, beating and beating and beating and-

“Buck?” Lucy asked again. “Hey, Cap, I think something’s wrong with Buck.”

“I’m fine,” he managed to gasp out. “I’m j- just fine.”

Three things he could hear:

Bobby, asking over the radio what Lucy meant; Lucy, responding that Buck was breathing hard and fast and was barely responding to her; the sound of disturbed rocks pinging off the side of ladder truck below, and that still wasn’t helping, Buck.

“I’m fine,” he insisted again, breaths coming just a little bit easier. This wasn’t as helpful when it wasn’t Eddie guiding him through the checklist, but that was to be expected. “I’m fine.”

Two things he could smell:

Disturbed dirt, reminding him of the time he helped the other Eddie with Abuela’s garden; the strong, pungent odor of-

Buck sniffed the air, and then turned his head to stare incredulously at Eugene.

“Were you smoking pot before this?”

“Hey, it’s California!” the man snapped. “It’s legal here!”

“From the way she described things, it sounds like he had a panic attack,” Bobby had told him. It was not normal for Eddie to field calls from the captain in the middle of both their shifts, and so he was already half-expecting for something to be wrong with Buck before he even answered. “But it’s Buck, so he’s been insisting that he’s fine. And he seems fine, but…”

But it’s Buck, and that month away - or four to five years, depending on perspective - had apparently given him plenty of practice when it came to faking it.

“So you’re going to act with the assumption that he’s not actually as okay as he claims,” Eddie guessed. “Does he know you’re sending him home?”

“He will when you arrive to pick him up.”

Eddie hadn’t bothered to ask why he was the one Bobby called to fetch Buck, when he was working and Maddie wasn’t. Hell, after he had heard about the 118’s ladder truck falling off a cliff, but before Linda had told him that no one was hurt, he’d been a heartbeat away from running out of the awful temporary Dispatch Center to check on him.

Going with the assumption that he was merely a guest and so didn’t need permission for his comings and goings, he barely bothered to tell anyone he was leaving. He just made a vague mention to Josh on his way past about a family emergency. Which wasn’t entirely inaccurate, because Buck was definitely family.

It just wasn’t quite an actual emergency. Probably. Hopefully.

Eddie spent the drive to the station preparing himself to go toe-to-toe with Buck at his most stubborn. He might- Okay, he would definitely be pleased to get an unexpected visit from Eddie, but that would quickly sour when he learned why Eddie was there. And from that point, it was probably going to be like pulling teeth to get him to go along with it.

There was no one in the bay when Eddie strode into the station, keys clutched in his hand. There was an empty spot on the floor where the ladder truck should have been, if it hadn’t gone the way of Newton’s apple. And these days, he was perfectly willing to contemplate the veracity of curses, so perhaps they should start figuring out just why the 118 had such terrible luck with ladder trucks.

Bobby and Buck were seated around one of the taller tables when he made it up to the loft, and he took the moment to take in Buck before his partner realized he was there. And what he saw told him that Bobby had been right to be worried about him. The man was smiling, because of course he was smiling, because the man seemed to believe that a smile could cover up anything and everything.

But there was a wobbly, plastic-y look to the corners of it.

“Eddie,” Bobby greeted, nodding at him when he noticed his presence, and Eddie watched Buck’s smile become so much more real when he twisted his head in happy surprise.

“Eds! What are you doing here?”

Eddie didn’t say anything, he just held up his keys and let them dangle off his finger. It took a beat before Buck let out a groan and turned accusing eyes on his captain.

“Really, Cap?”

“Go home,” Bobby ordered. “We’re down a truck now, so it’s not like we can’t spare you.”

Buck opened his mouth, and Eddie prepared himself for the argument that was bound to come while Bobby no doubt did the same, but… It didn’t happen. Buck merely let out a sigh and slumped a bit, before sliding off the stool he was perched on.

“Fine,” he said, conceding far too easily. “I’ll go get changed, then.”

He slipped past Eddie, giving him a smile that was no less real for how small it was, and squeezed his arm as he went by.

“Okay, that was weird,” Chimney commented as he and Hen waltzed up from the other end of the station loft. “I was expecting a knock-down, drag-out fight. I had the popcorn ready and everything.”

“I guess nearly ending up under another ladder truck really shook him up,” the other paramedic said. “Probably triggered some traumatic memories.”

The thing was, she was probably far more right than she could possibly imagine.

“I’ll take him home and throw Chris at him when he’s out of school,” Eddie said, as if he wasn’t so very concerned about how bad things had to be for Buck to not fight about being sent away before the end of his shift. “He’ll be right as rain in time for his next shift.”

“Home, huh?” Hen asked, smiling. Eddie shrugged, because he wasn’t going to deny that his home was Buck’s home, even if nearly all of the man’s things were still at the loft with Maddie. “So, come on, sit down. Tell us all about your date while we wait for Buck to get back.”

The date had been on Saturday, after Buck conspired with Hen and Karen for them to take Chris for a day and a night. Then he dragged Eddie to the beach with a basket of food and a cooler of drinks. It had been wonderful, but it also hadn’t been much different from anything else he and Buck did together.

Take out the kissing, add in Chris, and it pretty much would have been the exact same.

Then again, maybe that’s just how it went when you started dating your best friend.

“I don’t kiss and tell, Henrietta,” he said, evoking her full name just to watch her make the face.

“So there was kissing.” Chimney’s grin was shit-eating, and Eddie rolled his eyes.

“There might have even been a little bit of hand holding,” he snarked back, but moseyed over to the table all the same.

It was nice to sit around and talk with them again while they waited for Buck to come back from getting changed or for the bell to ring, whichever came first. Even if they did spend most of the time teasing the hell out of him and badgering him for details about Saturday. When his partner did finally come back, now wearing one of his hoodies and a pair of gym shorts, with his bag slung over his shoulder, Eddie was very much relieved.

“I guess we’re taking your truck?” Buck asked as they headed out, after saying their goodbyes.

“That we are,” Eddie confirmed. “We can come back for the jeep before or after we drop Chris off at school tomorrow.”

Buck was concerningly quiet on the ride home. Well, as quiet as the man ever was, because very rarely was a quiet Buck a silent Buck. He talked some about how Hen and Chimney had given him the same treatment that they did Eddie, trying to pry details about their picnic date at the beach out of him. And then teasing him for everything about it that they could think of. He talked about the call at the wellness retreat, where the guy had intentionally drugged a bunch of people with psychedelic mushrooms who had then chased him off a cliff when they found out.

He didn’t talk about the ladder truck nearly falling on his head. In fact, the moment he almost started to, he shut his mouth and really did go silent. It took Eddie coming up with a new topic of conversation - how, gasp, he’d seen Claudette actually laugh at something May had said - for Buck to start speaking again instead of just glaring out the window.

Or Eddie had assumed he was glaring, based on the set of his jaw.

“We have a couple hours before Chris needs to be picked up,” Buck said as they pulled into the driveway. “How are we filling the time?”

“I was thinking,” Eddie started, shutting off the engine and hopping out, “that I’ll get changed and then you’ll help me move everything in the bedroom and put down plastic so we can start on the walls.”

“Oh. Fun.”

“Needs doing, Buck. And it would’ve been done already if you hadn’t distracted me last week.”

“If I hadn’t- No, it was a mutual distraction!”

“Yeah, yeah. But no more distractions until it’s done.”

Buck huffed as they got inside, dropping his bag by the door as Eddie went to get changed out of his uniform. When they were both wearing clothes they wouldn’t mind getting paint on, he and Buck worked together to move all the furniture away from the patched-up wall. Soon there was plastic covering the floor and a can of paint was popped open.

“So, Pacific Pleasure, huh?” Buck mocked as he started with a roller on one end of the wall. “Looks rather blue.”

“I’m pretty sure I told you to shut up about this,” Eddie groused. Maybe he had decided to go with a blue instead of just more of the awful off-beige, and maybe Buck’s arguments had been part of the reason why. But he wasn’t going to just admit to it, not that easily.

“You did,” he confirmed with a grin and a nod. “But how often have I done what I was told?”

“You did today,” Eddie pointed out. “When Bobby told you to go home early and you did. Without any argument.”

“Would you have preferred that I fought him about it?” Buck asked, shoulders hunched up.

“Maybe. Bobby said you had a panic attack.”

“Of course I had a panic attack,” his partner snapped. “The ladder truck almost fell on my head, just like-”

Buck cut himself off, but Eddie finished the sentence for him.

“Just like the first time you told me you died. With the boulder,” he said. “But here you are, still not dead.”

“I just don’t understand why!” Buck shouted, nearly slapping the wall in his frustration before stopping himself at the last moment, his palm hovering off the wet paint. He tossed his paint roller into its tray. “I mean, I- It’s not like I want to hurry up and die, but today I thought- I thought that was it, y’know? The perfectly ironic way to go that would rub my nose in whatever the fuck I did wrong this time. I mean, getting crushed by a falling ladder truck? In May? Would have been poetic, I’m sure. I bet Ichabod would have spent forever cackling at me about it.”

Eddie did not quite understand what the month had to do with anything, but there were more important things to focus on.

“You’re talking like you’re expecting to be punished for something,” he said. “But we’ve talked about this, and there’s nothing you could have possibly changed since you’ve been back. Not knowingly. Not like you had with your leg.”

“Doesn’t mean that aren’t other things I fucked up.”

“Like what?” Eddie demanded. “You’ve made up for the awful, stupid night you came in here drunk. We’ve both been trying to make up for Christmas and everything leading up to it. What could you have possibly done that being crushed by a municipal vehicle, again, seemed like a suitable punishment?”

Predictably, Buck did not answer. Instead, he picked up the roller again and started angrily painting the wall, like he could literally cover up the argument.

“I asked you once what your nightmares were about, and I’m pretty sure I can guess some of them by now,” Eddie said, and watched as Buck got even harsher with the paint. “But you said you’d tell me one day, and then that only some of them were about you dying. And I told you we were going to talk about whatever you were avoiding.”

The other man continued to ignore him, and he let out a sigh.

“What could possibly be worse than everything you’ve told me so far?” he asked. “You can tell me. I am begging you to tell me.”

“Eddie, please,” Buck finally said. “Just drop it.”

“No.”

“Eds-”

“No.”

“What, you really want to hear about all the people that are dead because of me?” Buck demanded.

“Is this about the Dispatch fire?” Eddie asked, unbelieving. “I told you, even if the other me wasn’t there, someone-”

“Abby’s fiancé died because I wasn’t there! Because I was a fucking idiot and let my head get broken open, and so I wasn’t there to argue for that third option!” Buck snapped, finally giving Eddie his full attention and letting the paint roller fall onto the plastic. “People drowned in the tsunami that I could have saved, because I was too much of a fucking coward to be there! There- There’s a version of you who woke up the morning after to find Chris and Shannon gone, and m- me…”

“And you, what?” he asked, his own paint roller hanging uselessly by his side and dripping onto the plastic.

“I didn’t know they were going to be there,” Buck whispered. “I thought- Chris was only ever in danger because I put him in danger. And- And that time around, I managed to avoid the embolism, I managed to avoid being held back, so I- I started back on the day of the tsunami, and I was so happy to have an excuse to not be there. And then…”

Eddie wanted him to stop talking. He wanted to grab him and tell him to shut up, because he did not want to hear about this. He desperately did not want to listen to Buck tell him of a scenario where Chris was lost to them.

But he couldn’t bring himself to stop him.

“I didn’t even know Shannon was going to take him there, not until she was sending y- The other you a photo. We- We searched and searched, and then finally someone told us to look in the black tent,” he said, and nodded at the anguished sound Eddie let out. “And we- We found him. Just him. And he was… Afterwards, after we got you home, I… I cut myself. On some broken glass. 

“And I remembered how, uh. How every time before, Ichabod would send me back to the day of the truck bombing. So I- I was on the blood thinners again. For the clots. So I took a knife and-”

“Buck, stop,” Eddie begged. “Please, just- Stop.”

He felt like he was going to puke. The lunch Buck had sent him off with that morning was threatening to make a reappearance all over the paint-splattered plastic. This was worse, so much fucking worse, than whatever he could have possibly imagined Buck was going to tell him.

“I thought- I had thought that it was just rewinding everything, or- or rewriting,” Buck continued. “But that wasn’t- There is a version of you that woke up to find I had abandoned him in the worst way. A version of you who lost everything, and then had to find me-”

“I get it,” Eddie interrupted, trying his best not to snap at him. “I- I get it.”

Buck nodded and fell silent, picking up the paint roller he’d dropped. 

“Please tell me that was everything,” Eddie begged. “Please. Please tell me there’s nothing else.”

Buck stared at him for several long moments, saying absolutely nothing. And then, finally, he nodded again and started running the roller in the paint tray.

“There’s nothing else,” he said, and nothing about the words sounded convincing.

And Eddie, selfishly, didn’t call him out on it.

Chapter End Notes

Me, looking at the past few chapters: Y'know, y'all were right, there has been a lack of angst...

Eddie, immediately after this chapter: *furiously searching the internet for 'Therapist for Time Traveling Trauma'*

The Wolves Outside Your Door

Chapter Notes

Oh look, a new tag...

See the end of the chapter for more notes

“So, how are things going with you and Eddie?” Maddie asked.

They were seated outside on the balcony of what used to be Buck’s loft apartment, but which was now increasingly becoming more and more his sister’s. He had spent so many years living somewhere else - that two-bedroom apartment, and then with Eddie - that much of his attachment to the place had long since faded, so it wasn’t exactly a problem for him. Especially since it gave his sister a place to stay.

“Things are going well,” he answered, grinning dopily. And it was true, even with the minor to moderate hiccup from the day before, when Eddie had pressed and Buck had word vomited all over the plastic. Things had been awkward for the rest of the day - how could things not be, when Buck had pretty much confessed to once killing himself for a do-over? - but they were still going well. Very, very well.

“That’s good,” his sister said, and then reached across to smack him on the arm. Hard.

“What was that for?” he whined, rubbing where she hit.

“For letting me hear about it from Chimney first,” she answered with a huff.

“And I apologized for that!”

Evidently, the paramedic had not wasted any time before announcing to everyone in Buckaroo Intervention about the relationship upgrade. Buck found out about it when his sister had called him in the middle of filling out the paperwork Bobby handed him, to both congratulate and shout at him for not telling her. His ear had been left ringing for what felt like hours afterwards, and he nearly got whiplash from the way she bounced between the two modes.

“Yeah, you did,” Maddie nodded. “But I haven’t seen you in person since you got back from Texas, and you deserved to be hit for it.”

“Wait, which part did I deserve to be hit for?” Buck asked, confused at her wording. “Not telling you before everyone else, or for not seeing you since I got back?”

“Yes,” was her simple, utterly unhelpful answer, and she grinned when he rolled her eyes.

“Y’know, you could be a little nicer to me,” he whined. “Considering you’re living here rent-free.”

“Oh! Actually, I’m starting back at Dispatch tomorrow,” she told him, and he broke out in a grin. “So I can start covering rent.”

“That’s great, Mads! But you know you don’t have to-”

“I do, though,” she countered. “Remember, way back when? I told you I needed to stand on my own two feet, and that’s just as true now.”

“So, does that mean we should start talking about putting you on the lease?” he joked.

“Because you’re pretty much living with Eddie, right? You really do just fast-forward your relationships,” Maddie said, shaking her head with a smile, and he could only shrug. “And no. Maybe. I don’t know.”

“You don’t know,” Buck echoed. “Well, are you planning to stay here? Are you going to find somewhere else? Or are you going to move back in with Chimney?”

“I don’t know,” she repeated with a sigh. “When I talked to him about working again, so that we could figure out what it meant for Jee-Yun, he apparently thought I wanted to talk about seeing other people. He said that he was okay with it, if I wanted to start seeing other people.”

“Do you?” Buck carefully asked. “Mads, what exactly is going on with the two of you? Do you want to date someone else?”

“I still love him,” she said, an answer that wasn’t an answer. “But I… He didn’t tell me you were missing. I told Eddie about it, did he tell you?”

“Uh, n- no.” Buck was confused by the statement for the implication that she and Chimney had been in communication enough for him to leave something out.

“He sent me an email, a week or so after New Years,” Maddie said, and that made much more sense. He knew that Chimney had been sending her emails to keep her updated about Jee-Yun, and that she’d been receiving them. “A simple little sentence: Something’s happened to Buck. And I saw it, and I called him, and he told me you hadn’t been seen since Christmas. Then, when we got here, Hen said… She said that she had told him on New Years. It took him a week for him to decide to let me know that something had happened to you.”

“W- Well, maybe he just didn’t want to worry you?” he hedged.

“Maybe. But that wasn’t his choice to make,” she told him. “And I’m not sure I can forgive him for that.”

“Is… Is that why the two of you broke up?”

“It was part of it.”

The answer cracked his heart. It was just another example of how his stupid, selfish choices had broken things even more than they’d already been.

“I’m sorry,” he said.

“His choice, my choice,” she said. “Where did you go?”

“W- What?” The sudden topic change caught Buck off-guard.

“That month you were gone,” Maddie clarified, staring at him intently. “Where did you go?”

“I don’t remember.”

“Liar,” she accused, and it was so very much like that aborted argument they’d had back in April that he was exhausted already.

“Are we really doing this again?”

“You told Chris that Eddie found you,” she said, as if he hadn’t spoken. “Eddie told me that he woke up to find you in his house one night. Both of those things can’t be true.”

“Eddie found me at his house. That’s what I meant.”

“Liar,” she accused again. “You came back different, changed-”

“Seriously? This again?”

“Seeing you in January was like when I first came to LA, when we surprised each other in your girlfriend’s apartment. Like I’d missed years of your life again, instead of just months,” Maddie said, and Buck swallowed the instinctive denial. He had known, and Eddie had told him, that people were going to notice how different he was. “You broke up with your girlfriend within days of coming back, even though if you’d really just suffered a fugue, then nothing should have changed between the two of you since Christmas. And I’ve had a terrible time, trying to figure out how to describe the way you were, the way you’ve been. I thought it was depression. It made sense for it to be depression, and maybe that is part of it, but it’s not all of it. It took me until I was working through my own emotions about the months I missed with Jee that I figured it out, because I was feeling it, however smaller and lighter.

“You were in mourning.”

“Why would I be in mourning?” Buck asked, trying to sound unaffected and knowing that he failed.

“I don’t know,” his sister said with a shrug. “But in the time between January and you starting your not-dating with Eddie, you acted more like someone who had lost everything rather than someone who got their memories and their life back.”

Buck did not know a way to get himself out of this conversation, short of getting up and walking out. And that was very tempting, except it would simply be a postponement. Buck did not know a way to get himself out of this conversation, and he couldn’t see a way through this that didn’t end in an awkward trip to the psychiatric center, at least.

“You can tell me,” she told him. “Out of everyone, I think I’m the last person in a place to judge someone for breaking and running away to hide somewhere.”

“You wouldn’t believe me if I told you,” he grudgingly admitted, and watched her eyes light up in victory.

“So you do remember that month.”

“Yes.” No, because technically he missed most of that particular month all the same. “But there’s literally nothing I can think of that would get you to actually believe me, and not think I was crazy.”

It had taken the secret about his birth and Daniel’s death to convince the other Maddie that he hadn’t completely lost the plot. But he didn’t have anything like that this time. There was no secret that he could pull out like a grand reveal to-

The thought froze in its tracks, because that wasn’t true, was it? There was something that the other Maddie had told him that he hadn’t known before meeting Ichabod. He just spent so much time trying to not think about the topic it was attached to, he had nearly pushed it out of his mind entirely.

Maddie must have seen something on his face, because she sat up straighter in her seat.

“Buck?”

“Doug told you once that his mom nearly named him Jason,” he said, and heard his sister draw in a breath in a sharp hiss. “That’s where he got the name from, when he was here stalking you and Chimney.”

“H- How do you know that?” she asked. “I haven’t told anyone here about that.”

“You told me,” he said. “Or, a, uh… Another you did.”

“What?”

Buck sighed and shifted in his own chair and said,

“I want to start by saying that I’m not crazy…”

Buck was supposed to be working today, but Bobby had - gently, paternally - insisted that he take another day off. Apparently, having actual panic attacks in the middle of a call were a cause for concern. The only reason he hadn’t been pressured into going to see Frank right alongside Eddie was because, well, most people had panic attacks when they were nearly crushed by a falling ladder truck. That, and the fact that he wasn’t fighting the captain about taking it easy.

Actually, that latter fact was probably only making everyone else even more worried, but it was a ‘damned if he did, damned if he didn’t’ scenario.

He spent the day painting the bedroom walls, because while it was fun to camp out on a mattress in the living room, it got pretty old, pretty fast. And besides, he didn’t have anything else to do except watch trashy daytime television. Partway into the morning, his phone had chimed with a message from Maddie, telling him about her very first call back at Dispatch, and he’d sent her a selfie with a blue-stained thumbs up.

His sister had been very clearly overwhelmed when he left her the night before. He wasn’t certain if she totally believed him, the Jason/Doug secret not nearly as world-altering as the Daniel one, but she hadn’t accused him of imagining the whole thing. And she hadn’t gotten patronizing with her words and her tones, like the other Athena and Bobby had, when he first told them.

Buck hadn’t told her everything, he very rarely told anyone even close to everything, especially about the many deaths of himself. He’d told this Eddie far more than he ever told anyone else, although the other him might have told the other Eddie even more than that by this point. And God, that gave him a headache to think about, the fact that there even was another him now.

Well, hopefully there was another him. He only had Ichabod’s word to go off of, after all.

The point was, he’d told Maddie a more detailed version of the sanitized tale he gave Chris, and then escaped home when her questions started getting closer and closer to things he didn’t want to dwell on.

(“Okay, but why did I- did she- Why did the other Maddie tell you about Doug’s name almost being Jason? What brought that on? Actually, you never once mentioned Doug.”

“Y’know what, I should be getting gone, I promised Eds I’d pick something up at the store for dinner…”)

When it got close to time to pick up Chris, he washed up and changed out of his painter's clothes and headed off. Eddie’s schedule at Dispatch didn’t quite perfectly line up with school, letting him drop his kid off but often keeping him past pick-up. Usually it was Pepa or Carla who would take him home, but Buck had told them he’d handle it.

Why would he pass up the opportunity to hang out with his bestest friend forever a little more? Especially now that he no longer spent their time together looking at Buck like he was something nasty on the butt of his crutch.

Christopher had been very excited to see him, which was always a very nice feeling. He chattered the entire time to the Jeep about what they did that day, filling Buck in on the pre-teen melodrama that seemed to have increased tenfold this spring.

Everything was sunny, everything was happy, everything was fine. They bundled into the Jeep and pulled out onto the road, Chris’ window down to let the breeze in and the radio blasting pop songs from Buck’s own teenage years, because he absolutely refused to let Chris live in ignorance about the wonders of Britney Spears.

And then, just as he was pulling through an intersection,

“Look, Buck!” At the sound of his name, he glanced in the rearview mirror, at where Chris was sitting in the backseat on the far side from him, and saw it. It was big, and it was blue, and it was perched on the boy’s extended finger. “It came in through my window!”

“Chris-”

He was interrupted by the sound of a horn, and he turned his head in time to see the car coming straight at him. There was very little time to do much of anything, he knew. So very little time, so he did what little he could.

He twisted the steering wheel, angling them so Chris was as far away from what was about to happen as he could be. Even if that meant Buck was in even more danger.

Just before the car hit them, just before his head bounced off his window, Buck had time for the most absurd of thoughts:

“Every Goddamn May.”

“How’s your first day back been so far?” Eddie asked, sitting down in front of Maddie with his brown paper bag of lunch. She was looking… Overwhelmed. She had come in looking overwhelmed.

“It’s been fine,” she said, waving a fork at him. “It’s been wonderful, actually. But hey, I thought Buck was working today?”

“He was supposed to be, but Bobby pretty much made him take the day,” he told her. Eddie had felt like he needed an entire year, and he’d only heard about the shit Buck had lived through. And he knew, he knew, that there was something worse that the man simply hadn’t told him about. Because these days, his partner seemed to be a never-ending spring of traumatic experiences. “Why?”

“Oh, just, I texted him and Chimney about my first call and got this picture back,” Maddie said, showing the selfie her brother had sent her. “What room is that?”

“Our bedroom,” he answered, ignoring the way she smirked at the possessive plural. “He decided that the off-beige was boring, and that blue would help me sleep better.”

“Of course he did,” she said, sounding fond. “And- Did he pack that lunch for you?”

“He might have,” Eddie hedged, peeling off the traditional pink post-it note that was attached to his Saran wrapped sandwich. Today’s message - a simple yet punny, “feeling Blue without you” - had him smiling, as always.

Maddie reached over and snagged it out of his fingers, letting out a coo when she read it.

“And he leaves you notes, that’s so sweet,” she said, passing it back. “Please tell me you keep them, and not just throw them away.”

“I used to put them on my monitor, back when I had my own office, before the fire,” he admitted. “I don’t have anywhere to put them at, but, yes. I keep them.”

She let out another coo.

“You two,” she said, shaking her head. “He told me. Yesterday, when he was over. He told me. About Ichabod.”

“He mentioned it.” Specifically, Buck had mentioned that Maddie may or may not think he was crazy. “And you believe him?”

“Do you?” Maddie asked.

“I spent a night being yo-yoed between different versions of the same crossroads by a black-eyed not-demon, where I was scolded and insulted by my alternate universe double for all of my life choices,” Eddie told her, tone as dry as a Texan desert. “Yes. I believe him. But do you?”

“I’m not sure,” she admitted. “It seems so…”

“Impossible? Weird? Fantastic?”

“Yes. To all of those,” she said. “I want to believe him, I do. It explains some things. It explains a lot of things. He’s seemed so much older since he came back, and it makes sense that it’s because he is.”

“He thinks he’s thirty-five now. Ish.”

“Ish?”

“It’s your brother and math,” he pointed out, and she nodded in understanding. “How much did he tell you?”

“Not nearly as much as you, I think,” she said, cocking her head as she looked at him. “I got the impression he skipped over a lot.”

“Be thankful,” he muttered, and then stuffed his mouth with food before she could ask him to clarify that statement.

“He didn’t once mention Doug,” Maddie said, and now that he thought about it, Eddie didn’t think he could remember Buck saying anything about the man, either. “He told me about Shannon, about hurting his leg again and the tsunami, about the shooting and the blackout. But he didn’t mention Doug. Which is weird, because… He told me something about Doug that I hadn’t told him, but that the other me did. And when I asked him why I - she? - told him, he… Ran away.”

“That tracks.” Maybe he’d bring it up with Buck himself, and see what he got. Or, even better, maybe he’ll never ask Buck anything about the other timeline again, and live in blissful ignorance.

That seemed like the better, healthier option.

“How are you handling it? Knowing that he was with another version of you, before he came back?” she asked. “Because, I’ll admit, that is a very good reason for why you two waited so long to get together.”

“I’ve just been thinking about it like any of his other exes,” he told her. “He’s been having a harder time, because he doesn’t want to leave me feeling like I’m competing with a ghost of myself. Apparently, it was a problem he and the other me had.”

“That… Makes sense, I guess. For however much any of this makes sense,” Maddie commented, nodding to herself. “He hasn’t mentioned anything to you about that other Doug? He hasn’t said anything at all?”

“Not a thing.”

“Weird.”

They fell silent then, eating their lunches and texting people. In Eddie’s case, it was Buck. In Maddie’s it was presumably Chimney. Or also Buck. Buck was very capable of holding five conversations at once, and only occasionally getting them confused.

Eddie was about to get up to throw away his trash, when Maddie made a sudden noise and looked up at him, eyes wide.

“Oh my God,” she whispered. 

“What?”

“Oh. My. God.”

“What?” he asked again.

“Does this mean a ghost really did call Nine-One-One?”

“That’s one of the things I’m refusing to think about,” Eddie told her. Much like the Doug thing, which he absolutely refused to dwell on, because that way undoubtedly led to something possibly as traumatic as the last thing Buck had confessed to him about.

Or, he tried not to dwell on it. Except, now that Maddie had stuck the question in his ear, it refused to leave. And now that he thought about it, he thought that maybe Buck did mention Doug. Once, and only once, and he’d cut himself off. But, when telling the story of his first death - querido Dios - he’d said something about stopping Doug.

He had cut himself off, though, and then hurried past the slip. Eddie didn’t know in what way Buck stopped Doug, but going by the pattern, it was likely traumatic. 

He spent the rest of the afternoon trying not to dwell and dwelling all the same because of it, until May dragged him out of his thoughts.

“Sue? I have Claudette on the line,” she called out, and he went over as well, because Claudette was one of theirs. Even if she was a bitch. “She says her brakes failed and she hit someone at an intersection.”

Eddie frowned at the intersection address even as he typed out the road closure to send it out, because it was uncomfortably close to the school. Which should have been getting out around this time.

“Chris?”

Eddie twisted his neck so fast at Maddie’s voice that he felt it pop. He bounded over to her station, the road closure tweet forgotten.

“What about Chris?” he demanded as he scrambled for an ear piece, and she held up a finger.

“I have your dad right here, do you want-? Okay, here he is,” she said, looking up at him and nodding.

“Chris?” he asked into the earpiece.

“Dad?” Chris replied, and Eddie let out a relieved breath that he sounded okay.

“Are you hurt?”

“No,” he said, and then, “My chest, a little.”

“Okay.” He glanced at Maddie, who was typing as she listened in. “Where are you?”

“We were going home, on the way from school, and then a car hit us,” Chris told him, and Eddie frantically tapped Maddie’s shoulder, gesturing over at May and Sue when he had her attention. It took a moment, but then her face suddenly cleared with understanding and she ran over to them.

“What about Buck?” Eddie asked, because he knew his partner was doing pick-up today. He glanced over and saw Maddie staring at him with wide eyes. “Is he hurt?”

“I don’t know,” Chris said. “Buck? Buck!”

“Buck!”

Buck blinked his eyes open, groaning slightly at the headache forming. There was something… Something important. Something-

“Chris!” he shouted, twisting around to look in the backseat. Or, he tried, only to stop with a hiss of pain. His ribs had taken a beating, and when he looked down, the metal of the door was twisted around his leg. “Chris, are you…?”

“I’m fine,” the boy answered, and he let out a sigh of relief. “Dad wants to know if you’re hurt.”

“Can you put him on speaker, kiddo?” he asked. “I can’t reach you right now.”

“Buck?” As always, Eddie’s voice was exactly what he wanted to hear in these situations. 

“I really hope you hijacked another call,” he replied. “Because I know we taught our kid to call Nine-One-One first.”

“I hijacked Maddie’s,” Eddie said, and Buck let out a groan. “Are you hurt?”

“My ribs,” he answered. “My leg’s stuck. And uh, I hit my head?”

“Is that a statement or a question?”

“It’s whichever one makes you worry less.”

“Buck.”

“I hit my head,” he said, giving in. He reached up and felt around, hissing at both the pain that sparked and the blood he found on his fingers when he drew them back. “Maybe a slight concussion? Not the worst I’ve ever had. A small one. I can totally go into work on my next shift.”

“I think it’s bleeding,” Chris chimed in.

“Traitor,” he hissed at him, without much feeling.

“Buck.” His name again, with a mixture of fondness and exasperation that only Eddie - either version of the man - ever managed to get right. “What did you mean, your leg’s stuck? Does it hurt?”

“I think part of the door is wrapped around it,” he said. “And yeah, it hurts. Also, I can feel my toes wiggling. If you’re about to ask that.”

“The One Twenty-two are on their way, they should be there soon,” Eddie said.

“Not…?” Buck trailed off, because he didn’t need to finish the question.

“They’re stuck on another call,” his partner told him. “Talk to me, what happened?”

“Well, we were on the way home and listening to the greatest artist of our generation-”

“Agree to disagree.”

“Your musical tastes are bad and you should feel bad,” Buck mocked. “We were at the intersection when-”

“There was a butterfly,” Chris interrupted. “It flew through my window and landed on my finger.”

“A butterfly?” Eddie’s voice was stiff.

“It was so big and blue,” the kid said. “It’s gone now, though.”

“Then there was a car horn,” Buck said. “And then-”

The sound of sirens approaching interrupted him, and he peered around to see the fire truck and ambulances finally arriving.

“They’re here,” he said.

“Good,” Eddie said. “I’ll see you at the hospital, okay? Both of you.”

“Okay, dad,” Chris said.

“What about Mads?” Buck asked.

“I’ll meet you there, too,” she broke in, and he’d forgotten that she’d been listening in as well.

There was soon the sound of running boots as they were swarmed by first responders. Some of them he recognized at a glance, their faces at least.

“Greenway!” one of them ordered. “Get the kid in the back, check him out.”

“On it!”

Buck tried to keep track of what was going on with Chris, even as he was partially deafened by the sound of the jaws being used on his door. He knew someone opened the back passenger door and started asking the kid questions, and he knew they got him out of the jeep. At one point, someone reprimanded Buck for moving his head so much, and he realized he should have known to keep still. He’d just been too focused on making sure Chris was okay to worry about a possible spinal injury.

Perhaps the concussion was worse than he thought.

Buck heard someone ask Chris if he wanted something for the pain. Was Chris hurt that bad? He’d said he was fine, and Eddie hadn’t sounded concerned. But it was Chris, maybe he really was hurt and he’d just been lying about being okay.

“Come on, sir, let’s get you out of there,” someone said, and he found himself being manhandled onto a gurney. He twisted his head around to try and catch a glimpse of his kid, ignoring the reprimands and reminders to keep his head still, and let out a breath of relief when he spotted him standing at the back of an ambulance, one of the 122’s paramedics with him.

And then, the breath left him entirely as he watched Chris collapse.

“Chris?” he called, pushing himself up on the gurney. Someone grabbed at him, but he smacked them away as he got to his feet. His left leg twinged with pain, but he ignored it. “Chris!”

The paramedic - Greenway, according to his coat - started doing compressions, as another rushed to assist.

“Chris!” Buck screamed, trying to run for him. Someone grabbed at his arm. He pulled himself free, pushed someone else away. “Let me- Chris!”

“Shit,” someone near him swore. “I need some help, here!”

“Chris!” Buck screamed. Arms wrapped around him, pulling him back. There was a grunt as his elbow hit something soft. “Chris!”

There was a sting in his arm, even as he tore himself free again.

“Chris!” he screamed again, and for some reason his limbs started feeling like lead as black started swirling along the edges of his vision.

The last thing he saw was Chris on the ground, Greenway crouched over him.

Chapter End Notes

(I'll hide now.)

Three Dreams and a Nightmare

Chapter Notes

I appreciate how you were all ready to personally murder a fictional character.

This is a dream.

Buck knows it’s a dream, because he doesn’t quite remember what he was doing before this moment in time. He knows it’s a dream, because it’s like there’s a fogginess clinging to most of the details. He knows it’s a dream, because he’s seated at Eddie’s dining table and sitting across from him is Shannon.

Although, he can’t quite remember why her being there means it’s a dream.

“This is really good,” she tells him, gesturing at her plate with her fork.

“Thank you,” he tells her, though he doesn’t know if he even deserves the credit. His own plate is empty in front of him. Not empty in the way that implies he’d cleared it, but empty like it never had anything on it to begin with. “Uh, what were we talking about?”

“You were telling me about the trip to Texas,” she answers, and he nods, because that made sense. “And how they liked you better than me.”

“I wouldn’t say that,” he laughs. “Pretty sure they were just trying to, uh. Keep the peace?”

That wasn’t what he wanted to say, but the right words are escaping him.

“They didn’t make any slights about your parenting skills?” she asks. “Or insult you with every second word?”

“Not even once,” he says. “They didn’t really know how to act around me, I guess. As Eddie’s boyfriend, I mean. And every time I saw them before, there was an argument about Chris.”

“Are you telling me they didn’t have any opinions about how their darling grandson was being raised?”

“Nope!” Buck pops the p, and laughs at the disbelief evident on her face. “Though, I got to watch Eddie and his dad get into it anyway.”

“Y’know, I never got to see him fight with his parents all that often,” Shannon says, resting her chin on her palm. “That would require him to be there at the same time I was. What did they fight about?”

“Happy childhood stories that turned out to not be as happy as they told it,” he answers. “I might have torn into Ramon a bit, too.”

“The marriage offer is still there,” she reminds him with a smirk. “If you ever decide to upgrade to the better Diaz.”

“You just want me for my money.”

“You,” she stresses the words, “aren’t the one with the money.”

It’s a statement that has Buck frowning, because it didn’t sound right, but it also rang true.

“Eddie’s world is better and brighter simply because I exist within it,” he says, instead of dwelling on it. “Or so he told his dad.”

“Oh, swoon.”

“You should have gone with us,” Buck says, nudging her foot with his own. “We would have protected you from the big, bad Diazes.”

“I wouldn’t go back to El Paso to save my life,” Shannon tells him, rolling her eyes. “Which honestly doesn’t mean much, considering that I’m dead.”

Oh.

Right.

That’s why her being there means it’s a dream.

“You forgot, didn’t you?” she asks, sounding like she was laughing at him.

“I might have,” he sheepishly admits.

“Rude. You’re having an entire conversation with a ghost, and it doesn't even dawn on you.”

“Are you a ghost?”

“This is a dream, Buck,” she says. “I’m whatever the hell your subconscious wants me to be.”

“And if my subconscious wants you to be nicer?”

“I’m whatever the hell your subconscious wants me to be,” she repeats. “Except that.”

He lets out a sigh, before pointing at her plate and asking,

“Want seconds?”

“Buck!” 

Maddie could hear someone start to stir in the background of the call as she listened in, even as she told Sue and May that she was on the line with the people Claudette hit. She had not expected this on her very first day back, had not expected to answer someone’s call for help and hear Chris’ voice on the other end.

“Chris!” she heard her brother shout, and she went back over so she could start typing down what the first responders were going to need to know. “Chris, are you…?”

“I’m fine,” she can hear the boy answer. “Dad wants to know if you’re hurt.”

“Can you put him on speaker, kiddo?” she hears Buck ask. “I can’t reach you right now.”

“Buck?” Eddie’s voice was calm and barely betrayed the fear and worry that Maddie could see on his face.

“I really hope you hijacked another call,” Buck quipped. “Because I know we taught our kid to call Nine-One-One first.”

Our kid. Maddie would normally be all over that to tease them, but now wasn’t the time. She was definitely going to tease them both for it later, though. When everyone was okay.

“I hijacked Maddie’s,” Eddie said, and Buck let out a groan. “Are you hurt?”

“My ribs,” he answered, and Maddie started typing away. “My leg’s stuck. And uh, I hit my head?”

“Is that a statement or a question?”

“It’s whichever one makes you worry less.”

“Buck.”

“I hit my head,” her brother finally confessed, and Maddie was quick to inform the 122 - the responding station - of a possible head injury. “Maybe a slight concussion? Not the worst I’ve ever had. A small one. I can totally go into work on my next shift.”

“I think it’s bleeding,” Chris chimed in.

“Traitor.”

“Buck.” Eddie was somehow, despite everything, managing to smile a little. “What did you mean, your leg’s stuck? Does it hurt?”

“I think part of the door is wrapped around it,” he said. “And yeah, it hurts. Also, I can feel my toes wiggling. If you’re about to ask that.”

“The One Twenty-two are on their way, they should be there soon,” Eddie said.

“Not…?” Buck trailed off, but both Maddie and Eddie knew what he was going to ask.

“They’re stuck on another call,” Eddie answered the unspoken question. “Talk to me, what happened?”

“Well, we were on the way home and listening to the greatest artist of our generation-”

“Agree to disagree.”

“Your musical tastes are bad and you should feel bad,” Buck mocked. “We were at the intersection when-”

“There was a butterfly,” Chris interrupted. “It flew through my window and landed on my finger.”

“A butterfly?” Eddie’s voice was stiff, and when she looked up, Maddie saw his face had paled. She didn’t understand why. What was it about a butterfly that had him looking so worried?

“It was so big and blue,” Chris said. “It’s gone now, though.”

“Then there was a car horn,” Buck said. “And then-”

He broke off as the sirens became audible on the other end of the line.

“They’re here,” he said.

“Good,” Eddie said. “I’ll see you at the hospital, okay? Both of you.”

“Okay, dad,” Chris said.

“What about Mads?” Buck asked.

“I’ll meet you there, too,” she answered, because she was not going to let her brother think she had just abandoned him. Covering her mic, she told Eddie, “Go talk to Sue, I’ll tell you where they’re taking them to as soon as I know.”

Eddie didn’t even bother saying anything, he just nodded at her and ran off to where the woman in question was still hovering over May. Maddie stayed there, eavesdropping on the radio chatter coming from the 122. She was mostly waiting to hear which hospital they were going to take them to, but she couldn’t help but pan for gold about her brother’s well-being, and Chris’.

Was Chris her nephew, now? Would it be too presumptuous to start calling him that? 

Finally, finally, she got the info she wanted - Byrne Memorial - and started to get up to tell Eddie. But then, one of the paramedic’s broke in with something that turned her heart to ice.

“Dispatch, inform them that we have a twelve-year old with CP,” they said. “Complained of chest pains before suffering from sudden cardiac arrest.”

“What?” Maddie blurted out. She frantically looked around, and caught both Sue and May staring at her wide-eyed. Of course, they were probably listening in as well. But where was Eddie?

“We got him back, but let them know.”

“What?” Maddie said again.

“We’ll tell them,” Sue said, stepping in and taking over. “Maddie-”

But Maddie didn’t stick around to listen to whatever Sue was going to say. She took off her ear piece and flung it to her desk, before leaping to her feet and looking around for Eddie. She spotted him coming around the corner with his bag over his shoulder, from the direction of the shared break room, and sprinted towards him.

“Hey, you got the hospital?” he asked.

“Yeah.”

“Then let’s…” he trailed off, finally taking in her appearance. And she wasn’t sure what her face looked like, but she watched his drop even further. “What is it?”

“Eddie…”

“What is it?” he repeated. “Is it Buck?”

“No, it’s… Eddie, it’s Chris.”

His bag fell to the floor with a thump.

This is a dream.

Buck knows it’s a dream, because he doesn’t quite remember what he was doing before this moment in time. He knows it’s a dream, because it’s like there’s a fogginess clinging to most of the details. He knows it’s a dream, because he’s sitting at a table in the station’s loft, playing cards with a pair of dead men.

“Do you have any twos?” Doug Kendell asks, looking intently at his hand.

“Go fish,” Mitchell Trent tells him, and Doug lets out a curse as he draws another card.

“Oh, hell no,” Buck says, throwing down his cards. It’s a dead man’s hand: a pair of eights and a pair of aces, clubs and spades. “I am not doing this.”

“What’s wrong, kid?” Mitchell is grinning at him, clearly pleased about something. “Not happy to see us?”

“I definitely wasn’t happy to see him,” Doug snipes. “Y’know, before he murdered me.”

“It’s like the set-up for a joke,” Mitchell says. “Two murderers and a wife-beater walk into a fire station…”

“Would you shut up!?” Buck snaps. “And stop comparing me to yourself!”

“If the glove fits…”

“I’m surprised he even had the balls,” Doug said, giving Buck a nasty glare. “He was always a snot-nosed brat growing up, constantly whining. All, ‘oh Maddie, please don’t leave me’ and ‘Mommy, please love me.’”

“This conversation is really relieving me of my guilt,” Buck tells him, and gets a card thrown at his face.

“Hey, hey, none of that,” Mitchell scolds. “We’re trying to play here. Buck, have any sevens?”

Buck stares at him, and then down at his plainly visible cards laying on the table, none of which were sevens.

“No.”

“Worth a shot,” Mitchell says, drawing a card. “Your turn, kid.”

Buck glares at him, before reaching out and swiping the cards off the table and onto the floor.

“You really have to ruin absolutely everything,” Doug grumbles, tossing his hand of cards carelessly onto the floor, as well. “Can never just leave well enough alone.”

“Oh, shut the hell up,” Buck snaps.

“Or what, you’ll murder me again?”

“Now, now, children. Play nice,” Mitchell chides, shuffling the cards he had kept in his hand and so didn’t end up on the floor with the others.

“Fuck you,” Buck snaps again. “Do you know how many nights Eddie spent not sleeping, thanks to you and your asshole friend?”

“That’s pretty boy, right? What did I do?”

“You listed off his address and then threatened to go after his kid!”

“Oh, right, I did do that,” Mitchell concedes, without an ounce of guilt on his face. “Hey, I got another joke. Three dead people sit down to play cards…”

“I’m not dead though,” Buck points out.

“Now,” Doug retorts. 

“Hey, that’s actually another thing we both have in common!” Mitchell is all jovial as he says it. “We both killed ourselves for our kids.”

“Would you stop comparing yourself to me?” Buck begs.

“Stop your whining, Evan,” Doug snaps. “Pick the cards up and deal, I want to play another game. Without you ruining things.”

“Y’know,” Mitch says as he leans in close to Buck, like they’re conspiring together. “We could get another syringe from one of the ambulances downstairs. He won’t be able to talk if he’s got another air bubble in his brain.”

Buck doesn’t say anything. He just gets up and walks away, like he should have at the beginning.

It was a minor kitchen fire that had them called out. A new parent got distracted by their crying infant and suddenly their lunch was a bit overdone. Luckily, it was noticed before it got too bad and they’d gotten there before it spread beyond the counters.

Now, Bobby had them doing clean-up, while Hen checked over both the profusely apologizing new mom and her infant.

“You’re both fine,” he heard her saying, sounding subtly amused.

“I feel so stupid,” the mother said. “He started crying and I went to find out what was wrong, and never once thought to turn off the burner.”

“It happens,” Hen assured her. “It takes awhile to get into the swing of things, and it’s not like anyone got hurt.”

“Just my pride. And my kitchen.”

“Both of those things can be fixed.”

Bobby got distracted by the sound of a phone ringing. He turned his head to where the others were putting away their gear, spotting Chimney digging out his phone with a smile.

“Hey, Maddie, how’s your-” He cut off, his face dropping along with the hose he’d been rolling up. “What?”

It’s the way his voice sounds that has everyone around him freezing in their tracks and giving him their attention. Ravi pauses in the middle of putting away an oxygen tank, and Lucy is still carrying her end of the hose Chimney had just dropped. Even the mother has turned a curious gaze upon him.

“Okay, okay, I- I’ll tell them,” the paramedic is saying. “Byrne Memorial? Okay.”

The call goes that way for a couple more minutes, him giving Maddie more reassurances before he manages to get off the phone. The moment he does, he’s looking up at Bobby with an expression that does not promise anything good.

“What’s going on?” the captain asks, making his way to his side.

“It’s, uh. It’s Buck and Chris,” Chimney answers, and Bobby’s heart drops much like Chimney had dropped the hose. “They were in a car accident.”

“Are they okay?”

“They had to sedate Buck.”

What?

“Why?” he demanded. “What about Chris?”

“They’re taking them to Byrne Memorial,” he says, a non-answer that told Bobby everything he needed to know. He spun around, barking out orders.

“Everyone, finish up what you’re doing!” He watched as they all double-timed, an undercurrent of anxiety present in every motion, and then grabbed his radio. “Dispatch, this is Captain Nash, One Eighteen. We’re going off-line.”

“Copy that, One Eighteen,” May responds, and then, “Hurry.”

This is a dream.

Buck knows it’s a dream, because he doesn’t quite remember what he was doing before this moment in time. He knows it’s a dream, because it’s like there’s a fogginess clinging to most of the details. He knows it’s a dream, because he’s standing on top of a half-submerged fire truck with Ichabod.

An Ichabod who is, for some reason, fishing off the edge with a pink, child-sized fishing rod.

“Howdy, Buck.”

“Howdy,” Buck replies without much feeling. “What, exactly, are you fishing for?”

“Compliments,” the not-demon answers, his black eyes focused on the waters below.

“And uh, where did you get that rod from?”

“Beats me, it’s your dream.”

Buck rolls his eyes and looks around. They are entirely surrounded by water, with barely a hint of anything else just beneath the surface. As he watches, a piece of the pier floats by them, carried away by a current that he couldn’t make out.

“You’re not actually here, are you?” he has to ask. “Because, I’ve gotta tell you, I did not miss you.”

“Do you really think I have nothing better to do than haunt your dreams?”

“Honestly? Yeah.”

“You’re probably right,” Ichabod sighs, an act that absolutely convinces Buck that this was a mere dream, because he didn’t think he had ever seen the entity do something so mundane.

Something tugs on the fishing line, and the not-demon reels it in. Buck watches as something breaks the surface, a waterlogged person wearing a salmon-colored button up over a white tee. The birthmark above their eye gives the game away entirely.

“No one wants that,” Ichabod tsks, unhooking his catch and throwing it back into the water.

“Y’know, maybe I should go back to therapy,” Buck remarks, watching himself sink out of sight.

“If anyone needs it, it’s probably you,” the not-demon says, casting out the line again. “Maybe that’s why I’m here?”

“To be my therapist? I hope not,” he replies. “Actually, why are we here?”

“Maybe we’re lost?” Ichabod suggests.

“I am feeling kinda lost,” Buck admits. “This is a dream, right?”

“Didn’t I already say that?” Ichabod asks, black eyes scowling at him.

“You did,” he admits. “I just don’t remember my dreams being so… Meta.”

“It’s probably the sedative they gave you.”

“Sedative?”

“Yes, sedative. Don’t you remember?”

And Buck… vaguely remembers. He remembers a sting in his arm. He remembers someone - many someones - grabbing and pulling at him. He remembers there had been something he needed to do. It had been important, so very important, and now he could not remember.

“I… I was trying to get to something…”

“Quite insistently. I think you might have thrown an elbow or two.”

“What was I trying to get to?” Buck asks.
