“Why are you asking me? I’m just your dream,” Ichabod reminds him.

As Buck watches, the fishing line gets caught again. Like last time, there’s no fish when he reels it in. It’s a pair of glasses, with the hook caught on the strap attached to them.

“Maybe these’ll help you remember,” Ichabod tells him, handing Buck the glasses. “And you know what you need to do now, right?”

“No,” Buck tells him. “What do I need to do now?”

“Take a dive,” the non-demon answers, and gives him a shove. Buck stumbles over his feet and then over the edge.

Someone’s name leaves his lips as he hits the water.

Buck opened his eyes, feeling the far too familiar muffled headache that told him he both had a concussion and was on painkillers for it, and probably for the ache in his side and leg, too. There was someone holding his hand, and he turned his head. Maddie was sitting beside him, focused instead on her phone. Through the window behind her, he could see the sun was nearly set.

“Mads?” he croaked out, and the sound of his voice nearly had her dropping her phone.

“Hey there, sleepyhead.” Her relieved smile was a bit strained.

“What happened?”

“What do you remember?”

“I…” What did he remember? He remembered painting the bedroom walls. He remembered going to pick up Chris from school. He remembered the butterfly, the car horn, the sudden impact. He remembered hearing Eddie’s voice, and then the paramedics arriving.

He remembered the both of them being removed from the jeep.

He remembered-

“Chris!” he cried out, struggling to sit up. “Where is he? Where’s Chris?”

“He’s down in the PICU,” Maddie told him, trying to get him to lay back down. “Eddie’s with him. They got him back, okay? He’s fine. They said he’s going to be- Buck!”

Buck had started yanking off the wires they’d attached to him, and then pulled out the IV. He ignored the way his sister tried to get him to stop, and ignored the way his leg threatened to give when he put weight on it.

“Buck, what are you doing?” Maddie demanded, getting between him and the door. “You shouldn’t be-”

“I am going to see my kid,” he snapped. “Now either help me get there or get out of the way.”

Buck didn’t give her the chance to make the choice, sidestepping her and limping out of the room. It took a moment, spent leaning on a wall, to figure out where the elevators were. He quickly made his way down the hall, in the direction a sign pointed, with Maddie hurrying along behind him.

“At least let me get a- Buck! At least let me get a wheelchair.”

“What floor?” he demanded as he pressed the button for the elevator.

“Third,” she told him, and he nodded as the doors opened.

It must have been a sight, he thought. A grown man in a hospital gown limping his way through the pediatric unit, leaning on every wall or column that he passed. Maddie finally accepted that she wasn’t going to stop or slow down her brother or get him to take it easier, and so had opted to lead him to the right room.

The moment he stepped into it, he had to cling to the wall or risk collapsing.

Chris lay in the hospital bed, asleep and so very small. There was an IV in the crook of his left arm, and a heart monitor keeping close track of everything. His glasses were sitting on the table beside the bed, on the same side his father was sitting on.

Buck must have made a noise, because Eddie suddenly looked up and jumped to his feet, dropping Chris’ hand.

“What are you doing out of bed?” he demanded, grabbing Buck and shoving him into the seat he’d just vacated. “You might not have broken your leg, but you shouldn’t be putting any weight on it.”

“He wanted to see Chris,” Maddie answered for him.

“Of course, he did.” Eddie sighed. “I tried to convince them to just stick the two of you in the same room, but…”

“I’ll go make sure the nurses know where you are,” she said. “So they don’t lockdown the hospital and start a manhunt.”

“I don’t understand,” Buck said, staring down at Chris’ mess of curls instead of watching his sister leave. “He- He was fine. He was awake, he was talking. He was fine.”

“They said he just collapsed at the scene,” Eddie told him. “They didn’t really know why. He complained about chest pains, but there’s nothing except a bruise over his chest from the seatbelt, and I don’t know if…”

“One of the paramedics gave him something,” Buck said, remembering. “He… He asked if he wanted something for the pain…”

“What?” Eddie demanded, protective rage clear as day. “Do you know which paramedic?”

“I think… I think it was someone named Greenway?” Buck had been too busy trying to keep track of Chris to keep track of the paramedics, but Greenway was the name the 122’s captain had mentioned. And Greenway had been the name on the back of the man crouched over Chris, before Buck lost consciousness.

“We’ll ask him what he remembers when he wakes up,” Eddie said.

“Right,” Buck said dully, unable to tear his eyes away from their kid in the bed.

This wasn’t a dream.

Buck knew it wasn’t a dream, because he remembered quite well each and every step up to this moment. He knew it wasn’t a dream, because there was no fogginess clinging to the details, no, they were quite clear and concise.

This wasn’t a dream.

This was a nightmare.

The Butterfly

Chapter Notes

Notice: Almost all of my medical knowledge comes medical dramas. Do not @ me about any medical inaccuracies herein

As it turned out, the nurses and doctors tended to be a mite upset when their patient wakes up and immediately goes missing. Especially when that patient had injuries from being t-boned by a car. Buck wasn’t precisely sure what his injuries were, beyond an injured leg that wasn’t broken. He was mostly going judging based on the pain, which was gradually making itself more and more known the longer he sat in Chris’ room.

His left side, starting from just below his nipple and going down, was pretty much one big bruise. There was something wrong with his ribs, judging from the pain he felt whenever he took a deep breath. His left leg had complained the entire trip to the PICU, which was concerning but at least it hadn’t collapsed under him. He had a minor headache from hitting his head on his window.

Honestly, it probably could have been worse, except his jeep sat rather high up and the car that hit him had been rather low to the ground. The only reason none of his shoulder muscles had been torn was because he had relaxed his hold on the steering wheel at the very last moment, as he reflexively raised his hands to shield himself.

The nurse who stomped into Chris’ room ten minutes after he arrived reminded him very much of Athena, enough that he almost started apologizing before remembering that he wasn’t sorry at all. It took a moment, but then he realized it was the glare she was aiming at him.

“Did you walk down here?” she demanded.

“Yes?”

For a brief moment, Buck thought he was about to be strangled to death by a medical professional.

“I’m going to get a wheelchair, and then you-”

“No,” he interrupted, without an ounce of guilt. “I’m staying here.”

“Mr. Buckley, you’re not even supposed to be up and about yet at all. You need to be-”

“I need to be here,” he told her. “I am staying here. If I can’t stay here as a patient, then get me the papers so I can check myself out.”

The nurse huffed and looked past him to Eddie for support, who immediately shook his head.

“I said to just stick them both in the same room,” he said.

“Don’t look at me,” Maddie said, raising her hands when the nurse turned to look at her. “I helped him get down here.”

The nurse’s eye twitched as she visibly took in calming breaths.

“I will get you a wheelchair,” she said. “The moment visiting hours are over, you’re going back upstairs.”

And then she turned and stomped out, as if she could win the argument simply by no longer being there to be argued with.

“You’re not leaving when visiting hours are over, are you?” Maddie asked, and Buck shook his head.

“No. No I’m not.”

“She sounded angry,” a weak voice croaked from the bed, and Buck almost reinjured his ribs twisting in his seat.

“She was very angry,” Eddie agreed, bending down to bury his face in his son’s curl. “How are you feeling, mijo?”

“Thirsty,” Chris answered. “And my chest hurts.”

“We’ll get you something to drink in a bit,” his father told him. “But does… Does it hurt like it did when you were on the phone with me?”

“Hurts more,” the boy grumbled, and the adults in the room all shared a look before Maddie went to fetch a nurse. Luckily, not the angry one.

Chris’ chest was hurting more because Greenway had broken a couple ribs, which was distressingly common and to be expected when doing CPR, but they still wanted to make sure there was nothing else now that he was awake. And besides the bruise from his seatbelt, there wasn’t anything else. There was nothing of note or worry on either the echocardiogram or the electrocardiogram, and the monitor had been keeping a steady beat his entire stay.

There was nothing, absolutely nothing, to explain what had happened.

Maddie left shortly before visiting hours ended, and just before the angry nurse from before showed back up with a wheelchair. It was adorable, how she honestly thought Buck was going to willingly leave Chris’ bedside. By the end of the argument, she very much looked as if she wanted to sedate him again and drag him back to his room, but ended up just throwing her hands in the air and walking away. But at least Eddie had his seat back.

“Do you think she’ll kill me if I call her back here for another dose of pain meds?” Buck asked, idly rolling himself back and forth in his wheelchair. Chris had fallen back asleep, resting on a veritable mountain of pillows. He’d probably be moved to the pediatric unit the next day, where he’d almost definitely spend another night or two. 

“Your ribs starting to bother you?” Eddie asked, glancing up from where he’d been watching his own thumb rub circles onto the back of his kid’s hand.

“And my leg,” Buck admitted. “Um, I’ll probably need you to keep the pills, when they give me the prescription for pain meds, depending on what they prescribe me. Or- Or Carla can.”

“Okay.”

“It’s just that, the ones they gave me for my leg the second time around helped me sleep like, really well. With like, no dreams, or none that I could remember or that woke me up. And they made me feel calm and made everything kinda fuzzy? And so, uh, so I realized I was looking forward to them because they made me feel good more than how much they helped with the pain, and-”

“Buck,” Eddie interrupted, placing his free hand on Buck’s knee. “It’s okay. You don’t need to explain it to me. And we’ll make sure to ask for something non-opioid.”

“Thank you,” Buck whispered, ducking his head and taking the man’s hand in his.

“You’re welcome. But the pills they gave you the first time around didn’t…?”

“They did, just... I read somewhere once that most addictions start as attempts at self-medication, and I didn’t have all of the, um. Baggage. The first time around.” He didn’t have the specter of what he did to Doug clinging to his every moment. Not wanting to think about that particular secret, he switched topics. “I’m going to be out of work for at least a month with these ribs. Probably closer to a month and a half.”

“That sucks, because that means you’re going to miss my first day back at the station.”

The words had Buck nearly breaking his neck with how fast he turned his head to stare at Eddie.

“Your first-? What?”

“I got the email this morning, before…” He gestured at Buck and Chris. “I was going to surprise you with the news.”

“That’s wonderful,” Buck told him, grinning. “When’s your first day?”

“The first of June,” Eddie said, before closing his eyes in apparent exhaustion. “Which means I have another couple weeks of working at Dispatch. We would have had to figure out how to get everyone where they needed to be, since we’re a single-vehicle household at the moment.”

The reminder of the crash sent Buck’s mood crashing, and he slumped a bit.

“This is my fault,” he whispered. “I’m sorry.”

“You’re- What?” His eyes snapped open to fix him with an incredulous look. “What are you-? Are you serious, Buckley?”

“The butterfly was there, Eds,” Buck pointed out, not meeting his eyes. “It was there. Whatever the hell I did, it-”

“Shut up. You didn’t cause the crash. You didn’t… You didn’t stop Chris’ heart. You didn’t do any of this,” Eddie said. “If it’s anyone’s fault, it’s mine.”

“How the-!?” Buck cut himself off when he realized he’d raised his voice and quickly made sure Chris was still asleep, before continuing in a harsh whisper. “How the fuck is it your fault?”

“I didn’t talk to you.” When Buck simply stared at him, uncomprehending, Eddie clarified, “I didn’t talk to you before Christmas. I blindsided you, and you panicked, and that’s what started this whole mess.”

“I started this whole mess by running away and getting lost,” Buck said, letting go of his hand and reaching to grab his shoulder, because he needed his partner to listen. “My choice, my actions.”

“You said it yourself, you wouldn’t have ran if I had talked to you.”

“You don’t control my actions, Eddie. You’re not at fault for my mistakes.”

“I am when my actions and my mistakes led to them,” Eddie said, and Buck let out a sigh. The other man could be just as self-flagellating as he was, and just as stubborn.

“Why didn’t you talk to me first?” he asked, instead of ramming himself into that wall again.

“Because I knew I was making a mistake,” his partner answered. “I knew, even as I did it, that I was making an awful, horrible mistake. I was scared even then, and when Christopher got upset, it was the excuse I needed. I had the opportunity to run away and hide, and not look like a coward for doing so. And I knew that if I talked to you about it first, you’d try and convince me not to do it. You would convince me not to do it, because I wouldn’t be able to say no to you.”

“I wouldn’t have tried to change your mind,” Buck said, and Eddie’s eyebrows were raised in obvious disbelief. “If you told me that leaving the station was what you needed to do, and you needed me to support you in that, I would have. I wouldn’t have been happy, I would have hated every moment of it, but I would have supported you. Especially if you told me it was for Chris.”

“That’s what the other Eddie said,” his partner told him. “While he was ripping me a new one for everything. That you loved me too much not to. And then threw in my face that I did the same thing with you that I did with Shannon, making a decision that affected you and not letting you have a say.”

“I mean, it wasn’t quite the same,” Buck hedged. “Shannon was your wife, I was just… I was just your friend.”

“You haven’t been just a friend in years,” Eddie corrected him. “Chris has been our kid since long before we finally got our heads out of our asses.”

“I think that’s what hurt the most,” he confessed, shifting in the wheelchair and wincing as the movement jostled his side. “After you told me about the will, it felt like you started to just drift away, like how you didn’t tell me at all about your panic attacks at first. And when you told us you were leaving the station, without even giving me a hint that it was something you were thinking about, it felt like whatever future I’d imagined with the two of you was just that. Imaginary. Like I’d just been suffering from an overinflated idea of what I was to the two of you. Like… Like I’d deluded myself into thinking I was important enough for you to trust me with the big things, and then it turned out I wasn’t.”

“I didn’t talk to you first because your opinion was too important.” Eddie shook his head, looking like he couldn’t decide if he wanted to laugh or cry at the irony of it all. “I was so pissed at you, when you left that day. There I was, freaking out over this life-changing decision I was making and already regretting, and you just turned and walked away. Didn’t even stop to say goodbye to Chris.”

“I’m so sorry for that,” Buck said, blinking away tears. “I am so, so sorry for that. I regretted it as soon as I stopped panicking and actually started thinking, but by then… You needed me and I abandoned you, and I don’t think I’ll ever quite manage to forgive myself for it.”

“You didn’t abandon me,” Eddie refuted, and Buck immediately shook his head in denial. “You didn’t. And… And I forgive you. That’s what it means to be a team, remember?”

At the words, Buck burst out in wet laughter that was quickly cut off with a grimace. He clutched at his side, trying his best to breathe evenly.

“Oh, that was a bad idea.”

“Okay, clearly we’ve put off getting you something more for the pain for too long,” Eddie told him, pushing his chair back and getting to his feet. “I’ll go grab a nurse and see what I can get done.”

“Careful,” Buck warned him, twisting his head to follow his departure. “They might take this chance to extort me into going back to my room.”

“If they did that, you and I both know it’ll be Chris sneaking up to your room next.”

“I mean, he did manage to sneak out of the house and get an Uber, I wouldn’t put it past him.”

Buck didn’t have to see Eddie’s face as he walked out to know that he was rolling his eyes.

Chris ended up spending four days at the hospital, with the doctors wanting to make sure there was absolutely nothing wrong with him. Buck had been discharged after the first night, when it was extremely evident that he wasn’t going to leave his kid’s room no matter what the nurses or doctors tried. The wheelchair was switched out for a single crutch, which was almost certainly Angry Nurse’s attempt at pettiness, and he was given a prescription of NSAIDs.

During their stay at the hospital, camping out in the room in pediatrics that Chris had been moved to, everyone seemed to drop by. Not just the 118 and extended family, either, but people from Dispatch. It was weird, to have people come in that knew both Eddie and Maddie by face and name, only for Buck to have no idea who they were. It was weird, to have someone come in and have inside jokes with the two of them, while Buck sat to the side with absolutely no idea what they were talking about.

Hen made fun of him, after he whined about it to her when she stopped by to visit at the same time Eddie was out getting them actual food.

“Did you want him to be an angry hermit the entire time?” she asked. They were both keeping their voices low, because Chris was once again asleep. The meds he was given for his ribs made him drowsy.

“Of course not,” Buck said, because that was absurd. “It’s just, for years we had the same friends and now…”

“Aw, are you feeling jealous?”

“Shut up,” he mumbled, and could feel himself pouting. Hen laughed at him for it, because she was the worst friend.

“You’re going viral, by the way,” she said, apropos of nothing.

“Oh God, why?”

Instead of directly answering him, Hen pulled out her phone and started searching for something. Buck’s own phone was lying dead on the bedside table, since his charger was still in the jeep. Which, according to Maddie, had been towed to a resting place on the front curb. When the paramedic finally found what she was looking for, she passed the phone over to him.

It was a video, and the moment he pressed play, he realized it was a recording from the crash. He was the focus, and thankfully everything else was offscreen, but he watched himself silently scream Chris’ name as he tried to get to his kid. He also watched as first one, then two, and then finally three full grown men piled on him, trying to get him back onto the gurney.

“Oh, wow.”

“Yeah, you were determined,” Hen commented, watching the video over his shoulder. “We knew things were bad when Chimney told us you had to be sedated.”

“I think I need to send them a handwritten apology.” Buck winced at his own elbow hitting one of them in the stomach as he wrenched himself loose from their grip. “With a basket of baked goods.”

“Baked goods are always welcome.”

Both he and Hen turned to face the newcomer, who was smiling pleasantly from the doorway. The man was perfectly normal looking, a blond All-American, Boy-Next-Door type of guy. He was wearing an LAFD t-shirt, and even though his voice sounded familiar, Buck didn’t recognize him at all.

“If you know where they’re from,” Hen said, getting up and holding out her hand. “Hen Wilson, One Eighteen.”

“Jonah Greenway, whichever station needs me,” he replied, shaking her hand. And Buck definitely remembered the name, would probably never forget his name or the sight of his back as he seemed to loom over a defenseless Christopher. 

“Evan Buckley, but everyone calls me Buck,” Buck said, passing Hen her phone. “You were-” The one who hurt Chris. “-the one who gave him CPR.”

“Gave us quite the scare,” Greenway said. “It was lucky I was there.”

“Right.” Hen glanced at Buck, eyebrow raised.

“Hey, uh, Chris wasn’t able to tell us,” Buck said, realizing it for the opportunity it was. “What did you give him?”

The man seemed to freeze at the question, not blinking, not even appearing to breathe.

“What?”

“I remember you saying something about giving him something for the pain. What was it?”

“I didn’t give him anything,” the man lied. “You must have remembered it wrong. You did hit your head rather hard, after all.”

“Right.” Buck exchanged another look with Hen. “I must have.”

“Well, anyway.” Greenway clasped his hands together, smiling brightly and fakely. “I just thought I’d swing by and see how the big guy was doing. But, he’s asleep…”

“Yeah, the pain meds they have him on make him drowsy. But uh, he’s doing good.”

“That’s good, that’s good. Then I’ll just get out of your hair, then.” Greenway nodded and smiled at them, and then was gone as quickly as he appeared.

“That was weird,” Hen declared, and Buck could only nod, feeling entirely unsettled by the visit. “Anyway, I haven’t told you yet, have I? Ma and Clive are getting married…”

After spending so many days in a row camped out at the hospital, it was very nice to be home. Even if it meant climbing in and out of Eddie’s truck with sore ribs and an injured leg. But Buck bit back the swears he wanted to say and toughed it out until he was on the couch with his leg stretched out. His partner got Chris set up in his room with an ice pack for his own ribs, before getting started on all of the household chores that had been neglected.

When Buck started leveraging himself up to help, Eddie not-so-gently shoved him back down.

It was peaceful, a mundane calmness after the stress of the week. Eddie was in the kitchen making them a home cooked meal that they desperately needed. Chris was in his room, alive and definitely not getting a start on any of the assignments the school had sent them. And Buck was sitting there, vegetating as he watched that Speed Wedding show.

Of course, it couldn’t last.

He noticed the movement out of the corner of his eye, the lazy flutter of blue, and turned his head in time to watch his personal harbinger of doom swoop in out of nowhere. The butterfly did a loop around his head, managing to dodge his swiping hand, before dashing forward to land on top of the television.

“No,” Buck said, trying to keep his breathing under control. Back when he was spending most of his off-hours drunk, the thing had been mostly irritating. Now, its presence was as infuriating as it was vaguely terrifying. “Nope. Nu-uh. Please go away.”

Of course, the damn thing didn’t leave. It just sat there, and Buck felt more and more like it was staring at him. The irritation from weeks before started to return, and he pushed himself off the couch to swipe at it again.

“Go the fuck away, seriously!”

It took to the air, and fluttered tauntingly around him as he swiped at it again.

“What the hell do you want?” Buck demanded. “Seriously, just fucking tell me. What the hell do you want?”

The butterfly landed on the TV again, which then started switching channels on its own. Buck watched as it landed first on a cartoon, and then on the news, and then to some sitcom from the nineties.

“I don’t understand. Can’t you just tell me? Ichabod said you talked, why can’t you just-?”

It felt like being hit by the ocean again. It was a wave of sound, of color, of information. It was a wave of toomuchtoomuchtoomuch. It took the breath out of him, and it took his feet out from under him. Maybe it took some time from him, too, because he was suddenly looking up at a very worried Eddie.

“Are you okay? What happened?”

“I… I don’t know,” Buck said, sitting up with his help.

“I heard you fall and came running in here,” Eddie told him. “Your nose is bleeding.”

Now that he mentioned it, Buck could feel the wet trickle of it dripping over his lips. He reached up to wipe at it, only for Eddie to catch his wrist.

“You’ll just smear it,” he said. “I’ll get you something to clean up with. But Buck, what happened?”

“I- I asked it to talk to me.”

“Asked what?” Eddie asked, and Buck just pointed past him, to where the butterfly was still sitting on top of the TV. His partner turned, and Buck felt him stiffen. “Oh. Should… Should I get the bug spray?”

The channels switched again, the suddenness of it making Eddie jump, before landing on a sitcom just as the laugh track started up.

“I’ll take the creepy channel surfing over whatever the hell that was, just now,” Buck said, letting his head fall forward to rest on Eddie’s shoulder.

“What is it doing here?” Eddie asked in a whisper. “What does it want?”

“I have no fucking clue,” he answered, felling so very tired already. He heard the sound of the channels changing again and let out a groan, wishing that the damn thing would just find a way to-

“I’m standing here with hero of the hour, Jonah Greenway.” Taylor’s voice made Buck want to chuck a shoe at the screen, but the name had him lifting his head up.

“That’s the guy from the crash,” he told Eddie as Greenway spoke about saving someone, all fake humility. “He was weird, when he stopped by to see Chris.”

The channel switched again, landing on the video Hen had shown him. Buck wasn’t even aware that it was worth showing on any news segment, but apparently it was, because he was once again watching himself struggle to get to Chris as a trio of firefighters and paramedics tried to pull him back.

Then it switched again, landing on the Beauty and the Beast, of all things. It was the scene of Belle begging the Beast to let her father go, saying,

“Please. I’ll do anything.”

Buck’s heart stopped.

(“Please,” he remembered himself drunkenly begging. “They need me. I- I’ll do anything.”

Once upon another time, Ichabod had warned him those were dangerous words to say.)

“What do you want?” Buck demanded, and the channel changed again, except what it landed on this time shouldn’t have been possible. It was another news report from Taylor, but it shouldn’t have been possible for it to be showing.

Because the Taylor of this timeline never recorded it.

“-the remains were in no state for an immediate identification, I can tell you now that the body found in Palos Vista has been confirmed by an anonymous source to be Doug-”

Buck lunged forward, nearly toppling the television over as he slammed his finger on the power button. He was breathing heavily, he realized. In the reflection of the black screen, he saw Eddie staring at him.

“What was she talking about?”

The television turned itself on, and Buck blinked at the sudden light.

“-the circumstances do point to homicide, although the police investigation-”

He slammed his finger into the power button again, hard enough to hurt.

“No. No. No-”

“-Evan Buckley repeatedly refused to sit down with me. As if he had something to hide-”

This time, Buck reached around and tore the cord out of the wall.

“Buck.” He flinched as Eddie laid a hand on his shoulder, turning him around so they could face each other. “What was that?”

He shook his head, refusing to answer and refusing to look at him. Instead, he focused on the butterfly that was sitting prettily atop the now powerless television set.

“What the fuck do you want?” he demanded, doing his best to keep himself calm, doing his best to keep himself from simply grabbing the TV and hurling it out the door with the offending not-an-insect.

The not-an-insect that remained entirely, frustratingly silent.

Come When Called

Chapter Notes

Yes, this is a couple weeks later than it should have been. Woops.

“What was that, Buck?” Eddie asked, wiping away at his face with a wet paper towel, keeping a gentle hold on his partner’s jaw.

They were in the kitchen, the closed door acting as another barrier between Christopher and the topic of conversation. The butterfly was still sitting on the television, or at least it had been when Eddie had helped him move to the kitchen. It was probably too much to hope for that it would be gone by the time they were done here.

It was probably too much to hope for that Buck could get away with not telling Eddie anything.

“That was the Butterfly Effect being an utter dick,” he answered, keeping his head still.

“Is that what that thing is?”

“I have no clue. But I told you before that it was haunting me.”

“I think I got all of it,” Eddie told him, inspecting his face for a moment longer before letting him go, tossing the red stained paper towel into the trash. “You’re not going to terrify Christopher if he sees your ugly mug. No more than usual, anyway.”

“You love this ugly mug,” Buck protested, and his partner smiled at him.

“I do,” he said. “I don’t love finding you on the ground with your face covered in blood. So, what the hell happened?”

Buck let out a groan.

“It was being annoying, and I asked it what it wanted,” he grumbled. “Because Ichabod talked to it, and he said it could talk to him? And it tried, maybe? Or maybe it took offense to my request and just mind-whammied me. I don’t know.”

“I miss the days I thought you were all stupid for believing in jinxes.”

“Ha! I know, right?”

“And the creepy channel surfing was, what? Its attempt to talk without making you bleed all over yourself?”

“I guess?” Buck shrugged.

“Do you know what it was trying to say?” Eddie asked, looking at him intently.

(“Please. I’ll do anything,” Belle told Beast.)

“Not a clue,” he answered.

(“Please, they need me. I’ll do anything,” Buck begged whatever was listening.)

Eddie did not look like he believed him. But to be fair, he really didn’t know. He could make guesses, based on what was shown, but he didn’t know. Not really. And as long as he didn’t bother trying to decipher it, Buck could continue to live in blissful ignorance. Just like Eddie could, as long as he didn’t ask him about-

“That was Taylor doing a news report about Doug,” he said, and Buck found himself very interested in the grain pattern of Eddie’s table top. “Which is odd, because I don’t remember her doing any story on Doug.”

“Maybe it was a different Doug.”

“Maybe,” Eddie agreed with a nod, and Buck let out a relieved sigh. “Wasn’t Palos Vista the place where we found the dead hiker? The one whose ghost you were so certain called Nine-One-One.”

“It- It might have been,” he hedged. “So many calls, they just kinda… Blur together, y’know?”

“They do,” the other man said, nodding again. “Maddie had a bit of a freak out moment, by the way, when she realized ghosts might be real.”

“So did the other Maddie,” Buck said, laughing a bit. “I might have enjoyed rubbing it in her face a bit too much, after she mocked me for believing in them.”

There was a look on Eddie’s face, like he wanted to say something. Buck could almost see it on the tip of his tongue, just waiting to trip out into the air between them. But instead of voicing whatever he was thinking, he screwed up his face before letting out a sigh and pushing himself out of his seat.

“I think it’s about time for another dose of meds,” he said, checking his watch. “For you and Chris. Then dinner.”

Buck nodded absently, still curious about whatever it was that Eddie had opted to keep to himself. Curious, but also relieved, because he didn’t want to risk him asking about Doug. There was no way Buck was going to talk about Doug, not if he could help it.

Though, with his luck, he wouldn’t be able to.

Eddie opened his eyes and squinted through the darkness, trying to figure out what had woken him. The red glow of the alarm clock told him it was a couple hours past midnight, long before he needed to be up. Letting out a sigh, he debated the pros and cons of getting up to go find what he had heard this time. It had been a while since a noise had woken him up in the middle of the night, not since Buck-

The thought froze in its tracks as he rolled over and reached across the bed, only to find empty space.

Sitting up, letting the blankets (normal and weighted) fall off him, he glanced about as he rubbed at his eyes. His partner was nowhere to be seen, although his spot was still warm beneath the palm of Eddie’s hand. Cooling, but still warm. The bedroom door was also cracked open, where they had closed it before going to bed.

“Buck?” he called out, getting out of bed and stumbling into the hall. The bathroom door was open, light spilling out of it, and he could hear the sound of running water. For once, he didn’t think to grab his bat before investigating middle-of-the-night noises. He simply crept down the hall, fingers trailing along the wall to guide his way.

During his senior year of high school, his English teacher had them reading Shakespeare plays. The classics, like Romeo and Juliet and Julius Caesar and Macbeth. He had found the language obtuse and difficult to follow back then, parts of it coming across as foreign as Latin even though it was all in English, and dreadfully dull as a result. 

Buck was standing in front of the bathroom sink, water running hot enough that there was a bit of steam. He was taking a sponge to his hands, focused so intently upon his task that he didn’t even look up, didn’t even notice Eddie watching him in the mirror. He just kept scrubbing away at his hands, scrubbing and scrubbing and scrubbing.

“Out, damned spot! Out I say!” he remembered reading, spoken by Lady Macbeth as she tried to wash her hands clean of blood - of guilt - even in her sleep. “Who would have thought the old man to have had so much blood in him?”

(“-the circumstances do point to homicide-”)

Eddie crossed the short distance in quick strides, gently grabbing Buck’s wrist. His partner froze beneath his touch, his eyes flicking up to look at him and then going wide.

“Eds?” he croaked. “D- Did I wake you?”

“No,” Eddie lied, reaching out with his free hand and turning off the sink. “I think your hands are clean.”

Buck’s hands had wrinkled like prunes, the pale skin red and raw from the abuse he’d done to it with the sponge. He was still clutching at it, so tightly that water was being squeezed out into the sink basin, so Eddie delicately pried it loose from his fingers and set it on the back of the sink. The hand towel hanging up by the sink was one of the fluffiest he could find, as per Christopher’s demands, and he took it now to dry Buck’s hands, careful not to irritate the skin further.

“Buck-” Eddie started, and then cut himself off. There were so many things on the tip of his tongue, questions he did not want to know the answers to but which he also desperately needed to know.

Why were you so terrified of Taylor’s report?

What happened to the other Doug?

What did you do, Buck?

“Let’s go back to bed, okay?” was what Eddie went with instead, stuffing those questions down with the ease borne from years of practice.

Buck nodded, eyes dropping like he couldn’t meet Eddie’s, and allowed himself to be led back to the bedroom. Eddie hit the light on their way out, and it was a short trip before he was closing their door behind them. It didn’t take much longer before he was getting Buck back into the bed, pulling the blankets up over both of them and then wrapping himself around the other man, careful of his ribs and leg.

Eddie opened his mouth to say-

(“I know what you were thinking,” he remembered telling Buck what seemed like forever ago, sitting beside him in hospital jail. “I got sisters, too.”)

To say nothing, because he closed it again and settled for pulling Buck in closer.

It took a while for either of them to drift back to sleep.

“Are you sure you’ll be okay?” Eddie asked, fretting a bit on the precipice of leaving. It was his first day back to work since the accident, and Buck could tell that he was anxious about leaving the two of them alone. It probably did not help that neither of them had gotten much sleep after Eddie had interrupted Buck’s attempt at scrubbing his hands clean of blood that wasn’t there.

“We’ll be fine,” he promised, unable to help the smile creeping over his face, despite how tired he was. “If I need something, I’ll call. You or Carla or Pepa.”

If he turned his head a bit to his right, he could see his jeep sitting on the front curb. But seeing it as it was now, the driver’s side smashed in, hurt almost as much as the wreck itself did. Buck still wasn’t certain what he was going to do with it, because although he would like to have it repaired, it might well be more cost effective to just get something new.

“If you need anything,” Eddie said, his serious face on, “I’ll tell Sue and Josh to screw themselves and rush right over.”

“I know you will,” Buck said, leaning in to peck him on the lips. “Now get.”

It looked as if it physically pained Eddie to pull himself away, and he kept glancing back even as he walked to the truck. Buck watched him go, leaning on the doorframe to keep the weight off his leg and giving the most over-the-top wave he could. He kept waving until the truck was pulling down the street, and then he went back inside.

He spent most of the morning with his leg propped up on the coffee table, watching questionable daytime television. Of course, he’d had to plug the TV back in first, which required crouching and kneeling, and oh how that had been a literal pain. At one point, he went and woke up Chris, so they could both take their pain meds and so the kid didn’t sleep the entire day away.

Afterwards, around an hour before noon, he was contemplating if it was worth the effort to drag himself to the kitchen and figure something out for lunch, or if he should just splurge on take-out for the both of them, when there was knocking at the door. Which was odd, because the only people he could think of who would come over at this time of day - Carla or Pepa - all had their own keys. So he had no idea who it could be, and it was a bit irritating because now he had to limp to the door, putting most of his weight on the one crutch to keep it off his leg.

“Can I- Oh.” There on the porch were a bunch of people he definitely hadn’t expected to be there, but also that he really should have expected to be there.

“Are you going to let us in?” Bobby asked, holding grocery bags. Athena was standing behind him, along with Hen and Karen, the former clutching  a clipboard and the latter with a large purse hanging off her shoulder, and Chimney, who was adjusting his hold on Jee-Yun.

“Uh, sure.” He mostly shuffled to the side, letting them crowd into Eddie’s house, feeling a bit wrong-footed. Literally and metaphorically. “I’ll just go tell Chris we have guests…”

The boy seemed equally as perplexed as he felt, when he mentioned that Bobby and them had shown up out of the blue. He didn’t seem quite eager to peel himself away from whatever game he was playing - and maybe Buck shouldn’t be letting him spend a school day playing video games, but he didn’t care - until he was informed that Bobby had brought food, and then he was getting up and trundling down the hall. Even without his forearm crutches - which didn’t put pressure on his injured ribs - he had an easier time moving around the house than Buck did at the moment, the man left following along with slow hopping motions.

After making sure that his kid was well-situated in the living room with Hen and Chimney and Karen, sitting on the couch beside Jee-Yun and looking entranced by her babbling away, he went in search of Bobby and Athena. He found them in the kitchen, and he flushed in embarrassment when he realized the sergeant was rinsing the dishes left in the sink from that morning and the night before.

“Hey, no, you don’t-”

“Hush,” Athena ordered, barely glancing at him. “You know I don’t mind helping. How’s your leg?”

“Bruised to hell and back,” Buck answered. “It hurts to put too much weight on it, hence…” He gestured at his crutch. “So uh, not that I don’t like having everyone here, but… Why is everyone here?”

“I’m not sure about Hen and Karen, or Chimney,” Bobby answered, going through the various things in the bags he’d carried in. It looked like a bunch of food ready to heat up and be served. “I had no idea they were coming. But you had several good points, when you were yelling at me in my own kitchen.”

Buck knew he did, that’s why he’d said them.

“You were right, when you told me that coming when you call doesn’t mean as much when that is the only time I show up,” the captain continued. “So that’s what I’m doing now. Showing up because I care, even if you didn’t call. It’s what I should have done for Eddie, long before- Oof!”

The crutch clattered to the floor when Buck dropped it, barreling forward to wrap his arms around the other man. Bobby rocked backwards a bit, unprepared to have someone of Buck’s size suddenly running into him, but he brought his own arms up to return the hug.

“We should have been here more for Eddie while you were gone,” Athena said, placing the last plate in the rack to dry.  “But it’s too late to fix that, so now we’re here for you and Chris.”

“Thank you,” Buck whispered, letting go of Bobby and stepping back, wiping at his eyes. He started to kneel down to pick up the crutch, the movement jostling both his leg and his ribs. His discomfort must have been clear on his face, because the captain hurried to pick it up for him.

“Go in there and sit down. The food shouldn’t take too long, we’ll bring it right out,” the older man told him. A little quieter, almost to himself, he added, “I hope we brought enough for everyone.”

Buck didn’t make it all the way to the living room. Instead, he pulled out one of the dining chairs and sat in it. From the dining room, he could see Christopher explaining to Chimney and an enthralled Jee-Yun what was happening with the telenovela that was now on the TV. Hen and Karen, meanwhile, got up to join him at the dining table.

“How’s your leg?” was the first thing out of Hen’s mouth.

“It’s fine, as long as I don’t put too much weight on it,” Buck answered, and then pointed at the clipboard she had put on the table. “What’s that?”

“I’m planning Toni’s wedding,” Karen gushed, digging into her large purse and dragging out folder after folder. “We’re having it in the backyard. Our backyard.”

“Well I would hope you meant yours and not ours,” he joked, ignoring the way both women smirked at each other. “I’m not sure Eddie could handle the stress of hosting a wedding.”

“We actually came over today because I knew you’d probably be bored out of your mind,” Hen told him as her wife started opening up the folders. “We weren’t really expecting to have everyone else show up as well, but maybe we should have. Anyway, we figured you wouldn’t mind helping.”

“To help plan your mother’s wedding?” Buck asked, making no effort to hide his incredulity. Smirking at him, the paramedic slid the clipboard across the table so it hit his arm. A temptation he could never resist. “Alright, fine, show me what you’ve got.”

It was fun, going through and picking things out for a wedding, even if he had no idea what he was doing. Bobby would probably have been better at this, even if his own wedding planning had been thrown to the wayside in favor of eloping, but Buck appreciated that this was less because Karen actually needed the help and more because they were concerned about him. Much the same why Bobby and Athena had shown up with food for him and Chris, and doubtlessly why Chimney had come over with Jee.

If he wasn’t careful, he was liable to start ugly crying.

Eventually, Bobby recruited the others to help carry food out. They sat around the dining table and in the living room, talking while they ate. And maybe some of their topics weren’t typically appropriate for dinner conversation, but most of them were first responders and their stomachs were stronger than others. Like the stylist who was getting real human hair from a mortuary, and accidentally gave one of his clients a scalp full of maggots.

Buck lost track of time, enjoying himself. He missed talking to everyone, he missed spending time with his family. It had been so, so long since they’d had everyone together like this without the threat of an alarm hanging over their heads. Even if they were missing some people.

“We have a full house,” Eddie said, and Buck twisted his head to look at him, the man standing at the door and looking around. He’d moved to the couch, and now had Jee-Yun napping against his chest, the non-bruised side.

“Oh, do you need one of us to move our car?” Chimney asked, already moving.

“No, I’ll move the truck later,” he told him, crossing the distance to the couch. He bent over the back of it, pressing a kiss against Buck’s forehead. “Good day?”

“Good day,” Buck agreed.

Buck sat at the café table, fiddling with his drink. Eddie was at work, and Carla had taken Chris to a physical therapy appointment. Which was, according to her, mostly going to consist of finding out how to work around the broken ribs so he could do the regular exercises he needed, especially with how he was currently going through a growth spurt. The last thing Chris needed was muscle pain added to everything. So, finding himself home alone, he made a call with a request to meet, before taking an Uber to the café.

During the impromptu get-together the other day, he had taken the opportunity to ask Hen about the video she had shown him, the one from the crash. Apparently, a new reporter had been nearby for something else, and so rushed to cover it. Buck was hoping they showed more than just him struggling to get to Chris, though he wasn’t looking forward to seeing those other parts again.

And there was also the identity of the reporter.

“You know, it isn’t often I have an ex call me, asking for my help with something,” Taylor remarked, sitting down across from him. She was stiff and glaring at him, and he could not blame her for it. “Especially after they called me toxic.”

“I called the both of us toxic,” Buck replied, letting out a sigh. He was already regretting this, but it was too late now. “The two of us together were toxic.”

“Right,” she drawled. “What did you need?”

“Is there any way to see more of the footage you took of my crash?”

“Why?” she asked.

“I think something happened during it, and I need proof that I didn’t just make it up,” he explained. When that still wasn’t enough, he let out another sigh and said, “Please, it’s for Christopher.”

And while Taylor Kelly was - understandably - still upset with him, she wasn’t that cruel. So she rolled her eyes and pulled her laptop out of her bag, telling him,

“You’re paying for my lunch. And I’m ordering the most expensive things on the menu.”

That’s how Buck found himself watching the aftermath of the crash again, anxiously drumming against the tabletop with his finger. And he got to watch, from a brand new angle, as Chris went from awake and talking to on the ground, Greenway giving him CPR.

“I just don’t understand,” Buck said, swallowing back the vomit threatening to erupt everywhere. “He was fine. He was talking, he was chatting away, and then… Can you rewind?”

Taylor frowned at him, but she did as he asked. She rewound the footage to when Greenway was getting Chris out of the car, and then Buck saw what he was looking for.

“There! Stop there!”

The reporter did as requested, pressing a button on her keyboard and freezing the video. It wasn’t close enough to make out details, but he could see Greenway putting a syringe to Chris’ arm as the boy frowned up at him.

“He lied to me,” Buck whispered, unsurprised and utterly horrified. “Right to my face. Right in front of Hen. I asked him what he gave Chris, and he just… He just lied to me right to my face.”

“So what does this mean?” Taylor asked.

“I don’t know.”

There Was a Noise

It took the rest of the day, and then most of the next, for Buck to figure out what to do. Taylor emailed him the video, and he watched it once more when he got home and again after Eddie left for his next shift at the call center. A part of him, the part that was nothing but a coldly burning rage at the idea of someone hurting his kid, was tempted to take matters into his own hands, the same as he had before. But the ghost of Doug still lingered like an albatross, and he was not sure he could go through with that again without losing even more of himself.

Besides, there was a difference between Doug - who had done his very best to break his sister; who had left Chimney for dead and murdered an innocent bystander just trying to help, in this timeline - and Jonah Greenway. He had no proof that the act caught on video was anything malicious. There was every bit the chance that the paramedic was simply an idiot who had made an honest attempt at helping - like that fake firefighter, Brian - and then lied about it to cover his own ass.

“I have to show you something,” Buck told Eddie, the following evening. He was sitting at the kitchen table, the door to the dining room closed and his laptop open in front of him. Eddie had been washing the last of the dishes from dinner - Bobby had come by again with more food to fill their fridge, so cooking was mostly just reheating - but he paused to frown at him over his shoulder. “And… And I have to tell you something.”

“Alright,” he said, going back to drying and putting away the last plate. “Does this have anything to do with the way you’ve been acting odd since I got home yesterday?”

“It might,” Buck answered. He hadn’t realized he’d been acting oddly, but he was unsurprised that Eddie had noticed. “It’s, uh- I took an Uber and met up with Taylor yesterday. After Carla had taken Chris to his physical therapist.”

The face Eddie made was easily recognizable, because Buck had seen him make it before. Or, another him. It was the same face the other Eddie had made, whenever his dormant jealous streak was roused but he was trying not to show it. The stiffening of the jaw, that shallow furrow in his brow.

“You met up with Taylor Kelly.”

That forcefully light and casual tone underlaid with something sharper.

“Yes, Taylor, my ex who I said to her face I deserved better than a toxic relationship with,” he said, just to soothe any worries the other man was having. It obviously helped, because he visibly relaxed a little. “But uh, I told you that Greenway stopped by while Chris was still at the hospital, right?”

“I think you did,” Eddie said, pulling out the chair beside him and sitting down. “He said he didn’t give Chris anything, when you asked him.”

“Right,” Buck nodded. “But he lied. Chris told us he gave him a shot, and I knew I remembered him talking about it.”

“And what does this have to do with Taylor?”

“Hen showed me a video that had gone viral-”

“The one with the One Twenty-two trying to pull you back,” Eddie interrupted, and Buck nodded.

“Exactly. And uh, apparently it was from a news report Taylor had done,” he said, and Eddie scowled. “She was down the road for something else when the crash happened, but I realized she might have gotten more than just what Hen showed me. So I asked her to meet me, and… He lied, Eddie. I know it, you know it, Chris knows it. And now I have proof. If- If you want to see it.”

Eddie hesitated a moment, and then another, before nodding. Buck pulled up the video, and the both of them sat quietly as it played. Taylor was talking, but the camera focused beyond her as the firefighters arrived and surrounded the jeep and the car that had hit them. Reaching out, Buck hit the button to pause it on the same image that had been stuck with him since leaving Taylor the day before.

(That he paused it before either of them had to watch Chris collapse - Eddie for the first time, Buck for the fourth - was only partially intentional.)

“He lied,” Buck repeated. “To me and to Hen.”

“He did,” Eddie agreed, glaring at the screen.

“And I- I don’t know what to do next,” he said. “I- I think I know what th- that thing wants me to do, but I- I can’t do that again, Eddie. I can’t.”

“You won’t have to,” his partner told him, grabbing his hand. Buck looked at his face, at the utter lack of confusion, and he realized that-

“You know.”

“I guessed,” Eddie corrected. “I don’t know anything, if you don’t want me to know. But like I said, you won’t have to do anything.”

There was a darkness to the man’s words, and Buck realized that if he was tempted to repeat that awful act in Christopher’s name, then Eddie was absolutely willing to do the same. 

“Eds-”

“We’ll take this to Bobby,” he interrupted, and Buck blinked in surprise. “We’ll show him what Greenway did, tell him that he lied to us about it. He’ll handle it from there.”

“I- Okay, yeah.”

That sounded better to Buck’s ears. So, so much better. He remembered the relief he felt, when he handed the other Bobby the problem of Hen’s crash and the captain calmly offered up a solution. That the solution ended up not working in the end was simply something which Buck was doing his best not to focus on.

They ended up inviting Bobby and Athena (and Harry) to dinner at theirs a couple days later, the opposite of how things were typically done. Normally, people went to the Grant-Nash for dinner. This plan, at least, came with the benefit of Buck being able to show off Eddie’s recently acquired abilities in the kitchen. The man was nervous, of course, because it was his first time cooking for someone not Buck or Chris.

Though, the reason for inviting them to dinner probably also had to do with the nerves.

After the dinner, after the dishes were dumped into the sink for later and the boys had run into Chris’ room, Buck pulled out his laptop and set it on the table. This got curious looks from Bobby and Athena, that morphed into worry when he and Eddie told them about Greenway, and then to absolute fury when they were shown the video.

“I’d seen the clip of you,” Athena said, eyes fixed on the screen just like her husband, looking every bit like she wanted to reach through it and strangle someone. “But I hadn’t realized… You have no idea what he gave Chris?”

“None,” Eddie told him, shaking his head. “Chris obviously didn’t know, and he lied to Buck when he asked about it.”

“And m- maybe it was just something for the pain?” Buck weakly suggested. “But we spent days in the hospital with him getting looked over, and nothing was found wrong with him. His heart just…”

His heart just stopped for absolutely no reason.

“Send me the video,” Bobby ordered, and finally looked away from the screen when Buck turned the laptop back around to do just that. “I’ll talk to the chief, see what we can do. Whatever his reason, whatever he gave him, this is evidence of gross misconduct that could have resulted in…” The captain swallowed, unwilling to finish the sentence. “Where did you get this?”

“Taylor Kelly,” Eddie answered, side-eying his partner, and Buck sheepishly ducked his head.

“I told her that something happened and I needed proof,” he said, shoulders hunching. “She doesn’t really know anything more than we do, though.”

“I’ll tell the chief about that, too,” the captain sighed. He reached out and gripped Eddie’s forearm. “We’ll take care of this.”

Eddie nodded, looking every bit as relieved as Buck felt.

The talk with the chief left Bobby feeling unfulfilled. Alonzo agreed that there was evidence of Greenway giving Chris something when he shouldn’t have. But they didn’t know what was in the syringe, so they had to run with the assumption that it was indeed just something for the pain. Though, why the paramedic would feel the need to give a child pain medication strong enough to stop his heart for the common aches received from a seatbelt, Bobby did not know.

At the most, this would just result in Jonah Greenway getting a dressing down. And that just left him feeling so dissatisfied.

So that was why Bobby was in his office, when normally he’d be upstairs with the others. There was a possibility that he was abusing his powers and access, but as long as no one complained about it, he should be okay. He had Greenway’s personnel file pulled up on his desktop, and what he found unnerved him even more. Apparently, he’d only been with the LAFD for just over a year, after bouncing between fire departments across the country. The man had been a paramedic in Chicago, Miami, Dallas, and Denver, the longest not quite two years and the shortest just a few months. 

There was a complaint from Chicago, filed by someone named Paul Strickland, and one from Miami under the name of Marjan Marwani, but Bobby couldn’t see what the complaints were about. They were just footnotes, and clearly nothing had come of them.

“Penny for your thoughts, Cap?” Chimney’s voice broke him out of his thoughts, and he looked up to see both him and Hen leaning through the open door. “Lookin’ real unhappy there.”

“Just… Frustrated,” Bobby sighed, messaging at his eyes as he leaned back in his chair.

“Anything we can do to help?” Hen asked, and the captain contemplated for a moment.

“Jonah Greenway,” he started, and paused when Hen let out an “Ohhh” and started nodding.

“He came by to visit Chris before he was discharged,” she said. “He was kinda weird, all ‘it was lucky I was there.’ And then he got defensive when Buck asked what he gave Chris, pretty much told him he was making it up before running away.”

“Buck wasn’t making it up,” Bobby said, and the tightly coiled anger in his tone had the paramedics glancing at him warily. “I talked to the chief, and he’s not going to do anything without proof of something more than just incompetence.”

“And you think this was more than just incompetence?” Chimney asked.

“Buck and Eddie certainly do.”

The paramedics looked at each other and nodded, before coming in and closing the door behind them.

“So, how can we help?”

Help came in the form of them reaching out to other paramedics that Jonah Greenway had worked with, in between being called out and responding to scenes. Almost every one of them said the same thing: Greenway was odd, overeager, and good at his job. Except for the one at the 122.

“They were responding to a fender bender,” Hen told him and Chimney, having dragged them back into the captain’s office towards the end of their shift.

“Okay,” Bobby nodded, crossing his arms and leaning on the desk.

“He said the driver just had a concussion and some abrasions from the airbag,” she continued. “So he left Greenway alone with him, but when he came back, the guy was in full arrest.”

“Just like with Chris,” Chimney said.

“Yeah, except this guy died on the scene.”

“A paramedic at the One Twenty-two?” Bobby asked, already reaching for his phone to call the chief. “What was his name?”

They had proof now of more than just gross incompetence. They had a story of Greenway doing this thing before, except last time, it hadn’t ended so well. Plus, there was the trail of complaints from other states that they could look into. All of that, plus the video of him and Chris, should be more than enough for the chief to at least open an investigation. Though, Bobby wasn’t putting much hope on anything coming out of it.

Across the city, Taylor Kelly was doing her own investigation. After meeting with Buck, she had been left with the feeling of standing at the edge of something huge. Like the video had just been the tip of an iceberg, and she just had to dive deep to see the rest of the story. So that’s what she did, and by God, was the iceberg big.

It was easy to get the name of the paramedic that her ex had been so interested in, especially after she realized she herself had done a puff piece on him not too long ago. After very little googling on Jonah Greenway, she stumbled upon the years old story of a thirteen year old saving the day, resuscitating his bus driver after he suffered a heart attack behind the wheel. There were dozens of articles and interviews, where she learned that the mayor had given him the key to the city and that his way through college had been fully paid.

There were more articles when he got older and became a paramedic, because Jonah Greenway apparently could not help himself. But they also gave Taylor an odd trail to follow, as the man jumped from city to city, department to department. He’d spend some time at each, months or a couple years, receiving adulation for miraculous saves before suddenly packing up and moving.

It was like he was running away, trying to stay ahead of something. And if there was anything Taylor Kelly recognized, it was the signs of someone trying to outrun something unsavory.

Before Buck, before the pandemic, she had spent many a night hanging out at hook-and-ladder bars, eager to get ahead on any juicy stories. First responders were, after all, a very gossipy bunch. That time had left her with many a name and a number to call upon now, and she patiently went through each. Not everyone recognized Greenway’s name, though some did.

And one, from the 122, had a very interesting tale.

(Unbeknownst to her, the paramedic had literally just gotten off the phone with Hen Wilson of the 118. He wasn’t sure why Jonah Greenway was suddenly so popular, but he definitely didn’t want any part of it.)

Though, now she was left with the conundrum of how to go about this story. It was big, it was huge. The implications of a hero paramedic purposefully putting his patients into harm’s way? It was as fascinating as it was terrifying, and it was the exact kind of thing people needed to be informed about.

She could call the LAFD’s PR people, ask for an official comment and wait for them to get back to her, or…

“I have something big,” Taylor said, the moment her producer picked up the phone. “Like, really, really big. I’ve sent you a file…”

Following their dinner with Bobby and Athena, Eddie could tell that Buck had relaxed a lot. He’d had one nightmare immediately after, the subject of which Eddie could only guess, but it didn’t draw him into the bathroom to scrub his hands raw. Instead, Eddie had woken up to his partner sitting on the edge of the bed calming himself down, before he slipped back under the covers.

They did not talk about the butterfly, or the fact that Eddie had admitted to - at least partially - figuring out what had happened to the other Doug. It was a subject they continued to dance around and avoid, as if it would simply cease to be if they ignored it. And that was perfectly okay with Eddie. He had been very much considering murder, after what Greenway had done.

He could only imagine how Buck had felt once confronted with Doug again, armed with the knowledge of what he had done to his sister, and what he was going to do if left to his own devices.

One night, a couple nights after their dinner with Bobby and Athena, they were both woken up by the sound of Buck’s phone going off. It was the ringtone that Eddie recognized as Maddie’s, and so he was not surprised when Buck rolled over and grabbed it off the nightstand.

“Mads? What-” he cut off, bolting upright and throwing the covers off. Whatever was going on, it clearly had Maddie in a state, because Eddie could almost hear her well enough to make out her words. “Alright, alright, I’m on my way. I- Yeah, I’m putting my shoes on right now. Okay?”

He wasn’t, he was busy trying to hop into a pair of Eddie’s sweats, but it was close enough. Eddie laid there and listened, growing increasingly concerned as Buck gave more hurried assurances to his sister. Finally, he managed to calm her down enough to get off the phone and finish getting dressed.

“Is everything okay?” Eddie asked.

“I don’t know,” Buck admitted, chewing on his lip. “She didn’t really say what was going on.”

“Alright, throw me some pants,” he said, sitting up, but paused when the other man shook his head.

“You have a shift in the morning, stay,” Buck told him. “And besides, there’s Chris.”

Right, their kid. Old enough to appreciate being woken up for a family emergency, maybe, but he also had school in the morning.

“Fine,” he grumbled, laying back. “You’ll be okay?”

“I’ll be okay,” Buck confirmed, bending down to steal a kiss. “I’m taking the truck.”

“Kinda figured,” Eddie answered, watching him leave the room. He let out a sigh when, a few minutes later, he heard the front door open and close, followed by the sound of the truck’s engine starting. 

There was the temptation to stay up anyway, to wait for word about Maddie, or for Buck to come back home. But he was tired, and he had work in the morning, and so he closed his eyes and let himself drift back to sleep.

Only to snap awake an unknown amount of time later. It sounded like glass shattering, he thought. Buck still wasn’t back, his side of the bed empty and cold, and it was those awful nights all over again. Those nights Eddie spent terrified of every noise outside, no matter how innocuous. He had thought, had hoped, that he’d gotten over the fear.

Apparently not.

“It’s nothing,” he told himself, reaching for the weighted blanket. It spent most of its time piled at the foot of the bed, because he didn’t wake up nearly as often while curled up with Buck. “It’s just the-”

Another noise, so much closer, so much clearer.

His heart racing, Eddie eased himself out of the bed, grabbing the bat from its place by the door. It was nothing, he kept telling himself, even as he opened the bedroom door and crept down the hall. It was nothing, it was nothing, it was nothing.

Just raccoons.

Just the wind.

He checked his son’s room, and was only slightly reassured by the sight of him sleeping soundly. He was stepping back and closing the door when he heard it, just behind him. The sound of a floorboard creaking, a person moving behind him that he immediately recognized as not Buck.

Eddie spun on his heel, and the bat swung through the air.

Someone had tried breaking into the loft.

Buck hadn’t managed to get a clear story from Maddie’s initial phone call, but he got enough. He rushed out of the house, adrenaline helping him to forget that his leg was an aching mess until he was in the truck and already halfway to his destination, his crutch left at home. It meant that he was left to suffer for his poor foresight as he limped from his parking space to the building’s elevator, and from there down the hall to what was now, for all intents and purposes, Maddie’s loft.

The door was wide open, and an officer was standing in front of it. A sight that immediately had Buck’s adrenaline pounding through his veins again, even as he fought to keep from rushing past the man.

“Maddie!?” he called out, and was very relieved when she practically ran past the uniformed officer and to throw an arm around him, Jee-Yun gripped tightly in the other. “You’re okay?”

“Yeah, we’re both fine,” she assured him, and the officer nodded as she led him into the apartment proper. “I was just- I was so scared.”

“What happened?”

“I was upstairs, trying to get this little one back to sleep,” Maddie told him, bouncing Jee a little in her arms. The toddler grumbled and hid her face in her mother’s shoulder. “When- I thought it was someone using the wrong key at first, y’know? Like they were drunk and got the rooms mixed up. So I started down the stairs to see who it was, when they practically broke it down.”

Buck glanced behind him at the door, and noticed that it indeed did look like someone had gotten frustrated and literally broke in. The wood around the handle, and around the latch in the doorframe, was splintered.

“So I screamed, and told them I was calling the cops, and they just… Ran away.”

“But you’re okay?” Buck asked again, and Maddie nodded. “Alright, have you called-”

“Maddie!” Chimney shouted from the hallway, as if answering Buck’s question himself. The paramedic practically shoved his way past the officer, who allowed it once it was clear Maddie recognized and knew him. “You’re okay? You’re not hurt?”

“I’m fine, we’re both fine,” Maddie repeated.

“Someone broke in,” Buck told him, and Chimney’s face paled. “I’ll uh, I’ll call the landlord, he should get someone to fix the door. I assume you’re not staying here tonight?”

Maddie shook her head and gave Chimney a hopeful look, as if he was ever going to say no. They made quick work of packing a couple bags for her, as she shook the entire time from the adrenaline comedown. Buck lingered behind as Chimney guided her downstairs, sticking close to her side, and got the officer’s info for the police report.

His landlord was definitely going to want that. Or rather, Buck would want it to keep the landlord from trying to shove the costs of repair onto him.

Afterwards, with his leg and ribs aching, he made a quick sweep to make sure there weren’t any valuables left unsecured before heading out. Most of his were at Eddie’s, and he was fairly certain the cop was going to stick around until the door got fixed. Or a cop, at least. The temptation to stay the night and make sure no one else broke in was strong, but he needed to get the truck back.

The sight that greeted Buck when he got home was not one he was expecting. He had hoped for a quiet house with his boyfriend and kid asleep in bed. What he got was, well.

“What the fuck?” he asked, freezing in the living room. Eddie, standing above a groaning Jonah Greenway with his baseball bat still raised, said,

“There was a noise.”

Defending His Castle

“What the fuck?” Buck repeated, feeling like that was the only thing he could say. As he watched, Greenway rolled onto his hands and knees and started trying to stand up, only for Eddie to slam the bat into the side of his head, sending him sprawling onto the floor again. “Eds!”

“What?” the other man snapped, glaring down at the intruder with his weapon at the ready.

“You’ll wake up Chris,” Buck pointed out, leaning against the living room wall as he pulled out his phone. He hit the number he was searching for and waited as it rang.

“It is near three in the morning,” Athena grumbled on the other end of the line. “Why are you waking me up?”

“Greenway broke into the house.”

“What!?” Buck could hear the sound of her scrambling out of bed, along with a confused and pained grunt as she bullied Bobby awake. “I’m on my way, are police there yet?”

“Oh, uh…”

“Evan Buckley, do not tell me that I was your first call before Nine-One-One.”

In lieu of answering, because there was no suitable answer that wouldn’t get him a scolding, he pushed himself off the wall and limped across the room to the couch. He was easing himself onto the cushions when there was another thwap of the bat and Greenway let out a pained yelp.

“What was that?” Athena asked.

“Just Eddie defending his castle,” Buck answered, before covering the receiver. “Could you keep it down?”

“If he’ll stop moving,” his partner said. Buck wasn’t certain if Greenway had even moved that time, or had merely twitched a little. He sincerely hoped this didn’t set back any of the progress they’d been making, although maybe this could be a moment of catharsis for Eddie.

“I’m hanging up now,” Athena said, reminding Buck that she was there. “What are you going to do after?”

Find a shovel, was what Buck immediately thought to say.

“Call Nine-One-One,” he dutifully replied.

“Good,” the sergeant said, and then the line went dead.

“Do you think you can keep from using him as a piñata for a little while?” Buck asked, side-eyeing Eddie.

“I make no promises,” the man replied. That was probably the best Buck was going to get, so he shrugged and dialed those three numbers, humming to himself while he waited. It might be 3am but it was Los Angeles, and the city slept as little as New York did.

“Nine-One-One, what’s your emergency?” asked a voice that he vaguely recognized as belonging to someone named Linda. He’d seen her after the dispatch fire, and she’d come by to visit Chris while he was in the hospital.

“Hi! I’d like to report a break-in at Forty-nine Ninety-five South Bedford Street.” He glanced over at Greenway, currently curled up on his side. “Uh, send paramedics as well.”

“Alright sir, I have a unit on the way.”

“Thank you, tell them the door is-” He was interrupted by the sound of wood hitting flesh again, and he let out a sigh. “Eddie, honey-”

“He was trying to get back up!”

“Tell them the front door is unlocked,” Buck finished telling Linda. “How far away is that ambulance?”

“This is going to be fun work gossip for the next week,” Linda commented. “The ambulance is just a few minutes out.”

She stayed on the line with him until the cops showed up, rushing through the door right behind Athena. By that time, Eddie had hit Greenway twice more with the bat. Judging from the state of his face - a swollen mess of blood and bruises - when one of the officers not-so-gently rolled him over onto his back to check him for weapons, Buck would guess that Greenway definitely had a concussion at the very least.

(When the officer found a syringe on the floor beside the man, along with another two tucked away in his pocket, what little sympathy Buck had for him dried up entirely.)

At that point, Chris was finally woken up by all the ruckus, stumbling out of his bedroom to stare wide-eyed at his house suddenly being filled with police. Bobby - who had followed his wife inside - took him back into his room, answering the boy’s concerned questions as he did, so Eddie and Buck were free to give their stories as the paramedics arrived.

“I was woken up by the sound of glass shattering,” Eddie said. “I still don’t know what it was-”

“Your backdoor’s window is broken,” one of the officers said, the one who had done a quick sweep when they arrived.

“Something else to fix,” he grumbled. “Anyway, I heard that, and then there were more noises, so I got up to check on everything. I was looking in on my son, when I heard someone behind me and… Reacted.” Read: introduced Greenway to his bat. Violently. “And then Buck came home like, right after.”

Everyone turned to look at him, and Buck shifted uncomfortably under their attention.

“I was out checking on Maddie, my sister. She called in a panic to say-” He cut himself off, turning to stare wide-eyed at Greenway, who was being carried out on a gurney with one of the officers following behind. “Someone tried to get into my loft tonight. My sister’s been staying there, and someone broke the door in. Sh- She’s fine, but…”

“But someone tried to get into your loft, on the same night someone got caught breaking into here,” Athena finished for him. “Go pack a bag for yourselves and Chris, you’re staying at ours tonight.”

“‘Thena,” Buck tried, only for the woman to shake her head.

“This is a crime scene,” she pointed out. “There’ll be people coming in and out, collecting evidence.” Pausing, she glared at one of the officers until they nodded frantically. “The backyard, the backdoor. And wherever he parked his car.”

“I guess Chris is missing another day of school,” Eddie commented, though he didn’t sound too upset about it. 

“Fine,” Buck grumbled, pushing himself off the couch with a wince.

“Where’s your crutch?” his partner asked, frowning at him.

“I forgot it in the bedroom,” he answered. “I don’t need it, I’m fine.”

“Sure,” Eddie said, the sarcasm heavy as he watched Buck limp for the hall.

After popping in to check on Chris and Bobby, and to tell the former to get a bag ready, they made quick work of packing a bag. The most important thing to remember was Eddie’s work uniform for in the morning, and also Buck’s crutch, even as he insisted that he was fine without it. The bat wasn’t going with them, being as it had been confiscated by one of the cops, an act that had Eddie pouting. They were halfway to the truck with Bobby, because Athena was staying behind to keep an eye on things and so needed the car they came in, when a thought struck Buck and he stumbled on the sidewalk in his haste to turn back.

“Hey, Eddie isn’t going to be charged with anything, is he?” Because if he was, then Buck needed to try and remember Attica Rose’s number. The woman might not know him in this timeline, but he knew she’d happily take the excuse to give law enforcement another headache.

Athena shook her head.

“Castle Doctrine, Buckaroo,” she said, and the detective she was standing beside nodded in agreement.

Letting out a relieved breath, and deciding he’d look up Attica’s number in the morning, just to be safe, Buck continued on to the truck. He pulled himself up into the driver’s seat, which Eddie had given up without even a thought. In the back, Chris was tiredly alert, with Bobby looking cramped beside him.

“You okay back there, Cap?” he asked.

“I’ll be fine,” the captain said.

“So,” Eddie started, leaning against the door as Buck started the engine and backed out, avoiding the cruisers that had gathered. “Honey?”

“It just slipped out,” Buck said, defending himself. “What would you prefer? Snugglebug? Sugarplum? Sweetums?”

“Snugglebug?” Bobby asked from the back, sounding amused.

“He turns into an octopus in his sleep,” Buck answered, grinning at Eddie’s embarrassed groan. “Or a koala.”

“So we’re thinking Greenway tried breaking into your loft, right?” his boyfriend asked, trying to change the topic.

“It could’ve been someone else,” Buck said, shrugging. “Oh! Remind me to call Maddie when we get there, she’ll murder me herself if I let her hear about this through the gossip grapevine.”

“I’ll defend you if she tries.”

It took longer for them to get to the Grant-Nash residence then it did for Athena to arrive at their house. Buck assumed it was because she had no qualms ignoring traffic laws at the time, deciding they were more akin to guidelines. He parked at the curb, leaving the driveway open for when Athena got home, and followed Bobby to the front door. It was then, after the captain let them in, that he remembered the problem with this arrangement:

“Oh God, stairs.”

Chris shared a commiserating look with him, before going down ahead like the independent trooper he was. Eddie looked torn between following behind and lingering with Buck, until Bobby pretty much shooed him on. When everyone made it to the bottom, which took twice as long as it normally did, they took a moment to figure out where everyone was going to sleep. May was apparently sleeping in her old room - “She was already spending the night, Buck, put that face away.” - so it left just the one guest room.

They ended up gathering a bunch of extra pillows and setting up the couch for Chris, since he couldn’t just sleep on the floor of Harry’s room. Or, he could, but his still healing ribs wouldn’t thank him for it. 

“You’re okay?” Buck asked, once he and Eddie were settled in Athena’s guest bed. “After…?”

“I’m fine,” his partner answered. “It was actually kind of a relief.”

“What do you mean?” he asked, rolling over on his (non-injured) side to face him better.

“I mean, for once I woke up because of a noise and it wasn’t just my fears getting the better of me,” Eddie whispered. “There was actually something there. And I could do something about it.”
