“I think I understand,” Buck said, letting out a yawn and letting his eyes fall closed. A moment later, they snapped open and he bolted upright, lunging for his phone. “Shit! Eddie, you were supposed to remind me!”

“Remind you of what?” Eddie asked, sitting up as well. Buck ignored the question, scrabbling through his contacts and hitting a name.

“Hey Mads! I uh, I’m sorry if I woke you up or interrupted something, but I figured you’d hear about it when you got to work, and you might murder me if I didn’t tell you first.”

“Hear about what?” his sister asked, sounding half-asleep still.

“I just want to start by saying everyone is fine…”

Buck was woken up the next morning by the sound of various alarms going off throughout the house. There was Eddie’s phone, plus May’s the next room over, and Bobby and Athena’s alarm down the hall. He grumbled unhappily as Eddie disentangled himself, feeling like he hadn’t gotten nearly enough sleep.

“I should just call in,” the other man said, groaning as he sat up.

“You really don’t like that job, do you?”

“I really don’t,” Eddie admitted. “But it’s just a few days more.”

Since Buck didn’t have anywhere to be for the day, he simply pulled himself out of bed, grabbed phone, and padded to the kitchen while Eddie pulled out his uniform for work. He passed by May on her way to the bathroom, who did a double take at the sight of him in his pajamas. Chris was stirring on his mountain of pillows on the couch, and he followed Buck to the kitchen.

The day went like this: Buck and Bobby put together breakfast, before everyone went to where they needed to be. The adults went to work - including Athena, who had come home sometime in the early hours long enough to have a nap - and Harry went to school. Buck called the landlord for the loft, directing him to where he could get a copy of the police report, and then looked up Attica’s number just to be safe. 

Afterwards, he set Chris up at Athena’s kitchen table with Eddie’s laptop - snagged on an impulse - and helped him work through the last of the make-up assignments the school had sent them. Well, he tried to help him with the assignments. History and science? That was fine, the internet was always his friend there. English? Eh, not his strongest, but it wasn’t as if the teacher wanted Chris to write a new classic or anything. Math? It had him feeling like an idiot, and Chris looking at him as if he was one, so he mostly just left the kid to it

In the early afternoon, when the two of them had moved to the den to watch television, Athena arrived home. She was still in uniform, and the look on her face immediately had Buck standing up to follow her out the sliding doors and onto the patio, where Chris could not overhear.

“He had adenosine in his house,” Athena told him, the first thing out of her mouth. “Vials and vials of it.”

Enough adenosine could stop a person's heart entirely, Buck knew. He wanted to puke.

“Is- Is that what he used?” he asked. “With Chris?”

“We think so,” the sergeant said. “The syringes he had on him were filled with Propofol. Ransone thinks he was planning on sedating you or Eddie. Maybe both.”

“And then what?” Because he clearly had something planned. One did not break into a house armed with sedatives as a prank. “Did Greenway say?”

“He hasn’t woken up,” Athena said, and Buck wasn’t sure how he felt about that. Actually, no, he knew very well how felt about it. He just wasn’t sure how he felt about how he felt. “I should not be telling you about this, but… They’ve started going through his paramedic transfer forms. Most of them aren’t much so far, but there’s been a couple that have been flagged. Ones where he reports patients went into sudden cardiac arrest, only to be saved due to his ‘valiant efforts.’ Actual quote from one Ransone showed me.”

“Why?” Buck blurted out.

“Not a clue,” she shrugged. “But if you listen to Taylor Kelly-”

“What the hell does Taylor have to do with this?”

“You haven’t been watching the news, I take it?” When Buck shook his head, she went on. “She’s been running the story. She thinks he was just chasing after the high of adulation. Apparently he saved someone as a kid, got smothered in adoration for it, and has been trying to recapture that feeling.”

“S- So what’s going to happen now?”

“When he wakes up, they’ll throw the book at him,” she said, and then, “Just how many times did Eddie hit him?”

“I honestly lost count,” Buck admitted, pulling out one of the patio chairs and sitting down, stretching out his leg. The bruises were fading, no longer nearly as dark as they had been after the crash, and it only ached half as much, but still. “Pretty sure at one point, Eddie smacked him just for twitching.”

“Well. Good on him,” Athena said, nodding firmly. They both looked inside as the man in question came stomping down the stairs, stopping first by the couch to check on Chris and then joining them on the patio. “I was just informing your boy here about what they’ve found so far.”

“Adenosine, I know,” Eddie said. “Josh heard about it and told me.”

“First responders are a gossipy bunch,” Buck said.

“That they are,” she sighed. “Harry should be home soon, but Bobby has an overnight. Your house has been cleared, though you’re both welcome to stay for dinner.”

Buck looked at Eddie; Eddie looked at Buck. They both nodded.

“Sure.”

Hope

Chapter Notes

See the end of the chapter for notes

In the days that followed Jonah Greenway’s breaking into the house, Buck and Eddie busied themselves with once again fixing and healing. There was a day spent repairing their back door, and another overseeing the replacement of the loft’s front door. Buck wasn’t quite certain what he was going to do with the loft - he was, undeniably, living with Eddie - but he didn’t want to break his lease just yet.

Maddie was still, ostensibly, staying there. Even though she’d obviously not been staying while the door was broken. She’d also been making noises about finding an apartment that was more child-friendly. But Buck was wondering how likely that was, after spending so many nights in a row with Chimney again. Either way, he wanted to make sure that the option was still there for his sister, until she decided one way or the other.

Chris went back to school for the last couple of weeks that were left. There had been a minor debate between Eddie and Buck on whether it was even worth it, but the kid had insisted upon it. He was one of those kids that actually liked school, the little weirdo. Still, they made sure the teachers and administration were aware of the still healing ribs, and that he had the chance to take his NSAIDs at lunch. 

Their kid going back to school emphasized the lack of multiple vehicles. Not that Eddie couldn’t take him to school on his way to work - that was, after all, what he’d been doing before Buck had practically moved in - but he was going to start back at the station before school let out for the summer. That meant there were going to be days where it would be tight getting people where they needed to be. Carla said she would help, no complaints, but they both felt guilty at the very idea of dragging her out simply to ferry Chris back and forth when she didn’t necessarily have to.

All of that to say, Buck was stuck making a choice of his own.

“I really liked that jeep,” he whined, watching it being towed away. After arguing over it with his insurance company and various repair shops, it became increasingly clear that it was cheaper to simply get a new vehicle rather than get it fixed. So he and Eddie had spent a day scouring the broken vehicle for anything and everything that had been left inside, in preparation for this.

“I know you did,” Eddie said, patting his shoulder with just enough condescension to have Buck pouting at him. “We’ll find you a new one.”

The good news was that it turned out Claudette had very good insurance. Even though she wasn’t found at fault - apparently her brakes had failed, or so Buck heard through the grapevine - they had still given him a not inconsequential sum. Which, combined with his own insurance, meant cost wasn’t as much of a limiting factor as it might normally have been. Especially since he didn’t have the luxury of a couple million dollars stashed away.

And wow, did that thought make him feel jealous of the version of him who “won” that coin toss.

Buck went into looking for a new car with the same focus he did everything else. He’d managed to dig a clipboard out from some dark corner of the house, and proceeded to fall into a spiral of safety ratings and fuel mileage. By the time Eddie came home with Chris, he suddenly had far too many opinions on cars.

“Why don’t you just get the same jeep?” Eddie made the mistake of asking, after listening to Buck rant about the differences between various choices. They’d been working on dinner together - a recipe from Bobby - with Buck perched on a stool after Eddie had freaked out about him putting weight on his leg for too long. “Just a newer model?”

“Have you seen the safety ratings on that thing!? It’s lucky we didn’t both die!”

(The safety ratings hadn’t been that bad, he was simply being as dramatic as the Diazes.)

By the end of it, he ended up making a shortlist of cars with the best scores, and then going with one that sparked the most joy.

“It’s very bright,” Eddie remarked as they stood on the front porch drinking coffee one Friday morning, a week or so after Greenway and a few days before he was due to start back at the station. They were watching the drop-off of Buck’s new vibrantly colored hybrid SUV. 

“It’s called ‘Cadmium Orange,’” Buck told him, grinning. “I love it.”

“Chris will too, I’m sure,” his partner said. “Speaking of, I should go make sure he’s ready. We need to be off.”

“Don’t forget your lunch!” Buck called after him, before his attention was required to sign what needed signing.

When he first started seeing Frank, way back when, after Shannon's death and the truck bombing and the tsunami and the lawsuit and the street fighting, Eddie had hated it. He dreaded going in, and he found himself wishing that time would just fast forward through the sessions. The decision to start seeing Frank again, this time of his own volition, hadn’t meant that he was suddenly enjoying it. It had been a necessity, like a dentist appointment, and about as pleasant.

The damndest thing about it, though, was that it helped.

It helped, even when he was left feeling like someone was breaking open his chest and pulling him inside-out. It was like he was subjecting himself to that awful night at the crossroads with that not-demon, as something reached down his throat and pulled the words out. Except he was the one forcing the words out - willingly, if with some prompting.

“It’s been a couple weeks since we’ve seen each other,” Frank said, at their session that afternoon. Buck had driven him to it in his new SUV, showing off all the bells and whistles to him and Chris. Eddie had made a comment about the seats being “rich Corinthian leather,” and was wholly unsurprised when it was obvious Buck had no clue what he was talking about. “How have things been?”

“We had someone break into our house the other day,” Eddie blithely said.

“Oh.” Frank blinked at him, seemingly at a momentary loss for words. “And, uh. How are you handling that? I know we’ve discussed your fears before regarding that scenario.”

“I am surprisingly fine,” he said. “I uh. I ended up taking care of it with my bat.”

They still weren’t sure what, precisely, Greenway had been intending to do with the Propofol. Not because Greenway wouldn’t tell them anything, but because he simply couldn’t. Not very easily or very well, at least. According to Athena, Eddie had managed to break his jaw, and it was now wired shut until the bones healed.

No tears were being shed. He hoped the man’s smoothies tasted the way his high school locker room smelled.

“It felt good, actually,” Eddie continued, and when he spotted Frank’s look of concern, hurried to clarify, “that there was something there that time. That it wasn’t just my mind or whatever. That there was more than the wind or a raccoon, and that I could do something.”

“I can imagine that it would be a relief,” the other man said. “Do you want to talk about it? Go over what happened?”

Eddie mulled it over, chewing on the inside of his lip as he did so. He’d talked about it on the night of, recounting it to the police that had responded. But he hadn’t really dwelled on it, hadn’t really focused on what had happened except for the bits that needed fixing because of it.

“Yeah, uh. I think I would,” he said, nodding. “Buck’s sister called, panicking because someone had broken into her place- Yeah, I know. He left to check on her, and I stayed in bed, because I had work in the morning and we didn’t want to wake up Chris…”

Sometime in the months since he started, so gradually that Eddie had missed it, the sessions with Frank became less of a mental dental appointment and instead turned into something he was looking forward to. He wasn’t sure when the change happened, but it did. At some point he had started voluntarily offering up his thoughts and feelings, instead of Frank having to pull it out of him like an abscessed tooth.

At the end of the fifty minutes - after talking about Greenway, and how he had felt during and afterward; after talking about his looming return to fighting fires rather than posting tweets, and how he felt about that - he left Frank’s office feeling lighter than when he went in.

“Done already?” Buck asked, looking up from his phone. As always, Chris had his homework sprawled across one of the tables.

“Yup,” Eddie said, nodding. “What are you working on?”

“Study guide for my test next week,” his kid said, frowning down at it.

“No smiley hearts this time?” he asked, and Chris shook his head with a laugh.

“C’mon, let’s get dad his reward of a greasy burger,” Buck said, helping him sweep everything back into his bag. 

“Are you sure you want to have food in your new car?” Eddie asked, picking up Chris’ bag and following along behind him.

“I’m figuring the food stains are inevitable,” Buck told him. “Honestly, I should just pour hot coffee on one of the seats and be done with it.”

“But they’re such fine Corinthian leather.”

“Eds, we talked about this on the way here. The seats aren’t leather.”

“It was a very lovely ceremony,” Buck said, brushing dirt off the stone. His leg was feeling much better than it had immediately after the car accident, especially when he actually remembered to take his pain meds on time, but it still complained about the kneeling. “I want to say you would have liked it and that you should have been there, but honestly? You probably would have gotten one of us in trouble for laughing.”

It was the Tuesday before Eddie was scheduled to start back at the station. Over the weekend, they’d attended what was supposed to have been Toni’s wedding to Clive. Except, she’d changed her mind at some point in the process and turned it into a vow renewal ceremony for Hen and Karen, instead. A change in plans that apparently her daughter and daughter-in-law had evidently not been informed about until the day of.

It was the Tuesday before Eddie was scheduled back at the station, and Buck wasn’t quite certain why he had decided to come here of all places. It would have almost made sense yesterday, as yesterday had been Memorial Day. But today he’d been home alone - always a dangerous prospect - and he had started thinking - also dangerous - and… Well, here he was.

“I wish I could have known you,” he told Shannon’s headstone. “This you, I mean. I miss the other you. I miss the others, too, though not as much as I did when I first came back. I don’t feel quite like a ghost anymore. Or like they’re all ghosts.”

Not that Buck didn’t miss the others like an ache in his chest. The other Eddie, the other Chris. The Bobby and Athena that had known what he went through, at least most of it. It still caught him off guard at times, the differences between Here and There. Like how this Maddie and Chimney were on the cusp of either falling apart entirely or working things out, while the other versions of themselves had been going strong at Christmas.

He should make a point of introducing Ravi and Albert again, now that he thought about it. Even if the latter wasn’t a firefighter. Their other selves had seemed to get along well enough.

“The other you was fiery,” Buck reminisced. “And I’m pretty sure she didn’t like me at first. To be fair, I didn’t like her much at first, either. But we got there eventually. I still don’t quite understand how you could have left Chris behind but… But I can definitely understand running away. Eddie - the other Eddie - once joked about a club for people who ran away. We never did get around to holding elections for president, though.”

A flicker of color in the corner of his eye caught his attention. A shade of vibrant blue that he immediately recognized, and he watched as that damnable pest fluttered through the air to land on the stone in front of him. He glared at it, resisting the urge to try and smash it, because he knew he’d only end up ramming his fist into the stone.

Buck still didn’t know what the thing had been trying to say, that day in Eddie’s living room. A warning, perhaps? A request for him to fix something he had sent off the rails? A simple desire for chaos? All three or perhaps none of those? He wasn’t sure, he didn’t care, and he didn’t think it mattered anymore. Jonah Greenway wasn’t going to be a problem for anyone except whichever person got stuck in a cell with him, and it was managed without Buck having to do anything.

The fact that Eddie had figured out what he’d done to the other Doug, through the context of the news clips somehow broadcasted through time and space, along with his nightmares, was agonizing. The fact that Eddie knew and accepted and didn’t judge, was… Well. It probably shouldn’t have left him feeling as awed as it did. The other Eddie hadn’t judged him either.

Both times, it had been an absolution he did not deserve.

“And it seems we have an uninvited guest,” Buck grumbled, as if Shannon could actually hear him. “Shoo. Seriously, you’re not wan- Ah!”

The shriek he let out as he toppled over backwards was not at all manly. The butterfly had suddenly flown straight in his face, landing on the tip of his nose. His eyes went cross as he focused on it, not daring to breathe. The blue of its wings took up the entirety of his vision, so very similar to that last night on the crossroads that he was half-expecting his body to be lit with agony again.

Except nothing happened. A moment passed, and then another, and then a third. And then, as suddenly as it appeared, the insect that wasn’t an insect lifted off from him and fluttered away. Buck twisted around, to see where it had gone, and saw nothing but the gray of the other headstones surrounding them and the orange of his SUV a few feet away.

“I will never understand what the hell that thing wants. I swear, it just likes bothering me,” he complained, sitting back up. Letting out a sigh, he turned to face Shannon’s headstone again. “It’s probably a good enough sign for me to head back, though. Got an important date, y’know.”

Buck stood up and brushed off his trousers, reaching out to rest a hand on the sun warmed stone.

“It was nice talking to you, Shan,” he said, smiling slightly. “Thanks for listening.”

According to the time on his car’s dash, Buck had just enough time to get home from the cemetery and set up. Chris was at school, and Eddie was at his last day of working dispatch - apparently they’d thrown a farewell party for him, which the accounting firm next door had invited themselves to - so casa de Diaz was empty when he pulled up. He threw off his shoes and grabbed his tablet, getting comfortable at the dining table. At 2pm, exactly, the screen lit up with a video call.

“Good afternoon, Evan,” Dr. Copeland said, the blond woman smiling at him when he answered. “It’s been quite a while.”

“Yeah, it uh, it really has,” he agreed, laughing awkwardly.

Buck wasn’t certain what, exactly, had convinced him to give therapy yet another go. Perhaps it was seeing Eddie flourishing with Frank, or perhaps it was having an actual panic attack in the middle of a call, or perhaps it had been how his own subconscious brought the subject up. Perhaps it was a combination of all of those things.

There was no shame, absolutely none, in needing help. No matter the form it had to take.

“Well. How about we catch up a bit?”

“Oh, God, where do I even begin?” He was definitely not going to talk to her about spending four to five years in an alternate reality. “Uh, I have a boyfriend now…”

“Excited for your big day?”

Eddie looked up at the question, pausing in the middle of making sure he had everything he needed in his duffel. Buck was leaning on the doorframe of their bedroom, smiling softly at him.

“I am,” he answered. “Excited and nervous. And a little…”

“A little scared?” Buck guessed, and Eddie nodded. The other man crossed the short distance between them, wrapping his arms around him. “There’s no shame in that.”

“I know,” Eddie said, stealing a quick kiss. “But thank you for reminding me.”

They stayed like that for a bit, not talking, simply holding each other. He would have happily stayed like that for the rest of the day, except they both had places to be. So, as much as it pained them both, he pulled away.

“I wish you were going in with me,” Eddie confessed.

“Me, too,” Buck said. “Soon, though.”

His ribs weren’t broken from the crash, but they’d been hurt badly enough that the doctor hadn’t wanted him doing anything that would strain them too much. It would not do, it had been pointed out, to be in the middle of trying to carry someone to safety only for his ribs to actually break. And his leg, too, had been a source of concern. Though, it was healed enough that Buck no longer winced when he put weight on it.

Buck was taking Chris to school a bit early, something about him wanting to talk to a teacher up there about something -”It’s fine, Eds, it’s nothing. I was, uh… Wanting to know about joining the PTA!” - so Eddie had plenty of time to see his kid off. 

“Remember kiddo, I won’t be home when you get out of school,” he told him.

“Because you’ll be busy saving people again,” Chris said, giving a decisive nod.

“That’s right.”

Chris wrapped him in a hug as best that he could while keeping a grip on his crutches.

“Be safe,” he demanded, before following a smiling Buck out the door.

Eddie stood on the porch, watching them climb into the obnoxiously colored car, and waved at them as they pulled away. When he imagined this day, when he imagined returning to the station, it had been with Buck by his side. But clearly, fate had different plans.

Oh well, like, the man had said: Soon.

With time left before he himself needed to be on the road, he did a quick check of his duffel. Clothes to change into at the end of his shift - he was already wearing his uniform, habit from working at dispatch - and a set of clothes to work out in. A towel; deodorant and various other toiletries; his comb. And most importantly of all, the notes from the lunches Buck had packed him since the fire at dispatch.

He wasn’t going to get any more of them, he knew. Buck had not packed him a lunch for the day, because he was going to get Bobby’s food again. Eddie had almost, stupidly and sappily, asked him to write a note anyways. Just because.

Buck probably would have, is the thing.

The drive to the station was achingly, pleasantly familiar. It didn’t take him long at all to pull into one of the parking spaces, and then he just sat there and stared at the building proper. The last time he’d been inside - not counting when he'd shown up to pick up Buck, or to have lunch after the dispatch fire - he had been emptying his things out of the locker he shared with Buck. At that point, Buck had been missing for days, already snatched away by Ichabod.

What a change a few months could make.

Taking a deep and fortifying breath, Eddie climbed out of his truck and grabbed his bag. He could not decide if he wanted to rush through the bay doors or make the moment last, but either way, it was a short journey into the bay. The inside of the station had not changed at all, though he knew that already. It just felt like maybe it should look different, somehow.

There was already a new ladder truck, sitting prettily on the bay floor. Hopefully this one would last longer than a couple years.

Making his way to the locker room, he paused in front of all the turnout gear hanging up. He paused because his eyes had caught his own name. It wasn’t the gear locker it had originally been stuck to, though that was to be expected. But his name was there, along with his helmet and his coat, and it was like a weight was removed at the sight of them.

Things were as they should have been, or they were getting there.

He had been sharing a locker with Buck before Christmas, and Eddie did not see the need to change that now. There was no need, after all, to try and separate their things like that. He opened up and gently stuffed his bag inside, but not before pulling out the notes from Buck and a roll of scotch tape.

When he’d been working at Metro Dispatch, before the fire, he had stuck all of the Post-It notes with the awful puns to his computer monitor. Now, he diligently set about taping them to the inside of the locker door, around the pictures that were already placed upon it.

“Cap’s waiting for you upstairs,” a semi-familiar voice said, and he looked up from his work to see Lucy Donato leaning halfway into the locker room. “Something about paperwork you need to fill out?”

“Ay Dios, more?” he grumbled, ripping off the last piece of tape and slapping it on, before tossing the dispenser into the locker. “I thought I was done.”

“Don’t look at me, I’m just the messenger,” she said, crossing the room to peer at the notes. “Feeling blue without you,” she read aloud. “Aw. Isn’t that Buckley’s handwriting?”

“Yes,” Eddie said, shutting the door with perhaps a bit too much force. “So, paperwork?”

“Like I said, I’m just the messenger,” she told him, following along as he made his way to the loft stairs.

“So you told him you were joining the PTA?” he heard Chimney ask, mocking laughter lacing the words.

“Yes!” The sound of Buck’s voice had him pausing on the steps. What was Buck doing here? “I didn’t know what else to say, okay? I’m awful at straight-up lying. But it worked!”

“Buck?” Eddie asked, cresting the top of the stairs. Everyone - Hen and Ravi and Buck and Chimney - froze where they stood, staring at him like a quartet of startled meerkats.

“Oh shit. Uh. Surprise!”

Hanging on the back wall, out of sight from the bay, was a giant banner reading “welcome back” in large and glittery letters. On the dining table was a cake that came complete with a firetruck topping, along with - of all things - a figurine of Batman.

“Lucy, you were supposed to give us a warning,” Hen complained, and the other woman shrugged without concern.

“Sorry. Heads up, Diaz is here,” she said, tone utterly flat. “What kind of cake is that?”

“So you lied about wanting to join the PTA?” Eddie asked as he sauntered up to his partner, smiling too much to pull off the faux-disapproval he was aiming for.

“There’s only a week left in the school year, Eds,” Buck pointed, wrapping his arms around him. “Pretty sure it’s too late to sign up for anything PTA related.”

“That’s… Yeah, okay, that’s on me,” he admitted with a sigh. He pointed at the cake. “What’s with the cake toppers? I get the firetruck, but the other one?”

“I thought it was obvious,” Buck said, plucking the figurine out of the icing. “He’s you. Bat-man.”

Eddie could not help the laughter, even as he rolled his eyes.

“That was your idea, wasn’t it?”

“It might have been.”

“So this is what Cap wanted me up here for? There’s not actually any more paperwork to fill out?”

Whatever relief Eddie had been feeling at the prospect was burst when Bobby announced his presence by throwing a small sheaf of paper onto the table beside the cake, along with a pen.

“Oh, there’s paperwork,” the captain told him, smirking at the groan he let out. “It’s relationship related. You can fill it out while we eat.”

It was not much to fill out, mostly signing and initialing, and he honestly didn’t pay much attention to what anything was saying. What was he going to do if he didn’t like what it said, not sign the forms and potentially have to switch shifts or stations? Yeah, no. So he signed and initialed in between bites of cakes and joking with the others, feeling more settled with himself than he had in literal months.

The alarm rang, not long after he was done, and the shrill noise of it had him leaping to his feet on unforgotten instinct. He started for the firepole, only to pause and look back.

“Go,” Buck told him, smiling brightly. “I’ll be with you soon, remember?”

Eddie nodded and grabbed hold of the pole, throwing him a mock salute before sliding down, just to hear him laugh.

And just two weeks later - after the doctor had given the all clear - Buck was following him down. Exactly the way it should have been.

 

Fin.
